    Size, length and numel
    

    

    M = [10 20 30 40; 15 25 35 45; 50 60 70 80];
size(M)             % [rows columns]
[nr, nc] = size(M)
size(M, 1)          % number of rows only
length(M)           % the largest dimension
numel(M)            % total number of elements
    

    ans =

   3   4

nr = 3
nc = 4
ans = 3
ans = 4
ans = 12
    

    
  
    For audio, size(x, 1) is the number of samples and size(x, 2) the number of channels. Avoid length for matrices: for a 2-sample stereo signal (2×2) or a 1-sample 6-channel array it returns the wrong dimension. numel is the clearest choice for vectors.
    
  
    Transposing, reshaping and concatenating
    

    

    r = [1 2 3];
r'                        % the apostrophe transposes: row becomes column
z = [1+2i 3-1i];
z'                        % ' also conjugates complex numbers
z.'                       % .' transposes without conjugating
reshape(1:6, 2, 3)        % fills column by column
    

    ans =

   1
   2
   3

ans =

   1 - 2i
   3 + 1i

ans =

   1 + 2i
   3 - 1i

ans =

   1   3   5
   2   4   6
    

    

    

    a = [1 2 3];  b = [4 5 6];
[a b]                     % side by side
[a; b]                    % one above the other
right = [15 20 30 45 60 65];
left  = [10 15 20 25 35 40];
A = [right; left];        % a 2x6 audiogram matrix: row 1 right, row 2 left
size(A)
    

    ans =

   1   2   3   4   5   6

ans =

   1   2   3
   4   5   6

ans =

   2   6
    

    
  
    Concatenation is how stimulus sequences are built: [tone; gap; tone] stacks three column vectors into one longer column (section 10). The pieces must have compatible sizes: all columns, or all rows.
    
  
    Element-wise and matrix operators
    
  
    This distinction causes more beginner errors than anything else in MATLAB. The operators *, / and ^ are matrix operations from linear algebra. Putting a dot in front, .*, ./ and .^, makes them element-wise: each element is combined with the element in the same position. Signal processing almost always wants element-wise operations: applying a ramp to a tone, squaring samples for RMS, dividing two spectra.
    
  
    
    
    Element-wiseMatrixRule of thumb
    
    
      
    a .* ba * bMultiplying two signals or a signal by a window: use .*
    
      
    a ./ ba / bDividing two vectors element by element: use ./
    
      
    a .^ 2a ^ 2Squaring every sample: use .^
    
      
    +, -(same)Addition and subtraction are always element-wise
    
      
    scalar * array(same)Multiplying by a single number needs no dot: 0.5 * x
    
        
  
    

    

    a = [1 2 3];  b = [4 5 6];
a .* b            % element by element
a * b'            % row times column: the dot product 1*4 + 2*5 + 3*6
a .^ 2
2 * a             % scalar times array: no dot needed
    

    ans =

    4   10   18

ans = 32
ans =

   1   4   9

ans =

   2   4   6
    

    

    

    a = [1 2 3];  b = [4 5 6];
a * b
    

    error: operator *: nonconformant arguments (op1 is 1x3, op2 is 1x3)
    

    
  
    MATLAB's message for the same mistake reads "Incorrect dimensions for matrix multiplication" and suggests using .* for element-wise multiplication. The size mismatch message for addition reads "Arrays have incompatible sizes for this operation" in recent MATLAB releases; older releases said "Matrix dimensions must agree".
    

    

    thr = [20 25 35 50 60 65];
corr_db = [0 0 5 5 10];        % one value short
thr + corr_db
    

    error: operator +: nonconformant arguments (op1 is 1x6, op2 is 1x5)
    

    

    

    thr = [20 25 35 50 60 65];
thr(7)
    

    error: thr(7): out of bound 6 (dimensions are 1x6)
    

    
  
    In MATLAB the last error reads "Index exceeds the number of array elements. Index must not exceed 6." It usually means a loop runs one step too far or a file had fewer samples than expected.
    
  
    Implicit expansion
    
  
    Since R2016b MATLAB (and Octave) automatically expands a row and a column to a full matrix when they are combined. This is convenient (subtracting each column's mean from a matrix) but can hide mistakes: adding a 1×6 row to a 6×1 column gives a 6×6 matrix instead of an error.
    

    

    row = [1 2 3];
col = [10; 20];
row + col          % a 2x3 matrix, no error
    

    ans =

   11   12   13
   21   22   23
    

    
  
    Audiology example: an audiogram as a 2 × 6 matrix
    
  
    Store the audiogram with one row per ear and one column per frequency. Then the pure-tone average (PTA) is a mean over selected columns, and interaural asymmetry is the absolute difference between the rows.
    


    

    f = [250 500 1000 2000 4000 8000];        % Hz
A = [15 20 30 45 60 65;                   % right ear, dB HL
     10 15 20 25 35 40];                  % left ear, dB HL
pta3 = mean(A(:, 2:4), 2)                 % 500, 1000, 2000 Hz; mean along each row
cols4 = ismember(f, [500 1000 2000 4000]);
pta4 = mean(A(:, cols4), 2)               % four-frequency average
asym = abs(A(1, :) - A(2, :))             % right minus left, per frequency
f(asym >= 15)                             % frequencies with a difference of 15 dB or more
    

    pta3 =

   31.667
   20.000

pta4 =

   38.750
   23.750

asym =

    5    5   10   20   25   25

ans =

   2000   4000   8000
    

    
  
    mean(X, 2) averages along dimension 2 (across columns), giving one value per row, that is one PTA per ear. ismember builds the column mask from the frequency list, so the code still works if the frequencies are stored in a different order. The asymmetry here is 15 dB or more at 2, 4 and 8 kHz. Criteria for flagging an asymmetry for further investigation vary between clinical protocols (the size of difference, how many frequencies, which frequencies), so the rule is a parameter you choose and document, not something MATLAB decides.
    

  
    
    
    Try it: indexing visualiser
    
    
    The matrix A holds right-ear thresholds (dB HL) of four patients (rows) at six frequencies (columns). Choose an expression: the selected cells are outlined, numbered in the order they appear in the result, and the output is shown as Octave printed it.
    
    
    
      
    
      
    show (n) in each cell
    
      
    A = [15 20 30 45 60 65; 10 15 25 40 55 70; 20 30 45 60 70 80; 5 10 10 15 35 50];
    
    
    
    
    
    
    
      
    Result
    
      
    Explanation
    
    
    
  
    

  
    6. Built-in functions for data
    
  
    MATLAB's data functions work down the columns of a matrix by default (dimension 1) and along a vector whichever way it points. An optional dimension argument chooses the direction: mean(A, 2) works across each row. The examples use a single right-ear audiogram and then the two-ear matrix.
    



    

    f = [250 500 1000 2000 4000 8000];
thr = [20 25 35 50 60 65];
sum(thr)
mean(thr)
median(thr)
std(thr)           % sample standard deviation (divides by N - 1)
    

    ans = 255
ans = 42.500
ans = 42.500
ans = 18.641
    

    
  
    max and min with the index
    
  
    With two outputs, max and min also return the position of the extreme value, which you then use to look up the corresponding frequency. The square brackets on the left collect several outputs.
    

    

    [worst, k] = max(thr)
f(k)                               % frequency of the worst threshold
[best, k2] = min(thr);
fprintf('Best threshold %d dB HL at %d Hz\n', best, f(k2))
A = [15 20 30 45 60 65; 10 15 20 25 35 40];
max(A)                             % column-wise: the poorer ear at each frequency
max(A, [], 2)                      % row-wise: the worst threshold of each ear
min(A)                             % the better ear at each frequency
    

    worst = 65
k = 6
ans = 8000
Best threshold 20 dB HL at 250 Hz
ans =

   15   20   30   45   60   65

ans =

   65
   40

ans =

   10   15   20   25   35   40
    

    
  
    min(A) gives the better-ear audiogram frequency by frequency, which is what better-ear averages such as the WHO grading (section 8) are based on. Note the empty [] placeholder in max(A, [], 2): the second input position is reserved for comparing two arrays.
    
  
    sort, cumsum and diff
    

    

    [s, order] = sort(thr, 'descend')
f(order)                  % frequencies from worst to best threshold
cumsum([1 2 3 4])         % running total
diff(thr)                 % change from one frequency to the next (dB per octave here)
[steepest, k] = max(diff(thr));
fprintf('Steepest slope %d dB/octave between %d and %d Hz\n', steepest, f(k), f(k+1))
    

    s =

   65   60   50   35   25   20

order =

   6   5   4   3   2   1

ans =

   8000   4000   2000   1000    500    250

ans =

    1    3    6   10

ans =

    5   10   15   10    5

Steepest slope 15 dB/octave between 1000 and 2000 Hz
    

    
  
    Because the audiometric frequencies are one octave apart, diff(thr) is the slope in dB per octave. A steep slope is relevant for hearing-aid fitting and for identifying possible cochlear dead-region candidates, which require separate testing.
    
  
    any, all and isempty
    

    

    any(thr > 60)             % is at least one threshold above 60?
all(thr <= 25)            % are all thresholds within 25 dB HL?
k = find(thr > 90);
isempty(k)                % true: no threshold above 90 dB HL
    

    ans = 1
ans = 0
ans = 1
    

    
  
    Missing values
    
  
    If one frequency was not tested, store NaN. Summary functions then return NaN unless you add the 'omitnan' flag (MATLAB and recent Octave).
    

    

    thr_m = [20 25 NaN 50 60 65];
mean(thr_m)
mean(thr_m, 'omitnan')
mean(thr_m(~isnan(thr_m)))     % the same, works in every version
    

    ans = NaN
ans = 44
ans = 44
    

    
  
    Rounding to 5 dB steps
    
  
    Audiometers present levels in 5 dB steps. To round any computed level to the nearest step, divide by 5, round, and multiply by 5. The same pattern rounds to any step size.
    

    

    x = [31.7 42.4 47.5 12.49];
5 * round(x / 5)
2 * round(x / 2)           % nearest 2 dB, for comparison
    

    ans =

   30   40   50   10

ans =

   32   42   48   12
    

    
  
    Interpolation with interp1
    
  
    Thresholds are often needed at frequencies that were not measured, for example 3 kHz and 6 kHz when only octaves were tested (the inter-octave frequencies are often tested in practice, but older records may lack them). interp1(x, y, xq) interpolates the curve y(x) at the query points xq. Because audiograms are plotted on a logarithmic frequency axis, interpolating in log-frequency is more natural than in linear frequency. The two methods give different answers.
    

    

    fq = [3000 6000];
lin_f = interp1(f, thr, fq)                     % linear in frequency
log_f = interp1(log2(f), thr, log2(fq))         % linear in log-frequency (octaves)
5 * round(log_f / 5)                            % as a 5 dB step value
    

    lin_f =

   55.000   62.500

log_f =

   55.850   62.925

ans =

   55   65
    

    
  
    At 3 kHz the linear-frequency value is exactly halfway between the 2 kHz and 4 kHz thresholds (55 dB HL); in log-frequency, 3 kHz lies 0.585 octave above 2 kHz (log2(1.5) = 0.585), so the estimate is 50 + 0.585 × 10 = 55.85 dB HL. interp1 returns NaN for query points outside the measured range unless you ask it to extrapolate, which is sensible: extrapolating an audiogram is rarely justified.
    
  
    Clinical linkInterpolated values are estimates for analysis and simulation, for example for a hearing-loss simulation or to fill a prescription formula's input. They never replace measured thresholds in a clinical record, and the method used should be stated in a report.
    

  
    7. Scripts and functions
    
  
    Typing into the Command Window is fine for exploring, but the commands disappear from view and cannot easily be repeated. Real work is saved in .m files of two kinds.
    
  
    
    
    Script
    • A list of commands saved in a file, run from top to bottom as if typed.
    • Uses and creates variables in the base Workspace, where you can inspect them afterwards.
    • No inputs or outputs: to change a setting you edit the file.
    • Best for one complete analysis: "load these data, compute, plot, save".
    
    
    Function
    • A file that starts with the keyword function, takes inputs and returns outputs.
    • Has its own private workspace: variables inside do not appear in, or depend on, the base Workspace.
    • Reusable: call it many times with different inputs, from scripts, other functions or the Command Window.
    • Best for one well-defined operation: "make a tone", "compute RMS", "mix at an SNR".
    
  
    
  
    Creating and running a script
    
  
      
    
    1. HOME > New Script (or type edit analyse_pta). An empty Editor tab opens.
      2. 
    
    2. Type the commands below. Lines starting with % are comments: MATLAB ignores them; they explain the code to humans, including you in six months.
      3. 
    
    3. Save as analyse_pta.m in the current folder. The file name must be a valid variable name (no spaces or hyphens, not starting with a digit) and must not clash with an existing function name.
      4. 
    
    4. Run it with EDITOR > Run (or F5), or by typing analyse_pta in the Command Window. The results print in the Command Window and the variables appear in the Workspace.
      5. 
    
    5. Lines starting with %% divide the script into sections. Place the cursor in a section and use EDITOR > Run Section (Ctrl+Enter on Windows and Linux, Cmd+Enter on macOS) to run only that section. This is the normal way to develop a script piece by piece.
      6. 
  
    


    

    %% analyse_pta.m  Pure-tone average and asymmetry for one patient
% Author: (your name)    Date: (date)
% Thresholds are air-conduction, dB HL, unmasked or masked as recorded.

%% Data
f = [250 500 1000 2000 4000 8000];     % test frequencies, Hz
right = [15 20 30 45 60 65];           % dB HL
left  = [10 15 20 25 35 40];           % dB HL

%% Pure-tone averages (500, 1000, 2000 and 4000 Hz)
use = ismember(f, [500 1000 2000 4000]);
pta_r = mean(right(use));
pta_l = mean(left(use));
fprintf('PTA4 right %.1f dB HL, left %.1f dB HL\n', pta_r, pta_l);

%% Asymmetry check (criterion chosen for this example: 15 dB or more)
d = abs(right - left);
fprintf('Asymmetry of 15 dB or more at: %s Hz\n', mat2str(f(d >= 15)));
    

    PTA4 right 38.8 dB HL, left 23.8 dB HL
Asymmetry of 15 dB or more at: [2000 4000 8000] Hz
    

    
  
    Comments are also used to switch code off temporarily: select lines and use EDITOR > Comment (Ctrl+R on Windows) and Uncomment (Ctrl+T). Three dots ... at the end of a line continue a long command on the next line.
    
  
    Anatomy of a function
    
  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.4d. Anatomy of a function file. The caller sees only the inputs it passes and the outputs it receives; every other variable inside the function is private.
    
  
    A function that makes a tone: make_tone
    
  
    This function is used throughout the rest of the topic. It creates a pure tone with a chosen frequency, duration, sampling rate and RMS level, with raised-cosine (cosine-squared) onset and offset ramps. Ramps are needed because switching a tone on or off instantly produces a click with energy spread over a wide frequency range (spectral splatter), which a listener may hear and which defeats frequency-specific testing. Ramps of about 5 to 25 ms are typical for audiometric and psychoacoustic tones; brief tone-burst stimuli for evoked potentials use shorter ramps chosen as a trade-off between frequency specificity and neural synchrony.
    
  
    x(n) = A sin(2π f n / fs),   n = 0, 1, ..., N-1
A    = √2 × 10^(L/20)
r(k) = 0.5 (1 - cos(π k / Nr)),   k = 0, 1, ..., Nr-1
where f is the tone frequency (Hz), fs the sampling rate (Hz), N the number of samples, L the RMS level in dBFS (dB re an RMS of 1), A the peak amplitude and r(k) the onset ramp of Nr samples (applied reversed at the offset).
    
  
    The factor √2 converts an RMS value to the peak of a sine. On the dB-re-RMS-of-1 scale used here, a full-scale sine (peak 1) has an RMS of 0.7071 and a level of −3.01 dBFS. (Some meters, following the AES17 convention, call a full-scale sine 0 dBFS; always state which convention you use.)
    

    

    function [x, t] = make_tone(f_hz, dur_s, fs_hz, level_dbfs, ramp_ms)
%MAKE_TONE  Pure tone with raised-cosine onset and offset ramps.
%   x = MAKE_TONE(f_hz, dur_s, fs_hz, level_dbfs, ramp_ms) returns a
%   column vector containing a sine tone of frequency f_hz (Hz) and
%   duration dur_s (s), sampled at fs_hz (Hz). level_dbfs is the RMS
%   level of the steady part in dB re an RMS of 1 (a full-scale sine is
%   -3.01 dBFS on this scale). ramp_ms is the length of each
%   raised-cosine ramp in ms. Defaults: level -20 dBFS, ramps 10 ms.
%   [x, t] = MAKE_TONE(...) also returns the time vector in seconds.
%
%   Example:  x = make_tone(1000, 0.5, 44100, -20, 10);

if nargin < 5, ramp_ms = 10; end
if nargin < 4, level_dbfs = -20; end

N = round(dur_s * fs_hz);                  % number of samples
t = (0:N-1)' / fs_hz;                      % time vector, s (column)
amp = sqrt(2) * 10^(level_dbfs / 20);      % peak amplitude for this RMS
x = amp * sin(2*pi*f_hz*t);

nr = round(ramp_ms / 1000 * fs_hz);        % samples in each ramp
if nr > 0
    if 2*nr > N
        error('make_tone:ramp', 'Ramps are longer than the tone.');
    end
    r = 0.5 * (1 - cos(pi * (0:nr-1)' / nr));   % rises from 0 towards 1
    x(1:nr) = x(1:nr) .* r;                     % onset ramp
    x(end-nr+1:end) = x(end-nr+1:end) .* flipud(r);   % offset ramp
end
end
    

    

    

    function r = sigrms(x)
%SIGRMS  Root-mean-square value of each column of x.
%   r = SIGRMS(x) returns sqrt(mean(x.^2)) for each column.
r = sqrt(mean(x .^ 2, 1));
end
    

    

    

    function L = dbfs(x)
%DBFS  RMS level of each column of x in dB re an RMS of 1.
%   A full-scale sine wave is -3.01 dBFS on this scale.
L = 20 * log10(sigrms(x));
end
    

    
  
    The functions are named sigrms and dbfs on purpose. Recent MATLAB releases include a function called rms (in older releases it came with the Signal Processing Toolbox), and Octave 8 has none. Writing your own file called rms.m would shadow MATLAB's version for every script in that folder, a source of subtle bugs. which rms tells you whether a name is already taken.
    

    

    fs = 44100;
[x, t] = make_tone(1000, 0.5, fs, -20, 10);
size(x)
fprintf('duration %.3f s, peak %.4f\n', numel(x) / fs, max(abs(x)))
nr = round(0.010 * fs);                    % ramp length in samples
fprintf('steady part: %.2f dBFS\n', dbfs(x(nr+1:end-nr)))
fprintf('whole tone including ramps: %.2f dBFS\n', dbfs(x))
fprintf('full-scale sine: %.2f dBFS\n', dbfs(sin(2*pi*1000*t)))
y = make_tone(500, 0.2, 16000);            % defaults for level and ramps
fprintf('default level: %.2f dBFS\n', dbfs(y(161:end-160)))
    

    ans =

   22050       1

duration 0.500 s, peak 0.1414
steady part: -20.00 dBFS
whole tone including ramps: -20.11 dBFS
full-scale sine: -3.01 dBFS
default level: -20.00 dBFS
    

    
  
    The ramps lower the overall RMS slightly (here by about 0.1 dB) because the tone is quieter during the 20 ms of ramps. Calibration and level statements should therefore refer to the steady part, or to a long calibration tone (section 10).
    
  
    Input and output arguments
    
  
      
    
    • Inputs are matched by position: make_tone(1000, 0.5, 44100) sets f_hz, dur_s and fs_hz. nargin inside the function reports how many were given, which is how defaults are set.
      • 
    
    • Outputs are requested with square brackets: [x, t] = make_tone(...). If you ask for only one, you get the first. Use ~ to ignore an output: [~, k] = max(thr).
      • 
    
    • MATLAB passes inputs by value: changing an input inside a function never changes the caller's variable. To "modify" a signal you return the new version: x = apply_gain(x, 6).
      • 
    
    • Recent MATLAB releases add an arguments block for validating inputs and giving name-value options; it is not supported in Octave, so nargin checks and validateattributes are the portable approach.
      • 
  
    
  
    Local functions
    
  
    A function file can contain additional local functions after the main one; they are visible only inside that file. Since R2016b, MATLAB also allows local functions at the end of a script. Octave does not accept that layout (in Octave, functions in a script must be defined before they are used, and the script must not start with a function definition), so for portable code put each reusable function in its own file.
    

    

    %% level_report.m (script)
x = make_tone(1000, 1, 44100, -20, 10);
fprintf('Peak-to-RMS ratio (crest factor): %.2f dB\n', crest_db(x));

function c = crest_db(x)
% local function: visible only inside this file
c = 20*log10(max(abs(x)) / sqrt(mean(x.^2)));
end
    

    
  
    Function handles and anonymous functions
    
  
    A function handle, written with @, is a variable that refers to a function. An anonymous function is a one-line function defined without a file: @(inputs) expression. They are ideal for short conversions and for passing a function to another function.
    

    

    db20   = @(ratio) 20*log10(ratio);        % amplitude ratio to dB
undb20 = @(db) 10.^(db/20);               % dB to amplitude ratio
spl    = @(p_pa) 20*log10(p_pa / 20e-6);  % pressure to dB SPL
octs   = @(f, fref) log2(f ./ fref);      % octaves between frequencies
db20(2)
undb20([-6 0 6 20])
spl(0.2)
octs([500 3000 8000], 1000)
    

    ans = 6.0206
ans =

    0.5012    1.0000    1.9953   10.0000

ans = 80
ans =

  -1.0000   1.5850   3.0000
    

    

    

    fh = @make_tone;                          % handle to our file function
x = fh(2000, 0.1, 16000, -30, 5);
numel(x)
f = [500 1000 2000 4000];
lv = arrayfun(@(fq) dbfs(make_tone(fq, 0.2, 16000, -30, 0)), f)
    

    ans = 1600
lv =

  -30  -30  -30  -30
    

    
  
    arrayfun applies a function to each element of an array and collects the results; here it makes a tone at each frequency and returns each level (all −30 dBFS, because the tones contain whole numbers of cycles and have no ramps). Function handles are also how callbacks in App Designer (section 14) and optimisation functions such as fzero receive the code they should run.
    

  
    8. Control flow
    
  
    Scripts so far run from top to bottom. Control flow statements make decisions (if, switch) and repeat work (for, while). Each block ends with the keyword end. Indent the lines inside a block (the Editor does it automatically with Ctrl+I) so the structure is visible.
    
  
    if, elseif, else
    
  
    Grading hearing loss from a pure-tone average is a natural first example. The scale below is the one in the WHO World Report on Hearing (2021), which uses the better-ear average of 0.5, 1, 2 and 4 kHz. It is used here only as an example: different clinics and countries use different scales and boundary conventions, and the code makes the chosen scale explicit and easy to change.
    
  
    
    
    Better-ear PTA (0.5, 1, 2, 4 kHz)Grade (WHO 2021)
    
    
      
    less than 20 dB HLNormal hearing
    
      
    20 to less than 35Mild
    
      
    35 to less than 50Moderate
    
      
    50 to less than 65Moderately severe
    
      
    65 to less than 80Severe
    
      
    80 to less than 95Profound
    
      
    95 or moreComplete or total
    
        
  
    

    

    function g = who_grade(pta_db)
%WHO_GRADE  Hearing-loss grade from a better-ear 4-frequency PTA.
%   g = WHO_GRADE(pta_db) returns the grade as text, using the scale of
%   the WHO World Report on Hearing (2021). Example scale only; check the
%   scale required by your service.
if isnan(pta_db)
    g = 'not available';
elseif pta_db < 20
    g = 'normal hearing';
elseif pta_db < 35
    g = 'mild';
elseif pta_db < 50
    g = 'moderate';
elseif pta_db < 65
    g = 'moderately severe';
elseif pta_db < 80
    g = 'severe';
elseif pta_db < 95
    g = 'profound';
else
    g = 'complete or total';
end
end
    

    
  
    MATLAB tests the conditions in order and runs only the first block whose condition is true; later conditions are not checked. That is why each elseif only needs the upper boundary. The order matters: if the pta_db < 95 test came first, every PTA below 95 would be called profound.
    

    

    pta = [12.5 23.75 38.75 50 71.25 96.25 NaN];
for k = 1:numel(pta)
    fprintf('PTA %6.2f dB HL: %s\n', pta(k), who_grade(pta(k)));
end
    

    PTA  12.50 dB HL: normal hearing
PTA  23.75 dB HL: mild
PTA  38.75 dB HL: moderate
PTA  50.00 dB HL: moderately severe
PTA  71.25 dB HL: severe
PTA  96.25 dB HL: complete or total
PTA    NaN dB HL: not available
    

    
  
    for loops
    
  
    for k = values runs the block once for each value, with k taking each value in turn. The values can be any row vector, including the frequencies themselves.
    

    

    f   = [250 500 1000 2000 4000 8000];
thr = [20 25 NaN 50 60 65];                % 1 kHz not tested
for k = 1:numel(f)
    if isnan(thr(k))
        fprintf('%5d Hz: not tested\n', f(k));
        continue                            % skip the rest of this pass
    end
    fprintf('%5d Hz: %3d dB HL', f(k), thr(k));
    if thr(k) > 25
        fprintf('  (above 25 dB HL)');
    end
    fprintf('\n');
end
    

      250 Hz:  20 dB HL
  500 Hz:  25 dB HL
 1000 Hz: not tested
 2000 Hz:  50 dB HL  (above 25 dB HL)
 4000 Hz:  60 dB HL  (above 25 dB HL)
 8000 Hz:  65 dB HL  (above 25 dB HL)
    

    
  
    continue jumps straight to the next pass of the loop; break leaves the loop entirely. Both are used below.
    
  
    while loops: a simulated threshold search
    
  
    A while loop repeats as long as its condition is true, which suits procedures whose number of steps is not known in advance. The example simulates a simplified manual threshold search in the spirit of the modified Hughson-Westlake method (down 10 dB after a response, up 5 dB after no response, threshold taken as the lowest level giving responses on two ascending presentations). The simulated listener responds whenever the level is at or above 37 dB HL, which is unrealistically consistent; real listeners have a psychometric function, which a research simulation would model with random responses. Adaptive computerised procedures are discussed in 5.3.
    

    

    true_thr = 37;                 % simulated listener, dB HL
levels = -10:5:120;            % audiometer levels
asc_yes = zeros(size(levels)); % responses on ascending runs, per level
level = 40;  ascending = false;  n = 0;  threshold = NaN;
while isnan(threshold) && n < 30          % guard against endless loops
    n = n + 1;
    heard = level >= true_thr;
    fprintf('trial %2d: %3d dB HL  %s\n', n, level, ...
            ifelse_text(heard, 'response', 'no response'));
    if heard
        if ascending
            k = find(levels == level);
            asc_yes(k) = asc_yes(k) + 1;
            if asc_yes(k) >= 2
                threshold = level;
                break
            end
        end
        level = level - 10;  ascending = false;
    else
        level = level + 5;   ascending = true;
    end
end
fprintf('Threshold: %d dB HL after %d presentations\n', threshold, n);
    

    trial  1:  40 dB HL  response
trial  2:  30 dB HL  no response
trial  3:  35 dB HL  no response
trial  4:  40 dB HL  response
trial  5:  30 dB HL  no response
trial  6:  35 dB HL  no response
trial  7:  40 dB HL  response
Threshold: 40 dB HL after 7 presentations
    

    

  
    The one-line helper ifelse_text(c, a, b) returns text a if c is true and b otherwise; save it as ifelse_text.m containing function s = ifelse_text(c, a, b), if c, s = a; else, s = b; end and end. The result, 40 dB HL for a true threshold of 37 dB HL, shows the 5 dB quantisation of clinical thresholds. The n < 30 guard is good practice in every while loop: without it, a bug in the update rule would make the loop run forever (press Ctrl+C to stop a runaway loop).
    
  
    switch
    
  
    switch chooses between several cases of one value, usually text. It is clearer than a long if chain when the choices are labels.
    

    

    ears = {'R', 'left', 'X'};
for k = 1:numel(ears)
    switch lower(ears{k})
        case {'r', 'right'}
            fprintf('%s: right ear, red circle\n', ears{k});
        case {'l', 'left'}
            fprintf('%s: left ear, blue cross\n', ears{k});
        otherwise
            fprintf('%s: unknown ear code\n', ears{k});
    end
end
    

    R: right ear, red circle
left: left ear, blue cross
X: unknown ear code
    

    
  
    The curly braces make a cell array, a container whose elements can be of different types and sizes (here, pieces of text of different lengths). ears{k} takes out the k-th element. Cell arrays are the usual way to hold a list of file names or labels in code that must also run in Octave.
    
  
    Logical conditions: && and ||
    
  
    In if and while conditions use && and ||, which work on single true or false values and stop early ("short-circuit"): in ~isempty(x) && x(1) > 0 the second test is skipped when x is empty, avoiding an index error. The single & and | work element by element on arrays and are used for masks.
    

    

    x = [];
if ~isempty(x) && x(1) > 0
    disp('first value positive')
else
    disp('empty or not positive')
end
thr = [20 25 35 50 60 65];
mask = thr >= 25 & thr <= 50     % element-wise: a mask
    

    empty or not positive
mask =

  0  1  1  1  0  0
    

    
  
    Vectorisation versus loops
    
  
    Most loops over samples can be replaced by a single array expression. This vectorised code is shorter, closer to the mathematics and usually much faster, because the looping happens inside compiled library code. Compare two ways of computing the RMS of a 10 s recording at 44.1 kHz:
    

    

    rng(2);
x = 0.1 * randn(441000, 1);
s = 0;                               % loop version
for n = 1:numel(x)
    s = s + x(n)^2;
end
r_loop = sqrt(s / numel(x));
r_vec = sqrt(mean(x.^2));            % vectorised version
fprintf('difference = %.1e\n', abs(r_loop - r_vec))
    

    difference = 0.0e+00
    

    

    

    tic;  s = 0;  for n = 1:numel(x), s = s + x(n)^2; end;  t_loop = toc;
tic;  r = sqrt(mean(x.^2));  t_vec = toc;
fprintf('loop %.4f s, vectorised %.4f s, ratio %.0f\n', t_loop, t_vec, t_loop / t_vec)
    

    
  
    The difference is zero or at the level of rounding error, so both are correct. The times printed depend on the computer. In MATLAB the loop is typically a few times to a few tens of times slower than the vectorised line; in Octave, which lacks MATLAB's just-in-time compiler, it is typically hundreds of times slower. tic starts a stopwatch and toc reads it; for reliable timing, repeat the measurement several times or use MATLAB's timeit.
    
  
    When a loop is genuinely needed, for example a recursive filter or an adaptive procedure where each step depends on the last, pre-allocate the output (y = zeros(N, 1)) before the loop. Growing an array inside a loop (y(end+1) = ...) forces MATLAB to copy it repeatedly and can be very slow for audio-length arrays.
    

  
    9. Plotting from the basics
    
  
    A figure is often the fastest check that an analysis is right: a stimulus with a click at its onset, a spectrum with an unexpected peak or an audiogram with a mistyped threshold is obvious in a plot and invisible in a column of numbers. MATLAB plotting is built up in layers: create the data, draw it, then add labels, limits and annotations.
    
  
    A first plot
    


    

    fs = 16000;
t = (0:round(0.010*fs)-1)' / fs;          % 10 ms of time
x = 0.5 * sin(2*pi*500*t);
figure;                                   % open a new figure window
plot(t*1000, x);                          % x axis in ms
xlabel('Time (ms)');
ylabel('Amplitude (full scale = 1)');
title('500 Hz tone, first 10 ms');
grid on;
    

    
  
    You should see five complete cycles (500 Hz means one cycle every 2 ms). plot(x_values, y_values) joins the points with straight lines; both vectors must have the same number of elements. Always label axes with quantity and unit.
    
  
    Line styles, markers and colours
    
  
    A third argument, the line specification, sets colour, marker and line style in a short code. Name-value pairs set further properties such as line width and marker size.
    
  
    
    
    CodeMeaningExample
    
    
      
    Coloursr red, b blue, k black, g green, m magenta, c cyanplot(x, y, 'r')
    
      
    Line styles- solid, -- dashed, : dotted, -. dash-dotplot(x, y, 'k--')
    
      
    Markerso circle, x cross, s square, ^ triangle, < and > left and right pointers, *, .plot(f, thr, 'ro-')
    
      
    Properties'LineWidth', 'MarkerSize', 'MarkerFaceColor', 'Color' with an RGB tripleplot(x, y, 'LineWidth', 1.5)
    
        
  
    
  
    Several lines: hold on and legend
    

    

    x2 = 0.25 * sin(2*pi*1000*t);
figure;
plot(t*1000, x, 'b-', 'LineWidth', 1.5);
hold on;                                  % keep the first line
plot(t*1000, x2, 'r--');
hold off;
legend('500 Hz, amplitude 0.5', '1000 Hz, amplitude 0.25');
xlabel('Time (ms)');  ylabel('Amplitude');  grid on;
    

    
  
    Without hold on, each new plot command replaces the previous one. A frequent beginner problem is the opposite: forgetting hold off (or figure) so that later plots pile on top of earlier ones.
    
  
    Several axes in one figure: subplot and tiledlayout
    

    

    figure;
subplot(2, 1, 1);                         % 2 rows, 1 column, first axes
plot(t*1000, x);   title('500 Hz');  ylabel('Amplitude');
subplot(2, 1, 2);                         % second axes
plot(t*1000, x2);  title('1000 Hz'); ylabel('Amplitude');
xlabel('Time (ms)');
    

    

    

    figure;
tl = tiledlayout(2, 1, 'TileSpacing', 'compact');
nexttile;  plot(t*1000, x);   title('500 Hz');
nexttile;  plot(t*1000, x2);  title('1000 Hz');
xlabel(tl, 'Time (ms)');  ylabel(tl, 'Amplitude');   % shared labels
    

    
  
    Axis limits and logarithmic axes
    
  
    xlim([a b]) and ylim([a b]) set the visible range; axis([x1 x2 y1 y2]) sets both. semilogx plots with a logarithmic frequency axis, which is how audiograms, filter responses and spectra are normally shown, because equal distances then correspond to equal frequency ratios (octaves). set(gca, 'XTick', ...) places ticks at chosen frequencies; gca means "get current axes".
    
  
    A complete audiogram plot
    
  
    An audiogram has frequency on a logarithmic horizontal axis and hearing level on a vertical axis with better hearing at the top, so the y direction is reversed with set(gca, 'YDir', 'reverse'). The symbols below follow the widely used ASHA convention: right air conduction red circle, left air conduction blue cross, unmasked bone conduction < (right) and > (left), with no-response points drawn with a downward arrow. Local conventions vary, so match your clinic's symbol key. A common recommendation is that one octave on the frequency axis spans the same length as 20 dB on the level axis, which the PlotBoxAspectRatio setting reproduces.
    

    

    function plot_audiogram(f, ac_r, ac_l, bc_r, bc_l)
%PLOT_AUDIOGRAM  Audiogram with conventional symbols (ASHA style).
%   PLOT_AUDIOGRAM(f, ac_r, ac_l, bc_r, bc_l) plots air-conduction (AC)
%   and unmasked bone-conduction (BC) thresholds in dB HL. Use NaN for
%   frequencies not tested. BC inputs are optional.
if nargin < 4, bc_r = NaN(size(f)); end
if nargin < 5, bc_l = NaN(size(f)); end
red = [0.80 0.10 0.10];  blue = [0.10 0.25 0.75];
figure;  hold on;
plot(f, ac_r, '-o', 'Color', red, 'MarkerSize', 9, 'LineWidth', 1.4);
plot(f, ac_l, '--x', 'Color', blue, 'MarkerSize', 10, 'LineWidth', 1.4);
plot(f * 0.94, bc_r, '<', 'Color', red, 'MarkerSize', 8);   % offset slightly
plot(f * 1.06, bc_l, '>', 'Color', blue, 'MarkerSize', 8);
set(gca, 'XScale', 'log', 'YDir', 'reverse', ...
    'XTick', [125 250 500 1000 2000 4000 8000], ...
    'XTickLabel', {'125', '250', '500', '1k', '2k', '4k', '8k'}, ...
    'YTick', -10:10:120, 'XMinorTick', 'off', ...
    'PlotBoxAspectRatio', [log2(16000/125) * 20, 130, 1]);
xlim([125 16000]);  ylim([-10 120]);
grid on;  box on;  hold off;
xlabel('Frequency (Hz)');  ylabel('Hearing level (dB HL)');
legend('Right AC', 'Left AC', 'Right BC', 'Left BC', 'Location', 'southwest');
end
    

    

    

    f    = [250 500 1000 2000 4000 8000];
ac_r = [15 20 30 45 60 65];
ac_l = [10 15 20 25 35 40];
bc_r = [10 15 25 40 55 NaN];            % BC not tested at 8 kHz
bc_l = [5 15 20 25 30 NaN];
plot_audiogram(f, ac_r, ac_l, bc_r, bc_l);
title('Example audiogram');
print('-dpng', '-r200', 'audiogram.png');   % PNG at 200 dpi
exist('audiogram.png', 'file')              % 2 means the file exists
    

    ans = 2
    

    
  
    The x-axis limits (125 Hz to 16 kHz) leave space around the tested range, so the plot box spans 7 octaves; 7 × 20 = 140 dB-equivalents of width against 130 dB of height. The bone-conduction symbols are shifted slightly left and right of the test frequency, as they are on paper audiograms, so they do not hide the air-conduction symbols.
    
  
    Saving figures
    
  
      
    
    • saveas(gcf, 'audiogram.png') saves the current figure (gcf) in the format given by the extension. Simple, but the resolution is fixed by the screen.
      • 
    
    • print('-dpng', '-r300', 'audiogram.png') sets the resolution in dots per inch; print('-dpdf', ...) or '-dsvg' give vector files that stay sharp at any size. Works in MATLAB and Octave.
      • 
    
    • exportgraphics(gca, 'audiogram.pdf', 'ContentType', 'vector') (MATLAB R2020a or later) crops tightly around the axes and is the recommended way to produce figures for reports and papers in recent MATLAB. Not available in Octave 8.
      • 
  
    
  
    Save figures from the script that makes them, into the project's figures folder, so they can be regenerated if the data change. Saving the .fig file as well (savefig) lets you reopen the figure in MATLAB and edit it later.
    
  
    Stem plot of a sampled signal
    
  
    A digital signal exists only at the sample instants. stem draws each sample as a vertical line with a marker, which shows this directly. The example samples a 1 kHz tone at 8 kHz (8 samples per cycle) and overlays the underlying continuous waveform (approximated with a much finer time step) as a dashed line. This is the core picture of sampling in Practical P2.
    

    

    fs = 8000;  f0 = 1000;
n = 0:23;                                 % sample indices
xs = sin(2*pi*f0*n/fs);                   % the samples
tf = linspace(0, 23/fs, 1000);            % fine time grid for the curve
figure;
plot(tf*fs, sin(2*pi*f0*tf), 'k:');  hold on;
stem(n, xs, 'filled');  hold off;
xlabel('Sample index n');  ylabel('x[n]');
title('1 kHz tone sampled at 8 kHz: 8 samples per cycle');
fprintf('first 9 samples: %s\n', mat2str(round(xs(1:9)*1000)/1000));
    

    first 9 samples: [0 0.707 1 0.707 0 -0.707 -1 -0.707 -0]
    

    
  
    Waveform and spectrum plots
    
  
    The two standard views of any signal are the waveform (amplitude against time) and the spectrum (level against frequency). The spectrum calculation is explained in section 11; here it is used as a ready-made recipe.
    

    

    fs = 16000;  N = 16000;                    % 1 s
t = (0:N-1)' / fs;
x = 0.5*sin(2*pi*440*t) + 0.2*sin(2*pi*3000*t);
X = fft(x);
A = abs(X(1:N/2+1)) / N;  A(2:end-1) = 2*A(2:end-1);   % single-sided amplitude
fk = (0:N/2)' * fs / N;
figure;
subplot(2, 1, 1);
plot(t*1000, x);  xlim([0 20]);
xlabel('Time (ms)');  ylabel('Amplitude');  title('Waveform');
subplot(2, 1, 2);
semilogx(fk(2:end), 20*log10(A(2:end) + 1e-12));
xlim([50 fs/2]);  ylim([-120 0]);  grid on;
xlabel('Frequency (Hz)');  ylabel('Level (dB re amplitude 1)');  title('Spectrum');
[pk, k] = sort(A, 'descend');
fprintf('%6.0f Hz  amplitude %.2f\n', [fk(k(1:2)) pk(1:2)]');
    

       440 Hz  amplitude 0.50
  3000 Hz  amplitude 0.20
    

    

  
    
    
    Try it: plotting playground
    
    
    Choose a signal and a plot type and move the sliders. The plot is drawn in the browser, and the box underneath shows the MATLAB code that would produce the same figure. Push the frequency above half the sampling rate to see aliasing (Practical P2).
    
    
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
    
    
    
    
    
    
    
  
    

  
    10. Working with audio
    
  
    In MATLAB an audio signal is a matrix with one row per sample and one column per channel. Everything learned so far (indexing, element-wise arithmetic, functions, plotting) now applies directly to sound.
    
  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.4e. The audio input and output chain in MATLAB. Processing always happens on double arrays scaled to ±1; conversion to and from integers happens at the file boundary.
    
  
    Reading, writing and inspecting audio files
    
  
      
    
    • [x, fs] = audioread(file) returns the samples as doubles scaled to −1 to +1 and the sampling rate in Hz. Formats include WAV, FLAC, OGG and (in MATLAB) MP3 and M4A on most platforms. audioread(file, [n1 n2]) reads only samples n1 to n2, useful for very long recordings.
      • 
    
    • audiowrite(file, x, fs) writes a file; the format follows the extension. The default for WAV is 16 bits; add 'BitsPerSample', 24 for 24-bit files. Values beyond ±1 are clipped (MATLAB issues a warning).
      • 
    
    • info = audioinfo(file) returns a structure with the sampling rate, number of channels, total samples, duration and bit depth, without loading the samples.
      • 
  
    


    

    fs = 44100;
x = make_tone(1000, 1.0, fs, -20, 10);   % 1 s, 1 kHz, -20 dBFS
audiowrite('tone1k.wav', x, fs);          % 16-bit WAV by default
info = audioinfo('tone1k.wav');
fprintf('%d Hz, %d channel(s), %d bits, %d samples, %.3f s\n', ...
    info.SampleRate, info.NumChannels, info.BitsPerSample, ...
    info.TotalSamples, info.Duration);
[y, fs2] = audioread('tone1k.wav');
fprintf('read back: fs = %d, class %s, size %d x %d\n', fs2, class(y), size(y));
fprintf('largest difference from original: %.2e (one step = %.2e)\n', ...
    max(abs(y - x)), 1/32768);
    

    44100 Hz, 1 channel(s), 16 bits, 44100 samples, 1.000 s
read back: fs = 44100, class double, size 44100 x 1
largest difference from original: 3.05e-05 (one step = 3.05e-05)
    

    
  
    The round trip changes each sample by less than one quantisation step of the 16-bit format (1/32 768 ≈ 3.05 × 10−5), because audiowrite rounds each value to the nearest integer step. The resulting quantisation noise is around 96 dB below full scale for 16 bits, far below the level of any practical stimulus, but relevant when very low-level stimuli are generated digitally (see the calibration discussion below).
    

    

    yi = audioread('tone1k.wav', 'native');
class(yi)
yi(441:445)'                              % raw integer sample values
double(yi(441:445))' / 32768              % the same samples as doubles
    

    ans = int16
ans =

   -659      0    658   1302   1920

ans =

  -0.020111          0   0.020081   0.039734   0.058594
    

    
  
    Sampling rate and bit depth
    
  
    
    
    SettingTypical valuesConsequence
    
    
      
    Sampling rate fs16 kHz (speech research), 44.1 kHz, 48 kHz (general audio), 96 kHz (high-frequency or ultrasonic work)Highest representable frequency is fs/2 (the Nyquist frequency). Stimuli must be generated at the playback rate or resampled.
    
      
    Bit depth16-bit (about 96 dB range), 24-bit (about 144 dB theoretical)Sets the quantisation noise floor. Use 24-bit when stimuli span a wide range of levels.
    
      
    Channels1 (mono), 2 (stereo)Column 1 is left, column 2 right, by the WAV convention.
    
        
  
    
  
    Stereo signals
    
  
    A stereo signal is an N×2 matrix: column 1 is the left channel and column 2 the right. This is how dichotic stimuli, masking noise in the opposite ear and interaural time or level differences are prepared.
    

    

    xl = make_tone(1000, 0.5, fs, -20, 10);
st = [xl, zeros(size(xl))];               % N x 2: left, right
size(st)
audiowrite('tone_left.wav', st, fs);
info = audioinfo('tone_left.wav');
info.NumChannels
fprintf('left %.2f dBFS, right %s\n', dbfs(st(:,1)), num2str(dbfs(st(:,2))));
    

    ans =

   22050       2

ans = 2
left -20.11 dBFS, right -Inf
    

    
  
    The right channel is digital silence, so its level is −Inf dBFS. Always check the channel order by listening with the headphones labelled correctly before using a dichotic stimulus with a participant.
    
  
    Checking for clipping
    
  
    Clipping happens when a sample value would exceed ±1: audiowrite cuts it off at the limit, producing distortion that adds harmonics and can make a stimulus sound harsh. Check the peak before writing any file.
    

    

    y = 12 * make_tone(1000, 0.5, fs, -20, 10);      % about +21.6 dB of gain
pk = max(abs(y));
nclip = sum(abs(y) >= 1);
fprintf('peak %.3f (%.2f dB re full scale), %d samples at or beyond full scale\n', ...
    pk, 20*log10(pk), nclip);
if pk >= 1
    y = y / pk * 0.99;                           % scale to leave headroom
    fprintf('rescaled: new peak %.2f\n', max(abs(y)));
end
    

    peak 1.697 (4.59 dB re full scale), 12862 samples at or beyond full scale
rescaled: new peak 0.99
    

    
  
    Listening: sound, soundsc and audioplayer
    
  
    These commands send samples to the computer's audio output. They need a working sound device and are therefore shown without output.
    

    

    sound(x, fs);             % plays x as-is: values beyond +-1 are clipped
soundsc(x, fs);           % scales so the peak is at full scale: level changes!
p = audioplayer(x, fs);   % an object you can control
play(p);                  % returns immediately; playblocking(p) waits
pause(p);  resume(p);  stop(p);
    

    
  
    SafetyBefore playing anything through headphones, set the computer volume low, take the headphones off the ears for the first playback, and check the level. A mistake such as omitting a scale factor or using soundsc on a quiet stimulus can produce a full-scale sound. Never present computer-generated stimuli to a patient or research participant without a calibrated output chain, an appropriate maximum-level limit and, for research, ethical approval. soundsc rescales every signal to full scale, so it must never be used where the presentation level matters.
    
  
    Generating stimuli
    
  
    The following stimuli cover most needs in audiology teaching and research. Each is a column vector of doubles that can be concatenated, scaled and written to a file.
    

    

    fs = 44100;  dur = 1;  t = (0:round(dur*fs)-1)' / fs;
fc = 1000;  fm = 10;  dev = 0.05;            % 10 Hz rate, +-5 % deviation
phase = 2*pi*fc*t - (dev*fc/fm) * cos(2*pi*fm*t);   % integral of instantaneous frequency
w = sqrt(2) * 10^(-20/20) * sin(phase);
finst = fc * (1 + dev*sin(2*pi*fm*t));       % instantaneous frequency, Hz
fprintf('instantaneous frequency %.0f to %.0f Hz, level %.2f dBFS\n', ...
    min(finst), max(finst), dbfs(w));
    

    instantaneous frequency 950 to 1050 Hz, level -20.00 dBFS
    

    
  
    A warble tone's frequency swings around the centre frequency. It is used in sound-field audiometry because it reduces standing-wave effects in the room. Modulation rates of roughly 4 to 20 Hz and deviations of a few per cent to about ±10 % are typical; check the specification of the equipment or protocol you are reproducing. The phase is the running integral of the instantaneous frequency, which is why the formula contains a cosine term.
    

    

    function y = set_level(x, level_dbfs)
%SET_LEVEL  Scale x so that its RMS level is level_dbfs (dB re RMS 1).
y = x * 10^((level_dbfs - dbfs(x)) / 20);
end
    

    

    

    rng(3);
n = randn(fs, 1);                  % 1 s of Gaussian white noise
n = set_level(n, -25);
fprintf('level %.2f dBFS, peak %.3f, crest factor %.1f dB\n', ...
    dbfs(n), max(abs(n)), 20*log10(max(abs(n)) / sigrms(n)));
    

    level -25.00 dBFS, peak 0.232, crest factor 12.3 dB
    

    
  
    The level is exact because set_level measures and corrects it. The peak depends on the random values: Gaussian noise has a crest factor (peak-to-RMS ratio) of roughly 12 to 14 dB over one second, much higher than a sine's 3.01 dB, so noise needs more headroom than a tone of the same RMS level.
    

    

    function L = band_level(x, fs, f1, f2)
%BAND_LEVEL  Level (dB re RMS 1) of the part of x between f1 and f2 Hz.
%   Computed from the FFT of the whole signal (Parseval's theorem).
x = x(:);  N = numel(x);
X = fft(x);
fk = (0:N-1)' * fs / N;
inb = (fk >= f1 & fk < f2) | (fk > fs - f2 & fk <= fs - f1);  % both halves
L = 10*log10(sum(abs(X(inb)).^2) / N^2);
end
    

    

    

    function x = pink_noise(N)
%PINK_NOISE  N samples (N even) of pink noise with an RMS of 1.
%   Power per FFT bin falls as 1/f (-3 dB per octave), so every octave
%   band contains the same power. Random phases, fixed magnitudes.
nh = N/2;
k = (1:nh-1)';
Xh = exp(1i * 2*pi*rand(nh-1, 1)) ./ sqrt(k);   % magnitude 1/sqrt(f)
X = [0; Xh; 1/sqrt(nh); conj(flipud(Xh))];     % Hermitian: real signal
x = real(ifft(X));
x = x / sqrt(mean(x.^2));
end
    

    

    

    rng(4);
N = 2^17;  fsn = 32768;                      % 4 s at 32.768 kHz
white = randn(N, 1);  white = white / sigrms(white);
pink  = pink_noise(N);
fcs = [125 250 500 1000 2000 4000 8000];
fprintf(' centre   white    pink  (dB re RMS 1)\n');
for fc = fcs
    lo = fc / sqrt(2);  hi = fc * sqrt(2);   % octave band edges
    fprintf('%6d  %6.1f  %6.1f\n', fc, band_level(white, fsn, lo, hi), ...
            band_level(pink, fsn, lo, hi));
end
    

     centre   white    pink  (dB re RMS 1)
   125   -22.3   -12.3
   250   -19.7   -12.3
   500   -16.8   -12.3
  1000   -13.7   -12.3
  2000   -10.6   -12.3
  4000    -7.7   -12.3
  8000    -4.6   -12.3
    

    
  
    White noise has equal power per hertz, so each octave band (twice as wide as the one below) has about 3 dB more power; the white-noise values rise by roughly 3 dB per octave (the decimals depend on the random values). Pink noise has equal power per octave, so its octave-band levels are identical; they do not depend on the random phases because the magnitudes were fixed. Pink noise is used for sound-field equalisation and loudspeaker checks; speech-shaped noise (shaped to the long-term average speech spectrum) is used for speech-in-noise tests.
    

    

    function y = fft_bandpass(x, fs, f1, f2)
%FFT_BANDPASS  Keep only the components of x between f1 and f2 Hz.
%   Zeroes all FFT bins outside the band (a brick-wall filter). Suitable
%   for making stimuli offline; apply onset and offset ramps afterwards.
x = x(:);  N = numel(x);
X = fft(x);
fk = (0:N-1)' * fs / N;
keep = (fk >= f1 & fk <= f2) | (fk >= fs - f2 & fk <= fs - f1);
X(~keep) = 0;
y = real(ifft(X));
end
    

    

    

    rng(5);
fs = 44100;
wn = randn(fs, 1);                            % 1 s white noise
lo = 1000 * 2^(-1/6);  hi = 1000 * 2^(1/6);   % one-third-octave edges
nb = fft_bandpass(wn, fs, lo, hi);
nb = set_level(nb, -30);
fprintf('band %.1f to %.1f Hz (bandwidth %.1f Hz)\n', lo, hi, hi - lo);
fprintf('in band %.2f dB, below band %.1f dB, above band %.1f dB\n', ...
    band_level(nb, fs, lo, hi), band_level(nb, fs, 20, lo*0.99), ...
    band_level(nb, fs, hi*1.01, fs/2));
    

    band 890.9 to 1122.5 Hz (bandwidth 231.6 Hz)
in band -30.00 dB, below band -352.9 dB, above band -339.2 dB
    

    
  
    The out-of-band levels are near the numerical noise floor (below −250 dB), confirming the brick-wall filter. Narrow-band noise centred on the test frequency is the standard masker for pure-tone audiometry; audiometer standards specify band limits and slopes, and a brick-wall FFT filter meets them easily for offline stimuli. For the time-domain filters used in real-time devices see section 12 and 1.8.
    

    

    fs = 48000;
nclick = round(100e-6 * fs);                 % 100 microseconds
fprintf('click: %d samples = %.1f microseconds\n', nclick, nclick / fs * 1e6);
rate = 21.1;                                 % clicks per second
period = round(fs / rate);                   % samples between clicks
train = zeros(10 * period, 1);
for k = 0:9
    pol = 1 - 2*mod(k, 2);                   % +1, -1, +1, ... (alternating)
    idx = k*period + (1:nclick);
    train(idx) = 0.5 * pol;
end
onsets = find(train ~= 0 & [0; train(1:end-1)] == 0);   % first sample of each click
fprintf('actual rate %.3f /s, %d clicks, %d positive\n', ...
    fs / period, numel(onsets), sum(train(onsets) > 0));
    

    click: 5 samples = 104.2 microseconds
actual rate 21.099 /s, 10 clicks, 5 positive
    

    
  
    At 48 kHz, 100 µs is 4.8 samples; rounding gives 5 samples (104.2 µs). Sample-rate rounding also changes the repetition rate slightly (21.099 instead of 21.1 per second). Such rounding is harmless if it is reported, and a common source of confusion if it is not. Alternating polarity is used in evoked-potential recording to reduce stimulus artefact and the cochlear microphonic in the average.
    

    

    fs = 44100;
gap = zeros(round(0.2 * fs), 1);             % 200 ms silence
seq = [];
for fq = [500 1000 2000]
    seq = [seq; make_tone(fq, 0.3, fs, -20, 10); gap];   % append tone and gap
end
seq = seq(1:end - numel(gap));               % remove the final gap
fprintf('sequence: %.3f s, %d samples, peak %.3f\n', numel(seq)/fs, numel(seq), max(abs(seq)));
audiowrite('sequence.wav', seq, fs);
    

    sequence: 1.300 s, 57330 samples, peak 0.141
    

    
  
    Growing seq inside the loop is acceptable here because there are only three passes; for hundreds of segments, collect the pieces in a cell array and concatenate once at the end with vertcat(pieces{:}).
    
  
    Levels in dBFS and a calibration tone
    
  
    MATLAB knows only digital levels (dBFS). The acoustic level at the eardrum or in a coupler depends on the sound card, the volume setting and the transducer. The relationship is established by calibration: play a steady tone at a known digital level, measure its acoustic level with a sound level meter and the appropriate coupler or ear simulator, and record the offset.
    
  
    offset = L_measured - L_digital          (dB SPL - dBFS)
L_out  = L_digital + offset              (same frequency, same settings)
where L_measured is the coupler level in dB SPL and L_digital the RMS level of the calibration tone in dBFS. The offset is valid only for that frequency, transducer, sound card and volume setting.
    

    

    fs = 44100;
cal = make_tone(1000, 30, fs, -20, 50);       % 30 s, 1 kHz, -20 dBFS
audiowrite('cal_1k_minus20dBFS.wav', cal, fs);
measured_spl = 74.3;                          % example reading in the coupler
offset = measured_spl - (-20)                 % dB SPL at 0 dBFS
target_spl = 60;
needed_dbfs = target_spl - offset             % digital level for 60 dB SPL
    

    offset = 94.300
needed_dbfs = -34.300
    

    
  
    If the coupler reads 74.3 dB SPL for the −20 dBFS tone, a 1 kHz stimulus at −34.3 dBFS will produce 60 dB SPL with the same settings. Because headphone responses are not flat, a separate offset is needed at each test frequency. Name calibration files with their digital level, fix the operating-system volume (and disable any audio "enhancements"), and repeat the check whenever hardware or settings change.
    
  
    Mixing speech and noise at a set SNR
    
  
    The signal-to-noise ratio in dB is the difference between the RMS levels of the signal and the noise. To create a mixture at a chosen SNR, keep the speech as it is, scale the noise so that its RMS level is the speech level minus the SNR, and add. If the sum would clip, scale the whole mixture down by one common factor, which leaves the SNR unchanged.
    
  
    SNR = 20 log10(rms(s) / rms(n))    dB
g   = rms(s) / (rms(n) × 10^(SNR/20))
where s is the speech, n the noise and g the gain applied to the noise.
    

    

    function [mix, s_out, n_out] = mix_at_snr(s, n, snr_db, peak_max)
%MIX_AT_SNR  Add noise to a signal at a given SNR (RMS-based, in dB).
%   [mix, s_out, n_out] = MIX_AT_SNR(s, n, snr_db) scales the noise n so
%   that 20*log10(rms(s)/rms(n_out)) = snr_db and returns s_out + n_out.
%   If the mixture peak exceeds peak_max (default 0.99), all outputs are
%   scaled down by the same factor, which preserves the SNR.
if nargin < 4, peak_max = 0.99; end
s = s(:);  n = n(:);
if numel(n) < numel(s)
    error('mix_at_snr:length', 'Noise is shorter than the signal.');
end
n = n(1:numel(s));                           % trim noise to signal length
g = sigrms(s) / (sigrms(n) * 10^(snr_db / 20));
s_out = s;
n_out = g * n;
mix = s_out + n_out;
pk = max(abs(mix));
if pk > peak_max
    c = peak_max / pk;
    mix = c * mix;  s_out = c * s_out;  n_out = c * n_out;
end
end
    

    

    

    fs = 16000;  rng(6);
s = make_tone(500, 1, fs, -15, 20);          % stand-in for speech
n = randn(2*fs, 1);                          % longer than s
for snr = [-5 0 10]
    [m, so, no] = mix_at_snr(s, n, snr);
    fprintf('target %3d dB: measured %6.2f dB, mix peak %.3f\n', ...
        snr, dbfs(so) - dbfs(no), max(abs(m)));
end
    

    target  -5 dB: measured  -5.00 dB, mix peak 0.990
target   0 dB: measured   0.00 dB, mix peak 0.881
target  10 dB: measured  10.00 dB, mix peak 0.447
    

    
  
    At −5 dB SNR the noise is louder than the signal and the mixture peak would exceed the limit, so the function scales the mixture down to a peak of 0.99; the measured SNR is still exactly −5 dB. Two practical points: SNR here is defined on the whole-file RMS, and for speech with pauses some tests define it on the active speech only, which gives a different number; and the mixture's overall level changes with SNR, which matters when the presentation level must be fixed (mini-project (a) handles this).
    

  
    11. Frequency analysis
    
  
    The discrete Fourier transform (DFT) expresses a block of N samples as a sum of N sinusoids at equally spaced frequencies, and the fast Fourier transform (FFT) is the efficient algorithm that computes it. In MATLAB, X = fft(x) returns N complex numbers; their magnitudes give the amplitude of each frequency component and their angles the phase. The theory is covered in the signal-processing topics of Unit 1; this section concentrates on getting the frequency axis and the scaling right in code, which is where most mistakes are made.
    
  
    The frequency axis
    
  
    Element k of fft(x) (MATLAB index k, starting at 1) corresponds to the frequency (k − 1) fs / N. Elements 1 to N/2 + 1 cover 0 Hz (DC) up to the Nyquist frequency fs/2; the remaining elements are the negative frequencies, which for a real signal mirror the positive ones and are discarded in a single-sided spectrum.
    
  
    f_k = (k - 1) fs / N,      k = 1, 2, ..., N/2 + 1
Δf = fs / N = 1 / T
A_k = 2 |X_k| / N   (k not DC or Nyquist);   A_1 = |X_1| / N
where fs is the sampling rate, N the number of samples, T = N/fs the analysis duration, Δf the bin spacing (frequency resolution) and A_k the amplitude of the sinusoid at f_k.
    


    

    fs = 8000;  N = 8000;                 % 1 s, so bins are 1 Hz apart
t = (0:N-1)' / fs;
x = 1.0*sin(2*pi*1000*t) + 0.5*sin(2*pi*2500*t);
X = fft(x);
A = abs(X(1:N/2+1)) / N;              % keep 0 to fs/2
A(2:end-1) = 2 * A(2:end-1);          % single-sided: double all but DC and Nyquist
fk = (0:N/2)' * fs / N;               % frequency of each bin
k = find(A > 0.1)                     % MATLAB indices of the peaks
fk(k)                                 % their frequencies, Hz
A(k)                                  % their amplitudes
    

    k =

   1001
   2501

ans =

   1000
   2500

ans =

   1.0000
   0.5000
    

    
  
    The peaks are at MATLAB indices 1001 and 2501, which are 1000 Hz and 2500 Hz because index 1 is 0 Hz. The amplitudes come out exactly as 1.0 and 0.5 only because of the single-sided scaling (divide by N, double everything except DC and Nyquist). Forgetting the division by N gives values thousands of times too large; forgetting the doubling makes every tone 6 dB too low.
    
  
    Frequency resolution and leakage
    
  
    The bin spacing is fs/N = 1/T: a 0.1 s analysis block has 10 Hz resolution whatever the sampling rate. When a tone falls exactly on a bin (as above, because each tone completes a whole number of cycles in the block), its energy appears in one bin. When it falls between bins, energy leaks into neighbouring bins and the peak amplitude is underestimated. Multiplying the block by a smooth window such as the Hann window reduces leakage far from the peak at the cost of a wider main peak.
    

    

    fs = 8000;  N = 800;                  % 0.1 s block: bins every 10 Hz
t = (0:N-1)' / fs;
x = sin(2*pi*1005*t);                 % halfway between the 1000 and 1010 Hz bins
w = 0.5 - 0.5*cos(2*pi*(0:N-1)'/N);   % Hann window (periodic form), base MATLAB
Ar = abs(fft(x)) / N * 2;             % rectangular (no window)
Ah = abs(fft(x .* w)) / sum(w) * 2;   % Hann, corrected for the window's gain
fk = (0:N-1)' * fs / N;
fprintf('peak amplitude: rectangular %.3f, Hann %.3f (true 1.000)\n', max(Ar), max(Ah));
k15 = find(fk == 1500);
fprintf('level at 1500 Hz re peak: rectangular %.1f dB, Hann %.1f dB\n', ...
    20*log10(Ar(k15)/max(Ar)), 20*log10(Ah(k15)/max(Ah)));
    

    peak amplitude: rectangular 0.638, Hann 0.849 (true 1.000)
level at 1500 Hz re peak: rectangular -41.3 dB, Hann -110.3 dB
    

    
  
    Without a window, a tone halfway between bins is underestimated by 3.9 dB (amplitude 0.64, close to the theoretical 2/π) and its leaked energy is only about 41 dB down 495 Hz away. With the Hann window the worst-case error is 1.4 dB and leakage at 1500 Hz is over 100 dB down. When a window is used, divide by sum(w) instead of N so that a tone's amplitude is still read correctly. The Signal Processing Toolbox provides hann(N) and hamming(N); the one-line formula above needs no toolbox.
    
  
    Zero padding
    
  
    fft(x, Nfft) with Nfft larger than the signal appends zeros before transforming. This samples the same underlying spectrum more densely, making plots smoother and peak frequencies easier to read, but it does not improve the true resolution, which is still 1/T. Two tones closer than about 1/T to 2/T apart cannot be separated by padding.
    

    

    Nfft = 8192;                          % pad 800 samples to 8192
Xp = fft(x .* w, Nfft);
Ap = abs(Xp(1:Nfft/2+1)) / sum(w) * 2;
fp = (0:Nfft/2)' * fs / Nfft;
[pk, kp] = max(Ap);
fprintf('padded: bin spacing %.3f Hz, peak at %.1f Hz, amplitude %.3f\n', ...
    fs/Nfft, fp(kp), pk);
    

    padded: bin spacing 0.977 Hz, peak at 1004.9 Hz, amplitude 1.000
    

    
  
    Speech and time-varying signals: the spectrogram
    
  
    Speech changes every few tens of milliseconds, so one FFT of a whole sentence mixes all its sounds together. The short-time Fourier transform (STFT) cuts the signal into overlapping frames, windows each frame and takes its FFT; the spectrogram displays the magnitudes as an image with time across, frequency up and level as colour. Frame length sets the trade-off: short frames (about 3 to 5 ms) give a wideband spectrogram that shows formants and individual glottal pulses; long frames (about 20 to 40 ms) give a narrowband spectrogram that resolves individual harmonics. The Praat topic 5.7 and Practical P4 use the same idea, and 3.8 and 3.9 build LPC and cepstral analysis on it.
    

    

    [x, fs] = audioread('sentence.wav');
win = hann(round(0.025*fs));                      % 25 ms frames
spectrogram(x, win, round(0.02*fs), 1024, fs, 'yaxis');   % 20 ms overlap
ylim([0 8]);                                      % kHz
colormap(parula);  title('Narrowband spectrogram');
    

    
  
    With the toolbox, spectrogram draws the image directly (frequency in kHz on the vertical axis with 'yaxis'). Without it, the base-MATLAB function below computes the same matrix, which you then display with imagesc.
    

    

    function [S, f, t] = stft_db(x, fs, win_len, hop, nfft)
%STFT_DB  Magnitude spectrogram in dB using a Hann window (base MATLAB).
%   [S, f, t] = STFT_DB(x, fs, win_len, hop, nfft): win_len and hop in
%   samples. S is (nfft/2+1) x frames in dB re amplitude 1; f in Hz; t
%   is the centre time of each frame in s.
x = x(:);
w = 0.5 - 0.5*cos(2*pi*(0:win_len-1)'/win_len);
nfr = 1 + floor((numel(x) - win_len) / hop);
S = zeros(nfft/2 + 1, nfr);
for m = 1:nfr
    seg = x((m-1)*hop + (1:win_len)) .* w;
    X = fft(seg, nfft);
    A = abs(X(1:nfft/2+1)) / sum(w);
    A(2:end-1) = 2 * A(2:end-1);
    S(:, m) = 20*log10(A + 1e-12);
end
f = (0:nfft/2)' * fs / nfft;
t = ((0:nfr-1) * hop + win_len/2) / fs;
end
    

    

    

    fs = 16000;  T = 1;  t = (0:T*fs-1)' / fs;
x = 0.5 * sin(2*pi*(500*t + 1500*t.^2));   % sweeps 500 Hz to 3500 Hz in 1 s
[S, f, tf] = stft_db(x, fs, 400, 160, 1024);   % 25 ms frames, 10 ms hop
size(S)
for tq = [0.1 0.5 0.9]
    [~, m] = min(abs(tf - tq));             % frame nearest the query time
    [~, k] = max(S(:, m));
    fprintf('t = %.3f s: peak at %6.1f Hz (expected %6.1f Hz)\n', ...
        tf(m), f(k), 500 + 3000*tf(m));
end
% display: imagesc(tf, f, S); axis xy; ylim([0 4000]); colorbar;
    

    ans =

   513    98

t = 0.102 s: peak at  812.5 Hz (expected  807.5 Hz)
t = 0.502 s: peak at 2000.0 Hz (expected 2007.5 Hz)
t = 0.902 s: peak at 3203.1 Hz (expected 3207.5 Hz)
    

    
  
    The instantaneous frequency of the chirp is 500 + 3000t Hz, and the spectrogram peak follows it to within the 15.6 Hz bin spacing (16 000 / 1024). In a spectrogram image, axis xy puts low frequencies at the bottom and colorbar shows the dB scale; limiting the colour range (caxis([-80 0]), called clim in recent MATLAB) hides the noise floor and makes speech structure clearer.
    
  
    Power spectral density: periodogram and Welch's method
    
  
    The spectrum of a single block of noise is very ragged: each bin of a random signal fluctuates by several decibels around the true value, however long the block. Welch's method (pwelch in the Signal Processing Toolbox) reduces this variability by cutting the signal into overlapping segments, computing the power spectrum of each windowed segment (a periodogram), and averaging them. The price is coarser frequency resolution, set by the segment length. The base-MATLAB sketch below shows the effect on white noise, whose true spectrum is flat.
    

    

    rng(7);
fs = 16000;  x = randn(16*1024, 1);
L = 1024;  w = 0.5 - 0.5*cos(2*pi*(0:L-1)'/L);
P1 = abs(fft(x(1:L) .* w)).^2;                 % one periodogram
starts = 1:L/2:numel(x) - L + 1;               % 50 % overlap
P = zeros(L, 1);
for s0 = starts
    P = P + abs(fft(x(s0:s0+L-1) .* w)).^2;
end
P = P / numel(starts);
band = 20:480;                                  % bins well inside 0 to fs/2
fprintf('segments averaged: %d\n', numel(starts));
fprintf('spread of dB values across bins: one block %.1f dB, averaged %.1f dB\n', ...
    std(10*log10(P1(band))), std(10*log10(P(band))));
    

    segments averaged: 31
spread of dB values across bins: one block 5.6 dB, averaged 0.8 dB
    

    

    

    [pxx, f] = pwelch(x, hann(1024), 512, 1024, fs);   % PSD, units^2/Hz
semilogx(f, 10*log10(pxx));  xlabel('Frequency (Hz)');  ylabel('PSD (dB/Hz)');
    

    
  
    For a single block the spread of the dB values is about 5.6 dB, which is the theoretical value for the periodogram of noise; averaging 31 half-overlapping segments reduces it to below 1 dB (the exact decimals depend on the random values). Welch averaging is the right tool for long-term spectra: the long-term average speech spectrum of a recording, the spectrum of a masking noise, or the noise floor of a hearing-aid test box.
    

  
    12. Filtering
    
  
    A digital filter computes each output sample as a weighted sum of recent input samples and, for recursive filters, recent output samples. The weights are the filter coefficients: b multiplies inputs and a multiplies past outputs. Base MATLAB's filter(b, a, x) applies any such filter. Filter theory, FIR and IIR structures and their clinical relevance are covered in 1.8; this section shows the code and is the basis of Practical P3.
    
  
    a1 y(n) = b1 x(n) + b2 x(n-1) + ... + b(M+1) x(n-M)
                    - a2 y(n-1) - ... - a(K+1) y(n-K)
where x is the input, y the output, b the numerator (feed-forward) coefficients and a the denominator (feedback) coefficients, with a1 normally 1. MATLAB vectors start at index 1, so b1 multiplies the current input. An FIR filter has a = 1.
    


    

    b = ones(1, 5) / 5;                   % five equal weights
x = [0 0 1 1 1 1 1 1];                % a step
y = filter(b, 1, x)
    

    y =

        0        0   0.2000   0.4000   0.6000   0.8000   1.0000   1.0000
    

    

    

    b = 0.1;  a = [1 -0.9];               % y(n) = 0.1 x(n) + 0.9 y(n-1)
imp = [1 zeros(1, 5)];
h = filter(b, a, imp)
    

    h =

   0.100000   0.090000   0.081000   0.072900   0.065610   0.059049
    

    
  
    The FIR moving average reaches the new value after exactly 5 samples and then stops changing; the IIR smoother's impulse response decays geometrically (0.1, 0.09, 0.081, ...) and in principle never ends. Recursive smoothers of this kind are how hearing aids compute signal envelopes for compression (mini-project (d)).
    
  
    Frequency response without a toolbox
    
  
    The frequency response H(f) says how much a filter changes the amplitude (and phase) of a sinusoid at each frequency. It follows directly from the coefficients, so a short base-MATLAB function can compute it; freqz in the Signal Processing Toolbox (and in Octave) does the same and draws the plot.
    
  
    H(f) = [Σ b(m) e^(-jω(m-1))] / [Σ a(k) e^(-jω(k-1))],   ω = 2πf/fs
where the sums run over all numerator and denominator coefficients; |H| in dB is 20 log10|H(f)|.
    

    

    function H = fresp(b, a, f, fs)
%FRESP  Complex frequency response of filter (b, a) at frequencies f (Hz).
w = 2*pi*f(:).' / fs;                           % row of radian frequencies
num = b(:).' * exp(-1i * (0:numel(b)-1)' * w);
den = a(:).' * exp(-1i * (0:numel(a)-1)' * w);
H = (num ./ den).';
end
    

    

    

    function h = fir_lowpass(fc, fs, M)
%FIR_LOWPASS  Linear-phase FIR low-pass filter by the windowed-sinc method.
%   h = FIR_LOWPASS(fc, fs, M) returns M+1 coefficients (M even) with a
%   cut-off (-6 dB point) near fc Hz, using a Hamming window.
n = (0:M)' - M/2;                          % symmetric about the centre
fcn = fc / fs;                             % cut-off in cycles per sample
h = 2 * fcn * ones(M + 1, 1);              % value at n = 0
nz = n ~= 0;
h(nz) = sin(2*pi*fcn*n(nz)) ./ (pi*n(nz)); % ideal (sinc) impulse response
w = 0.54 - 0.46*cos(2*pi*(0:M)'/M);        % Hamming window
h = h .* w;
h = h / sum(h);                            % exactly 0 dB at DC
end
    

    

    

    fs = 16000;
h = fir_lowpass(1000, fs, 100);           % 101 coefficients
fq = [250 500 800 1000 1200 1500 2000 4000];
g = 20*log10(abs(fresp(h, 1, fq, fs)));
fprintf('%5d Hz: %7.1f dB\n', [fq; g']);
fprintf('delay: %d samples = %.3f ms\n', 50, 50/fs*1000);
    

      250 Hz:     0.0 dB
  500 Hz:     0.0 dB
  800 Hz:    -0.3 dB
 1000 Hz:    -6.0 dB
 1200 Hz:   -29.7 dB
 1500 Hz:   -58.6 dB
 2000 Hz:   -59.6 dB
 4000 Hz:   -66.1 dB
delay: 50 samples = 3.125 ms
    

    
  
    The passband is flat, the gain is −6 dB at the cut-off and the stopband is more than 50 dB down by 1.5 kHz, typical of a Hamming-windowed design (its stopband attenuation is about 53 dB). A longer filter gives a steeper transition. Because the coefficients are symmetric, the filter has linear phase: every frequency is delayed by the same M/2 = 50 samples (3.125 ms), so waveform shapes are preserved, which matters for transient stimuli such as clicks and for evoked-potential waveforms.
    

    

    t = (0:fs-1)' / fs;
x = 0.5*sin(2*pi*500*t) + 0.5*sin(2*pi*3000*t);
y = filter(h, 1, x);
fprintf('500 Hz:  before %.2f dB, after %.2f dB\n', band_level(x, fs, 450, 550), band_level(y, fs, 450, 550));
fprintf('3000 Hz: before %.2f dB, after %.2f dB\n', band_level(x, fs, 2950, 3050), band_level(y, fs, 2950, 3050));
    

    500 Hz:  before -9.03 dB, after -9.04 dB
3000 Hz: before -9.03 dB, after -73.93 dB
    

    
  
    A high-pass filter is obtained by spectral inversion (subtract the low-pass from a unit impulse at the centre: hp = -h; hp(51) = hp(51) + 1), and a band-pass by subtracting two low-pass filters (fir_lowpass(f2, ...) - fir_lowpass(f1, ...), both with the same M). These three shapes cover most teaching needs without any toolbox.
    
  
    Filter design with the Signal Processing Toolbox
    
  
    The toolbox provides design functions that are shorter to use and more flexible. They are shown without output; the expected results are described below the code.
    

    

    fs = 16000;
[b, a] = butter(4, 1000/(fs/2));            % 4th-order Butterworth low-pass, 1 kHz
[bb, ab] = butter(2, [500 2000]/(fs/2), 'bandpass');   % band-pass 500 to 2000 Hz
h1 = fir1(100, 1000/(fs/2));                % 101-tap Hamming-windowed FIR, as above
y  = filter(b, a, x);                       % causal: introduces phase delay
y0 = filtfilt(b, a, x);                     % forward and backward: zero phase
freqz(b, a, 2048, fs);                      % magnitude and phase plot
d = designfilt('lowpassiir', 'FilterOrder', 8, ...
    'HalfPowerFrequency', 1000, 'SampleRate', fs);   % filter object
y2 = filtfilt(d, x);
    

    
  
      
    
    • butter(n, Wn) returns Butterworth IIR coefficients. The cut-off is normalised to the Nyquist frequency, so 1 kHz at 16 kHz is 0.125. A fourth-order Butterworth filter is −3 dB at the cut-off and falls at about 24 dB per octave well above it. The band-pass form doubles the order.
      • 
    
    • fir1(M, Wn) designs the same windowed-sinc FIR as fir_lowpass (Hamming window by default); its coefficients should match to within rounding, which is a good check of your own code.
      • 
    
    • filtfilt runs the filter forwards and then backwards, cancelling the phase shift (zero phase) and squaring the magnitude response. It is used for offline analysis of evoked potentials and recordings where timing must not shift. It cannot be used in real time, because it needs the future signal.
      • 
    
    • freqz(b, a, n, fs) plots the magnitude (dB) and phase against frequency in Hz; with output arguments, [H, f] = freqz(b, a, n, fs), it returns the values instead.
      • 
    
    • designfilt builds a filter object from a readable specification and can open an interactive designer. Filter objects are applied with filter(d, x) or filtfilt(d, x).
      • 
  
    
  
    A 50 Hz notch filter
    
  
    Mains hum at 50 Hz (60 Hz in some countries) and its harmonics contaminate recordings made near electrical equipment, and are a classic problem in evoked-potential recording. A second-order IIR notch places a pair of zeros on the unit circle at the hum frequency (infinite attenuation there) and a pair of poles just inside it at radius r, which restores the gain at neighbouring frequencies. The closer r is to 1, the narrower the notch: the −3 dB bandwidth is roughly (1 − r) fs/π Hz.
    

    

    fs = 16000;  f0 = 50;  r = 0.999;
w0 = 2*pi*f0/fs;
b = [1, -2*cos(w0), 1];                    % zeros on the unit circle at +-f0
a = [1, -2*r*cos(w0), r^2];                % poles just inside, same angle
b = b * sum(a) / sum(b);                   % unity gain at 0 Hz
fq = [0 25 45 49 50 51 55 100 1000];
g = 20*log10(abs(fresp(b, a, fq, fs)) + 1e-15);
fprintf('%5d Hz: %8.2f dB\n', [fq; g']);
fprintf('approximate -3 dB bandwidth: %.1f Hz\n', (1 - r) * fs / pi);
    

        0 Hz:     0.00 dB
   25 Hz:    -0.03 dB
   45 Hz:    -0.98 dB
   49 Hz:    -8.73 dB
   50 Hz:  -229.20 dB
   51 Hz:    -8.73 dB
   55 Hz:    -0.98 dB
  100 Hz:     0.01 dB
 1000 Hz:     0.02 dB
approximate -3 dB bandwidth: 5.1 Hz
    

    
  
    The notch removes 50 Hz completely, while 45 and 55 Hz lose only about 1 dB and 100 Hz is unaffected. Harmonics (100, 150 Hz and so on) need their own notches, which are applied one after another with repeated calls to filter. A notch does not fix the cause: check electrode impedances, cable routing and grounding first. In MATLAB with the Signal Processing Toolbox, designfilt('bandstopiir', ...) or iirnotch (in recent releases) design equivalent filters.
    
  
    Octave and one-third-octave bands
    
  
    Octave and one-third-octave band analysis divides the spectrum into bands whose widths are a constant fraction of their centre frequency, matching the roughly logarithmic frequency resolution of hearing. It is used for sound-field and room measurements, noise audits, hearing-aid and speech analysis (for example band importance in the Speech Intelligibility Index) and masking-noise specification. Band centre frequencies are defined in the relevant IEC and ANSI standards on octave-band and fractional-octave-band filters, most commonly by the base-10 system: exact one-third-octave centres are 1000 × 10k/10 Hz, and the familiar nominal values (for example 1250, 1600, 2000) are rounded labels.
    
  
    fm   = 1000 × 10^(k/10)            one-third octave, k integer
f1,2 = fm × 10^(±1/20)              band edges
fm   = 1000 × 10^(3k/10), f1,2 = fm × 10^(±3/20)    octave
where fm is the exact mid-band frequency and f1, f2 the lower and upper band-edge frequencies. The base-2 system (2^(k/3)) gives almost identical values.
    

    

    k = -3:3;
fm = 1000 * 10.^(k/10);
f1 = fm * 10^(-1/20);  f2 = fm * 10^(1/20);
fprintf('  k    lower    centre    upper   width\n');
fprintf('%3d  %7.1f  %8.1f  %7.1f  %6.1f\n', [k; f1; fm; f2; f2 - f1]);
    

      k    lower    centre    upper   width
 -3    446.7     501.2    562.3   115.7
 -2    562.3     631.0    707.9   145.6
 -1    707.9     794.3    891.3   183.3
  0    891.3    1000.0   1122.0   230.8
  1   1122.0    1258.9   1412.5   290.5
  2   1412.5    1584.9   1778.3   365.7
  3   1778.3    1995.3   2238.7   460.4
    

    

    

    rng(8);
fs = 32768;  N = 4*fs;  t = (0:N-1)' / fs;
x = set_level(sin(2*pi*1000*t), -20) + set_level(pink_noise(N), -40);
k = -10:10;                                 % 100 Hz to 10 kHz bands
fm = 1000 * 10.^(k/10);
Lb = zeros(size(fm));
for i = 1:numel(fm)
    Lb(i) = band_level(x, fs, fm(i)*10^(-1/20), fm(i)*10^(1/20));
end
nominal = [100 125 160 200 250 315 400 500 630 800 1000 1250 1600 2000 ...
           2500 3150 4000 5000 6300 8000 10000];
fprintf('%6d Hz: %6.1f dB\n', [nominal([9:13]); Lb(9:13)]);
    

       630 Hz:  -57.1 dB
   800 Hz:  -57.0 dB
  1000 Hz:  -20.0 dB
  1250 Hz:  -57.0 dB
  1600 Hz:  -57.0 dB
    

    
  
    The 1 kHz band contains the tone (−20 dB) plus a little noise; the other bands show the pink noise at an equal level in every one-third octave. The noise power (−40 dB in total) is spread over about 48 one-third-octave bands between the lowest FFT bin (0.25 Hz) and the Nyquist frequency, so each band holds about −40 − 10 log10(48) ≈ −57 dB.
