The Audio Toolbox provides octaveFilter and octaveFilterBank for standard-conforming real-time band filtering; the FFT method above is exact for offline analysis of stationary signals.
    
  
    A-weighting
    
  
    A-weighting is a standard frequency weighting that roughly follows the inverse of an equal-loudness contour at low levels. It attenuates low and very high frequencies and is used for environmental and occupational noise measurement in dB(A) (5.1 and the noise-audit practical P10). Its response is defined analytically in the sound-level-meter standard IEC 61672, so it can be computed exactly.
    
  
    R_A(f) = 12194^2 f^4 /
         [(f^2 + 20.6^2) √((f^2 + 107.7^2)(f^2 + 737.9^2)) (f^2 + 12194^2)]
A(f)   = 20 log10(R_A(f)) + 2.00    dB
where f is frequency in Hz; the +2.00 dB normalises the weighting to 0 dB at 1 kHz.
    

    

    aw = @(f) 20*log10(12194^2 * f.^4 ./ ((f.^2 + 20.6^2) .* ...
        sqrt((f.^2 + 107.7^2) .* (f.^2 + 737.9^2)) .* (f.^2 + 12194^2))) + 2.00;
fo = [31.5 63 125 250 500 1000 2000 4000 8000];
fprintf('%7.1f Hz: %6.1f dB\n', [fo; aw(fo)]);
Lz = [70 72 75 74 70 68 65 60 55];          % example octave-band SPLs, dB (unweighted)
LA = 10*log10(sum(10.^((Lz + aw(fo))/10)));
LZ = 10*log10(sum(10.^(Lz/10)));
fprintf('overall: %.1f dB unweighted, %.1f dB(A)\n', LZ, LA);
    

       31.5 Hz:  -39.5 dB
   63.0 Hz:  -26.2 dB
  125.0 Hz:  -16.2 dB
  250.0 Hz:   -8.7 dB
  500.0 Hz:   -3.2 dB
 1000.0 Hz:    0.0 dB
 2000.0 Hz:    1.2 dB
 4000.0 Hz:    1.0 dB
 8000.0 Hz:   -1.1 dB
overall: 80.1 dB unweighted, 73.2 dB(A)
    

    
  
    Adding the A-weighting value to each band level and summing the band powers gives the A-weighted level: in this example the low-frequency-heavy noise measures 80.1 dB unweighted but 73.2 dB(A). To apply A-weighting to a waveform, use the Audio Toolbox weightingFilter object, or multiply the FFT of a recording by the weighting magnitude (converted from dB) and transform back. A-weighting is a measurement convention for noise; it is not a model of an individual's hearing or loudness perception.
    

  
    13. Analysis mini-projects
    
  
    Each project below combines the skills of the previous sections into a complete, realistic analysis. Type (do not paste) the code for the first project you attempt: typing forces you to read every line. All base-MATLAB code was run in Octave and the printed results are shown; toolbox alternatives are given without output. The helper functions defined earlier (make_tone, sigrms, dbfs, set_level, band_level, fft_bandpass, pink_noise, mix_at_snr, fir_lowpass, fresp, who_grade) must be in the current folder or on the path.
    
  
    The projects need a speech recording. If you have one (a sentence recorded at 16 kHz or higher, with a little silence at each end), save it as sentence.wav. Otherwise the function below makes a speech-like test signal: a harmonic complex with a gliding fundamental frequency, a spectrum falling at about 6 dB per octave and a 4 Hz "syllable-rate" amplitude modulation with pauses. It is a stand-in for teaching, not a replacement for real speech materials.
    

    

    function s = speechlike(dur_s, fs)
%SPEECHLIKE  Speech-like test signal for teaching (not real speech).
%   s = SPEECHLIKE(dur_s, fs): harmonics of an F0 gliding between 100 and
%   140 Hz up to about 7 kHz, amplitudes 1/k (about -6 dB/octave), 4 Hz
%   full-depth amplitude modulation, RMS level -25 dBFS.
t = (0:round(dur_s*fs)-1)' / fs;
f0 = 120 + 20*sin(2*pi*0.5*t);            % Hz
ph = 2*pi*cumsum(f0) / fs;                % running phase of the fundamental
s = zeros(size(t));
for k = 1:50                              % harmonics up to about 7 kHz
    s = s + sin(k*ph) / k;
end
s = s .* (0.5 * (1 - cos(2*pi*4*t)));     % 4 Hz envelope with pauses
s = set_level(s, -25);
end
    

    
  
    (a) Speech-in-noise stimulus generator
    
  
    Goal: produce a set of files of the same sentence in speech-shaped noise at SNRs of −5, 0, +5 and +10 dB, with the speech level fixed, the noise starting 0.5 s before and ending 0.5 s after the speech, ramped noise edges, and a log of the levels actually produced. Speech-shaped noise is made by phase randomisation: the FFT magnitude of the speech is kept and its phases are replaced by random ones, giving a noise with exactly the speech's long-term spectrum. The speech level is held constant, so the overall level of the mixture rises as the SNR falls; the alternative convention (fixed overall level) is a one-line change.
    


    

    %% Settings
fs = 16000;
snrs = -5:5:10;                              % dB
speech_dbfs = -25;                           % fixed speech level
lead_s = 0.5;                                % noise before and after speech
rng(10);                                     % reproducible noise

%% Speech: a real recording if available, otherwise the test signal
if exist('sentence.wav', 'file')
    [s, fs_in] = audioread('sentence.wav');
    s = s(:, 1);                             % first channel only
    if fs_in ~= fs, error('Resample the recording to %d Hz first.', fs); end
else
    s = speechlike(2.0, fs);
end
s = set_level(s, speech_dbfs);

%% Speech-shaped noise by phase randomisation
pad = zeros(round(lead_s * fs), 1);
Ntot = numel(s) + 2*numel(pad);
S = fft([s; zeros(Ntot - numel(s), 1)]);     % speech spectrum at full length
R = fft(randn(Ntot, 1));                     % random phases (Hermitian)
nz = real(ifft(abs(S) .* R ./ abs(R)));
nr = round(0.05 * fs);                       % 50 ms ramps on the noise
ramp = 0.5 * (1 - cos(pi * (0:nr-1)' / nr));
nz(1:nr) = nz(1:nr) .* ramp;
nz(end-nr+1:end) = nz(end-nr+1:end) .* flipud(ramp);
s_full = [pad; s; pad];
in_sp = numel(pad) + (1:numel(s));           % samples where speech is present

%% Make one file per SNR and log the levels
fprintf(' SNR  speech  noise    mix   peak  file\n');
for snr = snrs
    g = 10^((speech_dbfs - snr - dbfs(nz(in_sp))) / 20);  % noise gain
    n_s = g * nz;
    mix = s_full + n_s;
    pk = max(abs(mix));
    if pk >= 1, error('Clipping at %d dB SNR: lower speech_dbfs.', snr); end
    fname = sprintf('sin_snr_%+03d.wav', snr);
    audiowrite(fname, mix, fs);
    fprintf('%+4d  %6.2f %6.2f %6.2f  %5.3f  %s\n', snr, dbfs(s), ...
        dbfs(n_s(in_sp)), dbfs(mix(in_sp)), pk, fname);
end
    

     SNR  speech  noise    mix   peak  file
  -5  -25.00 -20.00 -18.78  0.476  sin_snr_-05.wav
  +0  -25.00 -25.00 -21.95  0.329  sin_snr_+00.wav
  +5  -25.00 -30.00 -23.78  0.258  sin_snr_+05.wav
 +10  -25.00 -35.00 -24.56  0.223  sin_snr_+10.wav
    

    
  
    Points to notice: the noise level is computed over the speech portion only (in_sp), so the SNR refers to the part of the file the listener must understand; the function refuses to write a clipped file instead of silently rescaling it, because rescaling would change the calibrated speech level; the file names include the SNR with its sign (sin_snr_-05.wav), so they sort correctly and cannot be confused. For a clinical or research test, use the published recorded materials and their own calibration signals; this generator is for teaching, pilot work and student projects.
    
  
    Clinical linkSpeech-in-noise tests differ in whether the speech or the noise is held constant, in how SNR is defined (whole file, active speech, or a calibration signal matched to the speech) and in the noise type (steady speech-shaped, babble or modulated). A result expressed as a speech reception threshold in dB SNR is only comparable with norms obtained with the same definitions.
    
  
    (b) Audiogram database analysis from a CSV file
    
  
    Goal: read a table of audiograms, compute each person's four-frequency PTA and grade, flag a 4 kHz notch, calculate group mean audiograms, plot them and save a results table. The example file has twelve right-ear audiograms in three illustrative groups (normal controls, a noise-exposed group and an age-related group). It is invented teaching data, not from real patients. Create it with any text editor or with the code below, and save it as audiograms.csv.
    


    

    type audiograms.csv
    

    id,group,ear,f250,f500,f1000,f2000,f4000,f8000
P01,control,R,10,5,5,10,10,15
P02,control,R,5,10,5,5,10,10
P03,control,R,10,10,15,10,15,20
P04,control,R,0,5,5,10,5,10
P05,noise,R,10,10,15,25,55,30
P06,noise,R,15,15,20,35,60,40
P07,noise,R,10,15,15,30,50,35
P08,noise,R,5,10,10,20,45,25
P09,age,R,15,20,25,40,55,70
P10,age,R,20,20,30,45,60,75
P11,age,R,15,15,20,35,50,65
P12,age,R,20,25,30,45,65,80
    

    
  
    In MATLAB the natural tool is a table: readtable reads the file, keeps the column names from the header row and handles text and numbers together. Tables are part of base MATLAB (not a toolbox), but they are not available in Octave 8, so a portable version follows.
    

    

    T = readtable('audiograms.csv');                 % 12 rows x 9 variables
fcols = {'f250','f500','f1000','f2000','f4000','f8000'};
T.pta4 = mean(T{:, {'f500','f1000','f2000','f4000'}}, 2);
T.notch = (T.f4000 - T.f2000 >= 10) & (T.f4000 - T.f8000 >= 10);
T.grade = arrayfun(@who_grade, T.pta4, 'UniformOutput', false);
G = groupsummary(T, 'group', 'mean', [fcols, {'pta4'}]);   % one row per group
disp(G)
writetable(T, 'audiogram_results.csv');
writetable(G, 'group_means.csv');
    

    
  
    In MATLAB, disp(G) prints a table with a GroupCount column and columns named mean_f250 to mean_pta4; the values are identical to those printed by the portable version below.
    

    

    fid = fopen('audiograms.csv', 'r');
header = fgetl(fid);                             % first line: column names
C = textscan(fid, '%s %s %s %f %f %f %f %f %f', 'Delimiter', ',');
fclose(fid);
id = C{1};  group = C{2};
thr = [C{4:9}];                                  % 12 x 6 matrix, dB HL
f = [250 500 1000 2000 4000 8000];
fprintf('%d audiograms, %d frequencies\n', size(thr, 1), size(thr, 2));

pta4  = mean(thr(:, 2:5), 2);                    % 500, 1k, 2k, 4k
notch = (thr(:,5) - thr(:,4) >= 10) & (thr(:,5) - thr(:,6) >= 10);

groups = unique(group);                          % sorted: age, control, noise
fprintf('\n%-8s  n  %s   PTA4\n', 'group', sprintf('%6d', f));
gmean = zeros(numel(groups), numel(f));
for g = 1:numel(groups)
    in = strcmp(group, groups{g});
    gmean(g, :) = mean(thr(in, :), 1);
    fprintf('%-8s %2d  %s  %5.1f\n', groups{g}, sum(in), ...
        sprintf('%6.1f', gmean(g, :)), mean(pta4(in)));
end
fprintf('\n4 kHz notch (example criterion): %s\n', strjoin(id(notch)', ', '));
    

    12 audiograms, 6 frequencies

group     n     250   500  1000  2000  4000  8000   PTA4
age       4    17.5  20.0  26.2  41.2  57.5  72.5   36.2
control   4     6.2   7.5   7.5   8.8  10.0  13.8    8.4
noise     4    10.0  12.5  15.0  27.5  52.5  32.5   26.9

4 kHz notch (example criterion): P05, P06, P07, P08
    

    
  
    The example notch criterion (4 kHz at least 10 dB worse than both 2 kHz and 8 kHz) flags all four noise-exposed audiograms and none of the others. Published criteria for noise-induced notches are more detailed, and no single criterion diagnoses noise-induced hearing loss; the history and the whole audiogram are needed. Next, the per-person results are written to a CSV file that opens in any spreadsheet program, and the group means are plotted.
    

    

    fid = fopen('audiogram_results.csv', 'w');
fprintf(fid, 'id,group,pta4,grade,notch\n');
for k = 1:numel(id)
    fprintf(fid, '%s,%s,%.2f,%s,%d\n', id{k}, group{k}, pta4(k), ...
        who_grade(pta4(k)), notch(k));
end
fclose(fid);
txt = fileread('audiogram_results.csv');
lines = strsplit(strtrim(txt), '\n');
fprintf('%s\n', lines{[1 2 6 10]});             % header and three example rows

figure;  hold on;
mk = {'o-', 's-', '^-'};
for g = 1:numel(groups)
    plot(f, gmean(g, :), mk{g}, 'LineWidth', 1.5);
end
hold off;
set(gca, 'XScale', 'log', 'YDir', 'reverse', 'XTick', f, ...
    'XTickLabel', {'250','500','1k','2k','4k','8k'});
xlim([180 11000]);  ylim([-10 90]);  grid on;
xlabel('Frequency (Hz)');  ylabel('Mean threshold (dB HL)');
legend(groups, 'Location', 'southwest');  title('Group mean audiograms');
    

    id,group,pta4,grade,notch
P01,control,7.50,normal hearing,0
P05,noise,26.25,mild,1
P09,age,35.00,moderate,0
    

    
  
    The figure shows the flat, near-normal control curve, the noise group's dip at 4 kHz with recovery at 8 kHz, and the steadily sloping age group. With real data, add the number of people per group to the legend and show variability (for example the standard deviation with errorbar) as well as the mean.
    
  
    (c) Simulated ABR averaging and the √N rule
    
  
    An auditory brainstem response is a few tenths of a microvolt, buried in EEG and muscle noise that is typically ten or more times larger in a single sweep. Averaging N time-locked sweeps leaves the response unchanged while the random noise, which is uncorrelated with the stimulus, falls in RMS amplitude by √N. The simulation builds an ABR-like template (three peaks labelled I, III and V at typical latencies), adds independent Gaussian noise of 10 µV RMS to each sweep, and measures the residual noise in the average.
    
  
    noise_avg = noise_single / √N
SNR_avg (dB) = SNR_single (dB) + 10 log10(N)
where N is the number of sweeps averaged; the noise must be random and independent of the stimulus.
    


    

    rng(11);
fs = 20000;  ep = round(0.012 * fs);          % 12 ms epochs
t_ms = (0:ep-1)' / fs * 1000;
pk = @(lat, amp, wid) amp * exp(-((t_ms - lat) / wid).^2);
abr = pk(1.6, 0.20, 0.25) + pk(3.8, 0.25, 0.3) + pk(5.8, 0.50, 0.4) ...
      - pk(6.8, 0.30, 0.6);                    % microvolts; I, III, V and trough
noise_uv = 10;                                 % single-sweep noise RMS
Nmax = 4096;
sweeps = abr + noise_uv * randn(ep, Nmax);     % each column is one sweep
fprintf('   N   residual (uV)  predicted   +/- estimate\n');
for N = [1 4 16 64 256 1024 4096]
    avg = mean(sweeps(:, 1:N), 2);
    resid = sigrms(avg - abr);
    if N > 1
        pm = sweeps(:, 1:N) .* repmat((-1).^(0:N-1), ep, 1);   % invert every other sweep
        pm_est = sigrms(mean(pm, 2));          % estimates noise without knowing abr
    else
        pm_est = NaN;
    end
    fprintf('%5d   %8.3f     %8.3f    %8.3f\n', N, resid, noise_uv/sqrt(N), pm_est);
end
[vtrue, kv] = max(abs(abr));                  % wave V in the template
fprintf('wave V at %.2f ms: template %.2f uV, 4096-sweep average %.2f uV\n', ...
    t_ms(kv), abr(kv), avg(kv));
    

       N   residual (uV)  predicted   +/- estimate
    1     11.095       10.000         NaN
    4      5.196        5.000       5.322
   16      2.477        2.500       2.364
   64      1.303        1.250       1.310
  256      0.570        0.625       0.682
 1024      0.309        0.312       0.324
 4096      0.169        0.156       0.153
wave V at 5.80 ms: template 0.48 uV, 4096-sweep average 0.61 uV
    

    
  
    The measured residual follows the prediction closely (the exact digits depend on the random values), and the wave V amplitude read from the 4096-sweep average differs from the true 0.48 µV by about the size of the residual noise. Quadrupling the number of sweeps halves the noise: 1024 sweeps reduce 10 µV to about 0.31 µV, still comparable with the 0.5 µV wave V, which is why threshold searches near the response limit need 2000 or more sweeps and a quiet, relaxed patient. The plus-minus reference (alternately adding and subtracting sweeps) cancels the response and leaves an estimate of the residual noise; clinical systems use this and related statistics (for example Fsp) to judge whether a response is present, instead of relying on the eye alone.
    

    

    figure;
Ns = [16 256 4096];
for i = 1:3
    subplot(3, 1, i);
    plot(t_ms, mean(sweeps(:, 1:Ns(i)), 2), 'k', t_ms, abr, 'r--');
    ylim([-1.5 1.5]);  ylabel('uV');  title(sprintf('N = %d', Ns(i)));
end
xlabel('Time after stimulus (ms)');
    

    
  
    (d) Hearing-aid gain and compression (one-channel WDRC)
    
  
    A wide dynamic range compression (WDRC) hearing aid gives more gain to soft sounds than to loud ones. Its static behaviour is described by an input-output function: below the compression threshold (kneepoint) the gain is constant (linear), and above it every R dB increase in input produces only 1 dB increase in output, where R is the compression ratio. Its dynamic behaviour depends on how fast the level estimate follows the signal: the attack time (level rising) and release time (level falling). Hearing-aid compression is treated in detail in Unit 3; here both parts are simulated.
    
  
    G(L) = G0                              for L ≤ CT
G(L) = G0 - (L - CT)(1 - 1/R)          for L > CT
where L is the input level (dB SPL), G0 the linear gain (dB), CT the compression threshold (dB SPL) and R the compression ratio.
    


    

    G0 = 20;  CT = 50;  R = 3;                     % dB, dB SPL, ratio
gain = @(L) G0 - max(L - CT, 0) * (1 - 1/R);
Lin = 40:10:90;
fprintf('input %s dB SPL\n', sprintf('%6d', Lin));
fprintf('gain  %s dB\n', sprintf('%6.1f', gain(Lin)));
fprintf('output%s dB SPL\n', sprintf('%6.1f', Lin + gain(Lin)));
    

    input     40    50    60    70    80    90 dB SPL
gain    20.0  20.0  13.3   6.7  -0.0  -6.7 dB
output  60.0  70.0  73.3  76.7  80.0  83.3 dB SPL
    

    
  
    Above the 50 dB SPL kneepoint, a 40 dB range of input (50 to 90 dB SPL) is squeezed into a 13.3 dB range of output (70 to 83.3 dB SPL). The dynamic simulation below needs a calibration assumption to link digital levels to dB SPL; here 0 dBFS (RMS) is taken as 100 dB SPL.
    

    

    fs = 16000;  cal = 100;                        % dB SPL at 0 dBFS
seg = @(L) make_tone(1000, 0.5, fs, L - cal, 0);
x = [seg(55); seg(80); seg(55)];               % 55, 80, 55 dB SPL steps
att_ms = 5;  rel_ms = 50;
a_att = exp(-1 / (att_ms/1000 * fs));          % smoothing coefficients
a_rel = exp(-1 / (rel_ms/1000 * fs));
p = 10^((55 - cal)/10);                        % start the power estimate at 55 dB
y = zeros(size(x));                            % pre-allocate
g_db = zeros(size(x));
for n = 1:numel(x)
    p_in = x(n)^2;
    if p_in > p, a = a_att; else, a = a_rel; end
    p = a * p + (1 - a) * p_in;                % smoothed power
    L = 10*log10(p + 1e-20) + cal;             % RMS level estimate, dB SPL
    g_db(n) = gain(L);
    y(n) = x(n) * 10^(g_db(n)/20);
end
Lout = @(i1, i2) dbfs(y(i1:i2)) + cal;
N = numel(x) / 3;  last = round(0.1*fs);       % last 100 ms of each step
fprintf('steady output: %.1f, %.1f, %.1f dB SPL\n', ...
    Lout(N-last+1, N), Lout(2*N-last+1, 2*N), Lout(3*N-last+1, 3*N));
fprintf('static prediction: %.1f, %.1f, %.1f dB SPL\n', ...
    55 + gain(55), 80 + gain(80), 55 + gain(55));
w = round(0.002*fs);                           % 2 ms windows after the rise
fprintf('first 2 ms after the step up: %.1f dB SPL (overshoot)\n', Lout(N+1, N+w));
fprintf('first 2 ms after the step down: %.1f dB SPL (undershoot)\n', Lout(2*N+1, 2*N+w));
    

    steady output: 70.3, 78.6, 70.3 dB SPL
static prediction: 71.7, 80.0, 71.7 dB SPL
first 2 ms after the step up: 86.0 dB SPL (overshoot)
first 2 ms after the step down: 53.7 dB SPL (undershoot)
    

    
  
    In the steady parts the output is within about 1.5 dB of the static input-output function. The remaining difference is a property of the level detector: it uses the fast attack coefficient whenever the instantaneous power x2 rises above the estimate (near the peaks of every cycle) and the slow release coefficient otherwise, so the estimate settles above the true mean power (by about 2 dB here), the gain is set slightly lower and the output is slightly lower. Manufacturers characterise real detectors with standard test signals for exactly this reason. Immediately after the input jumps up by 25 dB, the gain is still set for the soft sound, so the output briefly overshoots; after the step down, the gain recovers slowly (release) and the output briefly undershoots. Plot g_db against time to see the gain trajectory. Real hearing aids use several channels, more elaborate level detectors and limiting, but the principles are the same.
    
  
    (e) Noise-vocoder simulation of cochlear-implant processing
    
  
    A noise vocoder is the standard acoustic simulation used to let normal-hearing listeners experience the spectral degradation of cochlear-implant processing. It keeps the slowly varying envelope in a small number of frequency channels and discards the temporal fine structure, as continuous-interleaved-sampling strategies largely do. The steps mirror a CI processor (Practical P7 and Unit 3): split the signal into N band-pass channels, extract each channel's envelope, use it to modulate a noise band covering the same frequency range, and add the channels. It simulates reduced spectral resolution only; it does not reproduce the electrode-neural interface, current spread or the experience of an individual implant user.
    


    

    fs = 16000;  rng(12);
if exist('sentence.wav', 'file'), [x, fs] = audioread('sentence.wav'); x = x(:,1);
else, x = speechlike(2.0, fs); end
nch = 8;                                       % number of channels
edges = logspace(log10(150), log10(7000), nch + 1);   % log-spaced band edges
env_cut = 160;                                 % envelope low-pass, Hz
y = zeros(size(x));
fprintf(' ch    band (Hz)        in (dB)  out (dB)\n');
for c = 1:nch
    lo = edges(c);  hi = edges(c+1);
    band = fft_bandpass(x, fs, lo, hi);        % analysis filter
    env = fft_bandpass(abs(band), fs, 0, env_cut);   % rectify and smooth
    env = max(env, 0);                         % envelopes cannot be negative
    carrier = fft_bandpass(randn(size(x)), fs, lo, hi);  % noise band
    ch = fft_bandpass(env .* carrier, fs, lo, hi);  % modulate, re-filter
    ch = ch * sigrms(band) / sigrms(ch);       % match channel level
    y = y + ch;
    fprintf('%3d  %6.0f to %6.0f   %7.1f  %7.1f\n', c, lo, hi, dbfs(band), dbfs(ch));
end
y = y * sigrms(x) / sigrms(y);
fprintf('input %.2f dBFS, vocoded output %.2f dBFS, output peak %.3f\n', ...
    dbfs(x), dbfs(y), max(abs(y)));
audiowrite('vocoded_8ch.wav', y / max(1, max(abs(y)) / 0.99), fs);
    

     ch    band (Hz)        in (dB)  out (dB)
  1     150 to    243     -36.1    -36.1
  2     243 to    392     -34.0    -34.0
  3     392 to    634     -36.2    -36.2
  4     634 to   1025     -38.3    -38.3
  5    1025 to   1657     -40.8    -40.8
  6    1657 to   2678     -42.6    -42.6
  7    2678 to   4330     -44.8    -44.8
  8    4330 to   7000     -48.2    -48.2
input -25.00 dBFS, vocoded output -25.00 dBFS, output peak 0.368
    

    
  
    The channel levels of the output match the input by design, so the long-term spectrum is preserved at the resolution of the channels, while all harmonic detail within each channel is replaced by noise. Listening to the result with 4, 8 and 16 channels is a powerful demonstration: with a real sentence, intelligibility in quiet is typically good with about 8 channels but falls sharply in noise, and pitch and music are poorly conveyed at any channel number. The band edges here are log-spaced for simplicity; many studies space them according to the Greenwood cochlear frequency-position function instead.
    

    

    [bl, al] = butter(2, env_cut/(fs/2));                % envelope smoother
for c = 1:nch
    [b, a] = butter(3, edges(c:c+1)/(fs/2), 'bandpass');   % 6th-order band-pass
    band = filter(b, a, x);
    env = max(filter(bl, al, abs(band)), 0);         % half-wave or full-wave rectify
    carrier = filter(b, a, randn(size(x)));
    ch = filter(b, a, env .* carrier);
    y = y + ch * sigrms(band) / sigrms(ch);
end
    

    
  
    The filter-based version is closer to real-time processing (causal filters, sample-by-sample operation) and gives a very similar sound. Replacing the noise carriers with sine carriers at each channel's centre frequency gives a tone vocoder, which sounds different and is used for some research questions.
    

  
    14. Good practice and debugging
    
  
    Reading error messages
    
  
    An error message is information, not a verdict. Read it from the top: the first line says what went wrong, and the lines below (in MATLAB, blue underlined links) say in which file and at which line. Click the link to jump there. Then ask three questions: which variable is involved, what size and class does it have (look in the Workspace or type size(x) and class(x)), and what did I expect it to be? Your own functions should produce messages that answer these questions too, as make_tone does when asked for impossible ramps:
    


    

    x = make_tone(1000, 0.01, 44100, -20, 10);   % 10 ms tone with 10 ms ramps
    

    error: Ramps are longer than the tone.
    

    

    

    x = make_tones(1000, 0.5, 44100);
    

    error: 'make_tones' undefined near line 4, column 5
    

    
  
    MATLAB reports the second case as "Unrecognized function or variable 'make_tones'" and may suggest the correct name. Check the spelling, then check that the file is in the current folder or on the path (which make_tone).
    

    

    thr = [20 25 35 50 60 65];
k = 0;
thr(k)
    

    error: thr(0): subscripts must be either integers 1 to (2^63)-1 or logicals
    

    
  
    MATLAB: "Index in position 1 is invalid. Array indices must be positive integers or logical values." It appears when an index calculation gives 0 (for example a sample number computed as t * fs with t = 0) or a fraction (0.0105 * 44100 = 463.05). Wrap index calculations in round and add 1 where a time of zero maps to sample 1.
    

    

    tone = make_tone(1000, 0.1, 8000, -20, 5);   % a column (800 x 1)
gap = zeros(1, 400);                         % a row by mistake
seq = [tone; gap];
    

    error: vertical dimensions mismatch (800x1 vs 1x400)
    

    
  
    MATLAB: "Dimensions of arrays being concatenated are not consistent." Make both pieces columns (zeros(400, 1) or gap(:)).
    
  
    Breakpoints and the debugger
    
  
    When code runs without an error but gives the wrong answer, the debugger lets you stop it part-way and inspect every variable.
    
  
      
    
    1. Open the script or function in the Editor and click the line number (or the dash next to it) where you want to stop. A red circle appears: a breakpoint. The command-line equivalent is dbstop in make_tone at 22.
      2. 
    
    2. Run the code. Execution stops before that line, a green arrow marks it, and the prompt changes to K>>. The Workspace now shows the variables inside the function.
      3. 
    
    3. Inspect: hover over a variable, type its name, or plot it (plot(x)). This is often where the problem becomes obvious: a ramp that is a row instead of a column, a level in the wrong units.
      4. 
    
    4. Step through: Step (F10) runs one line; Step In (F11) enters a called function; Continue (F5) runs to the next breakpoint; Quit Debugging (Shift+F5, or dbquit) stops.
      5. 
    
    5. dbstop if error makes MATLAB stop automatically at the line where any error occurs, with all variables available. Type dbclear all to remove all breakpoints afterwards.
      6. 
  
    
  
    Octave supports the same commands (dbstop, dbstep, dbcont, dbquit) and breakpoints in its editor. The keyword keyboard placed in code also pauses execution at that point in both programs.
    
  
    Common beginner errors and their fixes
    
  
    
    
    Symptom or messageUsual causeFix
    
    
      
    Unrecognized function or variableSpelling; file not in the current folder or on the path; variable created in another script or clearedCheck spelling and case; which name; cd or addpath; run the earlier section first
    
      
    Index exceeds the number of array elementsLoop runs past the end; data shorter than expectedUse numel(x) or end in loops; print size(x)
    
      
    Array indices must be positive integers or logical valuesIndex 0 or a fraction from a time-to-sample calculationround(t*fs) + 1; check the formula
    
      
    Arrays have incompatible sizes (older: Matrix dimensions must agree)Two vectors of different lengths combinedCheck size of both; trim or pad
    
      
    Incorrect dimensions for matrix multiplication* used instead of .*Use the dotted operators for element-wise work
    
      
    Dimensions of arrays being concatenated are not consistentMixing rows and columns in [a; b]Make all pieces columns with x(:)
    
      
    Unexpected 6x6 result instead of an errorImplicit expansion of a row with a columnMake orientations match; check size of results
    
      
    Error that a function such as butter or spectrogram is unrecognised on one computer onlyToolbox not installed or not licensed (or Octave package not loaded)ver; use a base-MATLAB alternative; pkg load signal in Octave
    
      
    Screen floods with numbersMissing semicolon on a line producing a long vectorCtrl+C to stop; add the semicolon
    
      
    -Inf in levels or plotslog10(0): digital silence or an empty bandAdd a tiny offset (+ 1e-12) for plotting; exclude silent segments
    
      
    Strange error on a line using sum, mean or iA variable with that name hides the functionclear sum; rename the variable
    
      
    Levels far too high or stuck at 32767int16 data from 'native' reading used in arithmeticdouble(x) / 32768, or use default audioread
    
      
    Written file sounds too high or low in pitch, or too shortWrong sampling rate passed to audiowrite or soundCarry fs with the signal; never type it twice
    
      
    Harsh, distorted stimulusClipping beyond ±1Check max(abs(x)) before writing; reduce the level
    
      
    A condition that is never true= instead of ==, or comparing text with ==Use == for numbers and strcmp for text
    
      
    Code works in MATLAB, fails in Octave (or the reverse)Strings, tables, toolbox functions, Octave-only syntaxKeep to the portable subset shown in this topic; test in both
    
        
  
    
  
    Reproducibility
    
  
    An analysis is reproducible when someone else (or you, next year) can run it and get the same numbers and figures. For dissertation and audit work this is not optional. The habits below are simple and prevent most problems.
    
  
      
    
    • Scripts, not clicks. Every number in a report should come from a script that loads raw data and produces the result. Never edit results by hand.
      • 
    
    • Seed random numbers. Put rng(seed) at the top of any script that generates noise or simulates responses, and record the seed.
      • 
    
    • Record versions. version prints the MATLAB release (OCTAVE_VERSION in Octave) and ver lists installed toolboxes with versions. Save this output with the results; toolbox updates occasionally change results.
      • 
    
    • Comment the why. Comments should explain choices ("RMS over speech only, following the test manual"), cite sources for constants and state units. The code itself shows the what.
      • 
    
    • One folder per project with a fixed structure (below). Raw data are never modified; derived files are written elsewhere and can be deleted and regenerated at any time.
      • 
    
    • Relative paths. Use fullfile('data', 'raw', name) instead of C:\Users\..., so the project runs on another computer.
      • 
    
    • Version control. Git (5.1) keeps a history of every change to your scripts. MATLAB has Git integration in the Current Folder panel.
      • 
  
    
  
    

    audiology_project/
  README.txt            what the project is, how to run it, MATLAB version
  data/raw/             original recordings and CSV exports (read-only)
  data/derived/         files created by scripts (can be regenerated)
  scripts/              main analysis scripts, numbered in run order
    s01_import.m
    s02_levels.m
    s03_figures.m
  functions/            make_tone.m, dbfs.m, mix_at_snr.m ...
  figures/              exported figures
  results/              tables, logs, version information
    
  
    
  
    Performance tips
    
  
      
    
    • Vectorise loops over samples (section 8), and pre-allocate arrays that must be filled in a loop.
      • 
    
    • Suppress output with semicolons and avoid printing inside long loops; printing is slow.
      • 
    
    • Read each file once, keep it in memory, and use audioread(file, [n1 n2]) to read part of a very long file.
      • 
    
    • Use the Profiler (EDITOR > Run and Time, or profile on, run the code, profile viewer) to find where time is actually spent before optimising anything.
      • 
    
    • Functions are usually faster than scripts, because MATLAB can optimise their private workspace.
      • 
    
    • Prefer clarity to speed. Most audiology analyses run in seconds; a readable script that is correct is worth more than a fast one that nobody can check.
      • 
  
    
  
    Live Scripts for lab records
    
  
    A Live Script (.mlx, HOME > New Live Script) is a single document that holds formatted text, headings, equations, code, printed results and figures, displayed next to or below the code that produced them. It is an excellent format for the lab records of Practicals P2 to P4: the aims and method are written as text, each analysis step is a code section, and the figures appear in place. Useful features:
    
  
      
    
    • Text formatting and equations from the LIVE EDITOR tab; section breaks run parts independently.
      • 
    
    • Interactive controls (sliders, drop-down lists) that change a variable and rerun the section, for example a slider for SNR or cut-off frequency.
      • 
    
    • LIVE EDITOR > Export to PDF, Word, HTML or LaTeX for submission. Export only after running all sections from the top (Run All), so the document matches the code.
      • 
  
    
  
    Live Scripts are MATLAB-only. Portable alternatives are publish('myscript.m', 'html'), which runs a plain script and builds a report from its %% sections and comments (available in MATLAB and Octave), or a Jupyter notebook with an Octave kernel, as used in the Python primer.
    
  
    App Designer: building a simple audiometer interface (concept)
    
  
    App Designer (HOME > New > App, or appdesigner) builds graphical apps. You drag components onto a canvas in Design View and write callback functions in Code View that run when the user interacts with a component. A teaching audiometer app shows how a clinical audiometer's controls map to code:
    
  
    
    
    ComponentPurposeCallback or property used
    
    
      
    Drop Down: frequency250 to 8000 Hzapp.FreqDropDown.Value
    
      
    Spinner: levelRequested level in 5 dB steps, with a maximumapp.LevelSpinner.Value, Step = 5, Limits
    
      
    Switch: earLeft or right channelapp.EarSwitch.Value
    
      
    Button: PresentPlays a ramped tonePresentButtonPushed callback
    
      
    Buttons: Heard / Not heardOperator records the responseAppends to a results array
    
      
    UIAxes: audiogramPlots recorded thresholds as they are enteredplot(app.UIAxes, ...)
    
        
  
    

    

    function PresentButtonPushed(app, event)
    f = str2double(app.FreqDropDown.Value);       % Hz
    L = app.LevelSpinner.Value;                   % requested level
    k = find(app.CalFreqs == f);                  % calibration table row
    level_dbfs = L - app.CalOffset(k);            % offset measured for this
                                                  % frequency and headphone
    if level_dbfs > -6                            % keep digital headroom
        uialert(app.UIFigure, 'Level above the calibrated range.', 'Limit');
        return
    end
    x = make_tone(f, 1.0, app.fs, level_dbfs, 25);
    st = zeros(numel(x), 2);
    if strcmp(app.EarSwitch.Value, 'Left'), st(:,1) = x; else, st(:,2) = x; end
    app.Player = audioplayer(st, app.fs);
    play(app.Player);
end
    

    
  
    SafetyAn app like this is a teaching tool for understanding how audiometers work, not a clinical device. A clinical audiometer must meet the audiometer standard (IEC 60645-1) and be calibrated to reference equivalent threshold levels for its specific transducer; it controls maximum output, signal purity, attenuator linearity, crosstalk and timing, and it is regulated as a medical device. A computer, its sound card and consumer headphones provide none of these guarantees. Do not use a home-made app to test or report anyone's hearing.
    

  
    15. MATLAB, Octave and Python side by side
    
  
    The same ideas appear in all three environments, and much published audiology code is in MATLAB or Python. The table translates common tasks. In the Python column, np is NumPy, plt is Matplotlib's pyplot, sf is the soundfile package and signal is scipy.signal; see the Audiology Python Primer for the Python details. The single most important difference is indexing: MATLAB and Octave count from 1 and include the end of a range, Python counts from 0 and excludes it.
    
  
    
    
    TaskMATLABGNU OctavePython (NumPy, SciPy)
    
    
      
    1. Row of 1 to 10x = 1:10samex = np.arange(1, 11)
    
      
    2. Equally spaced valueslinspace(0, 1, 5)samenp.linspace(0, 1, 5)
    
      
    3. Array of zeroszeros(3, 2)samenp.zeros((3, 2))
    
      
    4. First and last elementx(1), x(end)samex[0], x[-1]
    
      
    5. Elements 2 to 4x(2:4)samex[1:4]
    
      
    6. Element-wise producta .* bsamea * b
    
      
    7. Matrix productA * BsameA @ B
    
      
    8. Logical indexingf(thr > 40)samef[thr > 40]
    
      
    9. Column means of a matrixmean(A) or mean(A, 1)sameA.mean(axis=0)
    
      
    10. Seeded random noiserng(1); randn(N, 1)same syntax, different numbersnp.random.default_rng(1).standard_normal(N)
    
      
    11. Read a WAV file[x, fs] = audioread('a.wav')samex, fs = sf.read('a.wav')
    
      
    12. Write a WAV fileaudiowrite('b.wav', x, fs)samesf.write('b.wav', x, fs)
    
      
    13. FFTX = fft(x)sameX = np.fft.fft(x) or np.fft.rfft(x)
    
      
    14. Apply a filtery = filter(b, a, x)samey = signal.lfilter(b, a, x)
    
      
    15. Butterworth design[b, a] = butter(4, 1000/(fs/2)) (Signal Processing Toolbox)same after pkg load signalb, a = signal.butter(4, 1000, fs=fs)
    
      
    16. Plot with labelsplot(t, x); xlabel('Time (s)')sameplt.plot(t, x); plt.xlabel('Time (s)')
    
      
    17. Formatted printfprintf('%.1f dB\n', L)same (also printf)print(f"{L:.1f} dB")
    
      
    18. for loopfor k = 1:n ... endsame (also endfor)for k in range(n): (indented block)
    
      
    19. Define a functionfunction y = f(x) ... end in f.msame; in scripts, define before usedef f(x): return y
    
      
    20. Read a CSV tableT = readtable('d.csv')textscan or csvread (no tables in Octave 8)T = pandas.read_csv('d.csv')
    
        
  
    
  
    
    
    Choose MATLAB when
    • the department has a licence and colleagues or supervisors use it;
    • you are adapting published MATLAB code (auditory models, CI and hearing-aid simulations, many psychoacoustic toolboxes);
    • you want integrated tools: Live Scripts, App Designer, toolbox documentation and support.
    
    
    Choose Octave or Python when
    • you need free software, for home use or to share with others without licences;
    • Octave: you want to run existing MATLAB scripts unchanged at no cost;
    • Python: you need machine learning, web or database connections, or libraries such as librosa and Parselmouth (Praat in Python).
    
  
    

  
    16. Exercises and references
    
  
    Exercises
    
  
    The exercises are graded from easy to hard. Try each one before opening the model answer. All printed model answers were run in GNU Octave 8.4 with the functions from this topic in the current folder; where random numbers are involved, MATLAB prints different digits. For the laboratory record, submit a Live Script (or a published script) containing E1 to E12 with your own comments, plus one of E13 to E15 and one mini-project from section 13 with its figures.
    
  
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Calculate the SPL of RMS pressures of 0.2 Pa, 2 Pa and 63.2 mPa. Then find the pressure that corresponds to 120 dB SPL.
    


    

    p0 = 20e-6;
p = [0.2 2 0.0632];
spl = 20*log10(p / p0)
p120 = p0 * 10^(120/20)
    

    spl =

    80.000   100.000    69.994

p120 = 20
    

    

    0.2 Pa is 80 dB SPL, 2 Pa is 100 dB SPL and 63.2 mPa is about 70 dB SPL (a factor of √10 in pressure is 10 dB). 120 dB SPL is 20 Pa. Note the element-wise division by a scalar needs no dot, and log10 works on the whole vector at once.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Three machines each produce 70 dB SPL at a listener's position, and a fourth produces 64 dB SPL. What is the total level? By how much does switching off the 64 dB machine reduce it?
    


    

    L = [70 70 70 64];
Ltot = 10*log10(sum(10.^(L/10)))
L3 = 10*log10(sum(10.^(L(1:3)/10)));
reduction = Ltot - L3
    

    Ltot = 75.120
reduction = 0.3492
    

    

    The total is about 75.1 dB SPL; three equal sources give 70 + 10 log10(3) = 74.77 dB, and the 64 dB source adds only about 0.35 dB, so switching it off makes almost no difference. Uncorrelated sources add in power, hence 10.^(L/10) with a dot because L is a vector.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Create a vector of the audiometric frequencies from 125 Hz to 8000 Hz in half-octave steps. How many frequencies are there, and what are the 3rd and the last-but-one values?
    


    

    f = 125 * 2.^(0:0.5:6);
numel(f)
round(f([3 end-1]))
    

    ans = 13
ans =

    250   5657
    

    

    Thirteen frequencies; the 3rd is 250 Hz and the last-but-one is about 5657 Hz (4000 × √2). Clinical inter-octave frequencies are nominal values (750, 1500, 3000, 6000 Hz) that are not exact half-octave points; the exact values are 707, 1414, 2828 and 5657 Hz.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    A right ear has thresholds 25, 30, 40, 55, 70 and 75 dB HL at 250 to 8000 Hz. Compute the three-frequency and four-frequency PTAs, the worst threshold and its frequency, and the grade using who_grade (assume this is the better ear).
    


    

    f = [250 500 1000 2000 4000 8000];
thr = [25 30 40 55 70 75];
pta3 = mean(thr(2:4))
pta4 = mean(thr(ismember(f, [500 1000 2000 4000])))
[worst, k] = max(thr);
fprintf('worst %d dB HL at %d Hz\n', worst, f(k));
who_grade(pta4)
    

    pta3 = 41.667
pta4 = 48.750
worst 75 dB HL at 8000 Hz
ans = moderate
    

    

    PTA3 is 41.67 dB HL, PTA4 is 48.75 dB HL, which is moderate on the example WHO 2021 scale; the worst threshold is 75 dB HL at 8 kHz. Notice that PTA3 and PTA4 lead to the same grade here but would not for many sloping losses, so always state which average you used.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Right: 10 15 20 30 50 55; left: 10 10 20 45 70 60 (dB HL, 250 to 8000 Hz). List the frequencies where the ears differ by 15 dB or more and count them. Are two of them adjacent?
    


    

    f = [250 500 1000 2000 4000 8000];
R = [10 15 20 30 50 55];  L = [10 10 20 45 70 60];
d = abs(R - L);
flag = d >= 15;
f(flag)
nnz(flag)
any(flag(1:end-1) & flag(2:end))     % two neighbouring frequencies both flagged
    

    ans =

   2000   4000

ans = 2
ans = 1
    

    

    2000 and 4000 Hz differ by 15 and 20 dB; nnz counts the true values (2), and the last line tests every pair of neighbours at once by comparing the mask with itself shifted by one. Such adjacency rules appear in several asymmetry criteria; the exact rule depends on the protocol.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Write anonymous functions pa2spl and spl2pa, and check that converting 45, 65 and 94 dB SPL to pascals and back returns the original values to within 10−10 dB.
    


    

    pa2spl = @(p) 20*log10(p / 20e-6);
spl2pa = @(L) 20e-6 * 10.^(L / 20);
L = [45 65 94];
p = spl2pa(L)
max(abs(pa2spl(p) - L)) < 1e-10
    

    p =

   3.5566e-03   3.5566e-02   1.0024e+00

ans = 1
    

    

    The round trip is exact to rounding error (ans = 1). The dotted power 10.^ is essential because L is a vector. Testing a pair of inverse functions by a round trip is a quick and useful habit for any conversion code.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Use make_tone to make a 4 kHz tone of 200 ms at 44.1 kHz with 5 ms ramps and a steady level of −30 dBFS. How many samples does it have, how many samples are in each ramp, and what is the level of the steady part?
    


    

    fs = 44100;
x = make_tone(4000, 0.2, fs, -30, 5);
numel(x)
nr = round(0.005 * fs)
fprintf('steady part %.2f dBFS\n', dbfs(x(nr+1:end-nr)))
    

    ans = 8820
nr = 221
steady part -30.00 dBFS
    

    

    8820 samples; each ramp is 221 samples (220.5 rounded away from zero), and the steady part is at −30.00 dBFS. The half-sample rounding means the ramp lasts 5.01 ms, which is worth knowing when the specification is strict.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Single-sweep noise is 8 µV RMS. Using the √N rule, find the smallest number of sweeps that brings the residual noise below 0.1 µV, first with a formula and then with a while loop that doubles N from 1 until the target is met.
    


    

    noise1 = 8;  target = 0.1;
N_exact = ceil((noise1 / target)^2)
N = 1;
while noise1 / sqrt(N) >= target
    N = 2 * N;
end
fprintf('doubling: N = %d gives %.3f uV\n', N, noise1 / sqrt(N));
    

    N_exact = 6400
doubling: N = 8192 gives 0.088 uV
    

    

    The formula gives 6400 sweeps; doubling reaches 8192 (0.088 µV). In practice the rule holds only if the noise is random and stationary: artefacts, muscle activity and mains interference do not average away as neatly, which is why artefact rejection and a relaxed patient matter.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Sample a 7 kHz tone at 8 kHz for 1 s. Find the frequency of the largest FFT peak and explain the result. Then repeat at 16 kHz.
    


    

    for fs = [8000 16000]
    t = (0:fs-1)' / fs;
    x = sin(2*pi*7000*t);
    A = abs(fft(x)) / fs;
    [~, k] = max(A(1:fs/2+1));
    fprintf('fs = %5d Hz: peak at %d Hz\n', fs, (k - 1));
end
    

    fs =  8000 Hz: peak at 1000 Hz
fs = 16000 Hz: peak at 7000 Hz
    

    

    At 8 kHz the Nyquist frequency is 4 kHz, so the 7 kHz tone is aliased to |7000 − 8000| = 1000 Hz and is indistinguishable from a real 1 kHz tone. At 16 kHz it is correctly represented. With a 1 s block the bin spacing is 1 Hz, so index k corresponds to k − 1 Hz. This is the central observation of Practical P2; try it in the plotting playground too.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    The signal x = 0.3*sin(2*pi*750*t) + 0.1*sin(2*pi*3100*t + 1) is sampled at 16 kHz for 1 s. Without looking at the formula, write code that lists every component above 0.01 in amplitude with its frequency and amplitude.
    


    

    fs = 16000;  N = fs;  t = (0:N-1)' / fs;
x = 0.3*sin(2*pi*750*t) + 0.1*sin(2*pi*3100*t + 1);
A = abs(fft(x)) / N;  A = A(1:N/2+1);  A(2:end-1) = 2*A(2:end-1);
fk = (0:N/2)' * fs / N;
k = find(A > 0.01);
fprintf('%6.0f Hz  amplitude %.3f\n', [fk(k) A(k)]');
    

       750 Hz  amplitude 0.300
  3100 Hz  amplitude 0.100
    

    

    750 Hz at 0.300 and 3100 Hz at 0.100. The phase offset of the second component does not change its magnitude. Because both frequencies are whole numbers of hertz and the block is 1 s long, there is no leakage; with a non-integer frequency, apply a Hann window and divide by sum(w).
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Design a 65-coefficient (M = 64) low-pass filter with a 2 kHz cut-off at 16 kHz using fir_lowpass. Report its gain at 1, 1.8, 2, 2.2 and 3 kHz and its delay in milliseconds. What happens to the transition if M is 256?
    


    

    fs = 16000;
for M = [64 256]
    h = fir_lowpass(2000, fs, M);
    g = 20*log10(abs(fresp(h, 1, [1000 1800 2000 2200 3000], fs)));
    fprintf('M = %3d: %s dB, delay %.2f ms\n', M, sprintf('%7.1f', g), M/2/fs*1000);
end
    

    M =  64:    -0.0   -1.2   -6.0  -17.5  -60.0 dB, delay 2.00 ms
M = 256:    -0.0    0.0   -6.0  -63.2  -67.5 dB, delay 8.00 ms
    

    

    With M = 64 the response is already 1.2 dB down at 1.8 kHz and only 17.5 dB down at 2.2 kHz, with a 2 ms delay. With M = 256 it is flat at 1.8 kHz and more than 60 dB down at 2.2 kHz: the transition band is about four times narrower, but the delay is 8 ms. Longer FIR filters buy sharper edges with delay, a real constraint in hearing aids, where total processing delay is typically kept to a few milliseconds.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Speech at −26 dBFS is mixed with noise at +3 dB SNR. Predict the mixture level, then check it with mix_at_snr using a tone as the signal and seeded Gaussian noise.
    


    

    pred = -26 + 10*log10(1 + 10^(-3/10))
fs = 16000;  rng(20);
s = make_tone(1000, 1, fs, -26, 0);
[m, so, no] = mix_at_snr(s, randn(fs, 1), 3);
fprintf('SNR %.2f dB, mixture %.2f dBFS\n', dbfs(so) - dbfs(no), dbfs(m));
    

    pred = -24.236
SNR 3.00 dB, mixture -24.27 dBFS
    

    

    The prediction is −24.24 dBFS: the noise adds 10 log10(1 + 10−0.3) = 1.76 dB. The measured mixture level differs from the prediction by a few hundredths of a decibel, because a finite sample of noise is not exactly uncorrelated with the signal (the digits depend on the random values).
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Replace the deterministic listener in the up-down threshold search of section 8 with a logistic psychometric function, P(response) = 1 / (1 + exp(−(L − 37)/2)), draw responses with rand, run the procedure 500 times and report the mean and standard deviation of the threshold estimates.
    


    

    rng(30);
levels = -10:5:120;  est = zeros(500, 1);
for run = 1:500
    asc = zeros(size(levels));  L = 40;  up = false;  thr = NaN;  n = 0;
    while isnan(thr) && n < 60
        n = n + 1;
        heard = rand < 1 / (1 + exp(-(L - 37) / 2));
        if heard
            if up
                k = find(levels == L);  asc(k) = asc(k) + 1;
                if asc(k) >= 2, thr = L; end
            end
            L = L - 10;  up = false;
        else
            L = L + 5;  up = true;
        end
    end
    est(run) = thr;
end
ok = est(~isnan(est));                    % runs that reached a threshold
fprintf('%d runs: mean %.1f dB HL, SD %.1f dB, range %d to %d\n', ...
    numel(ok), mean(ok), std(ok), min(ok), max(ok));
    

    500 runs: mean 39.5 dB HL, SD 2.7 dB, range 30 to 45
    

    

    In the Octave run the estimates range from 30 to 45 dB HL with a mean of about 39.5 dB HL and a standard deviation of about 2.7 dB (MATLAB's random numbers give slightly different values). The mean lies above the 37 dB HL midpoint of the psychometric function because of the 5 dB steps and the ascending-response rule. The spread is test-retest variability arising purely from the procedure and the listener's psychometric function, which is why a 5 to 10 dB change between visits needs care before it is called a real change.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Write a script that finds every WAV file in the current folder, measures its duration, sampling rate and RMS level, and writes one CSV row per file. Test it on three tones you create first.
    


    

    fs = 16000;
audiowrite('batch_a.wav', make_tone(500, 0.5, fs, -20, 10), fs);
audiowrite('batch_b.wav', make_tone(1000, 1.0, fs, -30, 10), fs);
audiowrite('batch_c.wav', make_tone(2000, 0.25, fs, -25, 10), fs);
files = dir('batch_*.wav');
fid = fopen('batch_levels.csv', 'w');
fprintf(fid, 'file,fs,duration_s,level_dbfs\n');
for k = 1:numel(files)
    [x, fsk] = audioread(files(k).name);
    fprintf(fid, '%s,%d,%.3f,%.2f\n', files(k).name, fsk, size(x,1)/fsk, dbfs(x(:,1)));
end
fclose(fid);
type batch_levels.csv
    

    file,fs,duration_s,level_dbfs
batch_a.wav,16000,0.500,-20.11
batch_b.wav,16000,1.000,-30.06
batch_c.wav,16000,0.250,-25.23
    

    

    dir returns a structure array with one element per file; files(k).name is the k-th name. The levels are slightly below the requested values because they include the ramps (the shorter the tone, the larger the effect: compare file c with file b). In MATLAB you could collect the results in a table and use writetable; the fprintf version works everywhere.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Modify the noise vocoder of mini-project (e) so that the number of channels and the carrier type (noise or sine at the channel's geometric centre frequency) are inputs of a function vocode(x, fs, nch, carrier). Make 4-, 8- and 16-channel versions of a sentence with each carrier and describe what you hear.
    Model answer: the channel loop stays the same; the carrier line becomes if strcmp(carrier, 'sine'), car = sin(2*pi*sqrt(lo*hi)*t); else, car = fft_bandpass(randn(size(x)), fs, lo, hi); end with t = (0:numel(x)-1)'/fs, and the function returns y. Expected observations with real speech: intelligibility in quiet improves steeply from 4 to 8 channels and less from 8 to 16; sine carriers sound more tonal and can convey slightly more periodicity cues than noise carriers; voice pitch and music remain poor in all versions, illustrating why CI users find pitch-based tasks difficult. Report observations as a table (channels × carrier) with a sentence-recognition score from two or three listeners if time allows.
