    5.5
    Audacity: a complete hands-on lab manual
    
  
    Supplementary4 hoursPractical P12
    
  
    Audacity is a free, open-source waveform editor that runs on Windows, macOS and Linux. This lab manual takes a student who has never opened it to the point of preparing calibrated tones, band-limited speech, speech-in-babble mixtures at a known SNR and correctly formatted WAV files, while understanding the signal processing behind every menu item and keeping a processing log that makes the work reproducible.
    

  
    
    
    What it is
    A sample-based audio editor: it loads audio into a project, shows each channel as a waveform (or spectrogram), lets you select regions in time (and in frequency), applies effects, generators and analysers to the selection, mixes several tracks and exports the result to standard audio files.
    
    
    Why it is required
    Audiology clinics and research groups need a tool that anyone can install without a licence to trim recordings, set levels, create tones and noise, check spectra and build test stimuli. Audacity is the common denominator: a student can repeat the lab at home, and a research method written for Audacity can be reproduced in another centre.
    
    
    How it works
    Audio is decoded into 32-bit floating-point samples stored inside the project file. Every command is a mathematical operation on those samples: multiplication by a gain, a digital filter, a level-dependent gain, a short-time Fourier transform with modified magnitudes, or a sum of tracks. Nothing reaches a new audio file until you export, and export applies the chosen sample rate, bit depth, dither and channel mapping.
    
  
    

  
    RememberThis topic is the software-specific companion to 5.2 Audio editing, which explains the general concepts (destructive and non-destructive editing, fade shapes, compression curves). Here we go into the exact Audacity commands, parameters and exercises. 5.1 places Audacity among the other research tools, and 5.6 repeats the same exercises in Adobe Audition so that you can compare them. For scripted, fully reproducible stimulus generation see the Audiology Python Primer and 5.4 MATLAB.
    

  
    Version notes and how to use this manual
    
  
    This manual describes Audacity 3.x, specifically the 3.6 and 3.7 releases, which is the version installed in most teaching laboratories at the time of writing. Menu names and locations are taken from the official Audacity manual for 3.7. Audacity changes between versions, sometimes substantially:
    
  
      
    
    • From 3.2 onwards the Effect menu is grouped by function (Volume and Compression, Fading, Pitch and Tempo, EQ and Filters, Noise Removal and Repair, Delay and Reverb, Distortion and Modulation, Special, Spectral Tools, Legacy Effects). Older tutorials show a single long alphabetical list; the effect names are mostly the same. The grouping can be changed under Edit > Preferences > Effects.
      • 
    
    • From 3.4 onwards the old File > Export > Export as WAV, Export as MP3 and Export Multiple commands are replaced by a single File > Export Audio dialog with an Export Range option (Entire Project, Current Selection, Multiple Files).
      • 
    
    • From 3.6 onwards the Compressor and Limiter were rewritten (with a live compression curve and real-time preview); the previous versions remain available as Legacy Compressor and Legacy Limiter.
      • 
    
    • Audacity 4.0 was released in September 2026 with a rebuilt interface, a new project format (.aup4) and a redesigned clip editing model. Early release notes report that some 3.x features, including the Macro Manager, the Mixer Board, time tracks and some plug-in formats, are not present in 4.0. 3.x projects (.aup3) can be opened and converted, but the conversion is one-way. If your laboratory has moved to 4.x, the signal processing in this manual is unchanged but some menu paths will differ: check the Help menu and the official manual for your version.
      • 
  
    
  
    Menu paths are written as Menu > Submenu > Item. On macOS, Ctrl in the shortcuts is replaced by Cmd, and Preferences is under the Audacity menu. Each laboratory exercise lists the steps, what you should see after each step and why the step is done. Write every processing step in the log (template near the end of this topic) as you go.
    

  
    Background and principle of operation
    
  
    Project versus audio file
    
  
    An audio file (WAV, FLAC, MP3) contains one sequence of samples per channel plus a small header (sample rate, bit depth, channel count, optional metadata). An Audacity project is a different thing: a single .aup3 file (an SQLite database) that stores all the tracks, clips, labels, envelopes, effect settings in the real-time rack and the undo history for the current session, all in 32-bit float by default. When you import a WAV file, Audacity copies its samples into the project; the original file on disk is never changed by editing. Two consequences follow:
    
  
      
    
    • Saving the project (File > Save Project) does not produce an audio file that other software can play. To produce a WAV for an audiometer, a listening test program or MATLAB you must use File > Export Audio.
      • 
    
    • Exporting over the original recording is the only way to destroy it from inside Audacity. Always export to a new file name in a separate folder.
      • 
  
    
  
    Destructive, in-project editing and real-time effects
    
  
    Effects chosen from the Effect menu are applied destructively to the project's copy of the samples (you can still undo them during the session). Since 3.2 Audacity also offers real-time effects: click the Effects button in a track's control panel and add effects to a per-track rack. These are applied during playback and export without changing the stored samples, and their settings remain editable. Since 3.6 there is also a master effects rack applied to the whole mix. Real-time effects are useful for listening tests of settings; for stimulus files, applying effects destructively and logging each one is easier to document.
    
  
    Samples, full scale and the two dBFS conventions for RMS
    
  
    Inside Audacity a sample value of ±1.0 is full scale (0 dBFS). Peak level in dBFS is 20 log10 of the largest absolute sample. For RMS there are two conventions, and mixing them up is a frequent source of 3 dB errors in stimulus preparation:
    
  
      
    
    • Raw RMS: 20 log10(RMS). A full-scale sine wave has RMS 0.707 and reads −3.01 dB. Audacity's Analyze > Measure RMS, the Contrast tool and Loudness Normalization in RMS mode use this convention.
      • 
    
    • Sine-referenced RMS (as in the AES17 measurement standard): a full-scale sine reads 0 dB. Adobe Audition offers this as the option "0 dB = FS Sine Wave" in Amplitude Statistics.
      • 
  
    
  
    In this manual "dBFS RMS" always means raw RMS, which matches Audacity's own tools. State the convention in your log.
    

  
    Architecture and signal flow
    
  
    Figure 5.5a is a simplified schematic of the Audacity 3.x main window. Learn the names of the regions: the manual, forum answers and the exercises below refer to them.
    
  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.5a. Simplified schematic of the Audacity 3.x main window (not to scale). Toolbars can be moved or hidden, so your layout may differ; View > Toolbars > Reset Toolbars restores the default.
    

  
    Figure 5.5b shows the signal flow from microphone to exported file. Every block is a place where level, bandwidth or quality can be lost, so each must be checked and logged.
    
  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.5b. Signal flow through an Audacity session, from transducer to exported file.
    

  
    Components of the Audacity window
    
  
    
    
    Region or toolbarWhat it doesWhat can go wrong
    
    
      
    Menu barAll commands: File, Edit, Select, View, Transport, Tracks, Generate, Effect, Analyze, Tools, HelpEffect, Generate and Analyze items are greyed out when nothing is selected or while playback is running: press Stop first
    
      
    Transport toolbarPlay (Space), Stop, Record (R), Loop (L), skip to start and endRecord appends to the selected track at the cursor; Shift+R records a new track
    
      
    Tools toolbarSelection tool (F1), Envelope tool (F2), Draw tool (F3), Multi-tool (F6); older versions also have Zoom and Time Shift toolsEnvelope tool left active: clicks add gain points instead of selecting
    
      
    Audio Setup toolbarAudio host, playback device, recording device, recording channels, Audio Settings (project sample rate)Wrong device (e.g. laptop microphone instead of the interface); mono and stereo mismatch
    
      
    Recording and playback metersPeak and RMS bars in dBFS; click the recording meter to start monitoring without recordingMeter range set too small to see low levels (right-click the meter to change it)
    
      
    Edit and View controlsCut, copy, paste, trim, silence buttons; zoom in and out, fit selection, fit projectZoomed so far out that clicks and short gaps are invisible
    
      
    TimelineTime ruler; click-drag to quick-play a region; right-click for time format and loop settingsAccidental Quick-Play when trying to select
    
      
    Track control panelTrack name and menu (waveform or spectrogram view, rate, format, split stereo), Mute, Solo, gain slider, pan slider, Effects button, SelectGain and pan sliders here are applied only at playback and export (non-destructive); forgetting them changes the export
    
      
    Clips and clip handlesEach continuous piece of audio is a clip with a title bar; drag the bar to move it; double-click to select the clipMoving one clip out of alignment with another track
    
      
    Label trackNamed point or region labels (Ctrl+B); used for exporting multiple files and for documenting where stimuli areLabels not moved when audio is cut unless sync-lock is on
    
      
    Selection toolbarNumeric start, end, length or centre of the selection in chosen units (seconds, samples, hh:mm:ss + milliseconds)Units set to seconds when you need sample precision
    
      
    Snapping toolbarSnap selection edges to a time grid (seconds, milliseconds, frames, beats)Snapping on when you want free selection, or off when you need exact 10 ms boundaries
    
      
    Status barPlaying or recording state; actual sample rate reported by the deviceActual rate differs from project rate: resampling is taking place
    
        
  
    

  
    Key parameters and specifications
    
  
    
    
    ParameterTypical value or rangeClinical or research meaning
    
    
      
    Project sample rate44 100 or 48 000 Hz (8 to 384 kHz possible)Sets the audio bandwidth (Nyquist = fs/2). 44.1 kHz covers the full audiometric range; 16 kHz is enough for telephone-band speech but not for high-frequency audiometry stimuli
    
      
    Default sample format32-bit floatInternal processing format; values above 1.0 are kept, so clipping appears only at export or playback
    
      
    Export bit depth16 bit (about 98 dB dynamic range) or 24 bit (about 146 dB, theoretical)16 bit is adequate for most speech stimuli; 24 bit or 32-bit float for research recordings and very quiet stimuli
    
      
    Recording peak levelabout -12 to -6 dBFSLeaves headroom for unexpected loud sounds; far above the noise floor of modern interfaces
    
      
    Processed peak before integer export≤ -1 dBFSPrevents clipping; also leaves room for inter-sample peaks in lossy encoders
    
      
    Calibration tone leveloften -20 dBFS RMS (other laboratories use -12 to -26)Reference for setting the audiometer VU or sound-level meter; speech stimuli are then matched to it
    
      
    Stimulus onset and offset rampstypically 5 to 25 ms (10 ms common)Avoids audible clicks and spectral splatter at tone onsets
    
      
    Noise Reduction defaultstypically 6 to 12 dB reduction, sensitivity 6, smoothing 3 to 6 bands (defaults differ between versions)Mild settings; stronger settings cause musical noise and dull speech
    
      
    Loudness Normalization default-23 LUFS (EBU R 128), or RMS mode at -20 dBFor broadcast-style loudness matching of speech lists
    
      
    Plot Spectrum size128 to 65 536 points (default 2048 or 4096 depending on version)Frequency resolution = fs/size; 4096 at 44.1 kHz gives about 10.8 Hz bins
    
        
  
    

  
    Algorithms and equations used throughout
    
  
    The equations below recur in almost every exercise. Each effect section later adds its own.
    
  
    A = 10^(G/20)          L_peak = 20 log10(max|x[n]|)   dBFS
L_rms = 20 log10( sqrt( (1/N) sum x[n]^2 ) )   dBFS (raw)
where G is a gain in dB, A the linear multiplier, x[n] the samples and N the number of samples in the measured region.
    
  
    Worked example (calibration tone amplitude). Audacity's Tone generator asks for a peak amplitude between 0 and 1. A sine of peak amplitude a has RMS a/√2. For −20 dBFS raw RMS we need RMS = 10^(−20/20) = 0.1, so a = 0.1 × √2 = 0.1414. The peak level is then 20 log10(0.1414) = −16.99 dBFS. If your laboratory uses the sine-referenced convention, the amplitude for −20 dBFS would be 0.1 instead. Always write down which one you used.
    
  
    SNR = L_rms(speech) - L_rms(noise)          dB
L_sum = 10 log10( 10^(L1/10) + 10^(L2/10) )   (uncorrelated)
where L1 and L2 are RMS levels in dBFS of two independent signals that are added (mixed).
    
  
    Worked example (mixing budget). A sentence at −26 dBFS RMS mixed with babble at −26 dBFS RMS gives SNR = 0 dB and a mixture RMS of 10 log10(2 × 10^(−2.6)) = −22.99 dBFS, that is 3 dB above either component. Peaks also add, sometimes coherently, so a mixture whose components peak at −8 dBFS can peak near −2 dBFS: check the mix peak before export.
    
  
    SNR_q = 6.02 B + 1.76   dB     (full-scale sine, B-bit PCM)
where B is the bit depth. For B = 16, SNR_q = 98.1 dB; for B = 24, 146.2 dB (converters achieve less in practice).
    
  
    Worked example (file size). One minute of 16-bit, 44.1 kHz mono PCM occupies 60 × 44 100 × 2 bytes = 5.29 MB. The same minute in 24-bit 48 kHz stereo is 60 × 48 000 × 3 × 2 = 17.28 MB. An MP3 at 128 kbit/s needs 128 000 × 60 / 8 = 0.96 MB, but its samples are no longer identical to the original.
    

  
    Lab 1: setting up, importing and recording
    
  
    Importing an existing file
    
  
      
    
    1. Start Audacity. An empty project opens. Choose File > Import > Audio (Ctrl+Shift+I) and pick a WAV file, or drag the file onto the window. You should see one track per file: a mono file gives one waveform, a stereo file gives a track with two waveforms (left above right). Why: import copies the samples into the project, leaving the original untouched.
      2. 
    
    2. Read the track's properties: click the track name menu (the name at the top of the track control panel). The menu shows the Rate and Format of the track. Note them in your log, because they are the properties of the source.
      3. 
    
    3. Check the project rate: Audio Setup > Audio Settings, Project Sample Rate. Audacity usually sets the project rate from the first file imported. If the track rate and project rate differ, playback and export involve resampling.
      4. 
    
    4. If a file will not open (for example some compressed video audio), install the optional FFmpeg library as described in the Audacity support pages; WAV, AIFF, FLAC, MP3 and Ogg Vorbis are supported without it.
      5. 
  
    
  
    Configuring a recording
    
  
      
    
    1. Connect the microphone to an audio interface (preferred) or the computer's input. In the Audio Setup toolbar choose Host (on Windows, prefer Windows WASAPI or MME for simple use; on macOS Core Audio; on Linux ALSA), then Recording Device (the interface) and Recording Channels (1 for mono speech, 2 for stereo). Why: the wrong device is the most common cause of a poor recording.
      2. 
    
    2. Set Audio Setup > Audio Settings > Project Sample Rate to 44 100 Hz or 48 000 Hz, matching the device's own rate as set in the operating system's sound settings. In Edit > Preferences > Quality, set Default Sample Format to 32-bit float. Why: recording at the device's native rate avoids resampling; float storage prevents rounding errors during later processing.
      3. 
    
    3. Click the recording meter (the microphone meter) once, or choose Transport > Transport Options and start monitoring. Speak at the intended level. You should see the meter move without recording.
      4. 
    
    4. Adjust the gain on the interface preamplifier (not in software if possible) until the loudest syllables peak around −12 to −6 dBFS. The recording meter's peak-hold line and the clip indicator (red) show the maximum. Why: analogue gain before the converter sets the signal-to-noise ratio; a software gain after the converter cannot recover a signal that was too quiet or clipped.
      5. 
    
    5. For headphone monitoring, prefer the interface's direct (zero-latency) monitoring. Transport > Transport Options > Software Playthrough sends the input through the computer to the output, which adds latency (tens of milliseconds) that is disturbing when talking.
      6. 
    
    6. Press Record (R) to record at the cursor (appending to a selected track) or Shift+R to record into a new track. Press Space or Stop to end. Record 2 s of room silence before speaking: this is your noise-only segment for later analysis and noise profiling.
      7. 
    
    7. Check for clipping: View > Show Clipping in Waveform (red lines mark samples at full scale) and Analyze > Find Clipping, which places labels at runs of clipped samples. If any are found, record again with less gain. Clipping cannot be reliably undone (Clip Fix only estimates the lost peaks).
      8. 
    
    8. Save the project immediately (File > Save Project > Save Project As) and export an untouched copy of the raw recording as a 24-bit WAV (see Lab 9). This is your archival original.
      9. 
  
    
  
    Common errorOperating-system "enhancements" (automatic gain, noise suppression, echo cancellation) on laptop and USB headset microphones alter the signal before it reaches Audacity. Disable them in the operating-system sound settings for any recording used for voice or speech analysis, and note the device model in the log.
    

  
    Lab 2: navigating, zooming and selecting
    
  
    Precise selection is the basis of every later step: an effect is applied only to the selected time range of the selected tracks.
    
  
    
    
    ActionMenu path (Audacity 3.x)ShortcutUse
    
    
      
    Zoom in / outView > Zoom > Zoom In / Zoom OutCtrl+1 / Ctrl+3Zoom in until single cycles are visible before a precise cut
    
      
    Zoom to selectionView > Zoom > Zoom to SelectionCtrl+EInspect a word or a click
    
      
    Fit project in windowView > Track Size > Fit to WidthCtrl+FOverview of the whole recording
    
      
    Vertical zoomClick or drag on the vertical ruler of a track; right-click for optionsnoneSee low-level noise between words
    
      
    Select all / noneSelect > All; Select > NoneCtrl+A; Ctrl+Shift+AEffects on whole tracks
    
      
    Selection by numbersSelection toolbar: type start and end or lengthnoneExactly 10.000 ms ramps, exactly 1.000 s tones
    
      
    Snap to zero crossingsSelect > At Zero CrossingsZMoves selection edges to the nearest zero crossing so cuts do not click
    
      
    Snap to gridSnapping toolbar: enable and choose a unit (for example milliseconds)noneSelections on round time values
    
      
    Region labelEdit > Labels > Add Label at SelectionCtrl+BName words, noise segments, stimuli
    
      
    Point label during playbackEdit > Labels > Add Label at Playback PositionCtrl+MMark events while listening
    
      
    Select a clipDouble-click the clipnoneSelect a whole segment quickly
    
        
  
    
  
      
    
    1. Import a recorded sentence. Press Ctrl+F to fit it. Click and drag across the first word with the Selection tool. The selected region is shaded; the Selection toolbar shows its start and end.
      2. 
    
    2. Click the down arrow in the Selection toolbar and change the time format to "hh:mm:ss + milliseconds", then to "samples". Why: sample units show the exact number of samples in a ramp or a stimulus, which matters when files must be sample-aligned.
      3. 
    
    3. Press Ctrl+E to zoom to the selection, then Ctrl+1 repeatedly until individual cycles of the voiced sound are visible. Press Z: the selection edges move slightly to zero crossings.
      4. 
    
    4. Press Ctrl+B and type "word1". A label track appears with a region label. Label the 2 s silence at the start "noise". Why: labels document exactly which regions you measured and processed, and later allow export of multiple files by label.
      5. 
    
    5. Type exact values into the Selection toolbar, for example start 00 h 00 m 01.000 s and length 00 h 00 m 00.010 s. You have selected exactly 10 ms (441 samples at 44.1 kHz).
      6. 
  
    

  
    Lab 3: editing (cut, copy, paste, trim, split, silence, duplicate, undo)
    
  
    
    
    CommandMenu pathShortcutWhat happens to the samples and timeline
    
    
      
    CutEdit > CutCtrl+XRemoves the selection to the clipboard; later audio moves left to close the gap
    
      
    DeleteEdit > DeleteDeleteRemoves without copying; audio closes up
    
      
    Copy / PasteEdit > Copy; Edit > PasteCtrl+C; Ctrl+VPaste inserts at the cursor, pushing later audio right, or replaces a selection
    
      
    DuplicateEdit > DuplicateCtrl+DCopies the selection into a new track at the same time position (useful for keeping an unprocessed reference)
    
      
    Split Cut / Split DeleteEdit > Remove Special > Split Cut / Split DeleteCtrl+Alt+X / Ctrl+Alt+KRemoves the selection but leaves a gap, so later audio keeps its time position
    
      
    Silence AudioEdit > Remove Special > Silence AudioCtrl+LReplaces the selection with zeros; duration unchanged
    
      
    Trim AudioEdit > Remove Special > Trim AudioCtrl+TKeeps only the selection in that clip and removes the rest (the kept audio stays at its time position)
    
      
    SplitEdit > Audio Clips > SplitCtrl+IDivides a clip at the selection edges into separate clips that can be moved independently
    
      
    JoinEdit > Audio Clips > JoinCtrl+JJoins clips in the selection, filling gaps with silence
    
      
    Undo / RedoEdit > Undo; Edit > RedoCtrl+Z; Ctrl+YSteps backwards and forwards through the history
    
      
    HistoryView > HistorynoneLists every operation in the session; clicking an earlier step returns to it; can discard older steps to save space
    
        
  
    
  
    Key ideaSilence Audio and Delete differ in a way that matters for stimuli: silence keeps the timing (useful when a noise track must stay aligned with a speech track), delete shortens the file. Also, digital zeros are quieter than room noise: a zeroed gap inside a noisy recording is audible as a "hole". To shorten a pause naturally, delete part of the pause rather than silencing it.
    
  
      
    
    1. Select the silence before the first word, leaving about 200 ms before the onset, and press Delete. The sentence now starts 200 ms after zero.
      2. 
    
    2. Select from 50 ms after the last word to the end and press Delete. Why: a consistent lead and tail make stimuli easier to align in test software.
      3. 
    
    3. Select the second word only and press Ctrl+D. A new track appears containing the word at the same time position. Mute the new track: it is a reference copy.
      4. 
    
    4. Select the middle of a pause and press Ctrl+I (Split). Two clips appear, each with its own handle. Drag the second clip by its handle 500 ms to the right. You have inserted a pause without touching the words.
      5. 
    
    5. Cut out a 30 ms region in the middle of a vowel, without snapping to zero crossings, and listen: a click is audible at the join. Undo (Ctrl+Z), press Z to snap to zero crossings, cut again and listen: the click is smaller. Why: a cut joins two samples with different values and slopes; the discontinuity is a broadband click.
      6. 
    
    6. Open View > History. Every operation is listed. Click an earlier step to go back. Note that the history is lost when the project is closed unless it is saved in the .aup3 file; the history is not a substitute for a written log.
      7. 
  
    

  
    Amplitude effects: amplify, normalise, loudness, fades and envelopes
    
  
    All the effects in this section multiply samples by a gain. They differ in how the gain is chosen (constant, computed from a measurement, or varying in time).
    
  
    
    
    Effect (menu path)Parameters in plain languageUnderlying operationAudiology useCaution
    
    
      
    Amplify (Effect > Volume and Compression > Amplify)Amplification (dB): the gain. New Peak Amplitude (dB): what the peak will become. Allow clipping: permits gains that push peaks over 0 dBFSy[n] = A x[n], A = 10^(G/20); on opening, G is pre-set to the value that brings the peak to 0 dBSetting a known gain step, for example exactly −10 dB for a lower presentation levelWith "Allow clipping" ticked, float values above 1 clip on export
    
      
    Normalize (Effect > Volume and Compression > Normalize)Remove DC offset; Normalize peak amplitude to (dB); Normalize stereo channels independentlySubtract mean; A = 10^(target/20) / max|x|Bring recordings to a common peak before further processing; remove DC before editingEqual peaks do not mean equal loudness or equal RMS
    
      
    Loudness Normalization (Effect > Volume and Compression > Loudness Normalization)Normalize perceived loudness to (LUFS), or RMS to (dB); Normalize stereo channels independently; Treat mono as dual-monoGain = target minus measured integrated loudness (ITU-R BS.1770 K-weighting and gating) or RMSEqualising the level of a list of sentences or wordsLoudness uses K-weighting and gating, so it differs from raw RMS; choose one method and use it for all items
    
      
    Fade In / Fade Out (Effect > Fading)No parameters: fades over the whole selectionLinear gain ramp 0 to 1 (or 1 to 0)Onset and offset ramps on tones and wordsLinear ramps are less smooth than raised-cosine ramps; for tones prefer Adjustable Fade with an S-curve or cosine shape
    
      
    Adjustable Fade (Effect > Fading > Adjustable Fade)Fade Type (Fade Up, Fade Down, S-Curve Up, S-Curve Down); Mid-fade Adjust (%) bends the curve; Start and End as % or dB; handy presets (linear, exponential, logarithmic, rounded, cosine, S-curve)Gain follows a chosen curve across the selectionRaised-cosine style ramps; partial fades such as a fade from 100% to 50%Check the ramp duration: it equals the selection length
    
      
    Studio Fade Out (Effect > Fading)NoneSmooth fade with a gradual low-pass, designed for musicRarely used for stimuliChanges spectrum as well as level
    
      
    Envelope tool (Tools toolbar, F2)Control points on a gain curve drawn over the trackTime-varying gain, interpolated between points; applied at playback and exportDucking a noise track, shaping a masker onset, crossfades between clipsInvisible in the stored samples until Tracks > Mix > Mix and Render; remember it at export
    
        
  
    
  
    raised-cosine ramp:  g[n] = 0.5 (1 - cos(pi n / M)),  n = 0..M
equal-dB (exponential) ramp: g_dB[n] = -D (1 - n/M)
where M is the ramp length in samples (for 10 ms at 44.1 kHz, M = 441) and D the depth of an exponential fade in dB (for example 60 dB).
    
  
    Energy check. Over a raised-cosine ramp the mean of g² is 3/8. A 1.000 s tone with 10 ms ramps at each end therefore has a mean-square of (0.98 + 0.02 × 3/8) = 0.9875 of the steady tone, a level difference of only 10 log10(0.9875) = −0.05 dB. You can ignore the ramps when stating the RMS of a 1 s tone, but not of a 50 ms tone burst, where they reduce RMS by more.
    
  
    Lab 4: prepare a 1 kHz calibration tone at -20 dBFS RMS with 10 ms ramps
    
  
      
    
    1. Create a new project (File > New). Set the project rate to 44 100 Hz (Audio Setup > Audio Settings).
      2. 
    
    2. Choose Generate > Tone. Set Waveform = Sine, Frequency = 1000 Hz, Amplitude = 0.1414, Duration = 00 h 00 m 30.000 s (or as your protocol requires). Click Generate. You should see a solid band with peaks at about 0.14 on the vertical ruler. Why 0.1414: this peak amplitude gives an RMS of 0.1 = −20 dBFS (raw RMS), as calculated above.
      3. 
    
    3. Type a selection of start 0 and length 10 ms in the Selection toolbar. Choose Effect > Fading > Adjustable Fade, click the "S-Curve In" preset (or set Fade Type to S-Curve Up, Start 0%, End 100%) and click Apply. Zoom in: the tone starts from zero and grows smoothly.
      4. 
    
    4. Select the last 10 ms (type the start as 29.990 s and length 10 ms) and apply Adjustable Fade with "S-Curve Out" (S-Curve Down).
      5. 
    
    5. Verify: select the whole tone (Ctrl+A) and run Analyze > Measure RMS. It should report about −20.0 dB. Run Analyze > Plot Spectrum with Size 8192 and Hann window: a single peak at 1000 Hz, with no visible harmonics above the window's side-lobe level.
      6. 
    
    6. Check the peak: Effect > Volume and Compression > Amplify opens with an Amplification value of +16.99 dB (the gain that would bring the peak to 0 dBFS), confirming the peak at −16.99 dBFS. Click Cancel.
      7. 
    
    7. Export as WAV, Signed 16-bit PCM, mono, 44 100 Hz (Lab 9). Name the file with its content, for example cal_1k_-20dBFSrms_44k16_mono.wav, and record the settings in the log.
      8. 
  
    
  
    SafetyA calibration tone is only "calibrated" once the playback chain is measured. The file defines a digital level; the acoustic level in dB SPL or dB HL depends on the sound card, amplifier, transducer and coupler. Measure the tone with a sound-level meter in the appropriate coupler before any listener hears the stimuli (see 5.3 on transducer calibration). Never apply noise reduction, compression or EQ to a calibration tone or to the noise used for calibration.
    
  
    Using the envelope tool
    
  
      
    
    1. Press F2 (Envelope tool). A blue line appears along the top and bottom of each waveform (the gain curve, initially at 1).
      2. 
    
    2. Click on the envelope to add a control point, drag it down to reduce gain. Add points 0.5 s apart to create a dip. Listen.
      3. 
    
    3. Press F1 to return to the Selection tool. The waveform display reflects the envelope, but the stored samples are unchanged until you choose Tracks > Mix > Mix and Render (or export).
      4. 
  
    

  
    Dynamics: compressor, limiter and noise gate
    
  
    A dynamics processor measures the level of the signal over a short time and applies a gain that depends on that level. The static behaviour is described by the input-output curve; the dynamic behaviour by attack and release times. The general theory is in 5.2 and, for hearing aids, in Unit 3. Here are Audacity's implementations.
    
  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.5c. Structure of a feed-forward compressor or limiter as implemented in Audacity 3.6 and later. Lookahead delays the main path so that gain reduction can start before a peak arrives.
    
  
    static curve (knee width W, threshold T, ratio R), in dB:
  if 2(x - T) < -W:      y = x
  if |2(x - T)| ≤ W:     y = x + (1/R - 1)(x - T + W/2)^2 / (2W)
  if 2(x - T) > W:       y = T + (x - T)/R
smoothing:  g[n] = a g[n-1] + (1 - a) g_target[n],  a = exp(-1/(tau fs))
where x and y are input and output levels in dB, tau the attack time constant (when gain is falling) or release time constant (when gain is rising), and fs the sample rate. This is the standard textbook form; Audacity's exact internals may differ in detail.
    
  
    Worked example. Threshold −20 dB, ratio 4:1, hard knee (W = 0). A passage at −8 dB is 12 dB above threshold; the output is −20 + 12/4 = −17 dB, a gain reduction of 9 dB. A passage at −30 dB is below threshold and unchanged. With a 10 ms attack at 44.1 kHz, a = exp(−1/441) = 0.99773 per sample; with 100 ms release, a = 0.99977.
    
  
    
    
    Effect (menu path)Parameters in plain languageAudiology useCaution
    
    
      
    Compressor (Effect > Volume and Compression > Compressor), 3.6 and laterThreshold: level where compression starts. Ratio: dB in per dB out above threshold. Knee Width: range over which the curve bends gradually. Make-up Gain: fixed gain added after compression. Lookahead: how far ahead the detector looks. Attack and Release: how quickly gain falls and recovers. A live graph shows the curve and the gain reduction.Demonstrating hearing-aid style compression on speech; reducing level differences between talkers in counselling recordingsChanges the envelope cues and the SNR of any noise present; never apply to calibrated speech-in-noise lists
    
      
    Legacy Compressor (Effect > Legacy Effects)Threshold, Noise Floor (below which gain is not raised), Ratio, Attack Time, Release Time, Make-up gain for 0 dB after compressing, Compress based on PeaksReproducing processing described in older papers or protocolsIts "make-up to 0 dB" option normalises the peak, changing overall level
    
      
    Limiter (Effect > Volume and Compression > Limiter)Threshold, Make-up target (output ceiling), Knee width, Lookahead, ReleaseGuarding against overs in a mix before exportA limiter is a compressor with an infinite ratio; heavy limiting audibly flattens speech peaks
    
      
    Noise Gate (Effect > Noise Removal and Repair > Noise Gate)Select Function (Gate or Analyze Noise Level); Stereo Linking; Gate threshold (dB); Level reduction (dB); Attack, Hold, Decay (ms)Silencing breathing and room noise between words in a voice recording. Audacity 3.x has no separate expander; a Noise Gate with a modest Level reduction (for example 6 to 10 dB) and slow Decay behaves as a gentle downward expanderChops soft word onsets and final consonants; makes the background pump on and off
    
        
  
    
  
    Clinical linkCompression applied to a mixture of speech and noise does not preserve the input SNR. During speech pauses the gain rises and the noise is amplified; during speech peaks the gain falls. With fast time constants and high ratios the long-term output SNR is typically lower than the input SNR at positive input SNRs (analysed by Naylor and Johannesson, 2009). This is one reason why speech-in-noise test materials are mixed at a calibrated SNR and never compressed afterwards, and why the SNR effects of hearing-aid compression are studied as a separate question.
    
  
    Lab 5: compress a speech track and compare waveforms
    
  
      
    
    1. Import a speech recording with a range of loud and soft words, normalised to a peak of −1 dBFS. Select the track and press Ctrl+D to duplicate it; rename the copy "compressed" (track name menu > Name). Why: the original stays beside the processed version for a visual and numeric comparison.
      2. 
    
    2. Measure RMS of both (Analyze > Measure RMS). They are identical at this point.
      3. 
    
    3. Select the copy and choose Effect > Volume and Compression > Compressor. Set Threshold −30 dB, Ratio 4:1, Knee Width 5 dB, Make-up Gain 0 dB, Lookahead 1 ms, Attack 5 ms, Release 100 ms. Use Preview. Apply.
      4. 
    
    4. Compare the waveforms. The compressed track has smaller differences between loud and soft syllables; peaks are lower. Measure its RMS and peak (Amplify dialog shows the peak).
      5. 
    
    5. Apply Effect > Volume and Compression > Loudness Normalization, RMS mode, to both tracks with the same target (for example −23 dB). Why: comparisons of processing must be made at equal level, otherwise listeners judge the louder version as better.
      6. 
    
    6. Now repeat with a fast release (10 ms) and listen for pumping and distortion of low-frequency vowels. Record in the log the settings and the change in peak-to-RMS ratio (crest factor), which is a simple numeric index of compression.
      7. 
  
    

  
    Modulation, pitch and time effects
    
  
    Modulation means varying a parameter of the signal (gain, delay, filter frequency) periodically in time with a low-frequency oscillator (LFO). Audacity 3.x includes Tremolo, Phaser and Wahwah as built-in effects (Effect > Distortion and Modulation), together with Distortion and Vocoder. It does not include a built-in chorus, flanger or vibrato; these are available as third-party plug-ins (for example LV2, VST3 or Nyquist plug-ins added through Effect > Plugin Manager). Audition (5.6) has Chorus, Flanger and Chorus/Flanger built in. Pitch and time changes are in Effect > Pitch and Tempo.
    
  
    
    
    EffectParameters in plain languageDSPAudiology useCaution
    
    
      
    TremoloWaveform type (sine, triangle, sawtooth, square), Starting phase, Wet level (depth, %), Frequency (Hz)y = x (1 − d/2 + (d/2) m(t)), m an LFO between −1 and 1: amplitude modulationCreating amplitude-modulated stimuli for demonstrations of temporal envelope processing; the concept behind AM tones used in auditory steady-state response (ASSR) testingASSR stimuli need precise modulation depth and frequency: generate them in code (5.4) and verify, rather than relying on an effect
    
      
    PhaserStages (number of all-pass sections), Dry/Wet, LFO Frequency, LFO Start Phase, Depth, Feedback, Output gainInput plus a copy passed through a chain of all-pass filters whose frequencies are swept; creates moving notches that are not harmonically spacedDemonstrating phase and notch perceptionNot for stimuli
    
      
    WahwahLFO Frequency, Start phase, Depth, Resonance, Wah frequency offset, Output gainResonant band-pass filter whose centre frequency is swept by the LFODemonstrating formant-like moving resonancesNot for stimuli
    
      
    Chorus and flanger (plug-ins)Delay, depth, rate, feedback, mixy[n] = x[n] + g x[n − D(t)]: a comb filter whose delay is swept; flanger about 0.1 to 10 ms, chorus about 15 to 30 msShowing comb filtering, the same effect produced by a reflection near a microphone or by a hearing-aid vent path mixing with processed soundComb notches remove information; never on stimuli
    
      
    Vibrato (plug-ins)Rate (Hz), depth (cents or ms)Variable delay only (no dry signal): pitch rises and fallsVoice demonstrations: vocal vibrato is typically about 5 to 7 HzAlters F0 measurements
    
      
    Distortion (Effect > Distortion and Modulation > Distortion)Distortion type (hard clipping, soft clipping, soft overdrive, and others), DC blocking filter, threshold and parameter sliders, repeat processingA memoryless non-linear curve y = f(x)Demonstrating what clipping in an overdriven hearing aid or microphone sounds like; teaching harmonic distortion measurementNew harmonics cannot be removed
    
      
    Change Pitch (Effect > Pitch and Tempo)Semitones (or from/to note, from/to frequency, percent change); Use high quality stretchingTime-scale modification plus resampling: pitch ratio 2^(s/12), duration unchangedShifting a voice to simulate a different talker for demonstrationsFormants shift with pitch, so voices sound unnatural beyond a few semitones
    
      
    Change Tempo (Effect > Pitch and Tempo)Percent change, beats per minute, or new length; Use high quality stretchingTime-scale modification (overlap-add family): duration changes, pitch unchangedSlowed speech for auditory training; time-compressed speech testsArtefacts (phasiness, repeated fragments) increase with larger changes; document the percentage
    
      
    Change Speed and Pitch (Effect > Pitch and Tempo)Speed multiplier or percent change; new lengthResampling only: duration and pitch change together, like a tape played fasterRarely appropriate for speech stimuliA 10% speed-up raises all frequencies by 10%, including formants
    
      
    Paulstretch, Sliding Stretch (Effect > Pitch and Tempo)Stretch factor, time resolution; start and end tempo and pitchExtreme time stretching; gradual changesSound-design onlyNot for measurement
    
        
  
    
  
    pitch ratio  r = 2^(s/12)          cents c = 1200 log2(f2/f1)
comb notches (y = x + g x[n-D]):  f_k = (2k + 1) / (2D),  k = 0,1,2...
vibrato: D(t) = D0 + d sin(2 pi fm t)
   instantaneous frequency scaling  = 1 - dD/dt = 1 - 2 pi fm d cos(2 pi fm t)
where s is the shift in semitones, D the delay in seconds, fm the modulation rate and d the peak delay excursion.
    
  
    Worked examples. A shift of +2 semitones multiplies every frequency by 2^(2/12) = 1.1225; −3 semitones by 0.8409. A 1 ms delay mixed with the direct sound puts notches at 500, 1500, 2500, 3500 Hz and so on. For vibrato of ±50 cents at 5.5 Hz, the frequency ratio at the extremes is 2^(50/1200) = 1.0293, so d = 0.0293 / (2π × 5.5) = 0.85 ms of delay excursion.
    
  
    The three pitch and time effects are easily confused. Change Pitch keeps duration and changes pitch; Change Tempo keeps pitch and changes duration; Change Speed and Pitch changes both, exactly like playing a recording faster. For time-compressed speech tests, use Change Tempo (or a dedicated time-compression algorithm documented in the test's manual) and report the percentage compression.
    

  
    
    
    Try it: effect previewer on a speech-like signal
    
    
    Choose an effect and adjust its parameters. The grey traces show the original (a two-syllable speech-like signal with a little 50 Hz mains hum and hiss); the teal traces show the result as it would be exported (clipped at full scale). For vibrato, the top plot shows the instantaneous fundamental frequency; for chorus and flanger, the dashed amber curve is the comb-filter response (shifted down 10 dB for visibility).
    
    
    
      
    
        
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
    
    
    
    
    
    
      
    Peak before
    
      
    Peak after (float)
    
      
    RMS before
    
      
    RMS after (exported)
    
      
    Clipping at export
    
    
    
    
    BeforeAfter
    
    
    
  
    
  
    Things to try: high-pass at 300 Hz with 24 dB/octave (the 50 and 150 Hz hum lines drop, and so does the lowest harmonic of the voice); low-pass at 3400 Hz (energy above 3.4 kHz falls away); notch at 50 Hz with Q 10 (the 50 Hz line disappears, 150 Hz remains); amplify by +12 dB (clipping appears and the spectrum fills with new components between harmonics); tremolo at 6 Hz (sidebands appear around each harmonic); flanger at 1 ms (notches at 500, 1500, 2500 Hz).
    

  
    Filter and equaliser effects
    
  
    A filter changes the gain of a signal as a function of frequency. The theory of FIR and IIR filters, cut-off frequency, roll-off and phase is in 1.8 filters. Audacity offers the following in Effect > EQ and Filters (plus Classic Filters under Legacy Effects on some systems).
    
  
    
    
    EffectParameters in plain languageDSPAudiology use
    
    
      
    High-Pass FilterFrequency (Hz): the −3 dB cut-off. Rolloff (dB per octave): 6, 12, 24, 36 or 48IIR Butterworth-type sections; order = roll-off / 6Removing rumble, handling noise and hum below speech (for example 80 to 100 Hz); telephone-band lower edge 300 Hz
    
      
    Low-Pass FilterFrequency (Hz), Rolloff (dB per octave)As above, low-passBand-limiting speech (3400 Hz telephone edge); simulating reduced audibility of high frequencies for counselling
    
      
    Notch FilterFrequency (Hz), Q (sharpness: bandwidth ≈ frequency / Q)Second-order IIR notch (zeros on the unit circle at f0)Removing mains hum at 50 Hz (India, Europe) or 60 Hz (Americas) and its harmonics
    
      
    Bass and TrebleBass (dB), Treble (dB), Volume (dB), Link Volume ControlLow and high shelving filtersQuick tonal correction; rarely used for stimuli
    
      
    Shelf FilterType (low or high shelf), Frequency, GainShelving biquadPre-emphasis style lift of high frequencies
    
      
    Filter Curve EQDraw a gain curve; Length of Filter (number of taps); linear or logarithmic frequency scale; presets; Invert, FlattenFIR filter designed from the drawn curve (linear phase, applied by fast convolution)Arbitrary spectral shaping, for example matching the long-term spectrum of noise to speech, or applying an inverse headphone response
    
      
    Graphic EQSliders at fixed third-octave centre frequencies (about 20 Hz to 20 kHz), each ±20 dB; Length of FilterAlso FIR, from the slider valuesSimulating a sloping hearing loss for demonstration (for example progressively cutting 2 to 8 kHz)
    
        
  
    
  
    |H(f)| of an Nth-order Butterworth low-pass:
   |H(f)| = 1 / sqrt(1 + (f/fc)^(2N))       roll-off = 6N dB/octave
notch bandwidth (-3 dB):  BW = f0 / Q
where fc is the cut-off frequency, N the filter order and f0 the notch centre frequency.
    
  
    Worked example. A 4th-order (24 dB/octave) low-pass at 3400 Hz attenuates 6800 Hz (one octave above) by 10 log10(1 + 2^8) = 24.1 dB. A notch at 50 Hz with Q = 10 has a bandwidth of about 5 Hz; with Q = 2 the bandwidth is 25 Hz and the voice fundamental of a low-pitched male talker (about 85 to 155 Hz) is less affected than the hum, but the 100 Hz second harmonic of the hum is barely touched.
    
  
    Key ideaFilter Curve EQ and Graphic EQ are FIR filters with a length you choose. A longer filter follows the drawn curve more exactly at low frequencies (frequency resolution is roughly fs / length) but adds a longer pre-ring and post-ring around transients. For sharp band edges on speech, a long FIR (several thousand taps) or a high-order IIR section is needed.
    
  
    Lab 6: create a speech sample band-limited to 300 to 3400 Hz
    
  
      
    
    1. Import a clean sentence recorded at 44.1 kHz. Duplicate it (Ctrl+D) so that the original is kept for comparison.
      2. 
    
    2. Select the copy. Choose Effect > EQ and Filters > High-Pass Filter, Frequency 300 Hz, Rolloff 48 dB per octave. Apply. Why: a steep slope makes the lower band edge well defined, as in telephone channels.
      3. 
    
    3. Choose Effect > EQ and Filters > Low-Pass Filter, Frequency 3400 Hz, Rolloff 48 dB per octave. Apply.
      4. 
    
    4. Alternative: Effect > EQ and Filters > Filter Curve EQ. Draw a curve at 0 dB between 300 and 3400 Hz and at −60 dB elsewhere (or save the curve as a preset for reuse). Set Length of Filter to its maximum for sharp edges. Record which method you used.
      5. 
    
    5. Verify with Analyze > Plot Spectrum (Hann, size 4096, log frequency axis) on the original and on the copy. The copy shows a flat pass band from 300 to 3400 Hz with steep falls outside. Export the spectrum as text (Export button) for your report.
      6. 
    
    6. Listen: the band-limited speech sounds "telephone-like", intelligible but with less clarity for fricatives such as /s/ and /f/. Because filtering removed energy, measure RMS again and re-normalise if the stimulus must match the level of others.
      7. 
  
    
  
    Lab 7: remove 50 Hz hum from a recording
    
  
      
    
    1. Import a recording with hum (or create one: generate a speech track, then add a new track with Generate > Tone at 50 Hz, amplitude 0.01, and mix with Tracks > Mix > Mix and Render).
      2. 
    
    2. Diagnose: select a pause and run Analyze > Plot Spectrum with size 16384 (bins of about 2.7 Hz at 44.1 kHz). Read the peak frequencies: 50 Hz and often 100, 150, 250 Hz. Odd harmonics (150, 250 Hz) dominate when hum comes from magnetic coupling or rectifiers; the second harmonic suggests other sources.
      3. 
    
    3. Select the whole track. Apply Effect > EQ and Filters > Notch Filter, Frequency 50, Q 10. Repeat for each strong harmonic (100, 150, 200, 250 Hz, same Q). Why: a notch removes only a narrow band and leaves the voice fundamental and the rest of the speech intact.
      4. 
    
    4. If only the fundamental is present and the recording is speech, a High-Pass Filter at 80 Hz, 24 dB per octave is a simpler alternative. It also removes rumble, but will shave the lowest part of a deep male voice.
      5. 
    
    5. Re-plot the spectrum of the pause. The hum lines should fall by 20 dB or more. Listen with headphones at a moderate level. Log the frequencies, Q values and the before and after levels of the hum lines.
      6. 
    
    6. Better still, find the cause: ground loops, unbalanced cables near power supplies, laptop chargers. Removal in software is a repair, never a substitute for a clean recording.
      7. 
  
    

  
    Noise reduction and restoration
    
  
    How Audacity's Noise Reduction works
    
  
    Noise Reduction (Effect > Noise Removal and Repair > Noise Reduction) is a two-pass spectral process. In the first pass you select a region containing only noise and click Get Noise Profile: Audacity computes a short-time Fourier transform (STFT) of that region and stores, for every frequency bin, statistics of the noise power. In the second pass you select the region to clean, set the parameters and click OK: the audio is analysed again frame by frame, each bin is compared with a threshold derived from the noise profile, bins judged to be noise are attenuated, and the audio is resynthesised by overlap-add.
    
  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.5d. Two-pass spectral noise reduction as used by Audacity's Noise Reduction effect (simplified). The same principle underlies Audition's Noise Reduction (process) in 5.6.
    
  
    X(m,k) = STFT of input,  N(k) = mean noise power in bin k
G(m,k) = 1              if |X(m,k)|^2 > N(k) 10^(S/10)
       = 10^(-R/20)     otherwise
Y(m,k) = G_smoothed(m,k) X(m,k)  ->  inverse STFT, overlap-add
where m is the frame index, k the frequency bin, S the sensitivity (how far above the mean noise a bin must be to count as signal; Audacity's own scale is not exactly dB) and R the noise reduction in dB. This is a simplified spectral gating model of the effect.
    
  
    Parameters in plain language:
    
  
      
    
    • Noise reduction (dB): how much the bins classified as noise are turned down. Values of 6 to 12 dB sound natural; above about 20 dB the background becomes unnaturally dead and artefacts grow.
      • 
    
    • Sensitivity: how readily a bin is classified as noise. Higher sensitivity removes more noise but also removes quiet speech components (soft consonants, breathy voice, reverberant tails).
      • 
    
    • Frequency smoothing (bands): the computed gains are averaged across neighbouring bins. Smoothing reduces "musical noise"; too much makes the processing less selective. For speech, values of about 1 to 6 are typical.
      • 
    
    • Noise: Reduce (normal output) or Residue (output what would be removed). Listening to the residue is the best check: if you can hear speech in the residue, the settings are removing speech.
      • 
  
    
  
    DefinitionMusical noise is the characteristic "watery" or "twittering" artefact of spectral noise reduction. Random noise fluctuates; in some frames, a few isolated bins exceed the threshold by chance and pass through at full gain while their neighbours are attenuated. Those isolated time-frequency spots are heard as short tonal blips at random frequencies. Lower reduction, lower sensitivity and more frequency smoothing reduce it.
    

  
    
    
    Try it: spectral noise reduction and musical noise
    
    
    The top spectrogram shows a speech-like signal in steady low-frequency-weighted noise; the first 0.45 s is noise only and is used as the noise profile. The bottom spectrogram shows the result of spectral gating with your settings. Watch the noise-only region: isolated bright spots that survive are musical noise.
    
    
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
    
    
    
    
    
    
      
    Noise floor change (noise-only part)
    
      
    Speech energy change
    
      
    Noise cells passed (musical noise)
    
    
    
    
    Colour: dark = low level, yellow = high level (about -100 to -20 dB)
    
  
    
  
    Things to try: with sensitivity 0 dB (threshold at the mean noise power) many noise cells pass through and musical noise is strong; raising sensitivity to 6 to 9 dB removes most of them but begins to reduce weak speech harmonics (the speech energy readout falls). Increasing frequency smoothing reduces the isolated cells without raising sensitivity. Pushing reduction to 48 dB lowers the floor dramatically but makes any surviving cells stand out more against a dead background.
    

  
    Other repair tools

    Spectral selection
    
  
      
    
    1. Open the track name menu and choose Spectrogram (or Multi-view to see waveform and spectrogram together). Adjust Spectrogram Settings in the same menu: window size 2048, Hann window, scale Mel or Logarithmic for speech.
      2. 
    
    2. Enable spectral selection in the spectrogram settings. Drag a rectangle around the unwanted sound (for example a narrow whistle).
      3. 
    
    3. Apply Effect > Spectral Tools > Spectral Delete. The box is removed. Listen and view the result; undo if speech was damaged.
      4. 
  
    
  
    Lab 8: noise reduction on a recorded sentence, with a residue check
    
  
      
    
    1. Import a sentence recorded in a room with steady fan noise, including 2 s of noise only. Duplicate the track (Ctrl+D) and mute the copy (reference).
      2. 
    
    2. Select about 1 s of the noise-only part (at least 2048 samples are required; longer is better). Open Effect > Noise Removal and Repair > Noise Reduction and click Get Noise Profile. The dialog closes.
      3. 
    
    3. Select the whole track (click in the track control panel or Ctrl+A with only that track selected). Open Noise Reduction again. Set Noise reduction 12 dB, Sensitivity 6, Frequency smoothing 3, Noise: Residue. Click Preview: you should hear only noise. Any speech in the residue means the settings are too aggressive.
      4. 
    
    4. Switch to Reduce and click OK. Compare the processed track with the reference using Analyze > Contrast (foreground: a vowel; background: the noise-only region) before and after. Record both contrast values.
      5. 
    
    5. Repeat on another duplicate with 30 dB reduction and sensitivity 12. Listen for musical noise and dull high frequencies; compare Plot Spectrum of an /s/ in the two versions.
      6. 
    
    6. In the log, record the profile region times, the three parameters and both contrast values.
      7. 
  
    
  
    Common errorNever noise-reduce material used for audiometric calibration (tones, calibration noise, speech-shaped noise used to set levels), speech-in-noise test lists, or recordings that will be analysed for voice quality (jitter, shimmer, harmonics-to-noise ratio, cepstral peak prominence; see 5.7 Praat). Noise reduction changes the level and spectrum of the signal and invents time-frequency structure, so any measurement afterwards describes the algorithm as much as the voice or the stimulus.
    

  
    Analysis tools
    
  
    
    
    Tool (menu path)What it reportsHow to use it wellAudiology use

    frequency resolution  df = fs / N_fft
where N_fft is the Plot Spectrum "Size". At 44 100 Hz, size 4096 gives df = 10.77 Hz; size 16 384 gives 2.69 Hz, enough to separate 50 Hz hum from a 55 Hz component.
    

  
    Generating signals for calibration and stimuli
    
  
    The Generate menu writes new audio at the cursor, replaces a selection, or (with no track) creates a new track. Generators are exact and repeatable, which makes them ideal for calibration signals and simple stimuli.
    
  
    
    
    Generator (Generate > ...)ParametersSignalAudiology useCaution
    
    
      
    ToneWaveform (Sine, Square, Sawtooth, Square no alias, Triangle), Frequency (Hz), Amplitude (0 to 1, peak), Durationx[n] = a sin(2π f n / fs)Calibration tones (1 kHz), pure-tone stimuli at audiometric frequencies, test signals for frequency-response checksAmplitude is peak, not RMS; add ramps with Adjustable Fade; square and sawtooth waves alias at high frequencies unless the "no alias" variant is used
    
      
    ChirpWaveform, Frequency start and end, Amplitude start and end, Interpolation (Linear or Logarithmic), DurationFrequency sweep; logarithmic sweeps spend equal time per octaveMeasuring the frequency response of headphones, loudspeakers or hearing-aid test boxes (record the output and compare spectra)A linear chirp spends most of its time at high frequencies
    
      
    NoiseNoise type (White, Pink, Brownian), Amplitude (0 to 1), DurationWhite: flat power per Hz; pink: power falls 3 dB per octave (equal power per octave); Brownian: falls 6 dB per octaveMasking noise demonstrations; base for speech-shaped noise (shape white noise with Filter Curve EQ to the long-term average speech spectrum); pink noise for loudspeaker level checksAmplitude is not RMS: always measure RMS after generating and set the level by Loudness Normalization in RMS mode
    
      
    DTMF TonesDTMF sequence (0 to 9, *, #, A to D), Amplitude, Duration, Tone/silence ratioSum of two sines per key (one from a low group 697 to 941 Hz, one from a high group 1209 to 1633 Hz)Teaching two-tone signals; telephone-related test material; synchronisation markers in recordingsNot an audiometric stimulus
    
      
    SilenceDurationZero-valued samplesPadding stimuli to exact lengths; inter-stimulus intervals; lead-in before speechDigital silence is quieter than room noise; within a noisy recording use a noise segment instead
    
      
    Pluck, Rhythm Track, Risset DrumVariousMusical tones and click tracksRhythm Track can generate regular click trains for timing demonstrationsLevels and spectra not specified for audiological use
    
        
  
    
  
    Exam tipFor a pink noise and a sine at the same RMS on the same meter, the noise will have much higher peaks (a crest factor of around 10 to 13 dB compared with 3 dB for the sine). Setting noise by peak rather than RMS is a classic way to end up 7 to 10 dB too quiet.
    
  
    Lab 9a: a pure-tone stimulus set
    
  
      
    
    1. File > New. Generate > Tone, 250 Hz, amplitude 0.1414, duration 1.000 s. Ramps of 10 ms at each end as in Lab 4.
      2. 
    
    2. Place the cursor after the tone (End key), then Generate > Silence, 0.5 s. Continue with 500, 1000, 2000, 4000 and 8000 Hz tones separated by silences. Each tone is at −20 dBFS RMS.
      3. 
    
    3. Label each tone (select it, Ctrl+B, type the frequency). Export with File > Export Audio, Export Range: Multiple Files, split files based on labels, file names from label text (Lab 10). You get one file per frequency.
      4. 
  
    

  
    Basic multitrack: tracks, mute and solo, gain and pan, alignment and mixing
    
  
    Audacity mixes all audible tracks at playback and export. Each track's control panel has Mute and Solo buttons, a gain slider (−36 to +36 dB) and a pan slider. These sliders and envelopes are non-destructive: they are applied while mixing and do not change the stored samples.
    
  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.5e. Audacity's mixing model. Muted tracks (or non-soloed tracks when any track is soloed) are left out of the mix.
    
  
    
    
    TaskMenu path or controlNotes
    
    
      
    Add a trackTracks > Add New > Mono Track / Stereo Track / Label TrackGenerated or pasted audio can then be placed in it
    
      
    Mute, soloButtons in the track control panel; Tracks > Mute/Unmute for allSolo mode (simple or multi-track) is set in Preferences > Tracks Behaviors
    
      
    Track gain, panSliders in the track control panel (double-click a slider for a numeric entry)Applied at mix time; logged as part of the mix settings
    
      
    Move audio in timeDrag the clip handle (title bar); or select and use Tracks > Align TracksAlign Together, Align Start to Zero, Start to Cursor or Selection Start, End to Cursor
    
      
    Keep tracks in stepTracks > Keep tracks synchronized (Sync-Lock)Edits that change length in one track also change the others in the group
    
      
    EnvelopeEnvelope tool (F2)Fades, ducking, masker onsets
    
      
    Combine into one trackTracks > Mix > Mix and Render (replaces the selected tracks) or Mix and Render to New Track (Ctrl+Shift+M)Applies gain, pan and envelopes; the result can clip if the sum exceeds full scale (it is stored in float, so reduce gain before exporting)
    
      
    Make a stereo pairTrack name menu of the upper track > Make Stereo TrackThe upper mono track becomes the left channel, the track below the right
    
      
    Mixer viewView > Mixer Board (3.x; absent in 4.0 according to its release notes)Faders and meters for each track
    
        
  
    
  
    RememberAudacity 3.x has no per-clip gain control and no buses or sends. Adjust a clip's level by selecting it and using Amplify, or use the track gain slider or envelope. Routing to buses and effect sends is one of the reasons to use Audition (5.6) for larger mixing work.
    
  
    Lab 10: build a sentence-in-babble track at 0 dB SNR
    
  
    Aim: a mono file in which a sentence is embedded in multi-talker babble at an SNR of exactly 0 dB, with the babble starting 500 ms before the sentence and continuing 500 ms after it, and ramps on the babble.
    
  
      
    
    1. Import the sentence (mono, 44.1 kHz) and trim it so that the file starts at the first speech sample and ends at the last (Lab 3). Record its duration, for example 2.300 s.
      2. 
    
    2. Decide the SNR convention and write it in the log. Here: SNR = RMS of the whole trimmed sentence file minus RMS of the babble over the same duration. (Standardised tests may use other conventions, such as the active speech level of ITU-T P.56, which excludes pauses; follow the test's manual when reproducing a published test.)
      3. 
    
    3. Select the sentence and apply Effect > Volume and Compression > Loudness Normalization, option RMS, target −26 dB. Verify with Analyze > Measure RMS: −26.0 dB. Why −26: after adding a second signal at the same RMS, the mixture is about 3 dB higher (−23 dBFS), leaving room for peaks.
      4. 
    
    4. Import the babble file. It opens as a second track. Select the part of the babble that you will use, 3.300 s long (sentence plus 0.5 s on each side), using the Selection toolbar, and choose Edit > Remove Special > Trim Audio. Drag the babble clip so that it starts at 0.000 s (Tracks > Align Tracks > Align Start to Zero with only the babble selected).
      5. 
    
    5. Move the sentence clip so that it starts at exactly 0.500 s: select the sentence track, click at time 0.5 using the Selection toolbar, then Tracks > Align Tracks > Start to Cursor/Selection Start.
      6. 
    
    6. Set the babble level: select only the part of the babble that overlaps the sentence (0.500 to 2.800 s) and run Measure RMS. Suppose it reads −29.4 dB. The required change is −26 − (−29.4) = +3.4 dB. Select the whole babble clip and apply Amplify with Amplification +3.4 dB. Re-measure the overlap region: −26.0 dB. Why the overlap region: SNR is defined where speech and noise co-exist; the lead-in and tail do not count.
      7. 
    
    7. Apply 10 ms S-curve ramps at the start and end of the babble (Adjustable Fade).
      8. 
    
    8. Check the mix before rendering: select both tracks, play, and watch the playback meter. Then Tracks > Mix > Mix and Render to New Track. Open Amplify on the new track to read its peak (for example −9 dBFS: safe) and Measure RMS on the overlap region (about −23 dB).
      9. 
    
    9. Mute or delete the original two tracks and export the mixed track as 16-bit, 44.1 kHz mono WAV named, for example, sent01_babble_SNR0.wav. Keep the .aup3 project so that the exact alignment can be inspected later.
      10. 
    
    10. To make the −5 dB and +5 dB versions, change only the babble gain (+5 dB and −5 dB respectively) from the 0 dB version, re-render and re-check peaks. At −5 dB SNR the mixture RMS is about −19.8 dBFS and peaks rise accordingly.
      11. 
  
    
  
    Key ideaSetting SNR by changing only the noise keeps the speech at a constant level across conditions; setting it by changing only the speech keeps the noise constant. Clinical adaptive tests differ in which one they vary (for example a fixed noise with adaptive speech level). State which one your stimulus set uses.
    
  
    Lab 11: a stereo file with speech in the left channel and noise in the right
    
  
      
    
    1. Prepare two mono tracks of equal length: a sentence at −26 dBFS RMS (track 1) and speech-shaped noise at −26 dBFS RMS (track 2).
      2. 
    
    2. Method A (pan): set track 1's pan slider fully left (L100) and track 2's fully right (R100). Export with Channels: Stereo. The left channel then contains only speech and the right only noise.
      3. 
    
    3. Method B (explicit channels): make sure track 1 is directly above track 2, open track 1's name menu and choose Make Stereo Track. The pair becomes one stereo track with speech on top (left) and noise below (right). Export with Channels: Stereo. This method does not depend on the pan law.
      4. 
    
    4. Method C (custom mapping): in the Export Audio dialog choose Channels: Custom mapping, set 2 channels and assign track 1 to channel 1 and track 2 to channel 2.
      5. 
    
    5. Verify: re-import the exported file, split it (track name menu > Split Stereo to Mono) and measure the RMS of each channel. Play each channel alone with headphones to confirm that left is speech. Why: many clinical playback programs route channel 1 to the left transducer and channel 2 to the right; a swapped file silently presents the stimuli to the wrong ear.
      6. 
  
    

  
    Saving projects and exporting audio
    
  
    Saving
    
  
      
    
    • File > Save Project > Save Project (Ctrl+S) or Save Project As: writes the .aup3 file. File > Save Project > Backup Project writes a compacted copy.
      • 
    
    • The .aup3 file keeps everything needed to continue editing, but only Audacity (of a compatible version) can open it. Archive the exported WAV files and the processing log together with it.
      • 
  
    
  
    The Export Audio dialog (File > Export Audio, Ctrl+Shift+E)
    
  
    
    
    SettingOptionsRecommended for audiology work
    
    
      
    Export toComputer, or the audio.com cloud serviceComputer, into a folder separate from the raw recordings
    
      
    File name and folderFree text; extension added from the formatDescriptive names with content, level, SNR, rate and bit depth
    
      
    FormatWAV, AIFF, FLAC, MP3, Ogg Vorbis, Opus, WavPack and others (more with FFmpeg)WAV (or FLAC for lossless archiving); never MP3 or Ogg for stimuli or analysis
    
      
    ChannelsMono, Stereo, Custom mappingMono for single-channel stimuli; stereo or custom mapping when channels carry different signals
    
      
    Sample rateFrom 8000 Hz upwards; default is the project rate44 100 or 48 000 Hz, matching the playback software's requirement
    
      
    Encoding (WAV)Signed 16-bit PCM, Signed 24-bit PCM, 32-bit float and others16-bit for distribution to most clinical software; 24-bit or 32-bit float for research archives
    
      
    Export rangeEntire Project, Current Selection, Multiple Files (by labels or by tracks)Multiple Files for word lists cut by labels
    
      
    Trim blank spaceOn or offOff when leading silence is deliberate
    
      
    Edit metadataArtist, title, comments and custom tags (also Edit > Metadata)Put the log reference, level and SNR in Comments
    
        
  
    
  
    Dither is not in the Export dialog. It is set in Edit > Preferences > Quality, under High-quality Conversion: Sample Rate Converter and Dither (None, Rectangle, Triangle, Shaped). Dither adds a tiny random signal before the float-to-integer rounding, which turns quantisation distortion (correlated with the signal) into a steady, benign noise floor. For 16-bit export of quiet stimuli (for example tones near threshold that will be attenuated digitally), use Triangle dither; for 24-bit export dither makes no practical difference. Note that Shaped dither moves the noise to high frequencies, which you may not want in stimuli used for high-frequency audiometry: prefer Triangle.
    
  
    TPDF dither: d[n] = u1[n] + u2[n],  u uniform on (-q/2, q/2)
q = 2 / 2^B   (one LSB for full scale -1 to +1)
where B is the export bit depth. For B = 16, q = 3.05 × 10^-5, about -90.3 dBFS.
    
  
    Lab 12: export a 16-bit 44.1 kHz mono WAV and check it
    
  
      
    
    1. Select nothing (so the whole project is exported) or make a selection. Make sure only the intended tracks are audible (unmute or mute as needed).
      2. 
    
    2. Edit > Preferences > Quality: High-quality Conversion Dither = Triangle. OK.
      3. 
    
    3. File > Export Audio. Format: WAV (Microsoft). Channels: Mono. Sample Rate: 44100 Hz. Encoding: Signed 16-bit PCM. Export Range: Entire Project. Click Edit Metadata and write the log reference in Comments. Click Export.
      4. 
    
    4. If Audacity warns that the tracks will be mixed down to a single mono channel, that is expected when there are several tracks or a stereo track.
      5. 
    
    5. Verify: File > Import > Audio the exported file into a new project. Check in the track menu that the rate is 44100 Hz and the format is 16-bit PCM, measure RMS and peak, and compare with the values before export (they should agree within about 0.01 dB unless the signal clipped).
      6. 
  
    
  
    Exporting multiple files and batch processing with Macros
    
  
      
    
    1. Multiple files by labels: label each word (Analyze > Label Sounds can do this automatically), then File > Export Audio, Export Range: Multiple Files, Split files based on: Labels, Name files: using label/track name. Each labelled region becomes one file.
      2. 
    
    2. Macros (Audacity 3.x): Tools > Macro Manager > New, name it (for example "Stimulus_16bit"). Insert commands in order, for example Normalize (peak −1 dB, remove DC), Loudness Normalization (RMS −26 dB), Export as WAV. Edit each command's parameters in the Macro Manager.
      3. 
    
    3. Apply the macro to a folder of files: in the Macro Manager click Apply Macro to: Files, choose the files. The results are written to a macro-output folder (in the default export location or the folder set in Preferences > Directories). Tools > Apply Macro applies it to the open project instead.
      4. 
    
    4. Export the macro's command list into your log, or copy its text file, so that the processing can be repeated exactly. In Audacity 4.0 Macros are, according to the release notes, not available; batch work then moves to scripts (Audiology Python Primer) or to Audition's Batch Process (5.6).
      5. 
  
    

  
    Audio file formats
    
  
    
    
    FormatCompressionTypical settingsUse in audiologyNotes
    
    
      
    WAV (RIFF)None (PCM) or float16 or 24 bit PCM, 32-bit float; 44.1 or 48 kHzStimuli, calibration files, analysis dataUniversally supported; standard WAV headers limit files to about 4 GB (RF64 and W64 extend it)
    
      
    AIFFNone16 or 24 bitAs WAV, common on older macOS softwareBig-endian PCM
    
      
    FLACLosslesscompression level 0 to 8Archiving recordings and stimulus sets at about half the sizeDecodes to identical samples; not all clinical software reads it
    
      
    WavPackLossless (or hybrid)variousArchiving float or high-resolution recordingsLess widely supported
    
      
    MP3Lossy (perceptual)128 to 320 kbit/sCounselling and patient-education material onlyRemoves components judged inaudible to normal hearing; adds encoder delay and may low-pass above about 16 to 19 kHz; unsuitable for stimuli
    
      
    Ogg Vorbis, Opus, AAC (M4A)Lossyvariable bit rateWeb and streaming material; tele-audiology video calls use such codecsSame cautions as MP3; Opus is designed for speech and low latency
    
      
    AUP3 / AUP4Project databasenot an audio fileKeeping an editable record of the editReadable only by Audacity
    
      
    Label text fileTextstart, end, label (tab-separated)Documenting stimulus positions; importing into analysis scriptsFile > Export Other > Export Labels; File > Import > Labels
    
        
  
    

  
    Processing log template
    
  
    Every processed file must be traceable back to its source. Keep one row per operation. The template below can be copied into a spreadsheet; the example rows describe Lab 10.
    
  
    
    
    StepDate, operatorInput file (rate, bits, ch)Software, versionMenu pathParametersRegionMeasured beforeMeasured afterOutput fileReason
    
    
      
    1(date), ABsent01.wav (44.1 k, 24, 1)Audacity 3.7.xEdit > Remove Special > Trim Audiokeep 0.412 to 2.712 swhole file3.10 s2.300 sproject onlyremove lead-in and tail
    
      
    2(date), ABsent01 (trimmed)Audacity 3.7.xEffect > Volume and Compression > Loudness NormalizationRMS, −26 dBwhole clip−21.7 dB RMS−26.0 dB RMSproject onlycommon speech level
