    4(date), ABtracks 1 + 2Audacity 3.7.xTracks > Mix > Mix and Render to New Trackgain 0 dB, pan centre0 to 3.300 speak n/apeak −9.1 dBFSsent01_babble_SNR0.wav (44.1 k, 16, 1, TPDF)final stimulus
    
      
    ...
    
        
  
    
  
    Also record, once per session: the RMS convention (raw or sine-referenced), the SNR convention, the playback software the files are intended for, and a checksum or file size of each output file.
    

  
    Clinical applications
    
  
      
    
    • Stimulus preparation: word lists cut into single-word files, level-equalised by RMS or loudness, with consistent lead-in and ramps, exported in the format the test software requires.
      • 
    
    • Calibration material: 1 kHz tones at a documented digital level, speech-shaped noise matched to the long-term spectrum of the test material, used to set the audiometer's VU meter or the sound-field level.
      • 
    
    • Speech-in-noise materials: local-language sentences mixed with babble or speech-shaped noise at defined SNRs, for research or for teaching when validated materials do not exist (with the caution that home-made materials are not normed tests).
      • 
    
    • Counselling and education: filtered speech (Graphic EQ or Filter Curve EQ) that approximates what a patient with a sloping loss might hear; demonstrations of how noise and reverberation affect speech; accessible audio checked with Contrast.
      • 
    
    • Voice and speech recordings: trimming, labelling and checking recordings for clipping before acoustic analysis in Praat (5.7), with no noise reduction or compression.
      • 
    
    • Equipment checks: logarithmic chirps and pink noise to measure the frequency response of headphones, loudspeakers and hearing-aid test boxes by recording their output with a measurement microphone.
      • 
    
    • Tele-audiology preparation: checking how an MP3 or Opus version of a stimulus differs from the original (Plot Spectrum on both) before deciding whether remote delivery is acceptable (Unit 6).
      • 
  
    

  
    Limitations, errors and troubleshooting
    
  
    
    
    ProblemLikely causeWhat to check or do
    
    
      
    Effect menu items greyed outNothing selected, or playback or recording still runningPress Stop; select audio (Ctrl+A)
    
      
    Recording is silent or very quietWrong recording device or channel; operating-system input muted; interface gain at minimumAudio Setup toolbar device; OS privacy settings for microphone access; meter monitoring
    
      
    Recording has clicks and dropoutsBuffer too small, CPU overloaded, USB power savingIncrease latency buffer in Preferences > Audio Settings; close other programs; record to a local disk
    
      
    Exported file clips although the waveform looked fineFloat samples above 1.0 inside the project; track gain or envelope raised the mixCheck the peak of the rendered mix with Amplify; normalise to −1 dBFS before export
    
      
    Exported file is 3 dB too loud or too quiet relative to the protocolRMS convention confused (raw versus sine-referenced)State the convention; compute tone amplitudes accordingly
    
      
    Two tracks drift apart during playbackTracks at different sample rates, or a clip moved accidentallyTrack menu rate; Tracks > Resample; clip positions in the Selection toolbar
    
      
    Stimuli in the wrong earChannel order swapped at exportLab 11 verification step; test with a left-only tone before a session
    
      
    Metallic, "watery" background after noise reductionMusical noise: reduction or sensitivity too highLower reduction to 6 to 12 dB, increase frequency smoothing, check the residue
    
      
    Speech sounds dull after noise reductionSoft high-frequency components classed as noiseLower sensitivity; use a better noise profile; record in a quieter room
    
      
    Clicks at every editCuts at non-zero crossingsSelect > At Zero Crossings (Z); short crossfades or fades at joins
    
      
    Change Tempo artefacts (echo-like, phasey speech)Large tempo changeKeep changes modest; tick "Use high quality stretching"; document the percentage
    
      
    Plot Spectrum shows broad smeared peaksSize too small, or a selection with changing contentIncrease Size; select a steady segment
    
      
    A project from a colleague will not openNewer project format (for example .aup4) or missing audio files for old .aup projectsAsk for exported WAV files plus the log instead of project files
    
        
  
    

  
    Demonstration
    
  
    Equipment: a computer with Audacity 3.x, closed-back headphones, an audio interface and a dynamic or condenser microphone, a projector, a clean sentence recording, a babble file, and optionally a sound-level meter with a coupler for the calibration part.
    
  
      
    
    1. Show the window regions (Figure 5.5a) and the Audio Setup toolbar. Record a sentence with 2 s of room noise, deliberately once too hot (clipping) and once at −12 dBFS peak. Run Find Clipping on both. Students observe that clipping is permanent.
      2. 
    
    2. Generate the 1 kHz calibration tone (Lab 4) live, measure RMS and plot its spectrum with and without ramps (add a version with no ramps and plot the spectrum of the onset region). Students observe the spectral splatter without ramps.
      3. 
    
    3. Apply High-Pass and Low-Pass filters to make the 300 to 3400 Hz version, play both, and show Plot Spectrum of each.
      4. 
    
    4. Add hum, diagnose it with a 16 384-point spectrum and remove it with notches; compare with a high-pass approach.
      5. 
    
    5. Run Noise Reduction with the Residue option at mild and aggressive settings. Students listen for speech in the residue and musical noise in the output. Use the spectral noise-reduction demonstration above to explain why.
      6. 
    
    6. Build the 0 dB SNR mixture (Lab 10) with the class calculating the required babble gain from the measured RMS values.
      7. 
    
    7. Export with the log open on screen, re-import and verify numbers. Emphasise that the log is part of the deliverable.
      8. 
  
    

  
    Current developments
    
  
      
    
    • Rebuilt user interfaces and new project formats (Audacity 4.0 in 2026) change menus and clip editing; laboratories need version-specific handouts and should archive stimuli as WAV plus a text log rather than as project files.
      • 
    
    • Real-time and master effect racks make processing non-destructive in Audacity, bringing it closer to the multitrack workflow of commercial editors.
      • 
    
    • Machine-learning tools for noise suppression, speech separation and transcription are increasingly offered as plug-ins (for example through the OpenVINO-based plug-ins published for Audacity). They are powerful for counselling material but, like spectral noise reduction, are not appropriate for test stimuli or acoustic analysis.
      • 
    
    • Loudness measurement to ITU-R BS.1770 and EBU R 128 is now built into most editors, making loudness matching of speech lists simpler; researchers still need to state whether they matched RMS, active speech level or loudness.
      • 
    
    • Scripted pipelines (Python, MATLAB) are replacing manual editing for large stimulus sets, with editors like Audacity used for inspection, listening checks and teaching.
      • 
  
    

  
    Practical activity and assessment
    
  
    Practical P12 (Audacity part). Deliverables: the original recording, every processed file, the .aup3 project and a completed processing log.
    
  
      
    
    1. Record a 10-word list in your own voice at 44.1 kHz, 24 bit, mono, with 2 s of room noise at the start. Check for clipping.
      2. 
    
    2. Cut the list into 10 word files with labels and Export Multiple. Level each word to −26 dBFS RMS and apply 10 ms ramps.
      3. 
    
    3. Prepare a 30 s 1 kHz calibration tone at −20 dBFS RMS with 10 ms S-curve ramps.
      4. 
    
    4. Produce one sentence in babble at 0 dB and −5 dB SNR (Lab 10), a 300 to 3400 Hz band-limited version of the same sentence (Lab 6) and a stereo file with speech left and noise right (Lab 11).
      5. 
    
    5. Remove hum from a supplied recording (Lab 7) and report the hum line levels before and after.
      6. 
    
    6. Submit the log, a table of measured RMS and peak values for each output file, and a half-page reflection on which steps would change a clinical measurement if done carelessly.
      7. 
  
    
  
    Assessment: correctness of levels and SNR (checked by the instructor by re-measuring), correct formats and channel order, completeness of the log, and quality of reasoning in the reflection.
    

  
    Exercises
    
  
    
    
    
      
    
      
    A colleague sends you stimuli.aup3 and asks you to load it into the clinic's speech-audiometry software. What is wrong and what should you ask for?
    Model answer: an .aup3 file is an Audacity project (a database of tracks, edits and history), not an audio file; the clinical software cannot read it. Ask for exported WAV files at the rate, bit depth and channel format the software requires, together with the processing log.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    What Amplitude should you enter in Generate > Tone for a 500 Hz sine at −26 dBFS raw RMS? What peak level will it have?
    Model answer: RMS = 10^(−26/20) = 0.0501; amplitude = 0.0501 × √2 = 0.0709. Peak level = 20 log10(0.0709) = −22.99 dBFS.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    A 2-track project has speech in track 1 and time-aligned noise in track 2. You must remove a cough from the speech track without shifting the speech relative to the noise. Which command do you use, and what happens with each alternative?
    Model answer: Silence Audio (or Split Delete) on track 1 only keeps the timing. Delete would pull the later speech earlier and misalign it with the noise (unless sync-lock also shortened the noise track, changing the noise instead). Silence inserts digital zeros; because the noise track continues underneath, the gap is masked in the mixture.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    You need a sentence 25% longer for a slow-speech auditory training task, with the talker's pitch unchanged. Which effect and what setting? What would Change Speed and Pitch with the same length change do to a 120 Hz fundamental?
    Model answer: Change Tempo with a percent change of −20% (new length = old length / 0.8 = 1.25 times; check that the dialog's length field shows the target). Change Speed and Pitch to 1.25 times the length plays at speed 0.8, lowering every frequency by the same factor: 120 Hz becomes 96 Hz, and formants fall by 20% too.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Speech measures −24.0 dB RMS and noise −31.5 dB RMS over the same interval. (a) What is the SNR? (b) What gain applied to the noise gives −3 dB SNR? (c) What is the RMS of the mixture at −3 dB SNR?
    Model answer: (a) −24.0 − (−31.5) = +7.5 dB. (b) Noise must be at −21.0 dB, so gain = −21.0 − (−31.5) = +10.5 dB. (c) 10 log10(10^(−2.4) + 10^(−2.1)) = 10 log10(0.003981 + 0.007943) = 10 log10(0.011924) = −19.24 dBFS.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Plot Spectrum of a pause shows lines at 50, 150 and 250 Hz. The recording is of a female voice (F0 about 200 to 240 Hz) for later jitter analysis. Compare a high-pass filter and notch filters, and say whether any hum removal is acceptable.
    Model answer: a high-pass high enough to remove 250 Hz would remove the voice fundamental; notch filters at 50, 150 and 250 Hz with a Q of about 10 remove the hum lines with bandwidths of 5, 15 and 25 Hz, and the 250 Hz notch lies close to F0 and its harmonics may cross it as pitch varies. For jitter analysis the safest course is to re-record with the hum source fixed; if that is impossible, apply only narrow notches, analyse both processed and unprocessed versions and report the processing.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Design a Macro that takes 200 raw word recordings (48 kHz, 24 bit, stereo from a two-channel recorder where only the left channel has speech) and produces 44.1 kHz, 16-bit mono files at −26 dBFS RMS with a 5 ms fade in and out. List the commands in order and explain one limitation.
    Model answer: (1) Select All; (2) keep only the left channel (for example with a short Nyquist Prompt command, or Split Stereo to Mono followed by removing the right track if those commands appear in your version's macro command list; do not use Mix Stereo Down to Mono, which would add the empty right channel's noise), (3) Normalize with Remove DC offset ticked (peak −1 dB), (4) Loudness Normalization, RMS, −26 dB, (5) Select the first 5 ms and apply Fade In; select the last 5 ms and apply Fade Out (macro selection commands such as Select Time can do this), (6) Resample or set export rate to 44 100 Hz, (7) Export as WAV with 16-bit encoding and Triangle dither set beforehand in Preferences. Limitation: the macro applies the same operations blindly; it cannot check each word for clipping, noise or trimming errors, so a listening check or an automated measurement script is still needed, and the order matters because fades after level normalisation slightly lower the RMS of very short words.

    5.6
    Adobe Audition: a complete hands-on lab manual
    
  
    Supplementary4 hoursPractical P12
    
  
    Adobe Audition is a commercial audio workstation with two linked editors: a destructive Waveform Editor for single files and a non-destructive Multitrack Editor for sessions with many tracks, buses and effects. This lab manual repeats the stimulus-preparation tasks of 5.5 Audacity in Audition and adds the tools that Audition does better: spectral repair, a full mixer with sends and buses, loudness matching of many files and batch processing.
    

  
    
    
    What it is
    A digital audio workstation (DAW) and waveform editor, part of Adobe Creative Cloud, for Windows and macOS. It opens audio files for sample-level editing, builds multitrack sessions that refer to those files, and offers a large set of effects, restoration tools, analysis panels and batch tools.
    
    
    Why it is required
    Many hospitals, universities and media units already licence Creative Cloud. Audition's non-destructive multitrack sessions, spectral editing and batch processing make large stimulus sets, counselling media and research recordings faster to produce and easier to revise than in a purely destructive editor.
    
    
    How it works
    Audio is processed internally in 32-bit floating point. In the Waveform Editor, effects rewrite the samples of the open file (permanent when saved). In the Multitrack Editor, a session file (.sesx) stores references to audio files, clip positions, envelopes, track and bus routing and effect settings; the mix is computed on playback and written to a new file only on mixdown or export.
    
  
    

  
    RememberConcepts shared with Audacity (dBFS conventions, the calibration-tone calculation, SNR arithmetic, compressor equations, musical noise, dither, file formats) are explained in 5.5 and in 5.2. This topic refers back to them and concentrates on Audition's own tools and workflows. For the place of Audition among research software see 5.1.
    

  
    Version notes
    
  
    This manual describes Adobe Audition as distributed through Creative Cloud in 2026 (the 26.x releases; the January 2026 release added native support for Windows on ARM). Audition has kept its main menu structure (File, Edit, Multitrack, Clip, Effects, Favorites, View, Window, Help) for many years, but panels, effect names and default shortcuts do change: for example, some releases list both Dynamics Processing and a newer Dynamics effect under Amplitude and Compression, and the Essential Sound panel has gained options over time. Where your version differs, use the Help > Audition Help link and the online user guide. Shortcuts below are for Windows; on macOS replace Ctrl by Cmd and Alt by Opt, and Preferences are under Adobe Audition > Settings (or Preferences in older releases).
    

  
    Background and principle of operation
    
  
    Audio file versus session: the two editors
    
  
    The single most important idea in Audition is the difference between its two editors, selected with the Waveform and Multitrack buttons at the top left of the window (or View > Waveform Editor and View > Multitrack Editor).
    
  
    
    
    Waveform Editor (destructive)
      
      
    • Opens one audio file (WAV, AIFF, FLAC, MP3 and others).
      • 
      
    • Edits and effects change the samples of that file in memory; File > Save writes them back into the file.
      • 
      
    • Undo is available through the History panel until the file is closed.
      • 
      
    • Some processes exist only here: Noise Reduction (process), Hiss Reduction, the Diagnostics effects, spectral healing, Match Loudness on files.
      • 
      
    • Best for preparing individual stimulus files, repairing recordings and measuring.
      • 
    
    
    
    Multitrack Editor (non-destructive)
      
      
    • Opens a session (.sesx, an XML text file) plus a folder of referenced audio files.
      • 
      
    • Clips point to regions of source files; moving, trimming, fading or applying effects never changes the source.
      • 
      
    • Tracks have volume, pan, mute, solo, record-arm, effects racks, sends, and can feed buses.
      • 
      
    • The result exists only as a mix until you choose File > Export > Multitrack Mixdown.
      • 
      
    • Best for mixing speech with noise, building sequences, and anything you may need to revise later.
      • 
    
    
  
    
  
    Double-clicking a clip in the Multitrack Editor opens its source file in the Waveform Editor. Any destructive edit there changes the source file and therefore every session that uses it. To avoid this, make a copy first (Clip > Convert to Unique Copy in the Multitrack Editor, or save the source under a new name).
    
  
    Common errorOpening a raw recording in the Waveform Editor, applying an effect and pressing Ctrl+S overwrites the raw recording. Always use File > Save As (Ctrl+Shift+S) to a new name, or better, work on copies and keep raw files in a read-only folder.
    
  
    Internal format and dBFS
    
  
    Audition processes audio in 32-bit float, so intermediate values above 0 dBFS are not clipped until the file is saved in an integer format or played. The Amplitude Statistics panel can report RMS in either convention; its setting "0 dB = FS Sine Wave" selects the sine-referenced convention (a full-scale sine reads 0 dB RMS). With the option off, it matches the raw RMS used by Audacity (a full-scale sine reads −3.01 dB). Check this setting before any level work and state it in the log.
    

  
    Architecture and signal flow
    
  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.6a. Simplified schematic of the Audition workspace (Default workspace, not to scale). Panels can be docked anywhere; Window > Workspace > Reset to Saved Layout restores the default.
    

  
    Figure 5.6b shows how audio flows through a multitrack session. Every track passes through its clip settings, the track effects rack, volume and pan, then either directly to the Master or via a bus. Sends take a copy of a track's signal to a bus, for example to a shared reverb or to a "noise" bus that sets the level of all noise tracks together.
    
  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.6b. Signal routing in an Audition multitrack session. Only the path to the Master is audible and exported.
    

  
    Components of the Audition workspace
    
  
    
    
    Panel or element (Window menu)What it doesWhat can go wrong
    
    
      
    FilesLists open audio files and sessions with their sample rate, channels and bit depthTwo files with the same name from different folders; unsaved changes shown with an asterisk
    
      
    Media BrowserBrowse the disk, preview files, drag into the EditorPreviewing at a different level from the editor
    
      
    Effects RackUp to 16 effect slots with power buttons, input and output gain, mix; in the Waveform Editor "Apply" writes the rack into the file; in multitrack it applies to the selected clip or trackEffects left enabled in a track rack change every mixdown
    
      
    EditorWaveform or multitrack display, time ruler, zoom navigator, amplitude ruler; optional spectral displayEditing the wrong file when several are open (check the file name in the Editor tab)
    
      
    MixerConsole view of tracks and buses: inputs, sends, effects, pan, faders, metersConfusing track faders with clip gain
    
      
    HistoryEvery operation in order; click a state to go backHistory is lost on closing the file
    
      
    MarkersCue (point) and range markers with names; export audio of ranges; batchMarkers not saved when the file format cannot store them (use WAV with metadata)
    
      
    PropertiesFile or clip properties, metadata, clip volume and settingsChanges to the wrong clip
    
      
    LevelsPeak meter with clip indicators; right-click for range and ballistics optionsA clip light from earlier playback not reset
    
      
    Selection/ViewNumeric start, end and duration of selection and viewTime display format (decimal, samples) not set as needed
    
      
    Essential SoundTask-based panel (Dialogue, Music, SFX, Ambience) with Loudness, Repair and Clarity controls that add effects to the clipHidden processing: the added effects must be listed in the log
    
      
    Frequency Analysis, Amplitude Statistics, Phase Meter, Match Loudness, DiagnosticsMeasurement and batch correction panelsSettings (FFT size, RMS convention, target) not recorded
    
      
    Status barSample rate, bit depth, channels, file size, free disk space, dropped samplesDropped samples during recording indicate buffer problems
    
        
  
    

  
    Key parameters and specifications
    
  
    
    
    ParameterTypical value or rangeClinical or research meaning
    
    
      
    New file sample rate44 100 or 48 000 Hz (up to 192 kHz typical of interfaces)Bandwidth; match the playback software and the interface
    
      
    Internal bit depth32-bit floatNo clipping inside the editor; clipping appears at integer save or playback
    
      
    Save and export sample types16-bit, 24-bit integer, 32-bit float WAV; other formats16-bit for clinical playback software; 24 or 32 float for research archives
    
      
    Dither (Edit > Convert Sample Type)Triangular or shaped, depth in bitsUse when reducing to 16 bit
    
      
    Session file.sesx (XML) plus audio folderKeep the folder with the session; missing files cause offline clips
    
      
    Noise Reduction (process) FFT sizetypically 4096 to 8192 for speechLarger FFT: better frequency resolution, more pre-echo and smearing
    
      
    Frequency Analysis FFT sizeup to 65 536; Hann or Blackman windowsResolution fs/N; 8192 at 44.1 kHz gives about 5.4 Hz
    
      
    Graphic Equalizer bands10 (octave), 20 (half-octave), 30 (third-octave)Third-octave bands relate directly to audiometric frequency bands
    
      
    Parametric Equalizer5 bands + high-pass, low-pass, two shelves; slopes 6 to 48 dB/octavePrecise filtering and band-limiting
    
      
    Match Loudness targetsITU-R BS.1770 loudness (for example -23 or -24 LUFS), Total RMS, peak and true peak optionsLevel-matching lists of stimuli
    
      
    Multitrack pan modeL/R cut (centre at 0 dB in each channel) or equal power (about -3 dB at centre), chosen in Preferences > MultitrackChanges the level of centre-panned mono tracks in a stereo mixdown by up to 3 dB; hard-panned tracks are unaffected. Check and log the setting
    
        
  
    

  
    Algorithms and equations specific to Audition workflows
    
  
    equal-power pan:  gL = cos(theta), gR = sin(theta),
                  theta = (p + 1) pi/4,  p from -1 (left) to +1 (right)
bus of K uncorrelated tracks at level L:  L_bus = L + 10 log10(K)
Match Loudness gain:  G = L_target - L_measured    (dB)
where p is the pan position. At centre (p = 0) each gain is 0.707 (-3.01 dB); at p = -1, gL = 1 and gR = 0.
    
  
    Worked example. Three babble tracks, each at −32 dBFS RMS and uncorrelated, routed to one bus give −32 + 10 log10(3) = −27.23 dBFS at the bus. If the speech is at −26 dBFS RMS, the SNR is +1.23 dB; lowering the bus fader by 3.77 dB gives +5 dB SNR. Match Loudness applies the same logic file by file: a word measured at −22.4 dB Total RMS needs −3.6 dB to reach −26 dB.
    
  
    FFT Filter and Frequency Analysis resolution:  df = fs / N
group delay of a filter:  tau_g(f) = -(1/(2 pi)) d(phase)/df
where N is the FFT size. At 44.1 kHz, N = 8192 gives df = 5.38 Hz. A Bessel design keeps tau_g nearly constant across the pass band, so transients keep their shape.
    

  
    Lab A1: setting up, importing and recording
    
  
      
    
    1. Choose Edit > Preferences > Audio Hardware (macOS: Adobe Audition > Settings > Audio Hardware). Set Device Class (on Windows: ASIO for a professional interface with its driver, otherwise MME or WASAPI; on macOS: Core Audio), Default Input and Default Output, and the sample rate if shown. Why: Audition records only from the device chosen here.
      2. 
    
    2. In the same dialog, open the device's settings to set buffer size (latency). Around 256 to 512 samples is a safe start for recording speech.
      3. 
    
    3. Open an existing file: File > Open (Ctrl+O). It appears in the Files panel and in the Waveform Editor. Check the status bar: sample rate, bit depth and channels of the source. To import into a session instead, use File > Import > File (Ctrl+I) and drag the file from the Files panel to a track.
      4. 
    
    4. To record a single file: File > New > Audio File (Ctrl+Shift+N). Choose Sample Rate 44100 or 48000 Hz, Channels Mono, Bit Depth 32 (float). The Waveform Editor opens an empty file.
      5. 
    
    5. Set input level: start monitoring by right-clicking the Levels meter and choosing Meter Input Signal, speak at test level, and adjust the interface gain so that peaks reach about −12 to −6 dBFS without lighting the clip indicators. Click the clip indicators to reset them.
      6. 
    
    6. Press Record (the red button, or Shift+Space). Record 2 s of room noise, then the material. Press Space to stop. Check the Status bar for dropped samples.
      7. 
    
    7. To record in multitrack: File > New > Multitrack Session (Ctrl+N), choose sample rate, bit depth and Master (Mono or Stereo). On a track, choose the input in the track's input menu, click R (arm for record) and I (input monitoring, if you need to hear yourself), then Record. Each take is saved as a separate file in the session folder.
      8. 
    
    8. Check for clipping: Window > Amplitude Statistics, click Scan: "Possibly Clipped Samples" should be 0. Save the raw take with File > Save As as 24-bit WAV and set the file to read-only in the operating system.
      9. 
  
    
  
    Clinical linkFor voice recordings intended for acoustic analysis, disable any input processing in the interface software and in the operating system, record mono at 44.1 kHz or higher, and never apply Essential Sound or noise reduction to the analysis copy. See 5.7 Praat for the analysis side.
    

  
    Lab A2: navigating, zooming, selecting, markers
    
  
    
    
    ActionMethodUse
    
    
      
    Zoom in / out in time= and − keys; mouse wheel over the Editor; drag the edges of the zoom navigator barPrecise cuts at zero crossings
    
      
    Zoom to selectionView > Zoom > Zoom In to Selection (or the zoom buttons in the Zoom panel)Inspect a click or a word boundary
    
      
    Zoom out fully\ key or View > Zoom > Zoom Out FullOverview
    
      
    Amplitude zoomMouse wheel over the vertical ruler, or right-click the rulerSee low-level noise
    
      
    Select time rangeTime Selection tool (T); drag in the Editor; type values in Selection/ViewExact 10 ms ramps, 1 s tones
    
      
    Select one channelClick near the top or bottom of a stereo waveform, or use the channel enable buttons at the right edgeProcess only left or right
    
      
    SnappingEdit > Snapping (toggle with S): snap to markers, ruler, clips, loops, zero crossingsClean cuts; aligning clips
    
      
    Adjust to zero crossingsEdit > Zero Crossings > Adjust Selection Inward / OutwardAvoid clicks at edits
    
      
    Add markerM key, or Window > Markers and the add button; a selection gives a range markerLabel words, noise regions, stimuli
    
      
    Time display formatRight-click the time display: Decimal (mm:ss.ddd), Samples, Bars and Beats and othersSample-accurate stimulus lengths
    
        
  
    
  
      
    
    1. Open a sentence file. Zoom so that the whole sentence fills the Editor. Drag across the first word with the Time Selection tool; Selection/View shows start, end and duration.
      2. 
    
    2. Right-click the time display and set Samples. Type a duration of 441 in Selection/View: you have selected exactly 10 ms at 44.1 kHz.
      3. 
    
    3. Select the first word and press M. Rename the range marker in the Markers panel to "word1". Select the leading noise and add a marker "noise". Why: markers document regions and later drive batch export of ranges.
      4. 
    
    4. In the Markers panel select several range markers and use the export option (Export Audio of Selected Range Markers) to write each as a separate file, choosing a folder, name prefix and format. This replaces Audacity's Export Multiple by labels.
      5. 
  
    

  
    Lab A3: editing in the Waveform and Multitrack Editors
    
  
    
    
    OperationWaveform EditorMultitrack EditorNotes
    
    
      
    Cut, copy, paste, deleteEdit > Cut / Copy / Paste (Ctrl+X, C, V); Delete keySame commands act on clips or time selections; Edit > Ripple Delete closes gapsUp to five clipboards can be kept (Edit > Set Current Clipboard)
    
      
    Copy to a new fileEdit > Copy to NewnoneCopies the selection into a new untitled file: an easy way to extract a word
    
      
    Trim (keep selection only)Edit > Crop (Ctrl+T)Drag clip edges; Clip > Trim to Time SelectionIn multitrack, trimming hides audio without deleting it
    
      
    Splitnone (select and copy to new)Clip > Split (Ctrl+K) at the playhead; Razor tool (R)Creates separate clips
    
      
    SilenceEffects > Silence (replaces the selection with zeros)Silence a clip region by a clip envelope or by splitting and deletingDuration unchanged
    
      
    Insert silenceEdit > Insert > SilenceLeave a gap between clipsPads stimuli
    
      
    DuplicateCopy to New, or Save As a copyMultitrack > Track > Duplicate Selected Track; Ctrl-drag or copy-paste a clipKeep an unprocessed reference
    
      
    Move in timenoneMove tool (V): drag clips; Clip > Nudge; snapping to other clipsAlignment of speech and noise
    
      
    Undo and historyEdit > Undo (Ctrl+Z); History panelSameClosing a file clears its history
    
      
    Destructive or notDestructive (saved into the file)Non-destructive (session references files)Convert to Unique Copy before opening a clip for destructive editing
    
        
  
    
  
      
    
    1. In the Waveform Editor, select the silence before the first word leaving 200 ms, press Delete. Select from 50 ms after the last word to the end, press Delete.
      2. 
    
    2. Select the second word and choose Edit > Copy to New. A new file "Untitled" appears in the Files panel containing only the word. Save it with File > Save As.
      3. 
    
    3. Make a deliberate click: cut 30 ms from a vowel without zero-crossing adjustment and listen. Undo. Select again, use Edit > Zero Crossings > Adjust Selection Inward, cut and listen: the click is reduced.
      4. 
    
    4. Open the History panel and click the earlier states to see the undo chain. Note the order of operations in your log.
      5. 
    
    5. In a multitrack session, drag the sentence file onto Track 1. Place the playhead in a pause and press Ctrl+K to split the clip. Drag the second clip 500 ms later with the Move tool. The source file is unchanged; only the session has changed.
      6. 
  
    

  
    Amplitude effects: amplify, normalise, match loudness, fades and envelopes
    
  
    
    
    Effect (menu path)Parameters in plain languageAudiology useCaution
    
    
      
    Amplify (Effects > Amplitude and Compression > Amplify)Gain per channel in dB; Link Sliders to keep channels equalExact level steps; setting babble level for a target SNRCan push peaks over 0 dBFS (float): check before saving as integer
    
      
    Normalize (process) (Effects > Amplitude and Compression > Normalize)Normalize To (percent or dB, peak); Normalize All Channels Equally; DC Bias AdjustPeak normalisation before further processing; removing DC offsetPeak normalisation does not equalise loudness or RMS
    
      
    Match Loudness (Window > Match Loudness, or Effects > Match Loudness)Drag files in, Scan to measure; settings: Match To (ITU-R BS.1770 loudness, Total RMS and other options depending on version), Target, Tolerance, Max True Peak, Look-ahead and Release for the limiterEqualising a whole list of word or sentence files to the same RMS or loudness in one operationIf the true-peak limiter acts, the file is also compressed; check that no limiting occurred, or lower the target
    
      
    Fade handles (both editors)Drag the square handles at the top left and right of a file or clip; drag up or down to change the curve (linear, logarithmic, cosine); Ctrl-drag for other shapesRamps on tones and wordsRead the fade length in Selection/View; make it exact by selecting and using Fade Envelope
    
      
    Fade Envelope and Gain Envelope (Effects > Amplitude and Compression)Draw keyframes on a gain curve; spline option; presetsPrecise shaped ramps; tapering a maskerDestructive in the Waveform Editor
    
      
    Clip volume envelope (Multitrack)Yellow line on each clip; click to add keyframes; blue line is panDucking noise, gradual onsets, crossfadesApplied only at mixdown; visible only when envelopes are shown (View > Show Clip Volume Envelopes)
    
      
    Track volume automation (Multitrack)Track automation lanes (Read, Write, Latch, Touch modes)Changing noise level during a sequenceLeftover automation changes future mixdowns
    
        
  
    
  
    Lab A4: calibration tone at -20 dBFS RMS with 10 ms ramps
    
  
      
    
    1. File > New > Audio File: 44 100 Hz, Mono, 32 (float).
      2. 
    
    2. Effects > Generate > Tones. Choose a sine waveform, Base Frequency 1000 Hz, no modulation, no additional components (overtones at 0), Duration 30 s. Set the level so that the peak is −16.99 dBFS (the Volume or amplitude field; its exact meaning can differ between versions, so verify in the next step). Click OK.
      3. 
    
    3. Window > Amplitude Statistics, with "0 dB = FS Sine Wave" unticked in its settings, click Scan Selection or Scan. Total RMS should read −20.00 dB and Peak Amplitude −16.99 dB. If not, apply Effects > Amplitude and Compression > Amplify with the difference (for example +0.5 dB) and scan again. Why: the generator's amplitude convention must be confirmed by measurement.
      4. 
    
    4. Select exactly the first 10 ms (type start 0 and duration 0:00.010 in Selection/View) and apply Effects > Amplitude and Compression > Fade Envelope with a smooth rising curve (spline on, from 0% to 100%), or drag the fade-in handle and set a cosine curve while reading the length. Repeat at the end.
      5. 
    
    5. Window > Frequency Analysis (FFT 8192, Blackman window) while the tone plays: one line at 1000 Hz.
      6. 
    
    6. File > Save As: format Wave PCM, Sample Type 44100 Hz Mono 16-bit (click Change and choose Triangular dither), name cal_1k_-20dBFSrms_44k16_mono.wav.
      7. 
  
    
  
    SafetyAs in 5.5: the digital level of a calibration file says nothing about the acoustic level until the playback chain is measured with a sound-level meter and coupler. Do not apply any noise reduction, EQ, compression or Essential Sound processing to calibration tones or calibration noise.
    

  
    Dynamics: Dynamics Processing, compressors, limiter, gate and expander
    
  
    Audition's dynamics tools are more extensive than Audacity's. The theory (threshold, ratio, knee, attack, release, make-up gain) and the compressor equations are given in 5.5; the list below maps them onto Audition's effects in Effects > Amplitude and Compression.
    
  
    
    
    EffectParameters in plain languageDSP and behaviourAudiology use and caution
    
    
      
    Dynamics ProcessingDynamics tab: a graph of input level against output level with draggable points (any number of segments), Spline Curves, Make-Up Gain. Settings tab: Level Detector (input gain, attack and release, Peak or RMS mode), Gain Processor (output gain, attack, release, look-ahead, link channels), Band Limiting (low and high cut-off of the side chain)A general static curve: compression above one point, expansion or gating below another, all in one effectBuilding a textbook WDRC curve with a kneepoint and ratio for teaching; building a downward expander below a noise threshold. Log the curve points exactly
    
      
    Dynamics (newer versions)Sections that can be switched on: AutoGate (threshold, attack, release, hold), Compressor (threshold, ratio, attack, release), Expander (threshold, ratio), Limiter (threshold, release); make-up gainA simpler combined processorQuick gating and levelling of dialogue; same SNR cautions
    
      
    Single-band CompressorThreshold, Ratio, Attack, Release, Output Gain, look-ahead and link channels optionsClassic feed-forward compressorDemonstrating hearing-aid compression on speech; comparing fast and slow time constants
    
      
    Multiband CompressorFour bands with adjustable crossover frequencies; per band Threshold, Ratio, Attack, Release, Gain, Solo and Bypass; output Limiter and Brickwall optionSignal split by crossover filters, each band compressed, then summedClose analogue of multichannel hearing-aid compression; ideal for showing how band count and time constants flatten the spectral contrast of speech
    
      
    Tube-modeled CompressorThreshold, Ratio, Attack, Release, Output GainCompressor with a coloured, saturated characterNot for stimuli (adds harmonic colouration)
    
      
    Hard LimiterMaximum Amplitude (ceiling), Input Boost, Look-Ahead Time, Release Time, Link ChannelsVery high ratio with look-ahead; output never exceeds the ceilingProtecting a mix from overs before 16-bit export; heavy input boost makes speech sound squashed
    
      
    Speech Volume LevelerTarget Volume Level, Leveling Amount, Target Dynamic Range, Boost Low Signals, with internal compressor and noise gateSlow automatic gain control plus compressionLevelling lecture or counselling recordings; never on test materials
    
      
    DeEsserMode (broadband or multiband), Threshold, Center Frequency, Bandwidth, Output Sibilance OnlyFrequency-selective compressor acting on sibilant energyTaming harsh /s/ in narration; it reduces exactly the high-frequency cues important for listeners with high-frequency loss, so avoid on stimuli
    
        
  
    
  
    Clinical linkCompression applied to speech in noise changes the effective SNR (the gain rises in speech pauses, lifting the noise) and reduces the modulation depth of the speech envelope. Multiband compression also reduces spectral contrast between formant peaks and valleys. Such processing is a teaching tool for understanding hearing aids (Unit 3); it must never be applied after speech-in-noise materials have been mixed at a calibrated SNR.
    
  
    Lab A5: build a WDRC-style curve and compare waveforms
    
  
      
    
    1. Open a speech file normalised to −1 dBFS peak. Edit > Copy to New to make a working copy.
      2. 
    
    2. Effects > Amplitude and Compression > Dynamics Processing. In the Dynamics tab, reset the graph (a straight 1:1 line). Add a point at input −40 dB and drag the part above it so that it rises by 1 dB for every 3 dB of input (ratio 3:1). In Settings, choose RMS detection, attack 5 ms, release 50 ms. Add Make-Up Gain so that loud speech peaks return near the original level.
      3. 
    
    3. Apply. Place both files side by side (Window > Workspace, or open both in the Multitrack Editor on two tracks). The compressed file shows smaller differences between loud and soft syllables.
      4. 
    
    4. Measure both with Amplitude Statistics: peak, total RMS and their difference (crest factor). Compressed speech has a lower crest factor.
      5. 
    
    5. Repeat with Multiband Compressor, same ratio in all four bands, and compare Frequency Analysis of a vowel before and after: peaks and valleys of the spectrum come closer together.
      6. 
    
    6. Equalise overall levels with Match Loudness (Total RMS) before listening comparisons.
      7. 
  
    

  
    Modulation, pitch and time effects
    
  
    
    
    Effect (menu path)Parameters in plain languageDSPUse and caution
    
    
      
    Chorus (Effects > Modulation)Voices (number of delayed copies), Delay Time, Delay Rate, Feedback, Spread, Modulation Depth and Rate, Dry and Wet outputSeveral delayed copies (tens of milliseconds) with slowly varying delaysDemonstrating why a few voices sound like a crowd; not for stimuli
    
      
    Flanger (Effects > Modulation)Initial and Final Delay Time, Stereo Phasing, Feedback, Modulation Rate, Mode (inverted, special effects, sinusoidal), Dry/WetShort swept delay mixed with the direct sound: a moving comb filter, notches at odd multiples of 1/(2D)Teaching comb filtering (vent and processed-sound interaction, reflections)
    
      
    Chorus/Flanger (Effects > Modulation)Mode (Chorus or Flanger), Speed, Width, Intensity, TransienceSimplified combined effectQuick demonstrations
    
      
    Phaser (Effects > Modulation)Stages, Intensity, Depth, Modulation Rate, Phase Difference, Upper Frequency, Feedback, Output Gain, Dry/WetSwept all-pass cascade mixed with the input; unevenly spaced notchesDemonstrations of phase effects
    
      
    Pitch Shifter (Effects > Time and Pitch)Semitones, Cents, Ratio, Precision, splicing and overlap settingsPitch change with duration unchangedSimulating other talkers; formants move with pitch
    
      
    Stretch and Pitch (process) (Effects > Time and Pitch)Algorithm (iZotope Radius or Audition), Stretch (%), New duration, Pitch Shift (semitones), Lock Stretch and Pitch Shift (resample), Preserve Speech Characteristics, Formant preservation options depending on algorithmHigh-quality time-scale and pitch modificationSlowed or time-compressed speech for auditory training and research; record algorithm and percentage
    
      
    Automatic and Manual Pitch Correction (Effects > Time and Pitch)Scale, speed, reference; drawn pitch curvePitch detection and correctionNot for clinical stimuli
    
      
    Distortion (Effects > Special)Transfer curve graphs for positive and negative samples, Curve Smoothing, Time Smoothing, dB range, linear or log scaleMemoryless (or smoothed) non-linear transfer curveDemonstrating overload of a hearing aid or microphone; teaching THD
    
      
    Tremolo and vibratoNot separate built-in effects in recent versions. Tremolo: draw a periodic Gain Envelope or use a third-party plug-in. Frequency modulation: Effects > Generate > Tones has Modulate By (frequency deviation, Hz) and Modulation Frequency (rate, Hz), which produce frequency-modulated (warble) tonesAmplitude modulation; frequency modulationWarble tones for sound-field audiometry demonstrations (typical deviation of a few percent around the centre frequency at rates of about 5 to 20 Hz); verify by Frequency Analysis. ASSR-type AM tones are better generated in code (5.4)
    
        
  
    
  
    The distinction between pitch shifting, tempo change and speed change is the same as in Audacity (5.5). In Stretch and Pitch, ticking Lock Stretch and Pitch Shift turns the process into a simple resample (speed change), which changes pitch and duration together.
    

  
    Filter and EQ effects
    
  
    
    
    Effect (Effects > Filter and EQ)Parameters in plain languageDSPAudiology use
    
    
      
    Parametric EqualizerUp to five peaking bands (frequency, gain, Q or width), high-pass and low-pass (frequency and slope 6 to 48 dB/octave), low and high shelves; Constant Q or Constant Width; Ultra-Quiet mode; display rangeCascaded IIR biquad sectionsBand-limiting (300 to 3400 Hz), rumble removal, simulated sloping losses, correcting headphone responses
    
      
    Graphic Equalizer (10, 20 or 30 bands)Sliders for octave, half-octave or third-octave bands; Range; Accuracy; Master GainBank of band filtersThird-octave version maps to audiometric bands for counselling demonstrations
    
      
    FFT FilterDraw an arbitrary gain curve; Scale (log or linear); Spline Curves; FFT Size; WindowFrequency-domain (FFT) filtering with a drawn response; very sharp edges possibleBrick-wall band-limiting; spectrally shaping noise to match speech; removing a narrow band
    
      
    Notch FilterUp to six notch frequencies with individual gain (depth); Notch Width (Narrow, Very Narrow, Super Narrow); Ultra-Quiet; Fix gains to (fixed depth)Parallel narrow IIR notchesMains hum at 50 or 60 Hz and harmonics; a feedback whistle
    
      
    Scientific FilterType (Bessel, Butterworth, Chebychev, Elliptical), Mode (Low Pass, High Pass, Band Pass, Band Stop), Order, Cutoff (and High Cutoff for band modes), Transition Bandwidth, Pass Ripple, Stop Attenuation, GainClassical IIR designs with textbook trade-offsResearch-grade band-limiting with a documented filter type and order; teaching filter families (1.8)
    
        
  
    
  
    
    
    Scientific Filter types
      
      
    • Butterworth: maximally flat pass band, moderate roll-off; the default choice.
      • 
      
    • Chebychev: steeper roll-off for the same order, with ripple in the pass band.
      • 
      
    • Elliptical: steepest transition, ripple in both pass and stop bands.
      • 
      
    • Bessel: gentle roll-off but nearly constant group delay, so waveform shape (onsets, clicks) is preserved.
      • 
    
    
    
    Choosing for audiology
      
      
    • Band-limited speech: Butterworth order 6 to 8 or FFT Filter.
      • 
      
    • Click or tone-burst stimuli: Bessel, to avoid ringing and overshoot.
      • 
      
    • Very narrow bands (for example narrowband noise maskers): FFT Filter or Band Pass with a high order; verify with Frequency Analysis.
      • 
      
    • Always state filter type, order and cut-offs in the log.
      • 
    
    
  
    
  
    Lab A6: speech band-limited to 300 to 3400 Hz
    
  
      
    
    1. Open a clean sentence and Save As a copy named sent01_bl300-3400.wav.
      2. 
    
    2. Effects > Filter and EQ > Scientific Filter. Type Butterworth, Mode Band Pass, Order 8, Cutoff 300 Hz, High Cutoff 3400 Hz, Gain 0 dB. Preview with the power button to compare with bypass. Apply.
      3. 
    
    3. Alternative: Parametric Equalizer with only HP (300 Hz, 48 dB/octave) and LP (3400 Hz, 48 dB/octave) enabled, all bands off. Note which method you used.
      4. 
    
    4. Window > Frequency Analysis: play the original and click a Hold button, then play the filtered version; compare the held and current curves.
      5. 
    
    5. Re-level: Match Loudness (Total RMS) to the level of the rest of the stimulus set, and log the change.
      6. 
  
    
  
    Lab A7: remove 50 Hz hum
    
  
      
    
    1. Open a recording with hum. Select a pause and open Window > Frequency Analysis with FFT size 16 384 (about 2.7 Hz resolution at 44.1 kHz). Identify 50 Hz and harmonics.
      2. 
    
    2. Select the whole file. Effects > Noise Reduction / Restoration > DeHummer. Set Frequency 50 Hz, Q about 30 to 50, Gain (depth of reduction), Number of Harmonics to cover the lines you saw (for example 5: 50 to 250 Hz), Harmonic Slope to control how much the higher harmonics are reduced. Tick Output Hum Only to listen to what will be removed: it should be only hum. Untick and apply. Why: DeHummer is a comb of notches locked to the mains frequency and its harmonics.
      3. 
    
    3. Alternative: Effects > Filter and EQ > Notch Filter with notches at 50, 100, 150, 200, 250 Hz, Notch Width Very Narrow. Or the DeHum option in Essential Sound (Dialogue > Repair).
      4. 
    
    4. Re-analyse the pause and log the hum-line levels before and after. Listen for a thin or hollow sound, which indicates notches that are too wide.
      5. 
  
    

  
    Noise reduction and restoration
    
  
    Audition provides several families of restoration tools. Most are under Effects > Noise Reduction / Restoration; the clip repair tools are under Effects > Diagnostics (Waveform Editor); quick task-based versions are in the Essential Sound panel; and the most precise are the spectral editing tools.
    
  
    
    
    ToolEditorParameters in plain languageWhat it doesAudiology use and caution
    
    
      
    Capture Noise Print + Noise Reduction (process)Waveform onlyNoise Reduction (%): how much of the estimated noise to remove. Reduce By (dB): maximum attenuation. Output Noise Only: listen to what is removed. Advanced: Spectral Decay Rate (how quickly reduction follows level changes), Smoothing, Precision Factor, Transition Width (range between signal and noise over which reduction fades in), FFT Size. Noise print graph with adjustable threshold curveTwo-pass spectral noise reduction from a captured noise profile (same principle as Figure 5.5d)Cleaning recordings for counselling or teaching. Never on stimuli, calibration material or voice-analysis recordings
    
      
    Adaptive Noise ReductionBoth (real-time)Reduce Noise By, Noisiness, Fine Tune Noise Floor, Signal Threshold, Spectral Decay Rate, Broadband Preservation, FFT SizeLearns the noise continuously without a noise printChanging noise (traffic, crowd); more artefacts at onsets
    
      
    DeNoiseBoth (real-time)Amount, Processing Focus (all, low, mid, high frequencies), Output Noise Only, GainSimple one-control broadband noise reductionQuick cleanup; same cautions
    
      
    DeHummerBothFrequency (50 or 60 Hz), Q, Gain, Number of Harmonics, Harmonic Slope, Output Hum OnlyComb of notches at the mains frequency and harmonicsHum removal (Lab A7)
    
      
    DeReverbBothAmount, Processing Focus, GainEstimates and suppresses the reverberant tailTidying room recordings; alters temporal cues, so not for analysis
    
      
    Automatic Click RemoverBoth (real-time)Threshold, ComplexityDetects and interpolates clicksCable crackle; can soften plosive bursts
    
      
    Click/Pop EliminatorWaveformSensitivity, Discrimination, detection and rejection thresholds, FFT Size, Pop Oversamples Width, Run Size, PassesDetailed click detection and repair with a list of found clicksOld tape or vinyl recordings of test materials
    
      
    Diagnostics: DeClicker, DeClipper, Delete Silence, Mark Audio (Effects > Diagnostics)WaveformDeClicker: Threshold, Complexity. DeClipper: Gain, Tolerance, Min. Clip Size, Interpolation (cubic or FFT). Delete Silence and Mark Audio: amplitude and duration definitions of silence and audioScan the file, list the problems found, repair or mark them individually or all at onceDeClipper rescues clipped recordings (the restored peaks are estimates); Mark Audio places markers around words, useful for splitting word lists
    
      
    Hiss Reduction (process)WaveformCapture Noise Floor, Noise Floor, Reduce By, Output Hiss Only, advanced spectral settingsReduces broadband high-frequency hissCassette or old recordings; removes high-frequency speech cues if overdone
    
      
    Sound Remover (process)WaveformLearn Sound Model from a selection, model complexity, refinement passes, Enhance for SpeechRemoves a learned sound (for example a phone ring) from a mixtureRemoving an intrusive sound from a unique recording; log exactly what was removed
    
      
    Essential Sound: Dialogue > RepairBothCheckboxes and amount sliders for Reduce Noise, Reduce Rumble, DeHum (50 or 60 Hz), DeEss, Reduce ReverbAdds the corresponding effects to the clip's rackFast for counselling media; the added effects must be listed in the log
    
      
    Spectral Frequency Display with Spot Healing BrushWaveformView > Show Spectral Frequency Display (Shift+D); selection tools: Marquee (E), Lasso (D), Paintbrush (P); Spot Healing Brush (B) with brush sizeSelect a time-frequency region and attenuate it, apply any effect only there, or let the healing brush replace it with an estimate from surrounding audioRemoving a cough, a door click or a narrow whistle without touching the rest of the spectrum. Record time and frequency limits
    
        
  
    
  
    Lab A8: Capture Noise Print and Noise Reduction (process) with an audition of the residue
    
  
      
    
    1. Open a sentence recorded with steady fan noise and 2 s of noise before speech. File > Save As a working copy.
      2. 
    
    2. Select about 1 s of noise only. Choose Effects > Noise Reduction / Restoration > Capture Noise Print (commonly Shift+P). Why: the noise print is the average noise spectrum used as the threshold for every frequency band.
      3. 
    
    3. Select the whole file (Ctrl+A). Choose Effects > Noise Reduction / Restoration > Noise Reduction (process) (commonly Ctrl+Shift+P). The graph shows the captured noise print (and the threshold curve).
      4. 
    
    4. Start mild: Noise Reduction 50 to 70%, Reduce By 10 to 12 dB, FFT Size 4096, default advanced settings. Tick Output Noise Only and Preview: you should hear only noise. If words are audible, reduce the settings.
      5. 
    
    5. Untick Output Noise Only, Apply. Compare Frequency Analysis of the noise-only region before (use History to go back, or keep the original open) and after.
      6. 
    
    6. Try an aggressive setting (Reduce By 40 dB, Noise Reduction 100%) on another copy and listen for musical noise and dull fricatives. The interactive demonstration in 5.5 shows why.
      7. 
    
    7. Log the noise-print region, all parameters and the before and after levels. Mark the file clearly as "processed, not for analysis".
      8. 
  
    
  
    Lab A9: spectral repair of a single event
    
  
      
    
    1. Open a recording with a single cough, click or phone beep between words. Press Shift+D to show the Spectral Frequency Display. Drag the divider so that the spectral view is large.
      2. 
    
    2. For a broadband click: choose the Spot Healing Brush (B), set a brush size just wider than the click, and paint over it. Audition replaces the painted region with audio estimated from its surroundings.
      3. 
    
    3. For a narrowband beep: choose the Marquee selection tool (E) and draw a rectangle tightly around the beep in time and frequency. Apply Effects > Amplitude and Compression > Amplify with −30 dB, or use the heal command (Favorites > Auto Heal Selection in many versions). Only the selected time-frequency region changes.
      4. 
    
    4. Compare the spectrogram and listen. Undo and retry with a smaller region if speech harmonics were damaged.
      5. 
  
    
  
    Common errorSpectral editing looks so clean that it is tempting to use it on research recordings. Any healed or attenuated region is synthetic. If the recording will be measured (formants, voice quality, SNR), analyse the unedited original, or exclude the repaired interval from analysis and report it.
    

  
    Analysis tools
    
  
    
    
    Tool (menu path)What it showsSettings to noteAudiology use
    
    
      
    Frequency Analysis (Window > Frequency Analysis)Spectrum of the playhead position or selection; up to eight Hold curves for comparison; linear or log frequency scaleFFT Size, window (Hann, Hamming, Blackman and others), reference level, scan selectionBand limits of filtered speech, hum lines, noise spectra, tone purity
    
      
    Amplitude Statistics (Window > Amplitude Statistics)General tab: peak amplitude, true peak, possibly clipped samples, DC offset, minimum, maximum, average and total RMS, loudness measures; RMS Histogram tab"0 dB = FS Sine Wave" (sine-referenced RMS) on or off; RMS window widthSetting stimulus levels and SNR; clipping checks; DC offset check
    
      
    Match Loudness (Window > Match Loudness)Loudness or RMS of each file in a list; after Run, all matched to a targetMatch To, target, tolerance, true-peak limitLevelling word and sentence lists in one operation
    
      
    Loudness Meter and Loudness Radar Meter (Effects > Special)Momentary, short-term and integrated loudness (LUFS), loudness range, true peakStandard (EBU R 128, ATSC A/85 and others)Checking the loudness of counselling media and videos
    
      
    Phase Meter (Window > Phase Meter)Correlation between channels from −1 to +1noneDetecting a polarity-inverted channel in stereo stimuli (a phase-inverted diotic stimulus is not the same as an in-phase one)
    
      
    Spectral Frequency Display (View > Show Spectral Frequency Display)Spectrogram under or instead of the waveformResolution, window, log or linear frequency, range (Preferences > Spectral Displays)Inspecting fricatives, formants, noise; spectral selection
    
      
    Spectral Pitch Display (View menu)Pitch contour over the spectrogramRangeQuick look at F0; use Praat for measurements (5.7)
    
      
    Diagnostics (Window > Diagnostics)Lists of clicks, clipped regions or silences found by a scanThresholdsQuality control of recordings
    
        
  
    

  
    Basic multitrack: tracks, clips, buses, sends, mixer and mixdown
    
  
    Each track in the Multitrack Editor has a header with Mute (M), Solo (S), Arm for Record (R) and Monitor Input (I) buttons, a Volume knob (dB), a Pan knob, an input selector, an output selector (Master or a bus) and a button to show the track's effects rack, sends or EQ. Clips on the track have their own clip gain (in the Properties panel, or by dragging the clip's gain control where shown), fade handles and volume and pan envelopes. Window > Mixer (or the Mixer tab beside the Editor) shows the same controls as a console.
    
  
    
    
    TaskMenu path or controlNotes
    
    
      
    New sessionFile > New > Multitrack SessionSet sample rate, bit depth and Master (Mono, Stereo, 5.1); files at other rates are converted when added
    
      
    Add tracks and busesMultitrack > Track > Add Mono Track / Add Stereo Track / Add Mono Bus Track / Add Stereo Bus TrackBuses carry no clips; they receive track outputs or sends
    
      
    Route a track to a busTrack output selector > choose the busOne fader on the bus then scales all noise tracks together
    
      
    SendShow sends in the track header; choose a bus, set send level and pre-fader or post-faderPre-fader sends are independent of the track fader
    
      
    Track effectsfx button in the track header, or Effects Rack panel with the track selectedApplied live to the track; appears in every mixdown
    
      
    Clip gain and envelopesProperties panel (clip volume); yellow volume and blue pan envelope lines on the clipClip gain is applied before the track's effects and fader
    
      
    Move and alignMove tool (V); snapping to clips and markers; Clip > Nudge; type the clip Start Time in PropertiesTyping a start time is the most exact
    
      
    Mute, soloM and S buttonsSolo mode (exclusive or not) is set in Preferences > Multitrack
    
      
    Bounce or check the mixMultitrack > Mixdown Session to New File > Entire Session / Time SelectionOpens the mix as a new file in the Waveform Editor for measurement
    
      
    Export the mixFile > Export > Multitrack Mixdown > Entire Session / Time Selection / Selected ClipsDialog with format, sample type and mixdown options (for example channel format of the master and separate files per track or bus, depending on version)
    
        
  
    

  
    
    
    Try it: a mini multitrack mixer
    
    
    Three mono tracks start at −26 dBFS RMS each: a sentence, six-talker babble and a 1 kHz tone. Set each track's gain and pan, mute or solo tracks, and watch the stereo mix, its peak and RMS levels, the clipping warning and the speech-to-babble SNR. Pan uses an equal-power law (−3 dB per channel at centre). The SNR readout compares the two tracks after gain (before panning), which is how SNR is usually specified.
    
    
    
      
    
      
    
      
     
    
      
    
      
    
      
     
    
      
    
      
    
      
     
    
    
    
    
    
    
    
      
    Mix peak L / R
    
      
    Mix RMS L / R
    
      
    Clipping
    
      
    SNR speech re babble
    
    
    
    
    SpeechBabbleTone / left channelRight channel
    
  
    
  
    Things to try: with both speech and babble at 0 dB gain and centre pan, the SNR is 0 dB and each channel's RMS is about −26 dBFS (two signals each at −29 dBFS per channel after the −3 dB pan law, summing to −26). Pan speech hard left and babble hard right: the SNR stays 0 dB, but each channel now contains only one signal at −26 dBFS. Raise babble by 12 dB and speech by 12 dB: the mix begins to clip. Unmute the tone and solo it: this is how a calibration tone at the head of a stimulus file is checked.
    

  
    Lab A10: sentence in babble at 0 dB SNR in a multitrack session
    
  
      
    
    1. Prepare the sentence file (trimmed to speech, 2.300 s) and a babble file in the Waveform Editor. Measure the sentence's Total RMS with Amplitude Statistics (raw RMS convention) and set it to −26 dB with Amplify or Match Loudness (Total RMS). Save.
      2. 
    
    2. File > New > Multitrack Session: 44 100 Hz, 32-bit, Master Mono. Drag the babble onto Track 2 at 0 s and the sentence onto Track 1. In the Properties panel set the sentence clip's Start Time to 0:00.500.
      3. 
    
    3. Trim the babble clip to 3.300 s by dragging its right edge with snapping to the ruler, or by entering the time selection and using Clip > Trim to Time Selection.
      4. 
    
    4. Measure the babble in the overlap region: set a time selection 0.500 to 2.800 s, choose Multitrack > Mixdown Session to New File > Time Selection with only the babble track unmuted, and read Total RMS in Amplitude Statistics. Suppose −29.4 dB.
      5. 
    
    5. Back in the session, set the babble clip gain (Properties) or the babble track Volume to +3.4 dB. Why use clip gain: it keeps the track fader at 0 dB, so the session shows at a glance which clip was adjusted and by how much.
      6. 
    
    6. Add 10 ms fades at the babble clip edges with the fade handles (cosine shape).
      7. 
    
    7. Verify: unmute both, Multitrack > Mixdown Session to New File > Entire Session, then scan: peak below −1 dBFS, overlap RMS about −23 dB. Measure the speech-only and babble-only mixdowns again if the protocol requires a documented SNR.
      8. 
    
    8. Export: File > Export > Multitrack Mixdown > Entire Session. Format Wave PCM, Sample Type 44100 Hz, Mono, 16-bit (with dither enabled in the sample type conversion settings). Save the session (.sesx) with its audio folder.
      9. 
    
    9. For other SNRs, save the session under new names and change only the babble clip gain (+5 dB for −5 dB SNR, −5 dB for +5 dB SNR). A bus is useful when noise comes from several tracks: route them all to a "Noise" bus and adjust only the bus fader.
      10. 
  
    
  
    Lab A11: stereo file with speech left and noise right
    
  
      
    
    1. Create a session with a Stereo Master. Place the speech on Track 1 and the noise on Track 2, both mono, equal length, both at −26 dBFS RMS.
      2. 
    
    2. Set Track 1 Pan to L100 and Track 2 Pan to R100 (double-click the knob value and type −100 and 100). Hard-panned tracks are sent at full level to one channel in either pan mode.
      3. 
    
    3. Export: File > Export > Multitrack Mixdown > Entire Session, Stereo, 44.1 kHz, 16-bit.
      4. 
    
    4. Alternative in the Waveform Editor: File > New > Audio File, Stereo. Disable the right channel with the channel enable button at the right edge of the Editor, paste the speech (it goes only to the left), then enable only the right channel and paste the noise. Or use Effects > Amplitude and Compression > Channel Mixer to route inputs to outputs.
      5. 
    
    5. Verify: open the exported file, watch the Levels meter (left bar speech only) and use Amplitude Statistics per channel. Play with headphones and confirm speech in the left ear.
      6. 
  
    

  
    Generating signals
    
  
    
    
    GeneratorMenu pathParametersAudiology use
    
    
      
    TonesEffects > Generate > TonesWaveform shape, Base Frequency, Modulate By and Modulation Frequency (frequency modulation), up to five overtone components with levels, Duration, Volume, start phase, DC offset; a Frequency Sweep with Start and End settingsCalibration tones, pure tones, warble tones, sweeps for response measurement
    
      
    NoiseEffects > Generate > NoiseColor (White, Pink, Brown, and Grey in some versions), Style (Spatial Stereo, Independent Channels, Mono, Inverse), Intensity, DC offset, DurationMasking noise, calibration noise, base for speech-shaped noise; set level by measured RMS
    
      
    DTMF TonesEffects > Generate > DTMF TonesDial string, tone and break times, amplitude, custom frequenciesTwo-tone signals; synchronisation markers
    
      
    SpeechEffects > Generate > SpeechText, voice from the operating system's speech engine, rate, volumeDraft instructions for test software; synthetic voices are not validated test material
    
      
    SilenceEdit > Insert > Silence (duration)DurationInter-stimulus intervals, padding
    
        
  
    
  
    Grey noise is shaped by an inverse equal-loudness contour so that it sounds equally loud at all frequencies; it is not the same as audiometric speech noise or narrowband masking noise. Narrowband noise can be made from white noise with the Scientific Filter in Band Pass mode (for example one-third octave around the centre frequency) and verified with Frequency Analysis.
    

  
    Saving, exporting, batch processing and Favorites
    
  
    
    
    TaskMenu pathOptions and advice
    
    
      
    Save an audio fileFile > Save (Ctrl+S), File > Save As (Ctrl+Shift+S)Format (Wave PCM, AIFF, FLAC, MP3, OGG and others), Sample Type (Change: sample rate, channels, bit depth, dither), Format Settings (for WAV: integer 16 or 24 bit, or 32-bit float), include markers and metadata
    
      
    Change sample type in placeEdit > Convert Sample Type (Shift+T)Sample rate conversion quality, channels, bit depth; Dither enabled, Dither Type (Triangular or shaped), noise shaping
    
      
    Save a sessionFile > Save (session selected)Writes .sesx; keep the audio folder with it; File > Save As can copy the referenced files into a new folder
    
      
    Export a file with conversionFile > Export > File (Ctrl+Shift+Alt+E)As Save As, without changing the open file's name
    
      
    Export the mixFile > Export > Multitrack Mixdown > Entire Session / Time Selection / Selected ClipsMixdown options for master channel format and, in current versions, separate files for tracks or buses
    
      
    Export range markersMarkers panel > Export Audio of Selected Range MarkersOne file per range; prefix and numbering; format and sample type
    
      
    MetadataWindow > Metadata (or Properties)BWF (broadcast WAV) description and originator fields, ID3 for MP3; put the log reference here
    
      
    Record a FavoriteFavorites > Start Recording Favorite; perform operations; Favorites > Stop Recording Favorite, name itA Favorite is a stored sequence of effects with their settings; also from an effect dialog's star (Save as Favorite)
    
      
    Batch processWindow > Batch ProcessDrag files in, choose a Favorite, set Export Settings (location, name template, format, sample type), Run
    
        
  
    
  
    Lab A12: export a 16-bit 44.1 kHz mono WAV, and batch-convert a list
    
  
      
    
    1. With a file open in the Waveform Editor, choose File > Export > File. Format: Wave PCM (*.wav). Sample Type: click Change, set Sample Rate 44100, Channels Mono, Bit Depth 16, and enable Dither (Triangular). Format Settings: 16-bit integer. Tick "Include markers and other metadata" if the playback software tolerates it (some do not; test first). Save.
      2. 
    
    2. Re-open the exported file: the status bar shows 44100 Hz, 16-bit, Mono. Scan with Amplitude Statistics: values should match the pre-export ones.
      3. 
    
    3. Create a Favorite that levels a word file: Favorites > Start Recording Favorite; apply Normalize (DC Bias Adjust on, peak −1 dB), then Amplify by a fixed value if needed; Favorites > Stop Recording Favorite; name it "Word_prep". (For RMS matching of many files, Match Loudness is simpler than a Favorite.)
      4. 
    
    4. Window > Batch Process. Drag in the 10 word files. Choose Favorite "Word_prep". Export Settings: folder processed, file name prefix or suffix, Wave PCM, 44.1 kHz, 16-bit mono. Run. Check the output folder and the Batch log.
      5. 
    
    5. Alternatively, drag the processed files into the Match Loudness panel, Scan, set Match To Total RMS (or ITU-R BS.1770 loudness) with target −26, Run, and export. Check whether any file was limited.
      6. 
  
    

  
    File formats in Audition
    
  
    The format guidance of 5.5 applies unchanged: WAV (PCM or float) or FLAC for stimuli and analysis, lossy formats only for patient-education media. Audition-specific points: the session file .sesx is XML and can be read in a text editor, which is useful for checking clip positions and gains in an audit; WAV files saved by Audition can carry markers and BWF metadata in extra chunks, which a few clinical players reject; and Audition can save 32-bit float WAV files, which some older playback programs cannot read. Always test a file in the target playback software before a study.
    

  
    Audacity and Audition compared
    
  
    
    
    AspectAudacity 3.xAdobe Audition (2026)
    
    
      
    Cost and licenceFree, open source (GPL); no account neededCommercial subscription (Creative Cloud, single app or suite; education pricing available); Adobe account required
    
      
    PlatformsWindows, macOS, LinuxWindows (including ARM from 2026) and macOS; no Linux
    
      
    Editing modelProject-based; Effect-menu effects are destructive within the project; real-time track and master racks are non-destructiveTwo editors: destructive Waveform Editor per file, non-destructive Multitrack sessions
    
      
    MultitrackTracks with gain, pan, mute, solo, envelopes, real-time effects; no buses, sends or clip gain in 3.xFull mixer: clip gain, track and bus effects racks, sends, buses, automation, mixer view
    
      
    DynamicsCompressor, Limiter (3.6), Legacy versions, Noise GateDynamics Processing (arbitrary curve), Single-band, Multiband, Tube-modeled compressors, Hard Limiter, Speech Volume Leveler, DeEsser
    
      
    Filters and EQHigh-pass, low-pass, notch, shelf, Bass and Treble, Filter Curve EQ, Graphic EQParametric, Graphic (10, 20, 30 bands), FFT Filter, Notch (6 notches), Scientific Filter (Bessel, Butterworth, Chebychev, Elliptical)
    
      
    Noise reductionNoise Reduction (profile-based), Noise Gate, Click Removal, Clip Fix, RepairNoise Reduction (process) with noise print, Adaptive NR, DeNoise, DeHummer, DeReverb, Click/Pop Eliminator, Automatic Click Remover, Hiss Reduction, Sound Remover, DeClicker and DeClipper, Essential Sound Repair
    
      
    Spectral editingSpectrogram view with spectral selection; Spectral Delete and spectral EQ toolsSpectral Frequency Display with marquee, lasso, brush selection and Spot Healing Brush
    
      
    AnalysisPlot Spectrum (with text export), Contrast, Measure RMS, Find Clipping, Label Sounds, Loudness NormalizationFrequency Analysis with holds, Amplitude Statistics (both RMS conventions, histogram), Phase Meter, Loudness meters, Match Loudness, Diagnostics
    
      
    BatchMacros (3.x; not in 4.0 according to its release notes); Export Multiple by labels or tracksFavorites, Batch Process panel, Match Loudness on many files, marker range export
    
      
    ReproducibilityMacros are text; project files open only in Audacity; version changes can alter menusFavorites and sessions (XML) document settings; subscription access may lapse, so archive WAV and logs
    
      
    Suitability for clinic and researchIdeal for teaching, home practice, simple stimulus preparation and checks; universally available; limited mixing and batch toolsEfficient for large stimulus sets, complex mixes, restoration of unique recordings and media production; licence cost; neither is a validated clinical device
    
        
  
    
  
    Key ideaNeither editor is a calibrated audiometric instrument. Both produce files with a known digital level; the clinical level depends on the playback chain. For large or published stimulus sets, a script (Python or MATLAB) that generates and measures every file is more reproducible than any sequence of manual edits; editors remain the best tools for listening, inspection and teaching.
