    Clinical applications
    
  
      
    
    • Building and revising speech-in-noise materials in regional languages: sessions keep every sentence, noise segment and gain editable, so a list can be re-levelled after pilot testing without re-editing audio.
      • 
    
    • Levelling complete word and sentence lists with Match Loudness, then batch exporting in the format required by the audiometer or test software.
      • 
    
    • Rescuing unique recordings (a patient's voice before surgery, archival speech materials) with Diagnostics and spectral repair, while keeping the untouched original for measurement.
      • 
    
    • Teaching hearing-aid signal processing: Dynamics Processing curves and the Multiband Compressor illustrate WDRC; Parametric and Graphic EQ illustrate gain-frequency responses; Chorus and Flanger illustrate comb filtering from vents or reflections.
      • 
    
    • Counselling and education media: clean narration with Essential Sound, loudness-checked to a broadcast standard, with filtered-speech demonstrations of hearing loss.
      • 
    
    • Equipment checks: generated sweeps and noise recorded through headphones or loudspeakers and compared with Frequency Analysis holds.
      • 
  
    

  
    Limitations, errors and troubleshooting
    
  
    
    
    ProblemLikely causeWhat to check or do
    
    
      
    Raw recording changed permanentlyEffect applied in the Waveform Editor and file saved with Ctrl+SKeep raw files read-only; always Save As; use Convert to Unique Copy for clips
    
      
    Clips show as offline in a sessionSession moved without its audio folderUse File > Save As with the option to copy referenced files; relink media
    
      
    Mixdown louder or quieter than expected by about 3 dBPan mode (L/R cut versus equal power) for centre-panned mono tracks in a stereo masterCheck Preferences > Multitrack; use a Mono master for mono stimuli
    
      
    Every mixdown has unexpected EQ or compressionEffects left in a track, bus or Master rack; Essential Sound added effectsInspect all racks (Mixer view); bypass or remove; log
    
      
    RMS values differ from Audacity by 3.01 dB"0 dB = FS Sine Wave" ticked in Amplitude StatisticsChoose one convention for the whole project and state it
    
      
    Match Loudness output sounds squashedTrue-peak limiter acting because the target was too highLower the target or raise the maximum true peak; check the report
    
      
    Noise Reduction produces watery artefactsReduction too strong, noise print from a region containing speechRecapture from pure noise; lower Reduce By; check Output Noise Only
    
      
    Recording has dropoutsBuffer too small; wrong device classAudio Hardware settings; status bar dropped samples
    
      
    Clinical player rejects the WAV32-bit float, extra metadata chunks, unusual rateExport 16-bit PCM without markers or metadata; test in the player
    
      
    Stimuli in the wrong earPan or channel order wrongVerify each channel after export (Lab A11)
    
        
  
    

  
    Demonstration
    
  
    Equipment: a computer with Adobe Audition (Creative Cloud), an audio interface, microphone, closed headphones, projector; a sentence recording, babble, a hum-contaminated recording, and one recording with a cough or phone beep.
    
  
      
    
    1. Show both editors with the same file: a destructive edit in the Waveform Editor and the same edit as a clip change in the Multitrack Editor. Students observe that only the Waveform edit changes the file on disk.
      2. 
    
    2. Generate the calibration tone and measure it with Amplitude Statistics with the sine-referenced option on and off: students see the 3.01 dB difference.
      3. 
    
    3. Build the 0 dB SNR session live using clip gain and a noise bus; change the bus fader to create −5 dB and +5 dB conditions; mixdown and measure.
      4. 
    
    4. Remove hum with DeHummer, listening to Output Hum Only first.
      5. 
    
    5. Run Noise Reduction (process) at mild and aggressive settings with Output Noise Only; compare with Adaptive Noise Reduction and Essential Sound Reduce Noise.
      6. 
    
    6. Heal a cough with the Spot Healing Brush in the spectral display; discuss why the healed file must not be used for analysis.
      7. 
    
    7. Create a Favorite and run a Batch Process on ten word files; export and verify one file.
      8. 
  
    

  
    Current developments
    
  
      
    
    • AI-assisted speech enhancement in Adobe's audio tools (for example the Enhance Speech function available in some Adobe products and in newer Audition releases) can make poor recordings sound studio-quality, but it resynthesises or heavily alters the speech signal and is unsuitable for stimuli or acoustic analysis.
      • 
    
    • Native builds for new processor architectures (Windows on ARM from 2026, Apple silicon) improve performance on laptops used in outreach clinics.
      • 
    
    • Loudness-based workflows (ITU-R BS.1770, EBU R 128) are now central to media production and are increasingly used for levelling speech materials; researchers still need to report which measure was used.
      • 
    
    • Closer integration with video editors (Premiere Pro) supports production of accessible, captioned counselling and tele-audiology videos.
      • 
    
    • Spectral and source-separation tools continue to improve, raising the need for clear reporting when recordings used in research have been repaired.
      • 
  
    

  
    Practical activity and assessment
    
  
    Practical P12 (Audition part). Repeat the Audacity deliverables of 5.5 in Audition and add:
    
  
      
    
    1. A multitrack session producing the same sentence at +5, 0 and −5 dB SNR from one session, using a noise bus, with mixdowns measured and logged.
      2. 
    
    2. A level-matched set of ten word files produced with Match Loudness (Total RMS) and a batch export to 16-bit, 44.1 kHz mono WAV.
      3. 
    
    3. A spectrally repaired copy of a recording with one intrusive event, with before and after spectrograms and a statement of the repaired region.
      4. 
    
    4. A one-page comparison of Audacity and Audition for your clinic's needs, using the comparison table as a starting point and adding cost, training and data-handling considerations.
      5. 
  
    
  
    Assessment: correct levels and SNRs (re-measured by the instructor), correct formats and channel order, clean sessions (no stray effects), completeness of the log and quality of the comparison.
    

  
    Exercises
    
  
    
    
    
      
    
      
    For each task choose the Waveform or Multitrack Editor: (a) removing a click from a single word file, (b) creating the same sentence at five SNRs, (c) Noise Reduction (process), (d) placing a calibration tone before a list of sentences with fixed gaps.
    Model answer: (a) Waveform (destructive repair of one file, keep the original). (b) Multitrack (one session, change only noise gain). (c) Waveform (the process effect exists only there). (d) Multitrack (clips can be positioned by start time and revised), or Waveform if a single file is simply assembled once.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Amplitude Statistics shows Total RMS −17.0 dB for a 1 kHz tone with "0 dB = FS Sine Wave" ticked. What will Audacity's Measure RMS report for the same file, and what is the tone's peak level?
    Model answer: sine-referenced −17.0 dB means raw RMS −17.0 − 3.01 = −20.01 dB, which Audacity reports. For a sine the peak is 3.01 dB above raw RMS, so the peak is −17.0 dBFS; for a sine the sine-referenced RMS equals the peak level.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    A mono speech track at −26 dBFS RMS is panned centre in a stereo master using an equal-power pan mode. What is the RMS in each output channel? What if the master is mono?
    Model answer: equal-power centre gain is cos 45° = 0.707 (−3.01 dB), so each channel carries −29.0 dBFS RMS. With a mono master the track is not split, so the output is −26 dBFS RMS. This is why mono stimuli should be mixed to a mono master.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    You need (a) 300 to 3400 Hz band-limited speech, (b) a band-pass filtered click stimulus with minimal ringing, (c) removal of 50 Hz hum and four harmonics. Which Audition effect and settings would you choose?
    Model answer: (a) Scientific Filter, Butterworth band pass, order 6 to 8, 300 and 3400 Hz (or Parametric EQ HP and LP at 48 dB/octave, or FFT Filter). (b) Scientific Filter, Bessel type, modest order, because its nearly constant group delay limits ringing and overshoot. (c) DeHummer, 50 Hz, 5 harmonics, high Q, checked with Output Hum Only; or Notch Filter with five narrow notches.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Two uncorrelated noise tracks, each −30 dBFS RMS, are routed to a Noise bus. The speech is at −26 dBFS RMS. What bus gain gives an SNR of +2 dB?
    Model answer: the bus input is 10 log10(2 × 10^(−3)) = −26.99 dBFS. The target noise level is −26 − 2 = −28 dBFS, so the bus gain is −28 − (−26.99) = −1.01 dB, about −1.0 dB.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    A research assistant used Essential Sound (Reduce Noise and DeEss) and a Spot Healing Brush on 40 voice recordings before measuring cepstral peak prominence and spectral tilt. Explain the problems and what should be done.
    Model answer: noise reduction removes or reshapes low-level spectral components, raising the apparent harmonic-to-noise structure and therefore cepstral peak prominence; the de-esser reduces high-frequency energy and changes spectral tilt; healed regions are synthetic. The measurements describe the processing as much as the voices, and the processing may differ between recordings. The analyses should be repeated on the unprocessed originals (excluding any intervals with intrusive events), the processed files kept only for listening, and the processing documented in the methods if any processed data are retained.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Design an Audition workflow to turn 300 raw 48 kHz, 24-bit sentence recordings into 44.1 kHz, 16-bit mono files at −26 dBFS RMS with 10 ms ramps, and name one check you would automate outside Audition.
    Model answer: (1) Mark Audio in Diagnostics or manual range markers to trim each file to speech plus fixed margins; (2) a Favorite containing Normalize with DC Bias Adjust (peak −3 dB) and fades, run through Batch Process with export to 44.1 kHz, 16-bit mono, triangular dither; (3) Match Loudness with Total RMS target −26, with the true-peak limit set high enough that no limiting occurs; (4) export. Because ramps and trimming vary per file, automate a check with a script that reads every output file, confirms rate, bit depth, channel count, RMS within ±0.1 dB of −26, peak below −1 dBFS and non-zero first and last 10 ms energy consistent with ramps.

    5.7
    Praat from the basics: speech and voice analysis
    
  
    Supplementary4 hours (2 lectures + 2 labs)Practical P4
    
  
    Praat is the free program that most speech scientists, phoneticians and many voice and hearing researchers use to look at, measure, annotate and modify speech. This topic starts with downloading the program and ends with a script that measures fundamental frequency, intensity, formants, jitter, shimmer and harmonics-to-noise ratio for every file in a folder. Every setting is explained from the physics upwards, so that the numbers you report can be defended in a dissertation or a clinical report.
    

  
    
    
    What it is
    Praat (Dutch for "talk") is a speech analysis, synthesis and annotation program written by Paul Boersma and David Weenink at the University of Amsterdam. It is free, open source and runs on Windows, macOS and Linux. It reads sound files, displays waveforms and spectrograms, tracks pitch, intensity and formants, measures voice perturbation, lets you label speech on time-aligned tiers, resynthesises speech with new pitch or timing, and can be driven completely by scripts.
    
    
    Why it is required
    Audiologists and speech-language pathologists need objective numbers for voice and speech: the F0 and jitter of a voice before and after therapy, the vowel space of a child with a cochlear implant, the duration of a word in a speech test list, or the long-term spectrum of a sentence set used to calibrate a speech-in-noise test. Praat gives these numbers with published, documented algorithms, which is why it appears in the methods section of a very large number of research papers.
    
    
    How it works
    Everything in Praat is an object: a Sound, a Pitch contour, a Formant track, a TextGrid of labels. You select an object in the Objects window and choose a command; the command either creates a new object (analysis) or returns a number (query). The same commands can be recorded into a script and repeated on hundreds of files, or called from Python through the Parselmouth library.
    
  
    

  
    This topic extends the software survey in 5.1, which introduced Praat's object structure and showed a first short script. Waveform editing (cutting, fading, normalising) is covered for editors in 5.2; here the same operations are done the Praat way, with measurement in mind. The acoustic theory behind the analyses is in 1.9 speech in time and frequency, 1.10 short-time features, 3.8 LPC and 3.9 cepstrum. Filters are explained in 1.8. If you prefer to work in code, the Audiology Python Primer and the Parselmouth section below show the same measurements in Python, and 5.4 covers MATLAB.
    

  
    
    
    SessionContent (sections below)Outcome
    
    
      
    Lecture 11 to 8: installing, the two windows, the object workflow, recording and reading, saving, the Sound window, spectrogram settings, pitchCan open a recording, read a spectrogram and choose pitch settings with a reason

    RememberThis topic describes Praat 6.x as distributed from the Praat website, with menu names checked against the online manual in 2026. Praat is updated often and occasionally renames commands; the 6.4 series, for example, renamed the pitch analysis methods. If a command is not where described, search for it with the Help menu or the manual, which always documents the current name. All measured values quoted below were produced by running the same commands through Parselmouth 0.4.7 (which contains the Praat 6.1.38 engine), and the scripts use command names that current Praat versions still accept.
    

  
    1. What Praat is, and installing it
    
  
    Praat has been developed since 1992 and is released under the GNU General Public License. The program is a single executable file of a few tens of megabytes. It needs no licence key, no internet connection and no administrator installation on most systems, which makes it convenient for clinic computers where software installation is restricted. Its manual is built into the program (Help menu) and is also available online at www.praat.org, which redirects to the University of Amsterdam page.
    
  
    Downloading and installing
    
  
      
    
    1. Go to www.praat.org and choose the link for your operating system (Windows, Macintosh, Linux; there are also builds for Chromebook and Raspberry Pi). You see a download page that names the current version number. Use this official site; copies on third-party download sites may be old or bundled with unwanted software.
      2. 
    
    2. Windows: download the zip file for 64-bit Windows, open it and drag Praat.exe to a folder such as Documents\Praat or the Desktop. There is no installer; double-clicking Praat.exe starts the program. Because the program is a single file, you can keep a copy on a USB stick for a clinic computer.
      3. 
    
    3. macOS: download the .dmg disk image, open it and drag Praat to the Applications folder. The first time, macOS may ask whether you want to open an application downloaded from the internet; confirm.
      4. 
    
    4. Linux: download the build from the website or install the praat package from your distribution. Distribution packages are sometimes several versions behind; for research use the website build.
      5. 
    
    5. Start Praat. Two windows appear: Praat Objects and Praat Picture. Check the version with Praat > About Praat (on macOS the About item is in the application menu). Write the version number into your lab notebook.
      6. 
  
    
  
    Common errorStudents often download a new version halfway through a dissertation. Measurements of pitch and voice quality can change slightly between versions, especially after an algorithm update such as the 2023 change of the preferred pitch method. Keep the version fixed for one data set and report it in the methods ("Praat version 6.x.yy, Boersma and Weenink").
    

  
    2. The Objects window and the Picture window
    
  
    Praat is organised around two permanent windows. The Objects window holds a list of everything you have loaded or created, and shows buttons for the commands that apply to whatever is selected. The Picture window is a drawing canvas for figures; you only use it when you want a figure for a report or paper. You can close the Picture window at any time and reopen it from the Praat menu.
    

  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.7a. Schematic of the two Praat windows (simplified; not a screenshot). The Objects window lists objects on the left and shows only the commands that apply to the selected object type on the right. The Picture window draws into the selected viewport (highlighted rectangle).
    

  
    Walk-through of the Objects window, from top to bottom:
    
  
    
    
    RegionWhat it containsTypical use in audiology work
    
    
      
    Praat menuAbout Praat, Preferences, New Praat script, Open Praat script, Goodies (calculator), QuitCheck the version; start a script
    
      
    New menuRecord mono Sound..., Record stereo Sound..., Sound (Create Sound from formula..., Create Sound as pure tone..., Create Sound as tone complex...), and creators for other object types such as TextGrid, PitchTier, FormantGridRecord a patient; generate a calibration tone or a stimulus
    
      
    Open menuRead from file..., Open long sound file..., Read separate channels from sound file...Load WAV recordings and saved TextGrids
    
      
    Save menu (active when an object is selected)Save as WAV file..., Save as FLAC file..., Save as text file..., Save as binary file... (the list depends on the object type)Store edited sounds, TextGrids and analysis objects
    
      
    Object listNumbered objects named "Type name", for example 1. Sound vowel_aClick to select; Ctrl-click (Command-click on macOS) or Shift-click to select several
    
      
    Dynamic menu (right)Buttons that change with the selected type. For a Sound: View & Edit, Play, Draw, Query, Modify, Annotate, Analyse periodicity, Analyse spectrum, To Intensity..., Manipulate, Synthesize, Filter, Combine, ConvertAll analysis starts here
    
      
    Fixed buttons (bottom)Rename..., Copy..., Inspect, Info, RemoveInfo gives a summary (duration, sampling frequency, number of channels); Remove frees memory
    
        
  
    
  
    Commands ending in three dots (...) open a settings dialog before they run; commands without dots run immediately. A minus sign after a button name (for example Query -) means it opens a sub-menu. The Info window, which opens automatically when a query returns text, is a third window that you will use constantly: every number Praat reports appears there and can be copied into a spreadsheet.
    

  
    3. The object-oriented workflow: select, then act
    
  
    New users of Praat look for an "Analyse" menu at the top of the screen, as in an audio editor. Praat works differently: the command you need is attached to the object type. To measure pitch you first create a Pitch object from a Sound object, then ask the Pitch object for its mean. This design keeps analysis steps explicit: the Pitch object is a record of exactly which settings were used, and you can inspect, edit, draw or save it separately from the sound.
    

  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.7b. The Praat workflow. Analysis commands create new objects; query commands return numbers; every step can be pasted into a script.
    
  
    The five steps in order:
    
  
      
    
    1. Read. Open > Read from file... loads a WAV file as a Sound object named after the file.
      2. 
    
    2. Select. Click the object. The dynamic menu on the right shows only commands valid for that type. Some commands need two objects at once (for example Sound plus TextGrid for annotation, or Sound plus PointProcess for shimmer); select the first, then Ctrl-click the second.
      3. 
    
    3. Analyse. A command such as Analyse periodicity > To Pitch... creates a new object (Pitch vowel_a) and selects it.
      4. 
    
    4. Query. With the Pitch selected, Query > Get mean... returns a number in the Info window. Queries never change objects.
      5. 
    
    5. Save. Save the objects you need (edited sounds, TextGrids, sometimes Pitch objects after manual correction) and copy numbers from the Info window.
      6. 
  
    
  
    
    
    Object typeCreated byWhat it holdsTypical queries
    
    
      
    SoundRead from file, Record, Create Sound from formulaSamples (pascals by assumption), sampling frequency, channelsGet root-mean-square, Get intensity (dB), Get total duration
    
      
    PitchSound: To Pitch (several methods)F0 candidates and the chosen F0 per frame (or unvoiced)Get mean, Get minimum, Get maximum, Get standard deviation, Count voiced frames
    
      
    IntensitySound: To Intensity...Intensity contour in dB per frameGet mean, Get maximum, Get value at time
    
      
    FormantSound: To Formant (burg)...Up to N formant frequencies and bandwidths per frameGet value at time, Get mean, Get bandwidth at time
    
      
    PointProcessSound: To PointProcess (periodic, cc)...Times of individual glottal pulsesGet jitter (local), Get number of periods
    
      
    HarmonicitySound: To Harmonicity (cc)...Harmonics-to-noise ratio per frameGet mean
    
      
    Spectrum, LtasSound: To Spectrum..., To Ltas...Spectrum of a whole sound; long-term average spectrumGet centre of gravity, Get band energy, Get mean
    
      
    TextGridSound: To TextGrid..., Read from fileTime-aligned labels on interval and point tiersGet number of intervals, Get label of interval, Get start time of interval
    
      
    ManipulationSound: To Manipulation...Sound, pulses, editable pitch and duration tiersGet resynthesis (overlap-add)
    
        
  
    

  
    4. Getting sound into Praat: recording and reading files
    
  
    Recording with Praat
    
  
    Praat can record directly from any microphone input that the operating system recognises. For research recordings a dedicated audio interface and a measurement or good condenser microphone are preferable to a laptop's built-in microphone, whose automatic gain control and noise suppression alter level and spectrum.
    
  
      
    
    1. New > Record mono Sound... opens the SoundRecorder window. It shows an input level meter, a choice of input source (on some systems) and a list of sampling frequencies. Mono is correct for single-microphone speech recordings; stereo is for two microphones or binaural recordings.
      2. 
    
    2. Choose the sampling frequency. For speech and voice analysis 44 100 Hz is a safe default; 16 000 Hz or 22 050 Hz is adequate if only formants below 5 kHz and F0 are needed. See the table below for reasons.
      3. 
    
    3. Ask the speaker to produce a test phrase at the loudness to be used. Watch the meter: the loudest parts should reach roughly half to three quarters of full scale and never touch the top. Adjust the gain in the operating system's sound settings or on the audio interface, not afterwards in software.
      4. 
    
    4. Click Record, record the material, click Stop. Click Play to check it.
      5. 
    
    5. Type a name in the Name field (for example p01_pre_a) and click Save to list (keeps the recorder open for the next item) or Save to list & Close. The recording appears as a Sound object in the Objects window.
      6. 
    
    6. Save it immediately as a WAV file (section 5). Objects in the list exist only in memory and are lost when Praat is closed.
      7. 
  
    
  
    
    
    Sampling frequencyHighest frequency represented (Nyquist)Suitable forNotes
    
    
      
    8 000 Hz4 000 HzTelephone-band material onlyLoses fricative energy and F4; avoid for new recordings
    
      
    16 000 Hz8 000 HzF0, formants, most speech perception materialCommon in speech corpora and recognition systems
    
      
    22 050 Hz11 025 HzSpeech including most fricative energyAdequate for children's speech analysis
    
      
    44 100 Hz22 050 HzGeneral purpose, stimuli, voice analysis, publication audioDefault choice; can be resampled down later without loss
    
      
    48 000 Hz or 96 000 Hz24 000 or 48 000 HzVideo workflows, high-frequency researchNo benefit for routine speech measures; larger files
    
        
  
    
  
    The Nyquist relation (the highest representable frequency is half the sampling frequency) and aliasing are explained in 1.6. Praat's analyses resample internally when they need to: formant analysis, for example, resamples to twice the maximum formant frequency, so a 44 100 Hz recording costs nothing in accuracy. Recording at a low rate, on the other hand, cannot be undone.
    
  
    Clinical linkFor voice analysis protocols, sustained vowels (typically /a/ for 3 to 5 s at comfortable pitch and loudness) and a standard read passage or sentence set are usually recorded with a constant mouth-to-microphone distance, often around 5 to 30 cm depending on the microphone type and protocol. Write the distance, microphone and room into the session record; without them, intensity and noise measures cannot be compared across sessions.
    
  
    Reading existing files
    
  
      
    
    1. Open > Read from file... and choose the file. Praat reads WAV, AIFF, AIFC, FLAC, MP3 (read only), NIST and several other formats, as well as its own text and binary object files and TextGrids.
      2. 
    
    2. The file appears as a Sound object. Click Info at the bottom of the Objects window and check the number of channels, sampling frequency and duration. A surprise here (stereo instead of mono, 8 kHz instead of 44.1 kHz) explains many strange results later.
      3. 
    
    3. For a stereo file where only one channel is needed, use Convert > Extract one channel... or Convert > Convert to mono (which averages the channels). Open > Read separate channels from sound file... reads each channel as its own object.
      4. 
    
    4. For very long recordings (a full therapy session, for instance) use Open > Open long sound file.... This creates a LongSound object that is read from disk on demand. You can view it and extract parts, but most analyses need an ordinary Sound, so extract the part you want first.
      5. 
  
    
  
    You can also drag a file onto the Objects window on most systems. Praat does not modify the file on disk when you edit the Sound object; changes are only written when you save.
    

  
    5. Saving your work
    
  
    Praat distinguishes between audio formats, which any program can read, and its own object formats, which store the full contents of any Praat object. The Save menu shows only the formats valid for the selected object.
    
  
    
    
    Command (Save menu)ObjectResultUse
    
    
      
    Save as WAV file...Sound16-bit PCM WAV by defaultStandard for recordings and stimuli; readable everywhere
    
      
    Save as 24-bit WAV file... or 32-bit WAV file... (where offered)SoundHigher-resolution PCMStimuli with a large dynamic range or very low levels
    
      
    Save as FLAC file...SoundLossless compressed audioArchiving large corpora (about half the size of WAV)
    
      
    Save as text file...Any object (TextGrid, Pitch, Formant, PitchTier)Readable text; TextGrids get the extension .TextGridAnnotations; corrected pitch contours; sharing with other software
    
      
    Save as short text file...Any objectCompact text without labels for each fieldSmaller files; still readable by Praat
    
      
    Save as binary file...Any objectCompact binary Praat formatLarge analysis objects that only Praat will read
    
        
  
    
  
    Common errorSaving a Sound whose samples exceed the range -1 to +1 as a 16-bit WAV file clips those samples. Praat warns about this. Before saving, check Query > Get maximum... and Get minimum..., or use Modify > Scale peak... 0.99. Saving to MP3 is not possible, and lossy formats should not be used for material to be analysed.
    
  
    Good practice is to keep original recordings unchanged in a raw folder, save edited and cut files in a separate folder with a clear naming scheme (participant, session, task), and save TextGrids with exactly the same base name as the sound (p01_pre_rainbow.wav and p01_pre_rainbow.TextGrid). Scripts can then find the matching pairs automatically.
    

  
    6. The Sound window (View & Edit)
    
  
    Select a Sound and click View & Edit. The Sound window (called SoundEditor in the manual; with a TextGrid selected as well, the TextGridEditor) shows the waveform above a spectrogram, with optional overlays for pitch, intensity, formants and glottal pulses. This is where most interactive measurement happens.
    

  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.7c. Schematic of the Sound window (simplified). The highlighted band is a time selection spanning both panes; the bars at the bottom play the parts before, inside and after the selection, the visible part and the whole sound.
    
  
    Navigating and measuring
    
  
      
    
    1. Click once in the waveform: a vertical cursor appears and the time is shown above it. Drag to make a selection; its start, end and duration appear at the top and in the play bars.
      2. 
    
    2. Play: click the bar under the selection, or press the Tab key to play the selection (or the visible window if nothing is selected). Press Escape to stop. Listening is a measurement step: you check that the selection contains what you think it contains.
      3. 
    
    3. Zoom: the buttons at the bottom left are all (show the whole sound), in, out, sel (zoom to selection) and bak (back to the previous zoom). The same commands are in the View menu with keyboard shortcuts, typically Ctrl+A, Ctrl+I, Ctrl+O and Ctrl+N (Command on macOS). Use the scroll bar to move along the sound.
      4. 
    
    4. Turn analyses on and off in the Spectrogram, Pitch, Intensity, Formants and Pulses menus (Show spectrogram, Show pitch, Show intensity, Show formants, Show pulses). Analyses are computed only for the visible window and only when it is shorter than the "longest analysis" setting (Analyses > Show analyses...); zoom in if the panes stay empty.
      5. 
    
    5. Measure: with the cursor at a point, Pitch > Get pitch, Formants > Get first formant, Intensity > Get intensity and Spectrogram > Get spectral power at cursor cross report values at the cursor; with a selection, the Get commands report the mean over the selection. The listing commands (Pitch > Pitch listing, Formants > Formant listing, Intensity > Intensity listing) print every frame in the visible part or selection to the Info window, ready to paste into a spreadsheet.
      6. 
    
    6. Extract: Sound > Extract selected sound (time from 0) creates a new Sound object containing only the selection, for example one vowel for a Voice report or one word for a test list. The spectrogram, pitch and formant menus also have Extract visible ... commands, which put the analysis of the visible part into the Objects window.
      7. 
    
    7. Edit: Edit > Cut, Copy, Paste and Set selection to zero work on the Sound in memory. For stimulus editing, cut at zero crossings (Select > Move start of selection to nearest zero crossing, and the same for the end) to avoid clicks.
      8. 
  
    
  
    Key ideaThe overlays in the Sound window are quick-look analyses computed with the editor's own settings, and they change when you zoom, because only the visible part is analysed. For numbers you will report, either note the editor settings carefully or, better, create the analysis objects from the Objects window (or a script) with explicit settings and query those.
    

  
    7. Spectrogram settings in depth
    
  
    A spectrogram shows how the spectrum of a sound changes over time: time runs left to right, frequency bottom to top, and darkness shows intensity. It is computed by cutting the sound into short overlapping windows, taking the Fourier transform of each and plotting the magnitude in dB. The physics of this short-time Fourier analysis is covered in 1.9; here the question is how each Praat setting changes what you see. Open the dialog with Spectrogram > Spectrogram settings....
    
  
    
    
    SettingStandard valueWhat it controlsWhen to change it
    
    
      
    View range (Hz)0 to 5000Frequency axis shown0 to 8000 or 10 000 Hz for fricatives (/s/ energy lies mostly above 4 kHz) and for children's speech; 0 to 3000 Hz to inspect F1 and F2 in detail
    
      
    Window length (s)0.005Duration of each analysis window; sets the time and frequency resolution0.005 s for wideband (formants, bursts, voice onset); about 0.03 s for narrowband (harmonics, F0 contour, subharmonics)
    
      
    Dynamic range (dB)50 (current manual)How many dB below the maximum are still drawn; weaker parts appear whiteIncrease (60 to 70 dB) to see weak high-frequency energy; decrease (30 to 40 dB) to suppress background noise in a noisy recording
    
      
    Advanced: window shapeGaussianShape of the analysis windowRarely changed; Gaussian gives smooth images without sidelobe artefacts
    
      
    Advanced: pre-emphasis (dB/oct)6.0Tilts the display so that high frequencies are boosted by 6 dB per octaveKeep at 6 for speech (compensates the natural fall of the voice spectrum); 0 for non-speech signals such as audiometric tones
    
      
    Advanced: autoscaling, maximum (dB/Hz)On; 100Whether darkness is scaled to the loudest part of the visible windowTurn autoscaling off when comparing loudness across files visually
    
      
    Advanced: time steps, frequency steps1000, 250Number of columns and rows computedOnly for very long windows or high-resolution figures
    
        
  
    
  
    Wideband and narrowband: the resolution trade-off
    
  
    A short window contains little more than one glottal cycle. Its spectrum cannot separate individual harmonics (they are closer together than the analysis bandwidth), so it shows the spectral envelope: broad dark bands at the formant frequencies. Because the window is short, each glottal pulse appears as a separate vertical striation and rapid events such as plosive bursts are sharp in time. This is the wideband (broad-band) spectrogram. A long window contains several cycles; its spectrum resolves the individual harmonics as horizontal lines spaced at F0, but events are smeared in time. This is the narrowband spectrogram. The Praat manual gives analysis bandwidths of about 260 Hz for the standard 5 ms window and about 43 Hz for a 30 ms window.
    
  
    B  ≈  1.3 / L          (Praat's Gaussian window)
harmonics resolved when  B < F0 (roughly B < F0 / 2 for clear lines)
where L is the window length in seconds and B the analysis bandwidth in Hz. The factor 1.3 follows from the manual's values: 1.3 / 0.005 = 260 Hz and 1.3 / 0.030 = 43 Hz.
    
  
    Worked example: a male voice at F0 = 110 Hz. With L = 0.005 s, B is about 260 Hz, more than twice F0, so harmonics merge and the formants appear as smooth bands: a wideband picture. With L = 0.03 s, B is about 43 Hz, well under half of 110 Hz, so each harmonic is a separate line. For a child at F0 = 300 Hz, even a 5 ms window has B (260 Hz) below F0, so a "wideband" spectrogram of a child's voice already shows individual harmonics, and formants are harder to see because the harmonics sample the envelope sparsely. This is the same reason formant measurement is less accurate for high-pitched voices (section 10).
    
  
    Clinical linkNarrowband spectrograms are the quickest way to see voice problems that numbers hide: subharmonics (extra lines between harmonics) in diplophonic or rough voices, noise filling the spaces between harmonics in breathy voices, and pitch breaks. Wideband spectrograms show articulation: formant transitions, voice onset time, frication and nasal murmur, which is why they are used for analysing the speech of children with hearing loss.
    

  
    
    
    Try it: wideband and narrowband spectrograms
    
    
    A synthetic three-vowel sequence (/i/, /a/, /u/) with F0 gliding from 100 to 180 Hz and back. Move the window-length slider from 5 ms to 50 ms and watch the formant bands turn into harmonic lines; lower the dynamic range to hide weak energy.
    
    
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
    
    
    
    
    
      
    Time resolution (window)
    
      
    Frequency resolution (Hann, about 1.44 / L)
    
      
    F0 range in the signal
    100 to 180 Hz
    
      
    What you should see
    
    
    
  
    
  
    The demo uses a Hann window, whose bandwidth (about 1.44 / L) is a little wider than Praat's Gaussian window of the same nominal length, but the behaviour is the same. Notice three things. First, formant positions do not change with window length; only the way they are drawn does. Second, as F0 rises in the middle of the sequence, the harmonic lines spread apart in the narrowband view. Third, turning pre-emphasis off makes the upper formants of /i/ nearly disappear, because the source spectrum falls by about 12 dB per octave.
    

  
    8. Pitch analysis
    
  
    Fundamental frequency (F0) is the rate of vocal-fold vibration, in Hz: the number of glottal cycles per second. Pitch is strictly the perceived height of a sound, but Praat (like most speech software) calls its F0 contour a Pitch object. Typical conversational F0 is around 85 to 180 Hz for adult men, 165 to 255 Hz for adult women and higher (often 250 to 400 Hz) for young children, with wide individual variation. F0 is measured to describe intonation, to track voice therapy, to compare voices of children with hearing loss or cochlear implants with norms, and to control the F0 of perception stimuli.
    
  
    A test vowel with known answers
    
  
    Before measuring real voices it is good practice to measure a signal whose true values are known. The procedure below builds a steady vowel by the source-filter method: a train of glottal pulses at a chosen F0 (the source), made to fall at 12 dB per octave like a real glottal source, then passed through five resonators at chosen formant frequencies (the filter). Optional alternating perturbations add a known amount of jitter and shimmer. It uses only standard Praat commands, so it also serves as a first example of a Praat script (section 18 explains the syntax). Save it as makevowel.praat; later scripts include it.
    
  
    

    # makevowel.praat : source-filter synthesis of a steady vowel
# jit, shim: alternating period and amplitude perturbation (0.005 = +/- 0.5 %)
procedure makeVowel: .name$, .f0, .f1, .f2, .f3, .jit, .shim, .dur
    # 0.1 s spare at each end, cut off at the end of the procedure
    .total = .dur + 0.2
    .pp = Create empty PointProcess: "pulses", 0, .total
    .t = 0.005
    .k = 0
    while .t < .total
        Add point: .t
        .t = .t + (1 / .f0) * (1 + .jit * (1 - 2 * (.k mod 2)))
        .k = .k + 1
    endwhile
    .train = To Sound (pulse train): 44100, 1, 0.05, 2000
    # two first-order low-pass stages: source slope of -12 dB per octave
    .s1 = Filter (de-emphasis): 50
    .src = Filter (de-emphasis): 50
    .fg = Create FormantGrid: "fg", 0, .total, 5, 500, 1000, 80, 50
    .ff# = {.f1, .f2, .f3, 3400, 4000}
    .bw# = {80, 90, 120, 150, 200}
    for .i to 5
        Remove formant points between: .i, 0, .total
        Add formant point: .i, .total / 2, .ff# [.i]
        Remove bandwidth points between: .i, 0, .total
        Add bandwidth point: .i, .total / 2, .bw# [.i]
    endfor
    selectObject: .src, .fg
    .full = Filter
    # alternate the amplitude of successive cycles (shimmer)
    Formula: "self * (1 + " + string$(.shim) + " * cos(pi * " + string$(.f0) + " * (x - 0.005)))"
    .id = Extract part: 0.1, 0.1 + .dur, "rectangular", 1, "no"
    Subtract mean
    Scale peak: 0.5
    Rename: .name$
    removeObject: .pp, .train, .s1, .src, .fg, .full
    selectObject: .id
endproc
    
  
    
  
    The Python equivalent, used in the Parselmouth cells of this topic, issues exactly the same Praat commands through parselmouth.praat.call, so both give identical numbers:
    
  
    

    import parselmouth
from parselmouth.praat import call

def make_vowel(f0, f1, f2, f3, jit=0.0, shim=0.0, dur=1.0):
    """Same Praat commands as makevowel.praat. jit, shim: alternating
    period and amplitude perturbation (0.005 = +/- 0.5 %)."""
    total = dur + 0.2                      # 0.1 s spare at each end
    pp = call("Create empty PointProcess", "pulses", 0, total)
    t, k = 0.005, 0
    while t < total:
        call(pp, "Add point", t)
        t += (1 / f0) * (1 + jit * (1 - 2 * (k % 2)))
        k += 1
    train = call(pp, "To Sound (pulse train)", 44100, 1, 0.05, 2000)
    # two first-order low-pass stages: source slope of -12 dB per octave
    src = call(call(train, "Filter (de-emphasis)", 50), "Filter (de-emphasis)", 50)
    fg = call("Create FormantGrid", "fg", 0, total, 5, 500, 1000, 80, 50)
    for i, (f, b) in enumerate(zip((f1, f2, f3, 3400, 4000), (80, 90, 120, 150, 200)), 1):
        call(fg, "Remove formant points between", i, 0, total)
        call(fg, "Add formant point", i, total / 2, f)
        call(fg, "Remove bandwidth points between", i, 0, total)
        call(fg, "Add bandwidth point", i, total / 2, b)
    full = call([src, fg], "Filter")
    call(full, "Formula", f"self * (1 + {shim} * cos(pi * {f0} * (x - 0.005)))")
    vowel = call(full, "Extract part", 0.1, 0.1 + dur, "rectangular", 1, "no")
    call(vowel, "Subtract mean")
    call(vowel, "Scale peak", 0.5)
    return vowel
    
  
    

  
    Pitch methods and settings
    
  
    Select a Sound and open Analyse periodicity. Current Praat versions list four pitch commands: To Pitch (filtered autocorrelation)..., To Pitch (raw cross-correlation)..., To Pitch (raw autocorrelation)... and To Pitch (filtered cross-correlation)..., plus To Pitch (shs)... (subharmonic summation). Older 6.x versions showed To Pitch..., To Pitch (ac)... and To Pitch (cc)...; these names still work in scripts. The manual's advice is to use filtered autocorrelation for intonation and most uses involving vocal-fold vibration (Praat's preferred method since 2023), and raw cross-correlation for voice analysis, because filtering removes some of the noise that voice measures are meant to capture. Raw autocorrelation is the classic method of Boersma (1993) and is what the plain To Pitch... command and most published studies before 2023 used.
    
  
    
    
    SettingStandard value (raw autocorrelation)Meaning
    
    
      
    Time step (s)0 (automatic)Distance between frames. 0 means 0.75 / pitch floor, which is 0.01 s for a 75 Hz floor
    
      
    Pitch floor (Hz)75Lowest F0 searched. Also sets the window length (3 periods of the floor: 0.04 s at 75 Hz)
    
      
    Pitch ceiling (Hz)600Highest F0 searched. Candidates above it are ignored
    
      
    Max. number of candidates15How many autocorrelation peaks per frame are kept for the path finder
    
      
    Very accurateOffOn: Gaussian window of 6 floor periods instead of a Hann window of 3; slower, slightly more accurate
    
      
    Silence threshold0.03Frames whose peak amplitude is below this fraction of the global peak are treated as silent
    
      
    Voicing threshold0.45Minimum normalised autocorrelation for a frame to count as voiced. Raise it (for example 0.5 to 0.6) if noise is being called voiced; lower it for breathy voices that drop out
    
      
    Octave cost0.01 per octaveFavours higher-frequency candidates slightly, to reduce octave-down errors
    
      
    Octave-jump cost0.35Penalises large F0 jumps between frames; raise it to discourage spurious octave jumps
    
      
    Voiced/unvoiced cost0.14Penalises switching between voiced and unvoiced; raise it to reduce short voicing gaps
    
        
  
    
  
    For filtered autocorrelation the standard range is 50 to 800 Hz (the upper limit is called the pitch top, because the filtering attenuates gradually towards it). In the Sound window, the Pitch menu offers the method choice and its settings; in recent versions each method has its own settings dialog, while older 6.x versions have one Pitch settings... dialog with an analysis-method choice and an option to optimise for voice analysis.
    
  
    Choosing the floor and ceiling
    
  
    The floor and ceiling are the two settings that matter most. They should bracket the speaker's F0 range with some margin, but not so widely that half or double the true F0 becomes a plausible candidate.
    
  
    
    
    SpeakerTypical floor (Hz)Typical ceiling (Hz)Notes
    
    
      
    Adult male60 to 75250 to 300Lower the floor to about 50 Hz for creaky or very low voices
    
      
    Adult female100 to 120450 to 500Raise the ceiling for singing or high expressive speech
    
      
    Child (about 4 to 10 years)150 to 200600 to 700Young children and infant cries can exceed 600 Hz
    
      
    Unknown speaker, first pass75600Then refine; one common rule sets the floor near 0.75 times the first quartile and the ceiling near 1.5 times the third quartile of the first-pass values
    
        
  
    
  
    These ranges are typical starting points, not norms. Always look at the pitch contour on a narrowband spectrogram: the contour should follow the lowest harmonic line.
    

  
    How the autocorrelation algorithm works
    
  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.7d. Pitch analysis by autocorrelation (after Boersma, 1993). Steps 1 to 5 are done for each frame; step 6 chooses one candidate per frame for the whole utterance.
    
  
      
    
    1. Frame. The sound is cut into overlapping frames. Each frame must contain at least a few periods of the lowest F0 searched, so its length is set from the floor: three periods, 3 / 75 = 0.04 s for a 75 Hz floor.
      2. 
    
    2. Window. The local mean is removed and the frame is multiplied by a Hann window so that it fades in and out.
      3. 
    
    3. Autocorrelation. The frame is compared with delayed copies of itself. For a periodic signal the similarity is high when the delay (lag, τ) equals one period, two periods and so on.
      4. 
    
    4. Normalise. Windowing makes the autocorrelation fall off with lag, which would bias the method towards short lags (high F0). Dividing by the autocorrelation of the window itself removes this bias; this is the key refinement in Boersma's method. The result r(τ) is 1 at lag 0 and close to 1 at the period of a clean periodic signal.
      5. 
    
    5. Candidates. Peaks of r(τ) with lags between 1 / ceiling and 1 / floor become candidates, with their heights as strengths. An "unvoiced" candidate is always added; it wins when no peak exceeds the voicing threshold or when the frame is silent.
      6. 
    
    6. Path finder. A dynamic-programming (Viterbi) search picks one candidate per frame so that the total cost is lowest, where cost combines weak candidates, octave jumps between frames and voiced/unvoiced switches.
      7. 
    
    7. Pitch object. The result stores, for each frame, all candidates and the selected F0 (or unvoiced). You can edit the selection later in the Pitch editor.
      8. 
  
    
  
    r(tau) = r_a(tau) / r_w(tau)
R = r(tau) - OctaveCost * log2(PitchFloor * tau)
F0 = 1 / tau_peak
where r_a is the autocorrelation of the windowed frame, r_w that of the window, both normalised to 1 at lag 0; R is the strength used to compare candidates; tau_peak is the lag of the chosen peak in seconds (found with sub-sample interpolation).
    
  
    Worked example: floor 75 Hz and ceiling 600 Hz give a lag search range from 1 / 600 = 1.67 ms to 1 / 75 = 13.3 ms. A voice at 220 Hz has its period at 1 / 220 = 4.55 ms, inside the range, so the correct peak is found. If the ceiling is lowered to 200 Hz, the shortest lag searched becomes 1 / 200 = 5.0 ms. The true peak at 4.55 ms is now outside the range and the next peak, at two periods (9.09 ms), is chosen: F0 = 1 / 0.00909 = 110 Hz, an octave too low. If instead the floor is set above the speaker's F0 (for example 150 Hz for a voice at 100 Hz), the true lag (10 ms) exceeds 1 / 150 = 6.7 ms and no strong peak remains; the frames become unvoiced. The cell below confirms both cases on the test vowel.
    
  
    

    from makevowel import *
voice = make_vowel(220, 850, 1600, 2800)          # high-pitched voice
for floor, ceiling in [(75, 600), (75, 200), (150, 500)]:
    p = call(voice, "To Pitch", 0, floor, ceiling)
    print(f"220 Hz voice, floor {floor}, ceiling {ceiling}: mean F0 = "
          f"{call(p, 'Get mean', 0, 0, 'Hertz'):.1f} Hz")
low = make_vowel(100, 700, 1220, 2600)            # low-pitched voice
p = call(low, "To Pitch", 0, 150, 500)
print("100 Hz voice, floor 150: voiced frames =", call(p, "Count voiced frames"))
    

    220 Hz voice, floor 75, ceiling 600: mean F0 = 220.0 Hz
220 Hz voice, floor 75, ceiling 200: mean F0 = 110.0 Hz
220 Hz voice, floor 150, ceiling 500: mean F0 = 220.0 Hz
100 Hz voice, floor 150: voiced frames = 0
    
  
    
  
    

    include makevowel.praat
@makeVowel: "high", 220, 850, 1600, 2800, 0, 0, 1.0
voice = makeVowel.id
p1 = To Pitch: 0, 75, 600
m1 = Get mean: 0, 0, "Hertz"
selectObject: voice
p2 = To Pitch: 0, 75, 200
m2 = Get mean: 0, 0, "Hertz"
writeInfoLine: "Ceiling 600 Hz: ", fixed$(m1, 1), " Hz; ceiling 200 Hz: ", fixed$(m2, 1), " Hz"
    

    Ceiling 600 Hz: 220.0 Hz; ceiling 200 Hz: 110.0 Hz
    
  
    
  
    Real voices produce octave errors even with sensible settings: in creaky voice (irregular, low, sometimes alternating pulses), at voice onsets and offsets, in strongly breathy voices, and in diplophonia, where alternate cycles really do differ and the true period is ambiguous. The test vowel shows this too: when successive cycles alternate strongly in amplitude, the signal genuinely repeats only every two cycles and the algorithm may report half the F0. Such an "error" is sometimes a correct description of the signal, which is why a narrowband spectrogram and listening must always accompany the numbers.
    

  
    
    
    Try it: autocorrelation pitch detection and octave errors
    
    
    One analysis frame of a synthetic voiced signal (top) and its normalised autocorrelation (bottom). The shaded band is the lag range allowed by the floor and ceiling. Lower the ceiling below the true F0, or raise the floor above it, and watch the chosen peak jump.
    
    
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
    
    
    
    
    
    
      
    Frame length (3 / floor)
    
      
    Lag search range
    
      
    Chosen lag, r
    
      
    Estimated F0 = 1 / lag
    
      
    Verdict
    
    
    
    
    r(lag)allowed lag rangechosen peak
    
  
    

  
    Querying and cleaning a Pitch object
    
  
    With a Pitch object selected, the Query menu gives the numbers you report. Each asks for a time range (0 and 0 means the whole object) and a unit (Hertz, mel, semitones re 1 Hz, semitones re 100 Hz, ERB).
    
  
    
    
    QueryReturnsNotes
    
    
      
    Get mean...Mean F0 over voiced framesUnvoiced frames are ignored, so the mean is only meaningful if octave errors have been removed
    
      
    Get minimum... / Get maximum...Extreme F0 and (with Get time of...) when it occursChoose Parabolic interpolation. Extremes are the values most affected by tracking errors; check them on the contour
    
      
    Get standard deviation...F0 variabilityReport in semitones for comparisons between speakers of different F0
    
      
    Get quantile...For example the 5th and 95th percentilesA robust alternative to minimum and maximum for F0 range
    
      
    Count voiced framesNumber of voiced framesMultiply by the time step for voiced duration
    
      
    Get value at time...F0 at one instantReturns undefined in unvoiced frames
    
        
  
    
  
    ST = 12 * log2(F0 / Fref)
where ST is the F0 in semitones relative to a reference Fref (often 100 Hz). A rise from 100 to 200 Hz is 12 semitones (one octave), and so is a rise from 200 to 400 Hz.
    
  
    To list F0 frame by frame, use Pitch > Pitch listing in the Sound window, or with the Pitch object selected choose Down to PitchTier or Tabulate candidates (for all candidates). To clean a contour:
    
  
      
    
    1. Re-analyse with a better floor and ceiling. This fixes most errors.
      2. 
    
    2. The Pitch command Kill octave jumps creates a new Pitch in which sudden jumps of about an octave are moved back towards the local median.
      3. 
    
    3. View & Edit on the Pitch object opens the Pitch editor, which shows all candidates as numbers or dots in each frame. Click a candidate to select it instead of the current path, or select a stretch and choose Unvoice to remove it. Save the corrected Pitch as a text file and record what was changed.
      4. 
    
    4. The Pitch command Smooth... removes small frame-to-frame fluctuations for intonation displays. Never smooth before measuring F0 variability or jitter.
      5. 
  
    

  
    9. Intensity
    
  
    Praat treats the sample values of a Sound as sound pressure in pascals. The intensity of a sound or frame is then the mean squared pressure expressed in dB relative to the standard reference 2 × 10-5 Pa (20 µPa). This number is a true dB SPL only if the recording chain has been calibrated so that one unit in the file really equals one pascal at the microphone. Otherwise it is a relative level: useful for comparing parts of the same recording made with unchanged gain, but not an absolute measure of loudness.
    
  
    L = 10 * log10( mean(x^2) / (2e-5)^2 )     dB
where x are the sample values (assumed pascals) and 2e-5 Pa is the reference pressure. A sine of amplitude A has mean(x^2) = A^2 / 2.
    
  
    Worked example: a 1 kHz sine of amplitude 0.1 has RMS 0.1 / √2 = 0.0707 Pa and intensity 20 log10(0.0707 / 0.00002) = 70.97 dB. The cell confirms this and also checks a gliding tone created by formula (section 15), whose instantaneous frequency 200 + 800t Hz is 280 Hz at 0.1 s and 920 Hz at 0.9 s.
    
  
    

    import parselmouth
from parselmouth.praat import call
tone = call("Create Sound from formula", "tone", 1, 0, 1, 44100, "0.1*sin(2*pi*1000*x)")
print("RMS (Pa):", round(call(tone, "Get root-mean-square", 0, 0), 4))
print("Intensity (dB):", round(call(tone, "Get intensity (dB)"), 2))
glide = call("Create Sound from formula", "glide", 1, 0, 1, 44100,
             "0.1*sin(2*pi*(200*x + 400*x^2))")
p = call(glide, "To Pitch", 0, 75, 1000)
print("Glide f0 at 0.1 s and 0.9 s:", round(call(p,"Get value at time",0.1,"Hertz","linear")),
      round(call(p,"Get value at time",0.9,"Hertz","linear")))
    

    RMS (Pa): 0.0707
Intensity (dB): 70.97
Glide f0 at 0.1 s and 0.9 s: 280 920
    
  
    
  
    

    tone = Create Sound from formula: "tone", 1, 0, 1, 44100, "0.1 * sin(2 * pi * 1000 * x)"
rms = Get root-mean-square: 0, 0
db = Get intensity (dB)
glide = Create Sound from formula: "glide", 1, 0, 1, 44100, "0.1 * sin(2 * pi * (200 * x + 400 * x^2))"
pitch = To Pitch: 0, 75, 1000
fa = Get value at time: 0.1, "Hertz", "linear"
fb = Get value at time: 0.9, "Hertz", "linear"
writeInfoLine: "RMS (Pa): ", fixed$(rms, 4)
appendInfoLine: "Intensity (dB): ", fixed$(db, 2)
appendInfoLine: "Glide f0 at 0.1 s and 0.9 s: ", round(fa), " ", round(fb)
    

    RMS (Pa): 0.0707
Intensity (dB): 70.97
Glide f0 at 0.1 s and 0.9 s: 280 920
    
  
    
  
    The intensity contour
    
  
    Select a Sound and choose To Intensity.... The settings are Minimum pitch (Hz), standard 100, which sets the analysis window long enough (an effective duration of about 3.2 / minimum pitch, 32 ms for 100 Hz) to smooth out the ripple of individual glottal periods; Time step, 0 for automatic; and Subtract mean pressure, yes, which removes any DC offset. Use a minimum pitch equal to your pitch floor so that low voices do not produce a rippling contour.
    
  
    When averaging an intensity contour with Get mean..., Praat asks for an averaging method:
    
  
      
    
    • energy: converts each frame back to power, averages, then converts to dB. This is the physically correct mean level and matches the level of the whole sound.
      • 
    
    • dB: averages the dB values directly. This gives a lower value whenever the level varies, because loud frames count no more than quiet ones.
      • 
    
    • sones: averages perceived loudness, an approximation useful for perception work.
      • 
  
    
  
    Worked example: two equally long frames at 60 dB and 80 dB. The dB average is 70 dB. The energy average is 10 log10((106 + 108) / 2) = 77.0 dB, dominated by the louder frame. State the method in your report.
    
  
    Clinical linkTo report intensity in dB SPL, record a sound level calibrator (for example 94 dB SPL at 1 kHz) or a tone measured with a sound level meter at the speaker's mouth position, through the same microphone, preamplifier gain and sampling settings as the speaker, then compute the offset. If the calibrator reads 71.5 dB in Praat, add 94 - 71.5 = 22.5 dB to every intensity measured in that session. Changing any gain setting afterwards invalidates the offset. Mouth-to-microphone distance must also be fixed: halving the distance raises the level by about 6 dB.
    

  
    10. Formants
    
  
    Formants are the resonances of the vocal tract. In a spectrum they appear as peaks of the spectral envelope; F1 is related mainly to tongue height (high vowels such as /i/ and /u/ have low F1) and F2 to tongue advancement and lip rounding (front /i/ has high F2, back rounded /u/ has low F2). Praat estimates formants with linear predictive coding (LPC), which models each short frame as the output of an all-pole filter excited by a flat source; the poles of that filter are the formants. The theory of LPC is explained in 3.8; this section concentrates on the settings.
    
  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.7e. Formant analysis with Sound: To Formant (burg).... The maximum formant and the number of formants together decide how many poles must fit into the analysed band.
    
  
      
    
    1. Resample. The sound is resampled to twice the maximum formant, so the analysed band is exactly 0 to the maximum formant.
      2. 
    
    2. Pre-emphasis. A first-order filter boosts frequencies above 50 Hz by 6 dB per octave, compensating the falling source spectrum so that LPC fits all formants evenly.
      3. 
    
    3. Window. Each frame is multiplied by a Gaussian window whose nominal length is twice the "window length" setting (0.025 s gives a 50 ms window with about 25 ms effective duration).
      4. 
    
    4. Burg LPC. The Burg algorithm computes 2N prediction coefficients (N = number of formants). Each pair of complex-conjugate poles can represent one resonance.
      5. 
    
    5. Roots. The roots of the prediction polynomial give pole frequencies (formant frequencies) and pole radii (bandwidths). Poles below 50 Hz or within 50 Hz of the maximum are discarded.
      6. 
    
    6. Formant object. Frequencies and bandwidths are stored per frame, every quarter window length (6.25 ms by default), numbered from the lowest upwards.
      7. 
    
    7. Query. Get value at time... returns a formant at an instant; Get mean... averages over a time range; Formant listing in the Sound window prints all frames.
      8. 
  
    
  
    
    
    SettingStandard valueGuidance
    
    
      
    Time step (s)0 (auto: 25 % of window)Leave at 0
    
      
    Max. number of formants5Usually 5 for adults up to 5000 to 5500 Hz; may be 4 or 4.5 (Praat accepts half values) if fewer formants lie below the ceiling, for example for some back vowels
    
      
    Formant ceiling (maximum formant, Hz)5500About 5000 Hz for adult male speakers and 5500 Hz for adult female speakers (the manual's advice); higher, often 6500 to 8000 Hz, for young children, whose shorter vocal tracts have higher formants
    
      
    Window length (s)0.025Shorter windows follow fast transitions but are noisier; longer windows average across transitions
    
      
    Pre-emphasis from (Hz)50Leave at 50
    
        
  
    
  
    The ceiling and the number of formants must match: the model assumes N resonances between 0 Hz and the ceiling. With too few poles for the formants present, neighbouring formants merge (F1 and F2 of a back vowel are reported as one peak between them). With too many poles, or too low a ceiling, the extra poles fit noise or harmonics and a spurious formant appears. The cell shows both failures on the test vowel (true F1, F2, F3 = 700, 1220, 2600 Hz, with F4 and F5 at 3400 and 4000 Hz).
    
  
    

    from makevowel import *
v = make_vowel(120, 700, 1220, 2600)
for n, ceiling in [(3, 5000), (4, 5000), (5, 5000), (6, 5000), (5, 3000)]:
    fm = call(v, "To Formant (burg)", 0, n, ceiling, 0.025, 50)
    f = [call(fm, "Get value at time", i, 0.5, "hertz", "linear") for i in (1, 2, 3)]
    print(f"{n} formants up to {ceiling} Hz: F1 {f[0]:.0f}, F2 {f[1]:.0f}, F3 {f[2]:.0f}")
    

    3 formants up to 5000 Hz: F1 572, F2 1082, F3 2762
4 formants up to 5000 Hz: F1 666, F2 1164, F3 2915
5 formants up to 5000 Hz: F1 713, F2 1220, F3 2605
6 formants up to 5000 Hz: F1 81, F2 720, F3 1218
5 formants up to 3000 Hz: F1 198, F2 714, F3 1204
    
  
    
  
    Only the correct model (5 formants up to 5000 Hz, because this synthetic voice has five resonances below 5000 Hz) returns values within about 15 Hz of the truth. Too few poles pull F1 and F2 towards each other; too many, or too low a ceiling, produce a spurious low "F1" and shift the numbering so that the true F1 is reported as F2. In real recordings you rarely know the truth, so the check is visual: the formant dots must lie on the dark bands of a wideband spectrogram. A common research practice is to analyse each speaker with a small range of ceilings and choose the one that gives the smoothest, most plausible tracks.
    
  
    High F0 and formant accuracy
    
  
    LPC sees the vocal-tract envelope only through the harmonics, which sample it at multiples of F0. For low voices the samples are dense and the estimate is good; for high voices the samples are sparse and the estimate is pulled towards the nearest strong harmonic.
    
  
    

    from makevowel import *
for f0 in (100, 120, 220, 300):
    v = make_vowel(f0, 700, 1220, 2600)
    fm = call(v, "To Formant (burg)", 0, 5, 5500, 0.025, 50)
    f1, f2 = (call(fm, "Get value at time", i, 0.5, "hertz", "linear") for i in (1, 2))
    print(f"F0 {f0} Hz: F1 {f1:.0f} Hz, F2 {f2:.0f} Hz   (true 700, 1220)")
    

    F0 100 Hz: F1 699 Hz, F2 1218 Hz   (true 700, 1220)
F0 120 Hz: F1 708 Hz, F2 1216 Hz   (true 700, 1220)
F0 220 Hz: F1 666 Hz, F2 1237 Hz   (true 700, 1220)
F0 300 Hz: F1 579 Hz, F2 748 Hz   (true 700, 1220)
    
  
    
  
    At 300 Hz, typical of a young child, F1 is underestimated by more than 100 Hz and F2 is wrong altogether (748 Hz lies between the harmonics at 600 and 900 Hz). This is a genuine limitation of formant analysis of children's and high female voices, not a fault of Praat, and it matters directly for studies of vowel production by children with hearing loss or cochlear implants. Mitigations: check every measurement against the spectrogram, raise the ceiling for children, try fewer formants, measure several frames around the midpoint, and report the settings and any manual corrections.
    
  
    Measuring F1 and F2 at vowel midpoints
    
  
      
    
    1. Open the recording with View & Edit, zoom so that one word fills the window, and turn on Show spectrogram and Show formants. Set Formants > Formant settings... to the speaker's ceiling and 5 formants.
      2. 
    
    2. Identify the vowel: the stretch with clear formant bands and regular glottal striations between the consonant boundaries. Listen to it.
      3. 
    
    3. Select the whole vowel, read its duration, then click at the temporal midpoint (start + duration / 2), where the formants are least affected by neighbouring consonants.
      4. 
    
    4. Formants > Get first formant and Get second formant (or Formant listing for a selection of a few frames around the midpoint). Check that the dots at that point lie on the dark bands.
      5. 
    
    5. Record F1 and F2 in a table with the word, vowel, time and settings. For many tokens, label vowels in a TextGrid (section 16) and let a script do the midpoint measurement, as below.
      6. 
  
    
  
    Vowel space and the F1-F2 plot
    
  
    Plotting each vowel as a point with F2 on the horizontal axis and F1 on the vertical axis, both reversed (F2 decreasing to the right, F1 increasing downwards), reproduces the traditional vowel quadrilateral: /i/ top left, /u/ top right, /a/ at the bottom. The area of the triangle /i/-/a/-/u/ (the vowel space area) summarises how distinctly a speaker separates the corner vowels. Reduced vowel space area has been reported in many groups with reduced auditory feedback or motor speech difficulties, and it is a common outcome measure in studies of children with hearing loss and cochlear-implant users. Values depend strongly on language, speaker sex and age, speaking style and measurement method, so compare only with norms gathered in the same way.
    
  
    Area = | F2_i (F1_a - F1_u) + F2_a (F1_u - F1_i) + F2_u (F1_i - F1_a) | / 2
where F1_v and F2_v are the formants of corner vowel v; the result is in Hz squared (the shoelace formula for a triangle).
    
  
    Worked example with the synthesis targets /i/ (300, 2300), /a/ (700, 1220), /u/ (320, 870): |2300 × 380 + 1220 × 20 + 870 × (-400)| / 2 = |874 000 + 24 400 - 348 000| / 2 = 275 200 Hz2. The script below builds the three vowels, labels them in a TextGrid, measures each at its midpoint and computes the area from the measured values.
    
  
    

    include makevowel.praat
# 1. three synthetic vowels, 0.3 s each, joined into one Sound
@makeVowel: "i", 120, 300, 2300, 3000, 0, 0, 0.3
vi = makeVowel.id
@makeVowel: "a", 120, 700, 1220, 2600, 0, 0, 0.3
va = makeVowel.id
@makeVowel: "u", 120, 320, 870, 2250, 0, 0, 0.3
vu = makeVowel.id
selectObject: vi, va, vu
seq = Concatenate
# 2. a TextGrid with one interval tier, boundaries and labels
tg = To TextGrid: "vowel", ""
Insert boundary: 1, 0.3
Insert boundary: 1, 0.6
Set interval text: 1, 1, "i"
Set interval text: 1, 2, "a"
Set interval text: 1, 3, "u"
# 3. formant analysis and a loop over labelled intervals
selectObject: seq
formant = To Formant (burg): 0, 5, 5000, 0.025, 50
selectObject: tg
n = Get number of intervals: 1
writeInfoLine: "vowel", tab$, "F1", tab$, "F2"
for i to n
    selectObject: tg
    label$ = Get label of interval: 1, i
    t1 = Get start time of interval: 1, i
    t2 = Get end time of interval: 1, i
    mid = (t1 + t2) / 2
    selectObject: formant
    f1[i] = Get value at time: 1, mid, "hertz", "linear"
    f2[i] = Get value at time: 2, mid, "hertz", "linear"
    appendInfoLine: label$, tab$, fixed$(f1[i], 0), tab$, fixed$(f2[i], 0)
endfor
# 4. triangle area by the shoelace formula (Hz squared)
area = abs(f2[1] * (f1[2] - f1[3]) + f2[2] * (f1[3] - f1[1]) + f2[3] * (f1[1] - f1[2])) / 2
appendInfoLine: "Vowel space area: ", fixed$(area, 0), " Hz^2"
    

    vowel	F1	F2
i	279	2290
a	709	1216
u	327	846
Vowel space area: 284539 Hz^2
    
  
    
  
    

    from makevowel import *
targets = {"i": (300, 2300, 3000), "a": (700, 1220, 2600), "u": (320, 870, 2250)}
parts = [make_vowel(120, *f, dur=0.3) for f in targets.values()]
seq = call(parts, "Concatenate")
tg = call(seq, "To TextGrid", "vowel", "")
call(tg, "Insert boundary", 1, 0.3)
call(tg, "Insert boundary", 1, 0.6)
for i, lab in enumerate(targets, 1):
    call(tg, "Set interval text", 1, i, lab)
formant = call(seq, "To Formant (burg)", 0, 5, 5000, 0.025, 50)
F = {}
for i in range(1, call(tg, "Get number of intervals", 1) + 1):
    lab = call(tg, "Get label of interval", 1, i)
    mid = (call(tg, "Get start time of interval", 1, i) + call(tg, "Get end time of interval", 1, i)) / 2
    F[lab] = [call(formant, "Get value at time", k, mid, "hertz", "linear") for k in (1, 2)]
    print(lab, round(F[lab][0]), round(F[lab][1]))
(a1, a2), (b1, b2), (c1, c2) = F["i"], F["a"], F["u"]
area = abs(a2 * (b1 - c1) + b2 * (c1 - a1) + c2 * (a1 - b1)) / 2
print("Vowel space area: %.0f Hz^2" % area)
    

    i 279 2290
a 709 1216
u 327 846
Vowel space area: 284539 Hz^2
    
  
    
  
    The measured area (284 539 Hz2) is about 3 % larger than the target area, mostly because F2 of /u/ and F1 of /i/ are measured about 20 to 25 Hz from their targets. Small formant errors at the corners change the area noticeably, which is one reason vowel space areas from different studies are hard to compare.

    11. Voice quality: jitter, shimmer, HNR and CPPS
    
  
    Perturbation and noise measures describe how regular the voice is from one glottal cycle to the next. A healthy sustained vowel is nearly, but not perfectly, periodic: successive periods differ slightly in duration (jitter) and in amplitude (shimmer), and some turbulent noise from the glottis is mixed with the harmonics (measured by the harmonics-to-noise ratio, HNR). Vocal-fold lesions, paralysis, poor glottal closure and neurological disorders tend to increase jitter and shimmer and lower HNR. These measures are part of the acoustic voice assessment protocols used by speech-language pathologists and ENT clinics, and audiologists meet them in studies of voice in people with hearing loss and in cochlear-implant users.
    
  
    Getting a Voice report
    
  
      
    
    1. Record a sustained vowel /a/ at comfortable pitch and loudness for about 3 to 5 s, with the microphone at a fixed distance, in a quiet room (section 4).
      2. 
    
    2. Open it with View & Edit. Set the pitch analysis for voice work: raw cross-correlation in current versions (or cross-correlation with "optimize for voice analysis" in older ones), with a floor and ceiling suited to the speaker (for example 60 to 300 Hz for a man, 100 to 500 Hz for a woman).
      3. 
    
    3. Turn on Pulses > Show pulses. Blue vertical lines appear at the detected glottal pulses. Zoom in and check that there is one line per cycle, at the same phase of each cycle. Missing or extra pulses make every perturbation number meaningless.
      4. 
    
    4. Select a stable stretch in the middle of the vowel, typically 1 to 3 s, excluding the onset and offset where F0 and amplitude change systematically.
      5. 
    
    5. Pulses > Voice report. The Info window lists pitch statistics, pulses, voicing, jitter, shimmer and harmonicity for the selection. Copy the whole report into your records along with the settings.
      6. 
  
    
  
    The same report can be obtained without the editor: select the Sound, create a Pitch with To Pitch (raw cross-correlation)... (older name To Pitch (cc)...), select Sound and Pitch together and choose To PointProcess (cc), then select Sound, Pitch and PointProcess and choose Voice report.... The report below was produced this way from the test vowel with alternating perturbations of ±0.5 % in period and ±5 % in amplitude (so the nominal local jitter is 1 % and the nominal local shimmer 10 %).
    
  
    

    from makevowel import *
vowel = make_vowel(120, 700, 1220, 2600, jit=0.005, shim=0.05)
pitch = call(vowel, "To Pitch (cc)", 0, 75, 15, "no", 0.03, 0.45, 0.01, 0.35, 0.14, 600)
pulses = call([vowel, pitch], "To PointProcess (cc)")
report = call([vowel, pitch, pulses], "Voice report", 0, 0, 75, 600, 1.3, 1.6, 0.03, 0.45)
print(report)
    

       From 0 to 0 seconds (duration: 1.000000 seconds)
Pitch:
   Median pitch: 120.095 Hz
   Mean pitch: 119.973 Hz
   Standard deviation: 0.333 Hz
   Minimum pitch: 119.357 Hz
   Maximum pitch: 120.338 Hz
Pulses:
   Number of pulses: 119
   Number of periods: 118
   Mean period: 8.333333E-3 seconds
   Standard deviation of period: 0.043676E-3 seconds
Voicing:
   Fraction of locally unvoiced frames: 0   (0 / 293)
   Number of voice breaks: 0
   Degree of voice breaks: 0   (0 seconds / 0 seconds)
