    include makevowel.praat
@makeVowel: "a_120", 120, 700, 1220, 2600, 0.005, 0.05, 1.0
vowel = makeVowel.id
pitch = To Pitch (cc): 0, 75, 15, "no", 0.03, 0.45, 0.01, 0.35, 0.14, 600
selectObject: vowel, pitch
pulses = To PointProcess (cc)
selectObject: vowel, pitch, pulses
report$ = Voice report: 0, 0, 75, 600, 1.3, 1.6, 0.03, 0.45
writeInfoLine: report$
    
  
    
  
    The Praat script prints the same report (verified line for line). The Voice report arguments are the time range, pitch floor and ceiling, maximum period factor (1.3), maximum amplitude factor (1.6), silence threshold and voicing threshold.
    
  
    What each measure means
    
  
    
    
    MeasureDefinition (Praat manual, paraphrased)What it reflects
    
    
      
    Jitter (local)Mean absolute difference between consecutive periods, divided by the mean period, in %Very short-term cycle-to-cycle F0 irregularity
    
      
    Jitter (local, absolute)The same difference in seconds (not divided by the period)Depends on F0; compare only voices of similar F0
    
      
    Jitter (rap)Relative average perturbation: mean absolute difference between a period and the average of it and its two neighbours, divided by the mean periodIrregularity with slow F0 drift partly removed
    
      
    Jitter (ppq5)Five-point period perturbation quotient: as rap but with the average of the period and its four closest neighboursFurther smoothing of slow drift
    
      
    Jitter (ddp)Mean absolute difference of consecutive differences between periods, divided by the mean period; equals 3 × rapReported for comparison with other software
    
      
    Shimmer (local)Mean absolute difference between the peak amplitudes of consecutive periods, divided by the mean amplitude, in %Cycle-to-cycle amplitude irregularity
    
      
    Shimmer (local, dB)Mean absolute value of 20 log10 of the ratio of consecutive amplitudesThe same in dB
    
      
    Shimmer (apq3, apq5, apq11)Amplitude perturbation quotients over 3, 5 and 11 points: difference between a period's amplitude and the average of it and its neighboursLonger averages smooth slow amplitude changes (apq11 is the version most often compared with the MDVP program)
    
      
    Shimmer (dda)Mean absolute difference of consecutive amplitude differences; equals 3 × apq3For comparison with other software
    
      
    HNRRatio of the energy of the periodic part to the energy of the noise, in dB, from the autocorrelation peakAdditive noise (breathiness, turbulence) and irregularity
    
      
    Voice breaks, unvoiced framesGaps between pulses longer than 1.25 / pitch floor; fraction of frames judged unvoicedPhonation interruptions; high values invalidate perturbation measures
    
        
  
    
  
    jitter(local)   = [ (1/(N-1)) Σ |T_i - T_(i-1)| ] / [ (1/N) Σ T_i ]
jitter(rap)     = [ (1/(N-2)) Σ |T_i - (T_(i-1)+T_i+T_(i+1))/3| ] / mean(T)
shimmer(local)  = [ (1/(N-1)) Σ |A_i - A_(i-1)| ] / [ (1/N) Σ A_i ]
shimmer(dB)     = (1/(N-1)) Σ |20 log10(A_i / A_(i-1))|
HNR             = 10 log10( r / (1 - r) )     dB
where T_i is the duration of period i, A_i its peak amplitude, N the number of periods, and r the normalised autocorrelation at the period lag (the periodic fraction of the energy).
    
  
    Worked example: in the test vowel the periods alternate between 1.005 T and 0.995 T. Every consecutive difference is 0.01 T, so jitter (local) = 1 %. For rap, a long period (1.005 T) is compared with the average of itself and two short neighbours, (1.005 + 0.995 + 0.995) / 3 = 0.99833 T, a difference of 0.00667 T, so rap = 0.667 %. For ppq5 the five-point average is (3 × 1.005 + 2 × 0.995) / 5 = 1.001 T, a difference of 0.004 T, so ppq5 = 0.4 %. Praat measured 1.044 %, 0.696 % and 0.418 %: close to theory. The mean autocorrelation of 0.958 gives HNR = 10 log10(0.958 / 0.042) = 13.6 dB, matching the report. Shimmer was measured as 9.6 % against the nominal 10 %. This example also shows that a regular, fully periodic pattern of perturbation produces an HNR of only 13.6 dB although there is no noise at all: HNR is not a pure noise measure.
    
  
    PointProcess: the pulses behind the numbers
    
  
    Jitter and shimmer are computed from a PointProcess, a list of times of glottal pulses. To PointProcess (periodic, cc)... finds them by cross-correlating neighbouring cycles, guided by an internal pitch analysis with the given floor and ceiling; To PointProcess (periodic, peaks)... places them at waveform peaks. Queries on the PointProcess (Get jitter (local)..., Get jitter (rap)...) take five arguments: the time range, the shortest period (0.0001 s), the longest period (0.02 s) and the maximum period factor (1.3). Periods outside the shortest-longest range or differing from their neighbours by more than the period factor are skipped, which stops voice breaks and octave errors from inflating jitter. Shimmer queries need the Sound and the PointProcess selected together and add a maximum amplitude factor (1.6).
    
  
    

    from makevowel import *
vowel = make_vowel(120, 700, 1220, 2600, jit=0.005, shim=0.05)
pitch = call(vowel, "To Pitch", 0, 75, 600)
formant = call(vowel, "To Formant (burg)", 0, 5, 5000, 0.025, 50)
intensity = call(vowel, "To Intensity", 100, 0, "yes")
pulses = call(vowel, "To PointProcess (periodic, cc)", 75, 600)
harm = call(vowel, "To Harmonicity (cc)", 0.01, 75, 0.1, 1.0)
print("Mean F0: %.1f Hz" % call(pitch, "Get mean", 0, 0, "Hertz"))
print("F1, F2 at midpoint: %.0f, %.0f Hz" % tuple(
      call(formant, "Get value at time", i, 0.5, "hertz", "linear") for i in (1, 2)))
print("Mean intensity: %.1f dB" % call(intensity, "Get mean", 0, 0, "energy"))
print("Jitter (local): %.2f %%" % (100 * call(pulses, "Get jitter (local)", 0, 0, 0.0001, 0.02, 1.3)))
print("Shimmer (local): %.2f %%" % (100 * call([vowel, pulses], "Get shimmer (local)",
                                              0, 0, 0.0001, 0.02, 1.3, 1.6)))
print("HNR: %.1f dB" % call(harm, "Get mean", 0, 0))
    

    Mean F0: 120.0 Hz
F1, F2 at midpoint: 713, 1221 Hz
Mean intensity: 81.7 dB
Jitter (local): 1.04 %
Shimmer (local): 9.71 %
HNR: 13.6 dB
    
  
    
  
    

    include makevowel.praat
@makeVowel: "a_120", 120, 700, 1220, 2600, 0.005, 0.05, 1.0
vowel = makeVowel.id
pitch = To Pitch: 0, 75, 600
f0 = Get mean: 0, 0, "Hertz"
selectObject: vowel
formant = To Formant (burg): 0, 5, 5000, 0.025, 50
f1 = Get value at time: 1, 0.5, "hertz", "linear"
f2 = Get value at time: 2, 0.5, "hertz", "linear"
selectObject: vowel
intensity = To Intensity: 100, 0, "yes"
db = Get mean: 0, 0, "energy"
selectObject: vowel
pulses = To PointProcess (periodic, cc): 75, 600
jitter = Get jitter (local): 0, 0, 0.0001, 0.02, 1.3
selectObject: vowel, pulses
shimmer = Get shimmer (local): 0, 0, 0.0001, 0.02, 1.3, 1.6
selectObject: vowel
harm = To Harmonicity (cc): 0.01, 75, 0.1, 1.0
hnr = Get mean: 0, 0
writeInfoLine: "Mean F0: ", fixed$(f0, 1), " Hz"
appendInfoLine: "F1, F2 at midpoint: ", fixed$(f1, 0), ", ", fixed$(f2, 0), " Hz"
appendInfoLine: "Mean intensity: ", fixed$(db, 1), " dB"
appendInfoLine: "Jitter (local): ", fixed$(jitter * 100, 2), " %"
appendInfoLine: "Shimmer (local): ", fixed$(shimmer * 100, 2), " %"
appendInfoLine: "HNR: ", fixed$(hnr, 1), " dB"
    

    Mean F0: 120.0 Hz
F1, F2 at midpoint: 713, 1221 Hz
Mean intensity: 81.7 dB
Jitter (local): 1.04 %
Shimmer (local): 9.71 %
HNR: 13.6 dB
    
  
    
  
    The shimmer here (9.71 %) differs slightly from the Voice report (9.64 %) because the PointProcess was made directly from the Sound rather than from a separately computed Pitch; small differences of this kind between routes are normal and are one more reason to use the same route for all recordings in a study.
    
  
    Interpreting the numbers, with caveats
    
  
    The Praat manual lists threshold values that the commercial MDVP program uses as limits for pathology (for example 1.04 % for local jitter and 3.81 % for local shimmer) and immediately warns that these thresholds may be unreliable and depend on the measurement method. Use them only to understand orders of magnitude. For clinical or research decisions:
    
  
      
    
    • Use published norms for your population and protocol: the same vowel, loudness, pitch, recording distance, microphone type, sampling rate, software, version and settings. Values from MDVP, Praat and other programs are not interchangeable.
      • 
    
    • Perturbation measures are valid only for nearly periodic signals. Titze's widely used signal typing distinguishes nearly periodic signals (type 1), signals with subharmonics or bifurcations (type 2) and aperiodic signals (type 3); jitter and shimmer are meaningful only for type 1. For severely dysphonic voices, spectral and cepstral measures such as CPPS, and perceptual ratings, are more appropriate.
      • 
    
    • Background noise, room reverberation, low recording level, clipping, lossy compression and automatic gain control all change shimmer and HNR more than they change F0.
      • 
    
    • Jitter and shimmer vary with F0 and loudness within the same healthy speaker. Instruct pitch and loudness consistently before and after therapy.
      • 
    
    • Report several repetitions and their mean or median rather than one token.
      • 
  
    
  
    SafetyAcoustic voice measures support, and do not replace, laryngeal visualisation and perceptual assessment. A "normal" jitter value does not exclude a lesion, and an abnormal one does not diagnose one. Refer any persistent hoarseness (commonly taken as longer than a few weeks) for laryngeal examination.
    

  
    
    
    Try it: jitter and shimmer calculator
    
    
    A synthetic pulse train with random (or alternating) perturbation of period and amplitude. The top plot shows the first cycles of the waveform; the lower plots show every period and every peak amplitude. The readouts apply the Praat formulas to these sequences.
    
    
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
    
    
    
    
    
    
    
      
    Jitter (local)
    
      
    Jitter (local, absolute)
    
      
    Jitter (rap)
    
      
    Shimmer (local)
    
      
    Shimmer (local, dB)
    
      
    Shimmer (apq3)
    
    
    
    
    waveform / periodsamplitudes
    
  
    
  
    With random Gaussian perturbation of standard deviation s (as a fraction of the period), consecutive differences have a standard deviation of s√2, and their mean absolute value is s√2 × √(2/π), about 1.13 s. So a 0.5 % random perturbation gives a local jitter of about 0.56 %, while a 0.5 % alternating perturbation gives exactly 1 %. The same number of "irregularity" produces different jitter depending on its pattern, and rap and ppq5 react differently again: this is why several measures exist.
    

  
    Cepstral peak prominence (CPP and CPPS)
    
  
    The cepstrum is the spectrum of the log spectrum (theory in 3.9). A periodic voice has regularly spaced harmonics, so its log spectrum ripples at a regular spacing F0, and its cepstrum has a clear peak at the quefrency 1 / F0. The height of that peak above a regression line through the cepstrum is the cepstral peak prominence (CPP). Noise and irregularity flatten the harmonic ripple and lower the peak. CPPS is a smoothed version averaged across time and quefrency. Unlike jitter and shimmer, CPP does not need individual cycles to be identified, so it can be computed on connected speech and on strongly dysphonic voices, and it has been recommended as a core acoustic measure in expert protocols for instrumental voice assessment.
    
  
    In Praat, select a Sound and choose To PowerCepstrogram... (pitch floor 60 Hz, time step 0.002 s, maximum frequency 5000 Hz, pre-emphasis from 50 Hz), then with the PowerCepstrogram selected choose Get CPPS.... The arguments of Get CPPS have changed between Praat versions, so fill in the dialog once and use the script editor's Paste history (section 18) to obtain the exact call for your version. The cell uses the Praat 6.1 form.
    
  
    

    from makevowel import *
v = make_vowel(120, 700, 1220, 2600)
def cpps(snd):
    pc = call(snd, "To PowerCepstrogram", 60, 0.002, 5000, 50)
    return call(pc, "Get CPPS", "yes", 0.02, 0.0005, 60, 330, 0.05,
                "Parabolic", 0.001, 0.05, "Exponential decay", "Robust")
def hnr(snd):
    return call(call(snd, "To Harmonicity (cc)", 0.01, 75, 0.1, 1.0), "Get mean", 0, 0)
print("Clean vowel: CPPS %.0f dB, HNR %.0f dB" % (cpps(v), hnr(v)))
noisy = call(v, "Copy", "noisy")
call(noisy, "Formula", "self + randomGauss(0, 0.05)")   # add breath-like noise
print("Noisy vowel: CPPS about %.0f dB, HNR about %.0f dB" % (cpps(noisy), hnr(noisy)))
    

    Clean vowel: CPPS 24 dB, HNR 96 dB
Noisy vowel: CPPS about 14 dB, HNR about 15 dB
    
  
    
  
    The clean synthetic vowel has an extremely high HNR (96 dB, which no voice produces) because it is perfectly periodic; adding white noise at a signal-to-noise ratio of about 14 dB brings HNR down to about 15 dB and CPPS from 24 to about 14 dB. The noise is random, so repeated runs vary in the last digit. The Acoustic Voice Quality Index (AVQI) combines CPPS, HNR, shimmer and spectral slope measures from sustained vowels and connected speech into one score; it is distributed as a Praat script by its authors (Maryn and colleagues) and is an example of how published Praat scripts become clinical tools.
    

  
    12. Spectral analysis: spectrum, slices, LTAS and spectral moments
    
  
    A spectrogram shows spectra changing over time. Often one needs a single spectrum: of a vowel, a fricative, a noise, or the long-term average of a whole speech recording.
    
  
    Spectrum of a sound or a slice
    
  
      
    
    1. For a whole (short) Sound: select it and choose Analyse spectrum > To Spectrum... (Fast: yes). A Spectrum object appears. View & Edit shows it; Draw... draws it into the Picture window. The spectrum is computed over the whole object, so extract the part of interest first.
      2. 
    
    2. For one moment in the Sound window: place the cursor (or select a short stretch) and choose Spectrogram > View spectral slice (named Spectrum > View spectral slice in some versions). A Spectrum object of that window is created and shown.
      3. 
    
    3. Queries on a Spectrum: Get centre of gravity..., Get standard deviation..., Get skewness..., Get kurtosis..., Get band energy..., Get band density.... The argument "power" is usually 2 (power spectrum).
      4. 
  
    
  
    centroid = Σ f |S(f)|^p / Σ |S(f)|^p
SD = sqrt( Σ (f - centroid)^2 |S(f)|^p / Σ |S(f)|^p )
where S(f) is the spectrum and p the power (2 for the power spectrum). Skewness and kurtosis are the normalised third and fourth moments.
    
  
    These spectral moments are standard measures of fricatives: /s/ has a higher centroid than /ʃ/, and reduced /s/-/ʃ/ contrast is a known feature in the speech of some children with hearing loss, whose access to high frequencies is limited. Measure them on the middle 20 to 40 ms of the fricative, with a high enough sampling rate (at least 22 050 Hz, preferably 44 100 Hz) and with any low-frequency noise filtered out.
    
  
    Long-term average spectrum (LTAS)
    
  
    An LTAS averages the power spectrum over a long stretch of sound, here in frequency bins of a chosen bandwidth. Select a Sound and choose Analyse spectrum > To Ltas... (bandwidth, for example 100 Hz). Ltas queries include Get mean... (over a frequency range, in dB), Get maximum... and Get frequency of maximum.... To Ltas (pitch-corrected)... is a variant for voice that reduces the influence of individual harmonics.
    
  
    

    from makevowel import *
v = make_vowel(120, 700, 1220, 2600)
spec = call(v, "To Spectrum", "yes")
print("Vowel centre of gravity: %.0f Hz" % call(spec, "Get centre of gravity", 2))
print("Vowel spectral SD: %.0f Hz" % call(spec, "Get standard deviation", 2))
noise = call("Create Sound from formula", "noise", 1, 0, 2, 44100, "randomGauss(0, 0.1)")
ns = call(noise, "To Spectrum", "yes")
print("White noise (0 to 22050 Hz) centre of gravity: about %d Hz" % round(call(ns, "Get centre of gravity", 2), -2))
ltas = call(v, "To Ltas", 100)
for lo, hi in [(0, 1000), (1000, 2000), (2000, 4000)]:
    print(f"LTAS mean {lo} to {hi} Hz: {call(ltas, 'Get mean', lo, hi, 'energy'):.1f} dB")
    

    Vowel centre of gravity: 261 Hz
Vowel spectral SD: 254 Hz
White noise (0 to 22050 Hz) centre of gravity: about 11000 Hz
LTAS mean 0 to 1000 Hz: 52.3 dB
LTAS mean 1000 to 2000 Hz: 33.6 dB
LTAS mean 2000 to 4000 Hz: 6.1 dB
    
  
    
  
    White noise has a flat spectrum, so its centroid lies in the middle of the 0 to 22 050 Hz band. The vowel's centroid is low (261 Hz) because the power of a vowel is dominated by its lowest harmonics and F1, and its LTAS falls steeply with frequency. Real connected speech has a less steep long-term spectrum than this steady synthetic vowel, because consonants add high-frequency energy.
    
  
    Clinical linkSpeech-in-noise tests usually use a masker whose long-term spectrum matches that of the speech material (speech-shaped noise), so that the signal-to-noise ratio is the same in every frequency region. With Praat: compute the Ltas of all sentences concatenated, compute the Ltas of the candidate noise with the same bandwidth, and compare them band by band (Get mean... for each band, or draw both Ltas objects in one Picture). Adjust the noise with Filter (formula)... until the difference is within the tolerance your protocol sets (often around 1 to 2 dB per band), then set the overall levels (section 15). The same comparison verifies that a recorded test list has not been altered by lossy compression or by an audio interface's processing.
    

  
    13. Manipulation and resynthesis
    
  
    Praat can change the F0 contour and the timing of recorded speech while keeping the voice quality and articulation largely intact. This is how perception experiments create stimuli that differ in one acoustic cue only: a question-statement continuum differing only in the final F0 rise, a vowel-length continuum, or sentences with flattened intonation to test how much listeners with cochlear implants rely on F0.
    
  
    The method is pitch-synchronous overlap-add (PSOLA). Praat marks each glottal pulse, cuts the signal into two-period, Hann-windowed segments centred on the pulses, and adds them back at new pulse positions. Moving the segments closer together raises F0; repeating or omitting segments changes duration without changing F0. Because each segment keeps its own spectral envelope, the formants stay where they were. Changes of up to roughly half an octave usually sound natural; larger changes and changes applied to breathy or creaky voices produce audible artefacts.
    
  
      
    
    1. Select the Sound and choose Manipulate > To Manipulation... (time step 0.01 s, minimum pitch 75 Hz, maximum pitch 600 Hz; set these to the speaker's range as for pitch analysis). A Manipulation object appears.
      2. 
    
    2. View & Edit opens the Manipulation editor: the waveform with pulses at the top, the pitch tier (large dots, F0 targets) in the middle, the duration tier at the bottom.
      3. 
    
    3. Simplify the contour with Pitch > Stylize pitch (2 st), which keeps only the points needed to stay within 2 semitones of the original. Drag points to new frequencies, add points with Pitch > Add pitch point at cursor, or remove them.
      4. 
    
    4. To change duration, add points to the duration tier: a value of 1.5 over a region makes it 1.5 times longer; 0.8 makes it shorter.
      5. 
    
    5. Listen with the play bars (they play the resynthesis). When satisfied, File > Publish resynthesis puts the new Sound in the Objects window; or, in the Objects window, select the Manipulation and choose Get resynthesis (overlap-add).
      6. 
    
    6. Save the new Sound as WAV and check its F0 and duration with the methods above. Stimuli are always verified by measurement, not assumed.
      7. 
  
    
  
    For systematic stimulus sets, scripts are faster and exactly repeatable: Extract pitch tier, modify it (Multiply frequencies..., Shift frequencies... in Hz or semitones, or Remove points between... and Add point... for a new contour), Replace pitch tier, and the same for duration tiers.
    
  
    

    from makevowel import *
v = make_vowel(120, 700, 1220, 2600)
man = call(v, "To Manipulation", 0.01, 75, 600)
tier = call(man, "Extract pitch tier")
call(tier, "Multiply frequencies", 0, 1, 1.25)          # raise f0 by 25 %
call([man, tier], "Replace pitch tier")
dt = call("Create DurationTier", "slow", 0, 1)
call(dt, "Add point", 0.5, 1.5)                        # 1.5 times longer
call([man, dt], "Replace duration tier")
new = call(man, "Get resynthesis (overlap-add)")
p = call(new, "To Pitch", 0, 75, 600)
print("New mean F0: %.1f Hz" % call(p, "Get mean", 0, 0, "Hertz"))
print("New duration: %.3f s" % call(new, "Get total duration"))
fm = call(new, "To Formant (burg)", 0, 5, 5000, 0.025, 50)
mid = call(new, "Get total duration") / 2
print("F1, F2 after resynthesis: %.0f, %.0f Hz" % tuple(
      call(fm, "Get value at time", i, mid, "hertz", "linear") for i in (1, 2)))
    

    New mean F0: 150.0 Hz
New duration: 1.500 s
F1, F2 after resynthesis: 727, 1220 Hz
    
  
    
  
    

    include makevowel.praat
@makeVowel: "a", 120, 700, 1220, 2600, 0, 0, 1.0
vowel = makeVowel.id
manip = To Manipulation: 0.01, 75, 600
tier = Extract pitch tier
Multiply frequencies: 0, 1, 1.25
selectObject: manip, tier
Replace pitch tier
dt = Create DurationTier: "slow", 0, 1
Add point: 0.5, 1.5
selectObject: manip, dt
Replace duration tier
selectObject: manip
new = Get resynthesis (overlap-add)
dur = Get total duration
pitch = To Pitch: 0, 75, 600
f0 = Get mean: 0, 0, "Hertz"
writeInfoLine: "New mean F0: ", fixed$(f0, 1), " Hz"
appendInfoLine: "New duration: ", fixed$(dur, 3), " s"
    

    New mean F0: 150.0 Hz
New duration: 1.500 s
    
  
    
  
    F0 rose exactly from 120 to 150 Hz and duration from 1.0 to 1.5 s, while F1 and F2 stayed within about 25 Hz of their original values: the formants belong to the segments, not to the pulse rate. To make an F0 continuum, put this in a loop with factors such as 1.00, 1.05, 1.10 ... and save each result under a systematic file name.
    

  
    14. Filtering
    
  
    Praat's filters work on the whole sound in the frequency domain: the sound is Fourier-transformed, the spectrum is multiplied by a filter shape, and the result is transformed back. They have zero phase shift and very steep, precisely defined edges, which suits stimulus preparation and analysis. They are not real-time filters of the kind in a hearing aid; filter theory is in 1.8. All are under the Filter button of a selected Sound and create a new Sound.
    
  
    
    
    CommandSettingsTypical use
    
    
      
    Filter (pass Hann band)...From (Hz), To (Hz), Smoothing (Hz): the width of the raised-cosine transition at each edge. To = 0 means no upper limitBand-limit speech (for example 300 to 3400 Hz to simulate a telephone), high-pass at 60 to 80 Hz to remove rumble, create low-pass filtered speech for perception tests
    
      
    Filter (stop Hann band)...From, To, SmoothingRemove mains hum at 50 Hz (India, Europe) or 60 Hz (Americas) and its harmonics
    
      
    Filter (formula)...A formula in which x is frequency in Hz and self the spectral valueAny custom shape: brick-wall bands, spectral tilts, notches
    
      
    Filter (pre-emphasis)... / (de-emphasis)...Frequency above which +6 or -6 dB per octave is appliedSpectral tilt; the test vowel uses de-emphasis to shape its source
    
      
    Filter (one formant)...Frequency and bandwidth of a single resonanceSimple resonance synthesis
    
        
  
    
  
    

    from makevowel import *
s = call("Create Sound from formula", "hum", 1, 0, 2, 44100,
         "0.1*sin(2*pi*1000*x) + 0.05*sin(2*pi*50*x)")
def level(snd, lo, hi):      # intensity of one band, dB re 20 uPa
    return call(call(snd, "Filter (pass Hann band)", lo, hi, 10), "Get intensity (dB)")
clean = call(s, "Filter (stop Hann band)", 40, 60, 10)
print("Whole signal: %.1f dB" % call(s, "Get intensity (dB)"))
print("50 Hz band  before %.1f dB, after %.1f dB" % (level(s, 30, 70), level(clean, 30, 70)))
print("1 kHz band  before %.1f dB, after %.1f dB" % (level(s, 900, 1100), level(clean, 900, 1100)))
    

    Whole signal: 71.9 dB
50 Hz band  before 64.9 dB, after 36.1 dB
1 kHz band  before 71.0 dB, after 71.0 dB
    
  
    
  
    

    s = Create Sound from formula: "hum", 1, 0, 2, 44100, "0.1*sin(2*pi*1000*x) + 0.05*sin(2*pi*50*x)"
clean = Filter (stop Hann band): 40, 60, 10
procedure level: .snd, .lo, .hi
    selectObject: .snd
    .band = Filter (pass Hann band): .lo, .hi, 10
    .db = Get intensity (dB)
    removeObject: .band
endproc
@level: s, 30, 70
before = level.db
@level: clean, 30, 70
after = level.db
writeInfoLine: "50 Hz band before ", fixed$(before, 1), " dB, after ", fixed$(after, 1), " dB"
    

    50 Hz band before 64.9 dB, after 36.1 dB
    
  
    
  
    The hum component was reduced by about 29 dB while the 1 kHz tone was unchanged. The attenuation is finite because the hum starts and stops abruptly at the ends of the file, which spreads a little of its energy outside the stop band. For a real recording, check the spectrum (section 12) for hum harmonics at 100, 150 and 200 Hz and add stop bands for those as well; better still, remove the cause (ground loops, unbalanced cables, mains-powered laptops) before recording.
    
  
    Common errorFilter (formula)... is applied to the spectrum, so in its formula x means frequency, not time. The formula if x < 500 or x > 1000 then 0 else self fi keeps only 500 to 1000 Hz. In Create Sound from formula... and Formula... on a Sound, x means time in seconds.
    

  
    15. Creating, scaling and joining sounds
    
  
    Many audiology tasks need synthetic sounds: a calibration tone, a warble or glide, a noise band, a click. Praat creates these with New > Sound > Create Sound from formula... (and ready-made commands such as Create Sound as pure tone... and Create Sound as tone complex...). The dialog asks for a name, number of channels, start and end time, sampling frequency and a formula that gives the sample value at each time x.
    
  
    
    
    FormulaResult
    
    
      
    0.1 * sin(2 * pi * 1000 * x)1 kHz sine, amplitude 0.1 (70.97 dB as computed in section 9)
    
      
    randomGauss(0, 0.02)White Gaussian noise with RMS 0.02 (about 60 dB)
    
      
    randomUniform(-0.1, 0.1)White noise with a uniform amplitude distribution
    
      
    0.1 * sin(2 * pi * (200 * x + 400 * x^2))Linear glide whose frequency rises from 200 Hz to 1000 Hz over 1 s (instantaneous frequency 200 + 800x)
    
      
    0.1 * sin(2 * pi * 1000 * x) * (1 + 0.5 * sin(2 * pi * 40 * x))1 kHz tone amplitude-modulated at 40 Hz, 50 % depth (the kind of stimulus used for auditory steady-state responses)
    
      
    0.1 * sin(2 * pi * (1000 * x + (50 / (2 * pi * 10)) * sin(2 * pi * 10 * x)))Warble (frequency-modulated) tone: 1 kHz carrier, ±50 Hz deviation at a 10 Hz rate (a pitch track of it runs from 950 to 1050 Hz)
    
      
    if x < 0.0001 then 1 else 0 fiA 0.1 ms rectangular click at the start
    
        
  
    
  
    Formula syntax: x is time in seconds; pi, e; functions sin, cos, exp, ln, log10, sqrt, abs, round, floor; powers with ^; conditions with if ... then ... else ... fi; and self, the current value, when modifying an existing Sound with Modify > Formula.... Ramps avoid clicks: multiply by a raised-cosine window, for example with Modify > Fade in... and Fade out... in versions that offer them, or with a formula.
    
  
    Scaling
    
  
      
    
    • Modify > Scale peak... sets the largest absolute sample to a given value (for example 0.99 before saving, to use the full range without clipping).
      • 
    
    • Modify > Scale intensity... sets the RMS so that the intensity equals the given dB value. This is the right tool for equalising the levels of test items and for setting signal-to-noise ratios.
      • 
    
    • Modify > Multiply... multiplies all samples by a factor (× 2 is +6.02 dB).
      • 
  
    
  
    To create a speech-in-noise item at +5 dB SNR: scale the speech to 65 dB, scale the noise to 60 dB (same duration), and add the noise to a copy of the speech with Formula... self + object[noise] (where noise is the noise object's ID). Then check that the peak of the mixture stays below 1, lowering both by the same amount if not. The level of the mixture is the power sum, 10 log10(106.5 + 106.0) = 66.2 dB, which the verified script below confirms.
    
  
    

    include makevowel.praat
@makeVowel: "speech", 120, 700, 1220, 2600, 0, 0, 2.0
speech = makeVowel.id
Scale intensity: 65
white = Create Sound from formula: "white", 1, 0, 2, 44100, "randomGauss(0, 1)"
noise = Filter (formula): "if x < 100 or x > 8000 then 0 else self fi"
Scale intensity: 60
selectObject: speech
mix = Copy: "mix_snr5"
Formula: "self + object[noise]"
db_mix = Get intensity (dB)
selectObject: noise
spec = To Spectrum: "yes"
cog = Get centre of gravity: 2
writeInfoLine: "Speech 65.0 dB + noise 60.0 dB: mix ", fixed$(db_mix, 1), " dB"
appendInfoLine: "Noise centre of gravity: about ", round(cog / 100) * 100, " Hz"
    

    Speech 65.0 dB + noise 60.0 dB: mix 66.2 dB
Noise centre of gravity: about 4000 Hz
    
  
    
  
    Note that Filter (formula)... returns a new object: the script keeps the filtered result in the variable noise, not the unfiltered white noise. Forgetting which object a command returns is the most common scripting mistake.
    
  
    Joining sounds
    
  
      
    
    • Select several Sounds (same sampling frequency and number of channels) in the order wanted and choose Combine > Concatenate. Concatenate recoverably also makes a TextGrid with one interval per original sound, so the parts can be found again. Concatenate with overlap... cross-fades the joins.
      • 
    
    • To insert silence, create a Sound from formula 0 of the required duration and include it in the concatenation, for example 1 s gaps between test words.
      • 
    
    • Combine > Combine to stereo makes a two-channel Sound from two mono Sounds, for example target speech in one ear and a competing message in the other for a dichotic test.
      • 
    
    • Convert > Resample... changes the sampling frequency (for example to 16 000 Hz for a speech recogniser or to 44 100 Hz for an audiometer's playback software). Resampling down includes the necessary low-pass filtering.
      • 
  
    

  
    16. TextGrid annotation
    
  
    A TextGrid is Praat's annotation object: a set of named tiers aligned to the time axis of a Sound. An interval tier divides time into consecutive intervals, each with a label (possibly empty), for words, syllables or phonemes. A point tier holds labelled instants, for events such as a plosive release, an F0 peak or a click. The TextGrid is stored separately from the sound, as a small text file, so the same recording can carry several independent annotations.
    
  
      
    
    1. Select the Sound and choose Annotate > To TextGrid.... In "All tier names" type the tier names separated by spaces, for example words phones events; in "Which of these are point tiers?" type events. A TextGrid object with the same name appears.
      2. 
    
    2. Select the Sound and the TextGrid together and click View & Edit. The TextGrid editor shows the waveform and spectrogram above the tiers, with a text field at the top.
      3. 
    
    3. Add a boundary: click in the waveform at the right time, then click the small circle that appears at the top of the tier (or press Enter to add a boundary on the selected tier). Boundaries can be dragged; select one and use Boundary > Remove (or Alt-Backspace) to delete it.
      4. 
    
    4. Label: click inside an interval and type the label in the text field at the top. For IPA symbols, Praat uses backslash codes (for example \as for ɑ, \sh for ʃ); the Help menu lists them. Plain orthography or SAMPA is simpler for scripting.
      5. 
    
    5. Place boundaries at acoustic landmarks: vowel onset and offset at the start and end of clear formant structure and regular striations; fricative boundaries at the start and end of high-frequency noise. Zoom in and use the spectrogram, not only the waveform. Write the segmentation rules down so that a second annotator can apply them for reliability checks.
      6. 
    
    6. Save: File > Save TextGrid as text file... in the editor, or Save > Save as text file... in the Objects window, with the same base name as the sound.
      7. 
  
    
  
    Queries for scripting, all on the TextGrid object (Query menu): Get number of intervals..., Get start time of interval..., Get end time of interval..., Get label of interval..., Get interval at time..., and for point tiers Get number of points..., Get time of point..., Get label of point.... The vowel-space script in section 10 shows the typical loop: for each interval with a label, find its midpoint and query an analysis object there. Tier and interval numbers start at 1.
    
  
    Key ideaAutomatic tools can create TextGrids for you. Forced aligners take a recording and its transcript and place word and phoneme boundaries automatically, writing the result as a TextGrid; Praat's own Annotate menu also offers silence detection (To TextGrid (silences)...), which marks sounding and silent intervals and is useful for cutting long recordings of word lists into items. Automatic boundaries must be checked by eye and ear, particularly for disordered speech, children's speech and the speech of people with hearing loss, on which aligners trained on typical adult speech are less accurate.
    

  
    17. Publication figures with the Picture window
    
  
    Screenshots of the Sound window are acceptable in lab notes but not in theses or papers: they are low resolution, include interface clutter and cannot be edited. The Picture window draws vector graphics with proper axes, which can be saved as PDF (for print) or high-resolution PNG (for slides and word processors).
    
  
      
    
    1. Open the Picture window (Praat > Show Picture window if it is closed). Edit > Erase all clears it.
      2. 
    
    2. Select a viewport by dragging with the mouse over the inch grid, for example 6 inches wide by 3 inches high. The pink rectangle shows where the next drawing goes. Select > Select outer viewport... sets it numerically, which keeps several figures identical in size.
      3. 
    
    3. In the Objects window select the Sound and choose Draw > Draw.... Give the time range (0 and 0 for all), vertical range (0 and 0 for automatic), tick Garnish (adds axes, labels and a box) and choose Curve as the drawing method. The waveform appears in the viewport.
      4. 
    
    4. Select a new viewport below it. Create a Spectrogram object (Analyse spectrum > To Spectrogram..., with the same settings as in section 7), then choose Paint... with Garnish. For a pitch contour on top of the spectrogram, draw the Pitch with Garnish off into the same viewport after setting Pen > colour and line width, then add a right-hand axis with Marks right every... and Text right....
      5. 
    
    5. From an editor, File > Draw visible sound..., Paint visible spectrogram..., Draw visible pitch contour... and Draw visible formant contour... send exactly what is on screen to the Picture window.
      6. 
    
    6. Add labels with World > Text... or Margins > Text top... and set the font and size in the Font menu (for example Times or Helvetica, 10 to 12 points).
      7. 
    
    7. Select the outer viewport enclosing all panels, then File > Save as PDF file... (vector, best for print) or Save as 300-dpi PNG file... (raster, for slides). Some versions offer 600-dpi PNG and EPS as well.
      8. 
  
    
  
    Because each drawing command can be pasted into a script (section 18), figures for all participants can be produced identically. Record the drawing commands with the analysis scripts so that a figure can be regenerated when a reviewer asks for a change.
    

  
    18. Scripting from the basics
    
  
    A Praat script is a text file of commands, written almost exactly as they appear on buttons and in dialogs. Anything you can click, you can script. Scripts matter for three reasons: they save time when there are many files; they guarantee that every file is analysed with identical settings; and the script itself documents the method, which is what examiners and journals increasingly ask for.
    
  
    Getting started
    
  
      
    
    1. Praat > New Praat script opens a script editor (a plain text window with File, Edit, Search, Convert, Font, Run and Help menus).
      2. 
    
    2. Do an analysis by hand in the Objects window: select a Sound, choose To Pitch..., click OK, then Get mean....
      3. 
    
    3. In the script editor choose Edit > Paste history. The commands you just clicked appear as script lines, with every dialog setting filled in, for example To Pitch: 0, 75, 600. Edit > Clear history empties the history first so that only the steps you want are pasted.
      4. 
    
    4. Run > Run (Ctrl+R, Command+R on macOS) runs the whole script; Run > Run selection runs only the selected lines, which is useful while developing.
      5. 
    
    5. File > Save as... saves the script as a .praat file. Keep scripts with the data they analysed.
      6. 
  
    
  
    The language in ten rules
    
  
    
    
    ElementSyntaxExample
    
    
      
    CommandsButton text, then a colon, then arguments separated by commas. Commands without arguments have no colonTo Pitch: 0, 75, 600; Play
    
      
    Numeric variablesStart with a lower-case letterfloor = 75
    
      
    String variablesEnd with $; strings in double quotes; join with +name$ = "p01" + "_a"
    
      
    Object IDsA command that creates an object returns its ID; store itpitch = To Pitch: 0, 75, 600
    
      
    SelectingselectObject, plusObject, minusObject, removeObjectselectObject: sound, pulses
    
      
    QueriesA query command assigned to a variable returns its number (or string with $)f0 = Get mean: 0, 0, "Hertz"
    
      
    OutputwriteInfoLine clears the Info window, appendInfoLine adds; writeFileLine and appendFileLine do the same for a file; fixed$(x, n) formats with n decimals; tab$ and newline$ are special charactersappendFileLine: out$, name$, ",", fixed$(f0, 1)
    
      
    Loopsfor i to n ... endfor; while ... endwhile; repeat ... untilfor ifile to nFiles
    
      
    Conditionsif ... elsif ... else ... endif; comparison =, <>, <, >=; logic and, or, notif f0 = undefined
    
      
    Forms, procedures, continuationform ... endform asks the user for settings; procedure ... endproc with @name: calls; a line starting with ... continues the previous line; # starts a commentSee the scripts below
    
        
  
    
  
    

    # my first script: numbers, strings, a loop and a condition
f0 = 220
period_ms = 1000 / f0
name$ = "p01"
writeInfoLine: name$, ": F0 ", f0, " Hz, period ", fixed$(period_ms, 2), " ms"
for i to 3
    f = f0 * 2 ^ i
    if f > 1000
        appendInfoLine: "Octave ", i, ": ", f, " Hz (above 1 kHz)"
    else
        appendInfoLine: "Octave ", i, ": ", f, " Hz"
    endif
endfor
    

    p01: F0 220 Hz, period 4.55 ms
Octave 1: 440 Hz
Octave 2: 880 Hz
Octave 3: 1760 Hz (above 1 kHz)
    
  
    
  
    A form at the top of a script makes Praat show a dialog when the script is run, so that a colleague can change the folder or the pitch range without editing code. Each field line gives a type (real, positive, integer, natural, word, sentence, text, boolean, choice or optionmenu, comment), a label and a default. Praat turns the label into a variable name by making the first letter lower case, replacing spaces with underscores and dropping anything in parentheses: the field positive Pitch_floor_(Hz) 75 becomes the variable pitch_floor, and sentence Folder recordings becomes the string folder$.
    
  
    A complete batch script
    
  
    The flowchart shows the structure of almost every batch analysis: ask for settings, write a header, list the files, and loop over them, measuring each one and writing one row of a CSV (comma-separated values) file that opens directly in a spreadsheet or statistics package.
    
  
    
  
  
    
  
    Figure 5.7f. Structure of the batch voice-analysis script below. Steps 4 to 8 run once for each WAV file in the folder.
    
  
    

    # voice_batch.praat
# Measures every WAV file in a folder and writes one CSV row per file.
# Praat 6.x. Run from a Praat script window: Run > Run (Ctrl+R).

form Voice measurements for a folder of sustained vowels
    sentence Folder recordings
    sentence Output_file voice_results.csv
    positive Pitch_floor_(Hz) 75
    positive Pitch_ceiling_(Hz) 600
    positive Maximum_formant_(Hz) 5500
endform

writeFileLine: output_file$, "file,duration_s,mean_f0_Hz,sd_f0_Hz,mean_dB,F1_Hz,F2_Hz,jitter_local_pct,shimmer_local_pct,HNR_dB"
list = Create Strings as file list: "list", folder$ + "/*.wav"
nFiles = Get number of strings

for ifile to nFiles
    selectObject: list
    fileName$ = Get string: ifile
    sound = Read from file: folder$ + "/" + fileName$
    dur = Get total duration
    # analyse the middle 1 s (or the whole file if shorter) to avoid onset and offset
    t1 = max(0, dur / 2 - 0.5)
    t2 = min(dur, dur / 2 + 0.5)

    pitch = To Pitch: 0, pitch_floor, pitch_ceiling
    f0 = Get mean: t1, t2, "Hertz"
    f0sd = Get standard deviation: t1, t2, "Hertz"

    selectObject: sound
    intensity = To Intensity: pitch_floor, 0, "yes"
    db = Get mean: t1, t2, "energy"

    selectObject: sound
    formant = To Formant (burg): 0, 5, maximum_formant, 0.025, 50
    f1 = Get value at time: 1, dur / 2, "hertz", "linear"
    f2 = Get value at time: 2, dur / 2, "hertz", "linear"

    selectObject: sound
    pulses = To PointProcess (periodic, cc): pitch_floor, pitch_ceiling
    jitter = Get jitter (local): t1, t2, 0.0001, 0.02, 1.3
    selectObject: sound, pulses
    shimmer = Get shimmer (local): t1, t2, 0.0001, 0.02, 1.3, 1.6

    selectObject: sound
    harm = To Harmonicity (cc): 0.01, pitch_floor, 0.1, 1.0
    hnr = Get mean: t1, t2

    appendFileLine: output_file$, fileName$, ",", fixed$(dur, 3), ",",
    ... fixed$(f0, 1), ",", fixed$(f0sd, 2), ",", fixed$(db, 1), ",",
    ... fixed$(f1, 0), ",", fixed$(f2, 0), ",", fixed$(jitter * 100, 2), ",",
    ... fixed$(shimmer * 100, 2), ",", fixed$(hnr, 1)
    removeObject: sound, pitch, intensity, formant, pulses, harm
endfor
removeObject: list
writeInfoLine: "Measured ", nFiles, " files. Results in ", output_file$
    

    Measured 3 files. Results in voice_results.csv
    
  
    
  
    The script was run on a folder of three 1.5 s test vowels made with makeVowel: 110 Hz with ±0.2 % alternating period perturbation (nominal jitter 0.4 %); 210 Hz with ±0.3 % (nominal 0.6 %) and light added noise; 160 Hz with ±0.6 % (nominal 1.2 %) and more noise. The contents of voice_results.csv:
    
  
    

    file,duration_s,mean_f0_Hz,sd_f0_Hz,mean_dB,F1_Hz,F2_Hz,jitter_local_pct,shimmer_local_pct,HNR_dB
spk01_a.wav,1.500,110.0,0.05,82.6,691,1218,0.42,0.80,32.4
spk02_a.wav,1.500,210.0,0.01,81.8,835,1477,0.28,1.06,27.9
spk03_a.wav,1.500,160.0,0.18,82.6,653,1166,1.19,1.64,16.6
    
  
    
  
    Read the table critically, as you would real data. F0 is exact for all three. Jitter is recovered well at 110 and 160 Hz (0.42 % and 1.19 % against 0.4 % and 1.2 %) but under-read at 210 Hz (0.28 % against 0.6 %): there the alternation is only about ±14 µs, less than one sample period at 44.1 kHz, so small perturbations of high voices are near the resolution limit of the method. HNR falls as noise is added. F1 of the noisiest file (653 Hz against a target of 750 Hz) shows how noise disturbs formant estimation. The intensities are about 82 dB only because the files were scaled to a peak of 0.5; they are not dB SPL.
    
  
    Making the script robust
    
  
      
    
    • Undefined values: if a file has no voiced frames, Get mean returns undefined and fixed$ writes --undefined--. Test with if f0 = undefined and write NA instead so that statistics software reads the column correctly.
      • 
    
    • Per-speaker settings: read the pitch range and maximum formant for each file from a small table (Read Table from comma-separated file...) keyed by participant and sex, rather than using one range for everyone.
      • 
    
    • Paths: on Windows Praat accepts forward slashes in paths. Keep folder names free of spaces and non-ASCII characters to avoid problems in other software.
      • 
    
    • Newer Praat versions offer shorter forms (for example a string vector of file names, files$# = fileNames$# (folder$ + "/*.wav")); the Strings-based version above works in all 6.x versions.
      • 
    
    • Log the Praat version and settings in the CSV or in a separate text file written by the same script (praatVersion$ holds the version string).
      • 
    
    • To run a script on the command line or from another program, Praat can be started with --run script.praat arguments; this is how Praat is embedded in larger pipelines.
      • 
  
    

  
    19. Parselmouth: Praat inside Python
    
  
    Parselmouth (Jadoul, Thompson and de Boer, 2018) is a Python library that contains Praat's own C and C++ code. Its measurements are therefore Praat measurements, not re-implementations, while the rest of the analysis (statistics, plotting, machine learning, reading spreadsheets) can use the Python tools introduced in the Audiology Python Primer. Install it with pip install praat-parselmouth (note the package name) and import it as parselmouth. Each Parselmouth release contains a specific Praat version; version 0.4.7, used for every cell in this topic, contains Praat 6.1.38, which is why the older pitch command names appear in the code.
    
  
    There are two ways to use it. The Pythonic interface has methods such as Sound.to_pitch(), to_formant_burg() and to_intensity(), returning objects whose values can be read as NumPy arrays. The parselmouth.praat.call function sends any Praat command, with exactly the arguments of the script language, to one or more objects; it covers every command, including those without a Python method. parselmouth.praat.run_file runs a complete .praat script file, which is how the Praat scripts in this topic were verified.
    
  
    

    import glob
import numpy as np
import parselmouth
from parselmouth.praat import call

for path in sorted(glob.glob("recordings/*.wav")):
    snd = parselmouth.Sound(path)
    pitch = snd.to_pitch(pitch_floor=75, pitch_ceiling=600)
    f0 = pitch.selected_array["frequency"]
    f0 = f0[f0 > 0]                                  # voiced frames only
    formant = snd.to_formant_burg(max_number_of_formants=5, maximum_formant=5500)
    mid = snd.duration / 2
    f1 = formant.get_value_at_time(1, mid)
    f2 = formant.get_value_at_time(2, mid)
    pulses = call(snd, "To PointProcess (periodic, cc)", 75, 600)
    jit = call(pulses, "Get jitter (local)", 0, 0, 0.0001, 0.02, 1.3)
    print(f"{path.split('/')[-1]}: f0 {np.mean(f0):.1f} Hz, "
          f"F1 {f1:.0f} Hz, F2 {f2:.0f} Hz, jitter {100 * jit:.2f} %")
    

    spk01_a.wav: f0 110.0 Hz, F1 691 Hz, F2 1218 Hz, jitter 0.42 %
spk02_a.wav: f0 210.0 Hz, F1 835 Hz, F2 1477 Hz, jitter 0.28 %
spk03_a.wav: f0 160.0 Hz, F1 653 Hz, F2 1166 Hz, jitter 1.19 %
    
  
    
  
    The values agree with the CSV from the Praat script (here jitter is taken over the whole file rather than the middle second, which happens to give the same rounded values). Unvoiced frames have frequency 0 in selected_array and must be removed before averaging, as in the code. Two further points: first, results can differ slightly from a newer desktop Praat if an algorithm has changed between versions, so a study should use one route throughout; second, Parselmouth makes it easy to combine Praat measures with other features for the machine-learning work discussed in 2.10, but the acoustic caveats in section 11 still apply.
    

  
    20. Clinical and research applications
    
  
    
    
    ApplicationPraat toolsPoints of method
    
    
      
    Voice analysis before and after intervention (voice therapy, phonosurgery, hearing-aid or cochlear-implant fitting in adults whose voice changed with hearing loss)Voice report on sustained vowels; CPPS on vowels and connected speech; F0 mean and range from a read passage; intensity with calibrationSame microphone, distance, room, instructions and settings at each session; several repetitions; compare with norms for the same protocol; report effect sizes relative to test-retest variability
    
      
    Speech perception stimulus creationCreate Sound from formula; Manipulation (F0 and duration continua); filters (low-pass, high-pass, band-pass speech); Scale intensity; Concatenate recoverablyVerify every stimulus by measurement (F0, duration, level, spectrum); cut at zero crossings; add ramps; document all parameters
    
      
    Speech of speakers with hearing loss and cochlear-implant usersTextGrid segmentation; formants at vowel midpoints and vowel space area; fricative spectral moments; F0 contour and range; vowel and word durations; voice onset timeCheck formant tracks carefully for children's high F0; use age- and language-appropriate norms; blind the annotator to group where possible; report inter-annotator reliability
    
      
    Speech-in-noise material calibrationLtas of the speech material and of the masker; Scale intensity for item-level equalisation; Get intensity (dB) for SNR checks; calibration tone creationMatch LTAS band by band; equalise item levels; check the mixture never clips; a calibration tone of the same RMS as the speech is used to set the audiometer or playback system
    
      
    Acoustic analysis for research dissertationsScripts for all measurements; TextGrids for all segmentation; Picture window for figures; Parselmouth for integration with statisticsSave raw files, TextGrids, scripts and CSVs together; state the Praat version and every setting; include the scripts in an appendix or a repository
    
      
    Audiological test materials in regional languagesRecording, cutting, level equalisation and duration measurement of word and sentence lists; LTAS comparisons across listsLists must be equivalent in level and spectrum; measure and report them
    
        
  
    
  
    Clinical linkPraat is a research tool. It has no regulatory clearance as a medical device, its algorithms are published but not validated for any particular clinical population, and its intensity values are not SPL without calibration. Use it to support clinical judgement, for research and for preparing test materials; when a clinical decision depends on a number, use a validated protocol with published norms and state its limitations.
    

  
    21. Limitations, errors and troubleshooting
    
  
    
    
    ProblemLikely causeWhat to check or do
    
    
      
    Pitch contour jumps to half or double the expected F0 (octave jumps)Ceiling below F0, floor too low, creaky or diplophonic voice, strong first formant near 2 × F0Set floor and ceiling for the speaker; view a narrowband spectrogram; raise the octave-jump cost; correct in the Pitch editor; report corrected frames
    
      
    Pitch missing in voiced partsFloor above the speaker's F0; voicing threshold too high; breathy or quiet voice; silence threshold too highLower floor; lower voicing threshold (for example 0.35); check level; try raw cross-correlation
    
      
    Pitch reported in unvoiced parts (fricatives, noise)Voicing threshold too low; periodic background noise (fans, hum)Raise voicing threshold; filter hum; improve the recording room
    
      
    Formant dots do not follow the dark bandsWrong maximum formant or number of formants for the speaker; high F0; nasal vowels; noiseTry ceilings in steps of 500 Hz and 4, 4.5 or 5 formants; compare with the spectrogram; measure several frames around the midpoint; hand-correct and document
    
      
    F1 and F2 reported as one formant, or a spurious formant appearsToo few or too many poles for the band analysedChange the number of formants or the ceiling (section 10 cell)
    
      
    Clipped recording (flat tops on the waveform)Input gain too highRe-record; clipping adds distortion that raises shimmer and lowers HNR and cannot be removed; check Get maximum and Get minimum near ±1
    
      
    Very low-level recordingGain too low; microphone too farRe-record with more gain; scaling up afterwards also scales up noise and quantisation error
    
      
    High shimmer and low HNR in a healthy voiceRoom noise or reverberation; lossy compression; automatic gain control; background humRecord in a quiet, treated room; use WAV; disable operating-system audio enhancements; check the noise floor in a silent stretch
    
      
    Intensity differs between sessions for the same speakerMouth-to-microphone distance or gain changed; no calibrationFix distance with a head-mounted microphone or a marked position; record a calibration tone each session
    
      
    Fricative spectra look truncated at 4 or 8 kHzSampling rate of 8 or 16 kHzRecord at 44.1 kHz; raise the spectrogram view range
    
      
    Different values from a colleague for the same fileDifferent Praat version, method, settings, time selection or editor versus object routeShare scripts, not descriptions; state version and settings; use one route for the whole study
    
      
    Analyses do not appear in the Sound windowVisible window longer than the longest-analysis settingZoom in, or raise Analyses > Show analyses... longest analysis
    
      
    Script stops with "Command not available for current selection"Wrong object selected after a command created a new oneStore object IDs in variables and call selectObject before each command
    
        
  
    

  
    22. Demonstration
    
  
    Equipment: a computer with Praat (version noted on the board), a projector, a USB audio interface with a headset or measurement microphone, headphones or speakers, a sound level calibrator if available, and the scripts makevowel.praat and voice_batch.praat from this topic.
    
  
      
    
    1. Start Praat and show the two windows. Record a volunteer saying the vowels /i a u/ and the sentence "She sells sea shells" at 44 100 Hz. Save to list, then save as WAV. Students observe the object appear and note the Info summary.
      2. 
    
    2. Open the sentence with View & Edit. Show selection, zoom and play. Switch the spectrogram between 0.005 s and 0.03 s windows and ask students to identify harmonics and formants; show /s/ and /ʃ/ with a view range of 0 to 10 000 Hz.
      3. 
    
    3. Show the pitch contour. Set the ceiling below the volunteer's F0 and show the octave drop; set the floor above it and show the contour vanish. Restore sensible settings.
      4. 
    
    4. Measure F1 and F2 at the midpoints of /i a u/; plot them in the vowel demo above and compute the vowel space area.
      5. 
    
    5. Record a sustained /a/ for 4 s. Show pulses, select the middle 2 s and run a Voice report. Then ask the volunteer to phonate breathily and repeat: students observe HNR fall and CPPS change. Repeat with the microphone twice as far away and discuss why the numbers changed.
      6. 
    
    6. Create a 1 kHz tone of amplitude 0.1 by formula and show that its intensity is 70.97 dB; if a calibrator is available, record it and compute the session's calibration offset.
      7. 
    
    7. Make a Manipulation of the sentence, flatten its F0 contour and raise it by 4 semitones; play the original and the resynthesis.
      8. 
    
    8. Annotate the vowels in a TextGrid, save it, then run voice_batch.praat on a folder of three recordings and open the CSV in a spreadsheet. Finally show the same measurements in a Python notebook with Parselmouth.
      9. 
  
    

  
    23. Current developments
    
  
      
    
    • Praat continues to be updated several times a year. Recent versions reorganised pitch analysis into explicitly named methods (filtered and raw autocorrelation and cross-correlation) and changed the recommended method for intonation, which makes stating the version and method in publications more important.
      • 
    
    • Cepstral and multiparametric measures (CPPS, indices that combine several measures) are increasingly preferred to jitter and shimmer alone for clinical voice assessment, and several are distributed as Praat scripts.
      • 
    
    • Python access through Parselmouth, and TextGrid libraries in Python and R, allow Praat measurements to be combined with large-scale statistics and machine-learning pipelines.
      • 
    
    • Automatic forced alignment and speech recognition produce TextGrids for large corpora, shifting the annotator's task from segmenting to checking.
      • 
    
    • Remote and smartphone recordings are used more often for voice monitoring; their effects on acoustic measures (compression, automatic gain, microphone response) are an active area of methodological research, and results should be compared only within the same recording set-up.
      • 
  
    

  
    24. Practical activity and assessment (P4)
    
  
      
    
    1. Install Praat and write its version in your lab record. Record, at 44 100 Hz in a quiet room with a fixed microphone distance: the vowels /i e a o u/ in isolation (three tokens each), a sustained /a/ for 5 s (three tokens), and five sentences from a standard passage or test list.
      2. 
    
    2. Make wideband (0.005 s) and narrowband (0.03 s) spectrograms of one sentence in the Picture window and save them as one PDF figure with labelled panels. In two or three sentences explain what each shows.
      3. 
    
    3. Measure mean F0, F0 standard deviation (in semitones) and F0 range (5th to 95th percentile) of the sentences, stating floor, ceiling and method; describe any octave errors and how you corrected them.
      4. 
    
    4. Label your vowels in a TextGrid, measure F1 and F2 at the midpoints with a script, plot them with the vowel demo, and report your /i a u/ vowel space area.
      5. 
    
    5. Run a Voice report on the middle 2 s of each sustained /a/. Tabulate jitter (local), shimmer (local), HNR and CPPS with mean and range across the three tokens, and write a short interpretation that states the caveats of section 11.
      6. 
    
    6. Adapt voice_batch.praat to your folder and settings, run it, and submit the script, the CSV and a one-page method paragraph (software, version, settings, recording conditions) written as for a dissertation.
      7. 
  
    
  
    Deliverable: a short report (figures, tables, method paragraph) plus the Praat scripts, TextGrids and CSV. Assessment weights accuracy of measurements, correctness and completeness of the method description, quality of figures, and critical interpretation.
    

  
    Exercises
    
  
    
    
    
      
    
      
    A student recorded fricative-rich speech of children with hearing loss at 16 000 Hz. What is the highest frequency in the recordings, and why is this a problem for /s/ analysis?
    Model answer: the Nyquist frequency is 16 000 / 2 = 8000 Hz, so nothing above 8 kHz is represented. The energy of /s/ in children often extends well above 8 kHz, so spectral moments (centroid, skewness) will be biased downwards and the /s/-/ʃ/ contrast underestimated. Record at 44 100 Hz; the files can be resampled down later if needed.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Using B ≈ 1.3 / L, what window length is needed for a bandwidth of 50 Hz? Would a 5 ms window show individual harmonics for a woman speaking at 220 Hz?
    Model answer: L = 1.3 / 50 = 0.026 s (26 ms). A 5 ms window has B ≈ 260 Hz, which is more than 220 Hz, so harmonics are not resolved and the display is wideband; the formants appear as bands (though less smoothly than for a low male voice).
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    A child's voice at 320 Hz is analysed with floor 75 Hz and ceiling 300 Hz. What lag range is searched, where is the true period, and what F0 will probably be reported? What would you change?
    Model answer: lags from 1 / 300 = 3.33 ms to 1 / 75 = 13.3 ms. The true period is 1 / 320 = 3.125 ms, just outside the range, so the next peak at 6.25 ms is likely chosen, giving 160 Hz (an octave too low). Raise the ceiling to about 600 to 700 Hz and the floor to about 150 Hz.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    An utterance has three equally long parts at 70, 70 and 80 dB. What mean does Praat report with the "dB" and with the "energy" averaging methods?
    Model answer: dB method: (70 + 70 + 80) / 3 = 73.3 dB. Energy method: 10 log10((107 + 107 + 108) / 3) = 10 log10(4 × 107) = 76.0 dB. The energy mean is the physically correct level of the whole utterance.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Five consecutive periods are 8.0, 8.1, 7.9, 8.0 and 8.2 ms. Compute jitter (local) and jitter (local, absolute).
    Model answer: absolute differences 0.1, 0.2, 0.1, 0.2 ms; mean 0.15 ms, which is jitter (local, absolute) = 150 µs. Mean period (8.0 + 8.1 + 7.9 + 8.0 + 8.2) / 5 = 8.04 ms. Jitter (local) = 0.15 / 8.04 = 1.87 %. (Praat's MDVP-based reference of 1.04 % would flag this, but a real decision needs norms for the protocol used.)
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    A speaker's corner vowels measure /i/ (F1 310, F2 2500), /a/ (F1 800, F2 1300), /u/ (F1 350, F2 900) Hz. Compute the triangle area.
    Model answer: |2500 × (800 - 350) + 1300 × (350 - 310) + 900 × (310 - 800)| / 2 = |1 125 000 + 52 000 - 441 000| / 2 = 736 000 / 2 = 368 000 Hz2 (0.368 kHz2). Check with the vowel demo.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    You need a sentence at -3 dB SNR with the speech at 65 dB. Which Praat commands do you use, what level should the noise have, and what is the level of the mixture?
    Model answer: Scale intensity: 65 on the speech; Scale intensity: 68 on a noise of the same duration; Copy the speech and Formula: "self + object[noise]". Mixture level = 10 log10(106.5 + 106.8) = 69.8 dB. Check Get maximum and Get minimum so that the mixture does not exceed ±1 before saving; if it does, scale both components down by the same amount.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Modify voice_batch.praat so that it (a) writes NA when F0 is undefined, (b) adds jitter (rap) and shimmer (apq11) columns and (c) writes the Praat version into a separate log file.
    Model answer: (a) after f0 = Get mean... add if f0 = undefined / f0$ = "NA" / else / f0$ = fixed$(f0, 1) / endif and write f0$. (b) with the PointProcess selected, rap = Get jitter (rap): t1, t2, 0.0001, 0.02, 1.3; with Sound and PointProcess selected, apq11 = Get shimmer (apq11): t1, t2, 0.0001, 0.02, 1.3, 1.6; add both to the header and the appendFileLine. (c) writeFileLine: "analysis_log.txt", "Praat ", praatVersion$, ", floor ", pitch_floor, ", ceiling ", pitch_ceiling.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Design a seven-step continuum from a falling (statement) to a rising (question) final F0 on the word "ready", for a study of intonation perception by cochlear-implant users. Describe the steps in Praat and how you would verify the stimuli.
    Model answer: record "ready" with a neutral contour; To Manipulation (range set for the speaker); Extract pitch tier; for each step k = 0 to 6, Remove points between the start and end of the final vowel and add a point at the vowel end at a value from, for example, -4 to +8 semitones relative to the vowel onset in equal steps (Shift frequencies... in semitones or computed Hz values); Replace pitch tier; Get resynthesis (overlap-add); Scale intensity to a fixed level; save as ready_step0.wav to ready_step6.wav. Verify by measuring F0 at the vowel end, total duration and intensity of each file, listening for artefacts, and plotting the seven contours in one Picture-window figure. Keep all other cues (duration, level, spectrum) constant so that only F0 differs.
