    5.8
    Computer-based assessment and intervention programs relating to hearing: in depth
    
  
    Supplementary4 hoursPractical P13Practical P14
    
  
    Topic 5.3 introduced computer-based screening, automated audiometry, digits-in-noise testing, auditory training and a basic evaluation checklist. This topic goes one level deeper: how each class of program actually works, what hardware and calibration it depends on, the mathematics of the adaptive procedures inside it, what the published evidence does and does not show, and how an engineer and an audiologist together would design, validate and evaluate such a program for Indian clinical use.
    

  
    
    
    What it is
    A survey of the software used to measure hearing (automated and tablet audiometry, speech-in-noise tests, auditory processing batteries, objective-test front ends, fitting and verification software, in-app hearing tests, tinnitus assessment), to treat or support hearing (auditory training, cochlear implant rehabilitation apps, tinnitus apps, speechreading training, captioning and alerting tools) and to connect patients with clinicians (tele-audiology platforms and remote programming).
    
    
    Why it is required
    Audiologists increasingly choose, recommend, run and interpret these programs, and are asked to advise on apps patients have already downloaded. Doing this safely requires knowing the difference between a calibrated, validated clinical system and an unvalidated app, understanding what an adaptive threshold estimate means, and being able to judge evidence, privacy and regulatory status. Students who move into research or product development also need to know how such programs are built and validated.
    
    
    How it works
    Almost every program in this topic shares one skeleton: a stimulus engine produces a sound at a calibrated level, a listener responds, an adaptive rule chooses the next stimulus, a stopping rule ends the test or session, and the result is stored and reported. Assessment programs estimate a point on a psychometric function; training programs keep the listener working near a target accuracy; tele-audiology platforms carry the same processes across a network.
    
  
    

  
    RememberThis topic assumes you have studied 5.3 (screening versus diagnosis, the Hughson-Westlake procedure, basic digits-in-noise design, sensitivity and specificity, the first evaluation checklist). Pure-tone audiometer instrumentation is covered in 6.1, OAE and other instruments in 6.6, ABR in 6.2 and ASSR in 6.4; tele-diagnosis and tele-rehabilitation in 2.5 and 2.6. For writing your own test scripts, see the Audiology Python Primer and 5.4 MATLAB.
    

  
    1. Landscape and classification
    
  
    The phrase "computer-based program relating to hearing" covers everything from a clinical diagnostic audiometer controlled by a PC to a free smartphone game. Sorting any program along three independent axes prevents most errors of judgement: its purpose, its delivery platform and its regulatory status.
    

  
    Axis 1: purpose
    
  
    
    
    PurposeQuestion answeredTypical outputWho acts on it
    
    
      
    Assessment (diagnostic)What are this person's hearing thresholds, speech perception or auditory processing abilities?Audiogram, SRT or SNR-50, percentage scores, test battery profileAudiologist, for diagnosis and management
    
      
    ScreeningDoes this person probably have the target condition and need full assessment?Pass or refer, sometimes a category (for example "likely hearing loss")Screener or the person, who follows a referral pathway
    
      
    InterventionCan listening, communication or tinnitus distress be improved?Training sessions, sound therapy, counselling content, progress scoresPatient, with clinician supervision
    
      
    MonitoringIs anything changing over time, and how is the device being used?Serial thresholds, questionnaires, hearing-aid data logs, usage hoursClinician and patient at review
    
        
  
    
  
    The same software can span purposes. A tablet audiometer used by a trained tester with calibrated headphones in a quiet room may be used diagnostically; the same app run with a single screening level becomes a screening tool. A hearing-aid app that runs an in-situ threshold test is simultaneously an assessment tool (it estimates thresholds), a fitting tool (it sets gain) and a monitoring tool (it logs use).
    

  
    Axis 2: delivery platform
    
  
    
    
    PlatformDescriptionStrengthsWeaknesses
    
    
      
    Clinic-based softwareInstalled on a clinic PC and paired with dedicated calibrated hardware: PC-based audiometers, OAE/ABR systems, fitting software, real-ear systems.Calibrated transducers, controlled environment, integration with clinic databases.Needs the patient to attend; hardware cost; licence and update management.
    
      
    Web-based programsRun in a browser; logic may run on a server or in the page. Includes online hearing checks, web auditory training and tele-audiology portals.No installation, easy updates, central data.No control over the listener's sound card, headphones or volume; browser audio processing; dependence on connectivity.
    
      
    Mobile appsRun on a phone or tablet. Includes tablet audiometers, DIN screening apps, hearing-aid control apps, tinnitus and training apps.Portable, touch interface, fixed hardware per model (so calibration per model is possible).Operating-system updates change audio behaviour; wide device variety; Bluetooth latency and processing.
    
      
    Built-in device featuresFunctions inside a hearing aid, cochlear implant processor, earbud or operating system: in-situ audiometry, data logging, hearing tests in earbuds, live captions, sound recognition.Known transducer, always with the user, large reach.Proprietary methods; results may not be comparable with clinic audiometry; limited transparency.
    
        
  
    

  
    Axis 3: regulatory status
    
  
    Regulators decide whether software is a medical device from its intended use, the claim the manufacturer makes in labelling and marketing. Software intended to diagnose, screen for, monitor or treat a disease or impairment is generally a medical device; when it runs on general-purpose hardware it is called software as a medical device (SaMD). Software that claims only to support general wellness, education or entertainment may sit outside device regulation. Two apps with nearly identical code can therefore have different status because one says "detects hearing loss" and the other says "check how well you hear for information". The clinician's questions are: what is claimed, in which country is it authorised for that claim, and does my intended use match it. Section 7 describes the regulatory pathways in more detail.
    

  
    

  
    
  
    Figure 5.8a. Landscape of computer-based hearing programs. Any program can be placed by its purpose (top), its delivery platform (middle) and its regulatory status (bottom). The three are independent: a mobile app may be a regulated diagnostic device, and clinic software may be an unregulated research tool.
    

  
    Key ideaClassify before you evaluate. A wellness app should not be judged against diagnostic audiometry standards, and a diagnostic claim should never be accepted on the evidence that would be sufficient for a wellness app.
    

  
    2. Computer-based assessment in depth
    
  
    For each class of assessment program, this section describes the principle, the hardware and calibration it needs, the procedure, the outputs, the validation status and the limitations. Adaptive procedures are referred to here and explained mathematically in section 3.
    

  
    2.1 Automated and computer-controlled pure-tone audiometry
    
  
    Principle. Automated audiometry replaces the tester's hand on the tone switch and attenuator with an algorithm. The algorithm is usually a coded version of the modified Hughson-Westlake procedure (see 5.3), a Békésy-type tracking method, or a newer adaptive method that chooses frequency and level together (section 3.5). The listener responds with a button or touch screen; the software records responses, detects false responses using catch trials (silent intervals in which no tone is presented) and response-time windows, and computes thresholds.
    
  
    Three families are in use:
    
  
      
    
    • Computer-controlled clinical audiometers. A PC-based or stand-alone audiometer meeting the audiometer standard (IEC 60645-1 or ANSI S3.6) with automated test modes. The hardware is the same as a manual audiometer, with standard transducers and an analogue attenuator, so calibration is conventional. AMTAS (Automated Method for Testing Auditory Sensitivity), developed by Margolis and colleagues and validated in published studies in the International Journal of Audiology (2010 and 2011), is a well-documented example that includes air and bone conduction with masking and computes quality indicators that flag results likely to be inaccurate.
      • 
    
    • Boothless audiometers. Systems designed to test outside a sound-treated room. They combine high passive attenuation (for example insert earphones placed inside noise-excluding circumaural cups) with continuous monitoring of ambient noise through microphones, so that a presentation is repeated or flagged when noise at the test frequency is too high. KUDUwave (eMoyo) and the hearTest occupational health system (hearX) are examples described by their manufacturers as boothless.
      • 
    
    • Tablet and smartphone audiometers. An app on a mobile device drives headphones calibrated for that device model. SHOEBOX Audiometry (iPad based) and hearTest (hearX, Android based) are examples that have peer-reviewed validation studies. In one adult validation of SHOEBOX (Bastianelli et al., 2019, Journal of Otolaryngology, Head and Neck Surgery), testing by non-experts outside a sound booth in a quiet room gave 95.7% of thresholds within 10 dB of standard audiometry; figures like these apply only to the tested population, device, transducer and environment.
      • 
  
    
  
    Hardware and calibration needs. The transducer must be the model the calibration table was made for, calibrated on the appropriate coupler or ear simulator (IEC 60318 series) using a sound level meter, with RETSPLs from the ISO 389 series (or manufacturer-derived equivalents for transducers not in the standards). For device-based audiometers the whole chain (device, operating system version, headphone amplifier, headphone) is calibrated as a unit, and volume and audio "enhancements" are locked by the app. Ambient noise limits for boothless testing depend on the attenuation of the headset; manufacturers publish maximum permissible ambient noise levels calculated from measured attenuation.
    
  
    Procedure. A typical automated session: instructions and a practice item; ear and frequency order chosen by the software (commonly 1 kHz first, then higher frequencies, a retest at 1 kHz for reliability, then lower frequencies); adaptive threshold search at each frequency with catch trials; masking, if supported, set by rule when interaural differences exceed the expected interaural attenuation; final report.
    
  
    Outputs. Audiogram with thresholds per frequency and ear; reliability indicators (false alarm rate, 1 kHz test-retest difference, test time); in some systems a quality score; notes on noise events.
    
  
    Validation status. Clinical automated audiometers have published agreement studies against manual audiometry, generally showing mean differences of a few decibels with the spread of differences similar to manual test-retest variability. Tablet and boothless systems have a growing number of validation studies in adults and children, in clinics and in the community. Validation is specific: a study of air-conduction thresholds in cooperative adults does not validate bone conduction, masking, young children or people with cognitive impairment.
    
  
    Limitations. Bone conduction and masking are hard to automate and many portable systems omit them, so the type of loss often cannot be determined; maximum output of portable transducers limits measurement of severe losses; children below about school age and people with cognitive impairment may not complete self-administered tests; low-frequency thresholds are the first to be affected by ambient noise.
    

  
    
    
    ExampleTypePlatform and transducerNotes
    
    
      
    AMTASAutomated diagnostic audiometryComputer-controlled audiometer with standard transducersAir and bone conduction with masking; quality indicators; published validation studies
    
      
    KUDUwave (eMoyo)Boothless PC-based audiometerInsert earphones within circumaural cups, ambient noise microphonesDesigned for testing without a booth
    
      
    SHOEBOX AudiometryTablet audiometeriPad with calibrated headphonesSelf-administered or tester-led; published validation in adults and children
    
      
    hearTest (hearX)Smartphone audiometerAndroid device with calibrated headphonesScreening and threshold modes; published validation studies
    
        
  
    
  
    Examples only; availability and features change; check current information.
    

  
    2.2 Speech-in-noise tests delivered by computer
    
  
    Pure-tone thresholds predict speech understanding in noise poorly. Speech-in-noise (SIN) tests fill the gap, and nearly all are now delivered from a computer, either from recorded material played through an audiometer or by dedicated software. Most estimate the speech reception threshold in noise (SRT, also called SNR-50), the signal-to-noise ratio at which 50% of the speech is recognised.
    
  
    
    
    TestMaterial and procedureOutputRemarks
    
    
      
    Digits-in-noise (DIN) or digit triplet testSpoken digit triplets in speech-shaped noise; triplet scored all-or-none; 1-down 1-up SNR track, typically 2 dB steps, a few tens of triplets. First described as a Dutch telephone test by Smits, Kapteyn and Houtgast (2004).SRT in dB SNR; pass/refer against a cut-offRelative level test, tolerant of unknown output level; versions in many languages; antiphasic variant (digits inverted in phase at one ear) increases sensitivity to sensorineural loss and reduces sensitivity to conductive loss.
    
      
    hearWHOWHO smartphone app based on digits-in-noise; according to WHO, 23 sets of three digits over background noise.Score 0 to 100, with advice bands (below 50: likely hearing loss, see a professional)Intended for people aged 12 years and over; a screening and awareness tool.
    
      
    HINT (Hearing in Noise Test)Phonemically balanced sentence lists in speech-shaped noise at a fixed noise level; sentence level varied adaptively (Nilsson, Soli and Sullivan, 1994).SRT in dB SNR, in several spatial conditionsAdaptive; many language versions; used in research and fitting evaluation.
    
      
    QuickSINLists of six sentences, each with five key words, in four-talker babble; SNR falls from 25 to 0 dB in 5 dB steps (Killion et al., 2004).SNR loss in dB relative to normal-hearing listenersFixed-step descending method scored with the Spearman-Kärber formula (section 3.6); about one minute per list.
    
      
    Matrix sentence testsFive-word sentences of fixed syntax (name, verb, numeral, adjective, object) built from a 50-word matrix; closed-set response possible; adaptive SNR. Oldenburg (OLSA) is the German original; many languages now exist.SRT in dB SNR, slope of the intelligibility functionLow redundancy, so repeated use with the same lists is possible after training; cross-language comparability by design.
    
      
    Indian-language materialPublished work from Indian audiology centres includes a Kannada digit triplet test evaluated in older adults with mild-to-moderate sensorineural loss, a Hindi digits-in-noise test, and a Kannada dichotic double-digit test.SRT, pass/refer, ear scoresCheck each test's validation population and whether normative data exist before clinical use; many other Indian-language speech tests exist as recorded lists used through audiometers.
    
        
  
    
  
    Examples only; availability and features change; check current information.
    
  
    Hardware and calibration. Adaptive SIN tests vary the SNR, so the relative level of speech and noise must be exact (the software controls this digitally, which is precise) while the absolute presentation level need only be known approximately and comfortably above threshold. For HINT, QuickSIN and matrix tests used clinically, the absolute level is calibrated through the audiometer using the calibration signal supplied with the recordings. DIN-type tests are deliberately designed to be robust to the unknown absolute output of consumer devices, provided the listener sets a comfortable volume and the speech is audible.
    
  
    Procedure and scoring. Adaptive tests start at an easy SNR, descend until errors occur, then track around the 50% point. Scoring typically averages the SNR over the later trials, excluding the initial descent, or fits a psychometric function to all trials (maximum-likelihood scoring). The DIN demonstration below uses the common rule of averaging trials 5 to 24.
    
  
    Validation and limitations. Normative values and cut-offs are specific to language, talker, noise type, transducer and scoring rule. A listener tested in a second language will have a poorer SRT for linguistic reasons; digit tests reduce but do not remove this effect. SIN tests measure a combination of audibility, suprathreshold distortion and cognitive-linguistic ability, so an abnormal result indicates difficulty but not its cause.
    

  
    2.3 Auditory processing assessment batteries
    
  
    Central auditory processing assessment uses behavioural tests that were historically played from tape and CD through a diagnostic audiometer, and are increasingly played from computer software with the audiometer as the calibrated output stage. The computer's role is precise stimulus timing, randomisation, scoring and normative comparison.
    
  
    
    
    Test typeWhat it probesStimulus and taskComputer delivery considerations
    
    
      
    Screening batteries (for example SCAN-3 for children and for adolescents and adults, by Keith)Several processes in one short battery: filtered words, auditory figure-ground, competing words and sentences, and othersRecorded subtests with standard scoringPlayed from CD or digital files through the audiometer; published norms by age.
    
      
    Dichotic tests (dichotic digits, dichotic words, competing sentences)Binaural integration and separationDifferent items to each ear at the same time; free or directed recallChannel timing alignment between ears must be exact; left-right assignment must be checked.
    
      
    Gaps-in-noise (GIN; Musiek et al., 2005)Temporal resolutionNoise segments containing silent gaps of varying duration; listener responds to each gapGap edges must be sample-accurate; audio drivers that resample or apply processing can smear gaps.
    
      
    Frequency (pitch) pattern and duration pattern testsTemporal patterning and interhemispheric transferSequences of three tones differing in frequency or duration; listener labels or hums the patternTone duration and inter-tone intervals must be exact; response mode affects interpretation.
    
      
    Masking level differenceBinaural processing at the brainstemTone or speech in noise, with the signal in phase or out of phase between earsNeeds stable interaural phase: wired stereo output, no independent processing per earbud.
    
        
  
    
  
    Examples only; availability and features change; check current information.
    
  
    APD test results depend heavily on age, language, attention and cognition, so normative data must match the population. Most published norms come from English-speaking populations; Indian clinics use a combination of translated tests with local norms and locally developed tests. Computer delivery helps standardise timing but does not replace the audiometric calibration and the interpretation by an audiologist.
    

  
    2.4 Software front ends of objective test systems
    
  
    OAE, ABR and ASSR systems are computers with specialised acquisition hardware (see 6.6, 6.2, 6.4). Screening versions add an automated pass/refer algorithm, so the operator (often a trained screener, not an audiologist) never interprets waveforms. The algorithm is itself a statistical test:
    
  
      
    
    • OAE screeners (TEOAE or DPOAE) compute the emission level and noise floor in several frequency bands and apply a rule such as "pass if the signal-to-noise ratio exceeds a set criterion in at least a set number of bands", together with artefact rejection and probe-fit checks. The criteria are set by the manufacturer and sometimes configurable.
      • 
    
    • Automated ABR (AABR) screeners present clicks or chirps at a fixed screening level and apply a statistical detection test to the averaged response, for example comparing it with a template of a normal newborn ABR or testing for response presence across many sweeps, stopping at pass once a criterion is reached or referring after a maximum number of sweeps.
      • 
    
    • Automated ASSR detection uses statistical tests on the spectrum or phase of the response at the modulation frequency (for example F-tests or phase coherence), so the software decides objectively whether a response is present.
      • 
  
    
  
    From a software point of view, the important points are that a statistical detector has a false-pass rate built into its criterion; that repeating a refer screen several times until it passes inflates the false-pass rate; that the criteria and version of the algorithm should be recorded; and that noise, artefact and probe-fit indicators displayed by the software are part of the result.
    
  
    Common errorRe-screening a baby repeatedly in one session until a pass appears. Each extra attempt is another chance for a false pass by chance alone. Programmes limit the number of screens per session for this reason.
    

  
    2.5 Hearing-aid fitting software, NOAH-type databases, real-ear measurement and data logging
    
  
    Fitting software from each manufacturer reads the audiogram, applies a prescription (a generic one such as NAL-NL2 or DSL v5, or the manufacturer's proprietary rationale), calculates target gain and compression parameters per channel, and writes them to the hearing aid over a wired or wireless programming interface. It also runs feedback calibration, sets programs, and may run in-situ audiometry through the hearing aid receiver.
    
  
    NOAH, from HIMSA (Hearing Instrument Manufacturers' Software Association), is an industry-standard framework that stores client records, audiograms, fittings and measurements, and launches each manufacturer's fitting module and compatible measurement modules. Because every module reads and writes the same database, the audiogram entered once is available to all fitting software and real-ear systems. The NOAH-type database concept (shared patient record, modules plugged in) is what allows a clinic to fit several brands and keep one history.
    
  
    Real-ear measurement (REM) software controls a probe-microphone system: it presents a calibrated test signal (often a speech-like signal such as the ISTS) from a loudspeaker, measures the SPL near the eardrum with a probe tube, and displays the measured real-ear aided response against the prescription target. Software features include automatic probe-tube placement checks, target calculation from the NOAH audiogram, and, in some systems, automatic adjustment of the hearing aid towards target. The calibration of the probe microphone against the reference microphone (probe-tube calibration) is a software-guided step that must be repeated as instructed.
    
  
    Data logging in hearing aids stores usage hours, time in each program, volume changes and estimates of the acoustic environments encountered. Read back into the fitting software, it supports counselling (for example low daily use) and fine-tuning (for example frequent volume increases in noisy environments). Logged environment classes come from the hearing aid's own classifier, so they are estimates and not measurements.
    

  
    2.6 Self-fitting and OTC hearing aids with in-app hearing tests
    
  
    In the United States, the FDA final rule establishing over-the-counter (OTC) hearing aids was published on 17 August 2022 and took effect on 17 October 2022. OTC hearing aids are for adults aged 18 and over with perceived mild to moderate hearing loss; the rule sets output limits (a general maximum of 111 dB SPL, or 117 dB SPL with input-controlled compression active) and requires user-adjustable features, but does not require that OTC devices be self-fitting. Many OTC products are nevertheless self-fitting: an app presents tones or other test signals through the hearing aid or earbud (in-situ testing) and uses the result to set gain.
    
  
    On 12 September 2024 the FDA authorised, through the De Novo pathway, the first OTC hearing aid software: Apple's Hearing Aid Feature for compatible AirPods Pro, for adults with perceived mild to moderate hearing impairment. The FDA's announcement described a study of 118 participants in which self-fitted users obtained similar perceived benefit to those fitted professionally. Apple's Hearing Test Feature uses the same earbuds for air-conduction pure-tone testing from 250 Hz to 8 kHz. Apple's published description states that it uses Gaussian process classification with Bayesian active learning to choose stimuli, checks ambient noise and earbud fit before proceeding, reports a four-frequency average and a WHO hearing-loss grade with an audiogram, and was validated against reference audiometry in 201 participants. It is intended for adults aged 18 and over.
    
  
    Clinical linkIn-situ thresholds measured through a hearing aid or earbud are not directly interchangeable with audiometer thresholds: the transducer, the acoustic coupling (open or closed fit) and the calibration reference differ. A patient who brings an earbud audiogram should be offered a full diagnostic assessment, including bone conduction and otoscopy, particularly if there is asymmetry, sudden change, ear disease symptoms or poorer-than-moderate thresholds. Regulatory status in the USA does not carry over to India or other countries automatically.
    

  
    2.7 Tinnitus assessment software
    
  
    Psychoacoustic matching. Software presents tones or narrowband noises and lets the patient adjust frequency and level until the sound matches their tinnitus. Pitch matching uses a two-alternative "which is closer" procedure, often with octave-confusion checks (presenting the match one octave higher and lower); loudness matching adjusts the level of a tone at the matched pitch until it is judged equally loud, usually expressed in dB SL (above the threshold at that frequency). Minimum masking level and residual inhibition can also be measured. Computer control helps because frequency can be varied continuously and sessions can be repeated with identical stimuli, but the results vary considerably from session to session and matched loudness is typically low in dB SL even for distressing tinnitus. They describe the percept; they do not measure distress.
    
  
    Questionnaires. Distress and impact are measured by validated questionnaires delivered electronically. The Tinnitus Handicap Inventory (THI; Newman, Jacobson and Spitzer, 1996) has 25 items and produces a total score with severity grades. The Tinnitus Functional Index (TFI; Meikle et al., 2012) has 25 items grouped into subscales and was designed to be responsive to treatment-related change. Electronic versions must keep validated wording, response scales and scoring; translated versions need their own validation.
    
  
    Outputs and use. A tinnitus software report typically combines audiogram, pitch and loudness match, questionnaire scores and patient goals, and is repeated at intervals to track change after intervention.
    

  
    3. Adaptive psychophysical procedures in depth
    
  
    Topic 5.3 introduced the idea that a threshold is a point on a psychometric function and that transformed up-down rules target particular points. This section develops the mathematics, because it determines how many trials a program needs, how precise its result is, and what a reported "threshold" actually means.
    

  
    3.1 The psychometric function
    
  
    The psychometric function gives the probability of a correct (or "heard") response, P, as a function of stimulus level L (dB HL, dB SL or dB SNR). A convenient model is the logistic function with four parameters:
    
  
    P(L) = γ + (1 − γ − λ) / (1 + exp(−β(L − T)))
where T is the threshold parameter (the midpoint of the rising part), β the slope parameter (per dB), γ the guess rate (lower asymptote) and λ the lapse rate (1 − λ is the upper asymptote).
    
  
      
    
    • Guess rate γ. In a yes/no detection task with no false alarms, γ = 0. In an n-alternative forced-choice (nAFC) task the listener picks one of n intervals or options, so pure guessing gives 1/n: γ = 0.5 for 2AFC, 0.333 for 3AFC. In a closed-set word test with 10 alternatives, γ = 0.1; for open-set sentence recognition γ is essentially 0.
      • 
    
    • Lapse rate λ. The probability of an error at levels that should be easy: inattention, pressing the wrong key. Typically assumed to be a few percent at most. Ignoring lapses when fitting can bias slope estimates.
      • 
    
    • Slope. For the logistic, the slope at the midpoint is (1 − γ − λ)β/4 per dB. With γ = λ = 0 and β = 0.5 per dB, the midpoint slope is 0.125, that is 12.5 percentage points per dB. Speech-in-noise functions for sentences in normal-hearing listeners are steep, often in the range of about 10 to 20% per dB; pure-tone detection functions are shallower when expressed per dB and vary between listeners.
      • 
  
    
  
    Inverting the function gives the level at which any target probability p is reached (for γ = λ = 0):
    
  
    L_p = T + ln(p / (1 − p)) / β
where ln is the natural logarithm. For p = 0.5, L = T. For p = 0.707, ln(2.414) = 0.881. For p = 0.794, ln(3.85) = 1.347.
    
  
    Worked example: with T = 30 dB and β = 0.5 per dB, the 70.7% point is 30 + 0.881/0.5 = 31.8 dB, and the 79.4% point is 30 + 1.347/0.5 = 32.7 dB. If the slope were shallower, β = 0.25 per dB, the same points would be 33.5 dB and 35.4 dB. The target percentage of a procedure therefore matters more for shallow psychometric functions.
    

  
    
    
    Try it: psychometric function explorer
    
    
    Change the task (guess rate), threshold, slope and lapse rate and watch where the 50%, 70.7% and 79.4% points fall. With a 2AFC task the 50% point disappears, because performance never falls below chance.
    
    
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
    
    
    
    
    
      
    Level at 50%
    
      
    Level at 70.7%
    
      
    Level at 79.4%
    
      
    Slope at midpoint
    
    
    
    
    P(correct)target pointsguess and lapse limits
    
  
    

  
    3.2 Why adaptive procedures
    
  
    The method of constant stimuli fits the whole function but wastes most trials at levels far from threshold, where every response is predictable and carries little information. An adaptive procedure chooses each level from the responses so far, concentrating trials where the function is steep. Every adaptive procedure has four design choices: the starting level, the rule for changing level, the step size (and whether it changes), and the stopping rule with the estimation rule that produces the final number.
    

  
    3.3 Staircase methods and Levitt's transformed up-down rules
    
  
    In a simple 1-up 1-down staircase, the level goes down one step after each correct response and up one step after each incorrect one. The level drifts until "up" and "down" are equally likely, which is where P = 0.5. Levitt (1971) showed that requiring more than one consecutive correct response before stepping down moves the equilibrium point up the function:
    
  
    n-down 1-up equilibrium:  P(L)^n = 0.5
n = 1:  P = 0.500
n = 2:  P = 0.5^(1/2) = 0.707
n = 3:  P = 0.5^(1/3) = 0.794
where P(L) is the probability of a correct response at the level L around which the track settles; the level goes down only after n consecutive correct responses and up after any error.
    
  
    The reasoning: at the equilibrium level, the probability of a downward step (n correct in a row, probability P^n) must equal the probability of an upward step before that happens, so P^n = 0.5.
    
  
    Why not always use 1-up 1-down? In forced-choice tasks the 50% point is at or below chance (for 2AFC, performance is 50% with no signal at all), so a 1-up 1-down track would drift downwards without limit. The 2-down 1-up rule (70.7%) and 3-down 1-up rule (79.4%) target points on the rising part of the forced-choice function. In yes/no clinical tasks, 1-up 1-down (targeting 50%) is standard. A second reason is precision: higher target points lie where a forced-choice function is steeper, but more trials are needed per reversal.
    
  
    Reversals. A reversal is a trial on which the direction of level change switches (from going down to going up or vice versa). The track is described in reversals because the level between reversals follows a predictable run. Typical programs start with a large step (for example 8 dB) to approach threshold quickly, halve it after the first two to four reversals (to 4 dB, then 2 dB), then run a fixed number of reversals (often 6 to 12) at the final step size. The threshold is estimated as the mean of the levels at the last even number of reversals (an even number balances peaks and valleys).
    
  
    Step size. Large steps converge fast but the estimate is coarse; small steps give fine resolution but the track takes longer to reach threshold and wanders more slowly. Levitt recommended step sizes of roughly the order of the spread of the psychometric function. For pure tones, 5 dB is the clinical convention (limited partly by audiometer resolution); speech-in-noise tests use 2 dB, because their functions are steep.
    

  
    

  
    
  
    Figure 5.8b. Flowchart of an n-down 1-up staircase. With n = 1 the track converges on 50% correct, with n = 2 on 70.7% and with n = 3 on 79.4%. Reversal levels are logged, the step size D is usually reduced after the first few reversals, and the threshold is the mean of the last N (even) reversal levels.
    

  
    3.4 Worked example: a 2-down 1-up staircase
    
  
    The code below simulates a yes/no listener with a logistic function (T = 30 dB, β = 0.5 per dB) tested with a 2-down 1-up staircase starting at 40 dB with 4 dB steps, stopping after 8 reversals. In the printed track, "+" is correct and "−" is an error.
    
  
    

    import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(3)
T, beta = 30.0, 0.5                 # simulated listener: 50% point (dB), slope (1/dB)
def p_correct(L):                   # logistic, yes/no task, no guessing, no lapses
    return 1 / (1 + np.exp(-beta * (L - T)))

level, step, run = 40.0, 4.0, 0     # start 40 dB, 4 dB steps, 2-down 1-up
direction, reversals, track = 0, [], []
while len(reversals) < 8:
    ok = rng.random() < p_correct(level)
    track.append((level, ok))
    if ok:
        run += 1
        if run < 2:
            continue                # need two correct in a row before going down
        run, new_dir = 0, -1
    else:
        run, new_dir = 0, +1
    if direction and new_dir != direction:
        reversals.append(level)     # level at which the direction changed
    direction = new_dir
    level += new_dir * step

print(" ".join("%d%s" % (l, "+" if ok else "-") for l, ok in track))
print("reversal levels:", reversals)
est = np.mean(reversals[-6:])
print("estimate (mean of last 6 reversals): %.1f dB" % est)
print("true 70.7%% point: %.1f dB" % (T + np.log(0.7071 / 0.2929) / beta))
    

    40+ 40+ 36+ 36+ 32+ 32+ 28- 32+ 32- 36+ 36+ 32+ 32+ 28- 32- 36- 40+ 40+ 36+ 36+ 32+ 32- 36+ 36+ 32- 36+ 36+
reversal levels: [28.0, 36.0, 28.0, 40.0, 32.0, 36.0, 32.0, 36.0]
estimate (mean of last 6 reversals): 34.0 dB
true 70.7% point: 31.8 dB
    
  
    
  
    Trace the start: two correct at 40 dB, step down to 36; two correct, down to 32; two correct, down to 28; an error at 28 turns the track upward, so 28 dB is the first reversal. At 32 dB one correct then one error sends the track up again (no reversal, because it was already going up); at 36 dB two correct turn it downward: second reversal at 36 dB. The mean of the last six reversals (28, 40, 32, 36, 32, 36) is 204/6 = 34.0 dB, 2.2 dB above the true 70.7% point of 31.8 dB. A single short track is noisy; with 4 dB steps and eight reversals, errors of a few decibels are normal. Increasing reversals, reducing step size after the first reversals, or averaging two tracks all reduce the error at the cost of time. Try the same experiment interactively below.
    

  
    
    
    Try it: adaptive staircase simulator
    
    
    A simulated listener with a logistic psychometric function is tested with the chosen rule. Filled circles are correct responses, crosses are errors and rings mark reversals. The panel on the right shows the true psychometric function and the point the rule targets. Change the seed to repeat the experiment with a new random sequence.

    Things to notice in the simulator: with a 2AFC task and the 1-up 1-down rule, the track sinks towards the bottom of the range because 50% correct is chance performance; the same listener tested with 1-up 1-down and 3-down 1-up gives different "thresholds" because the rules target different points; larger step sizes make the estimate scatter more from seed to seed; shallow slopes increase the gap between the 50% and 79.4% points and the variability of the track.
    

  
    3.5 PEST, maximum-likelihood and Bayesian methods
    
  
    PEST (Parameter Estimation by Sequential Testing; Taylor and Creelman, 1967) runs a sequential statistical test at each level: it keeps presenting at the current level until the count of correct responses departs significantly from what is expected at the target probability, then changes level. Step size is halved at each reversal and may be doubled after several steps in the same direction. PEST converges faster than a fixed-step staircase and was an early example of an "intelligent" adaptive rule.
    
  
    Maximum-likelihood (ML) methods keep a model of the psychometric function (usually with slope, guess and lapse rates assumed) and, after each trial, compute the likelihood of all responses so far for every candidate threshold. The next trial is placed at the current most likely threshold (or at a "sweet point" that maximises information). The likelihood after k trials is the product of the probabilities of the observed responses:
    
  
    Lik(T) = Π_i P(L_i; T)^(r_i) · (1 − P(L_i; T))^(1 − r_i)
where L_i is the level of trial i, r_i = 1 for a correct response and 0 for an error, and the product runs over all trials so far. In practice log-likelihoods are summed to avoid numerical underflow.
    
  
    Bayesian methods multiply the likelihood by a prior distribution of thresholds (what is known before testing, for example the distribution of thresholds in the population) to give a posterior distribution. QUEST (Watson and Pelli, 1983) places each trial at the mode (later versions the mean) of the posterior and stops after a fixed number of trials or when the posterior is narrow enough. The posterior standard deviation is a built-in estimate of uncertainty, which a staircase does not provide. Extensions estimate slope as well as threshold, and machine-learning audiometry (for example Gaussian process methods, the approach Apple describes for its earbud hearing test) treats the whole audiogram as one function of frequency and level, choosing each tone to be maximally informative about the whole curve.
    
  
    

    import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(7)
T_true, beta, gamma = 32.0, 0.4, 0.5      # 2AFC listener: guess rate 0.5
def pc(L, T):
    return gamma + (1 - gamma) / (1 + np.exp(-beta * (L - T)))

grid = np.arange(0, 60.5, 0.5)            # candidate thresholds (dB)
log_post = np.zeros_like(grid)            # flat prior (log scale)
for trial in range(30):
    post = np.exp(log_post - log_post.max())
    post /= post.sum()
    L = float(np.sum(grid * post))        # place next trial at posterior mean
    ok = rng.random() < pc(L, T_true)
    lik = pc(L, grid) if ok else 1 - pc(L, grid)
    log_post += np.log(lik)
post = np.exp(log_post - log_post.max()); post /= post.sum()
mean = np.sum(grid * post)
sd = np.sqrt(np.sum((grid - mean) ** 2 * post))
print("estimate after 30 trials: %.1f dB (posterior SD %.1f dB)" % (mean, sd))
    

    estimate after 30 trials: 30.4 dB (posterior SD 2.0 dB)
    
  
    
  
    After 30 trials the estimate is 30.4 dB with a posterior standard deviation of 2.0 dB (true value 32 dB, within one posterior SD). The program also "knows" how uncertain it is, which lets a test stop early for a consistent listener and continue for an inconsistent one.
    
  
    
    
    MethodAssumptionsEfficiencyRobustnessTypical use
    
    
      
    Fixed-step staircase (Levitt)Monotonic function onlyModerateHigh; simple to auditSpeech-in-noise tests, APD research, training programs
    
      
    Hughson-Westlake (clinical)Monotonic; 5 dB gridModerateHigh, familiar to cliniciansManual and automated audiometry
    
      
    PESTTarget probability; sequential testBetter than fixed staircaseModerateResearch psychophysics
    
      
    ML and Bayesian (QUEST family)Function shape, slope, guess and lapse ratesHighSensitive to wrong assumptions and early lapsesResearch, modern automated audiometry, apps
    
      
    Machine-learning audiogram estimationSmoothness across frequency, function shapeVery highDepends on model; less transparentNewer automated and earbud audiometry
    
        
  
    
  
    Common errorComparing "thresholds" across programs without asking which point they target. A 1-up 1-down speech track estimates 50% intelligibility; a 2-down 1-up training task estimates 70.7%; a Hughson-Westlake audiogram is close to the 50% detection point on a 5 dB grid. These are different numbers even for the same listener.
    

  
    3.6 SNR-50 estimation in speech tests
    
  
    Adaptive tracking. In DIN and HINT-type tests the SNR changes after every item by a fixed step, so the track oscillates around SNR-50. The SRT is the mean of the SNRs presented after the initial approach. For a DIN test with a 1-up 1-down rule, 2 dB steps and 24 triplets, a typical scoring rule is the mean SNR of trials 5 to 24 (20 values); some versions include the SNR that would have been presented on trial 25, which is determined by the last response. Matrix tests often use a more elaborate procedure with step sizes that shrink as the test proceeds and word-scoring (proportion of words correct) that allows a maximum-likelihood fit of both SRT and slope.
    
  
    Fixed descending method (QuickSIN). The Spearman-Kärber formula estimates the 50% point from a descending series with equal steps and an equal number of items per step:
    
  
    SNR-50 = i + d/2 − d × (C / w)
where i is the starting (highest) SNR, d the step size in dB, C the total number of key words correct and w the number of key words per step.
    
  
    Worked example: QuickSIN starts at i = 25 dB SNR, steps of d = 5 dB, w = 5 key words per sentence. The formula becomes SNR-50 = 25 + 2.5 − 5 × C/5 = 27.5 − C. A listener who repeats C = 21 key words correctly has SNR-50 = 6.5 dB. QuickSIN reports SNR loss, the SNR-50 relative to the average for normal-hearing listeners (about 2 dB on this material), which gives the published shortcut SNR loss = 25.5 − C: here 25.5 − 21 = 4.5 dB. Scoring two lists and averaging improves reliability.
    

  
    
    
    Try it: digits-in-noise adaptive track
    
    
    A simulated listener with a chosen true SRT and psychometric slope hears 24 triplets. The SNR starts at 0 dB and moves 2 dB down after a correct triplet and 2 dB up after an error. The estimate is the mean SNR of trials 5 to 24 (a typical scoring rule). The cut-off is illustrative only; real cut-offs come from validation of each language version and device.
    
    
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
    
    
    
    
    
      
    Estimated SRT (trials 5 to 24)
    
      
    Error (estimate − true)
    
      
    Triplets correct
    
      
    Result (illustrative)
    
    
    
    
    triplet correcttriplet incorrectestimated SRTillustrative cut-off
    
  
    
  
    Run several seeds for the same listener: the estimate typically varies by a decibel or so around the true SRT with a steep slope, and more with a shallow slope. A listener whose true SRT lies near the cut-off may pass on one run and be referred on the next. This is why screening programmes define what to do with borderline results (for example retest once, then refer) and why validation studies report test-retest reliability.
    

  
    4. Computer-based intervention in depth
    

  
    4.1 Principles of auditory training
    
  
    Neuroplasticity. The auditory system changes with experience throughout life. After hearing loss, and again after fitting a hearing aid or cochlear implant, the brain must learn to use a changed input. Perceptual learning studies show that repeated, attentive practice with feedback improves discrimination and identification, particularly when the task is difficult but achievable. Training is intended to accelerate and direct this learning.
    
  
    Adaptive difficulty. Programs keep the learner near a target accuracy, commonly around 70 to 80% correct, by adjusting SNR, the similarity of alternatives, talker variability or speaking rate. The adaptive rules are the staircases of section 3: a 2-down 1-up rule on SNR keeps performance around 70.7%. Too easy, and there is little learning signal; too hard, and motivation collapses.
    
  
    Feedback. Trial-by-trial feedback (correct or incorrect, with replay of the item and display of the correct answer) supports learning of the mapping between sound and category. Some programs add audiovisual replay so the learner can link the sound to the speaker's face.
    
  
    Motivation and engagement. Progress charts, levels, points and reminders help sustain practice. Game design must support the listening goal rather than distract from it.
    
  
    Transfer and generalisation. On-task learning is improvement on the trained task and materials. Transfer (near transfer) is improvement on untrained materials or conditions of the same kind, for example new sentences in noise. Generalisation (far transfer) is improvement in untrained abilities or in everyday listening and communication, measured by different tests, questionnaires or real-life outcomes. The value of training depends on generalisation; on-task learning alone may only reflect familiarity with the program.
    

  
    4.2 Bottom-up and top-down training
    
  
    
    
    Analytic (bottom-up)
      
      
    • Units: phonemes, syllables, vowel and consonant contrasts, environmental sounds, pitch and timing cues.
      • 
      
    • Tasks: same/different discrimination, closed-set identification, minimal pairs (for example /pa/ vs /ba/, /s/ vs /∫/).
      • 
      
    • Rationale: sharpen use of acoustic cues degraded by hearing loss or implant processing.
      • 
      
    • Risk: limited transfer to connected speech if trained in isolation.
      • 
    
    
    
    Synthetic (top-down)
      
      
    • Units: words in context, sentences, passages, conversations.
      • 
      
    • Tasks: open-set sentence repetition, comprehension questions, tracking stories in noise.
      • 
      
    • Rationale: train use of context, prediction, working memory and attention, and communication repair strategies.
      • 
      
    • Risk: learners may rely on guessing; progress is harder to measure precisely.
      • 
    
    
  
    
  
    Most current programs combine both, and many add cognitive components (working memory, speed of processing) and communication-strategy education. Training materials should reflect the learner's language; for Indian clients, programs recorded only in American English will have limited relevance for someone who converses mainly in Malayalam, Tamil or Hindi, and locally produced materials matter.
    

  
    

  
    
  
    Figure 5.8c. The auditory training loop. The inner loop (teal) runs every trial and holds the learner near a target accuracy. The session log feeds periodic untrained outcome tests, which are the evidence of transfer and generalisation, and the clinician review decides whether to continue, change or stop.
    

  
    4.3 Examples of training programs and apps
    
  
    
    
    ExamplePopulationContentNotes on evidence and access
    
    
      
    LACE (Listening and Communication Enhancement)Adults with hearing aidsHome-based adaptive training: speech in babble, competing speaker, time-compressed speech, auditory memory and missing-word tasks, plus communication-strategy tipsDeveloped by Sweetow and Sabes; an RCT across five sites with 65 participants reported improvements on most outcome measures (Sweetow and Sabes, 2006). Later reviews judged the wider evidence for such programs as limited.
    
      
    Angel SoundCochlear implant and hearing-aid users, auditory processing difficulties, second-language learnersPhoneme, environmental sound, music and speech-in-noise modules with adaptive difficultyDeveloped by TigerSpeech Technology and distributed free by the Emily Shannon Fu Foundation; PC-based with web and mobile editions.
    
      
    Cochlear CoPilot (Cochlear)Adult cochlear implant recipientsHearing skills training exercises and progress tracking, launched in 2021Manufacturer rehabilitation app; check regional availability.
    
      
    Manufacturer educational and rehabilitation apps for childrenChildren with hearing aids or cochlear implants and their familiesListening games, parent guidance, vocabulary activitiesSeveral implant manufacturers publish such apps; content is usually in English and a few other major languages.
    
      
    Clinic-built or research programs (for example in Python, MATLAB or web frameworks)Any, including Indian-language usersLocally recorded words and sentences with adaptive SNRNeeds local validation; see section 7 on design.
    
        
  
    
  
    Examples only; availability and features change; check current information.
    

  
    4.4 Music training
    
  
    Music perception is often poor with cochlear implants because pitch is coded coarsely and spectral detail is limited. Music training programs (as modules in some general training software or as separate apps) use melodic contour identification, instrument identification, rhythm tasks and familiar melody recognition, usually with adaptive difficulty such as semitone spacing in contour tasks. Research on music training in implant users and in older adults with hearing loss shows on-task improvements; effects on speech perception and quality of life are less certain and are an active research area. Clinically, music training is often offered for enjoyment and engagement, with realistic expectations discussed.
    

  
    4.5 Cochlear implant rehabilitation apps
    
  
    Implant manufacturers provide remote-control and rehabilitation apps alongside fitting software. The rehabilitation components typically include graded listening exercises (environmental sounds, closed-set words, sentences, telephone speech, music), progress displays and material for families of child recipients. They supplement, and do not replace, structured auditory-verbal therapy or clinic-based rehabilitation, particularly for children. Evaluate each app for its language, the audiological level it starts from (detection, discrimination, identification, comprehension) and whether results can be shared with the clinic.
    

  
    4.6 Tinnitus management apps
    
  
    Sound therapy apps provide environmental sounds, noise bands and music, often adjustable in pitch and level, sometimes streamed to hearing aids. Hearing-aid manufacturers publish such apps, for example ReSound Tinnitus Relief and Beltone Tinnitus Calmer. CBT-based apps deliver structured cognitive behavioural therapy content (education, relaxation, attention and thought exercises) for tinnitus distress; Oto is an example of an app offering a CBT-based tinnitus programme. Cognitive behavioural therapy has the strongest evidence base among tinnitus psychological interventions when delivered by trained professionals, and internet-delivered CBT has been studied in trials; the evidence for any specific app must be checked separately. A study of tinnitus apps (Sereda et al., 2019, JMIR mHealth and uHealth) combined a user survey with quality assessment and content analysis and found wide variation in app content and quality.
    
  
    SafetyTinnitus with sudden onset, pulsatile quality, unilateral symptoms, vertigo or significant distress (including any mention of self-harm) needs medical and audiological assessment and appropriate referral, not only an app. Check maximum output of sound therapy apps played through earphones for long periods.
    

  
    4.7 Speechreading and audiovisual training software
    
  
    Speechreading (lipreading) training software presents video of talkers with sound off, at reduced level, or in noise, and asks the learner to identify visemes (groups of speech sounds that look alike on the lips, such as /p b m/), words or sentences. Because many phonemes share visemes, training emphasises combining visual cues with residual hearing and context. Good programs use many talkers, natural speaking rates, varied lighting and angles, and allow the learner to replay items. For Indian clients, the talkers and material should match the languages and speaking styles the learner encounters.
    

  
    4.8 Apps for children and auditory-verbal therapy support
    
  
    Apps for young children with hearing loss usually support a family-centred programme: listening games graded through detection, discrimination, identification and comprehension; vocabulary and language activities; parent coaching videos; and logs of device use and listening hours. In auditory-verbal therapy (AVT) the parent is the primary agent of change, so apps are most useful as tools for the parent between sessions, not as child-alone screen activities. Game-based assessment also exists: Sound Scouts, an Australian app, embeds hearing tests for school-age children in a game, and its development was published in the International Journal of Audiology (Dillon et al., 2018). Screen-time guidance for young children should be considered when recommending any app.
    

  
    4.9 Dose and adherence
    
  
    Training programs prescribe a dose: for example 20 to 30 minutes per session, five sessions a week, for several weeks. Real adherence is often lower, and completion drops with time. Computer delivery gives exact adherence data (sessions started, completed, time on task), which should be reviewed at follow-up. Studies that report outcomes only for completers overestimate effect; intention-to-treat analysis includes everyone who started. Practical measures that help adherence: agreed goals, short sessions, reminders, clinician contact during the programme, content in the user's language, and tasks at the right difficulty.
    

  
    4.10 Evidence summary
    
  
    Henshaw and Ferguson's systematic review of individual computer-based auditory training for adults with hearing loss (PLOS ONE, 2013) examined 13 articles. On-task improvement was reported in 9 of the 10 articles that reported it; generalisation to untrained speech intelligibility measures was reported in 11 of 13, but the authors described the improvements as small and not robust, rated the study quality as very low to moderate, and concluded that the evidence was not robust enough to reliably guide intervention at that time. Subsequent reviews and trials have kept the same broad picture: consistent on-task learning, variable and generally modest transfer, and a need for larger, well-controlled trials with everyday outcomes. Evidence in cochlear implant recipients, children and people with auditory processing difficulties must be judged separately and for each outcome.
    
  
    Clinical linkPresent auditory training to clients honestly: it is likely to improve performance on the exercises and may help some people in daily listening, especially with engagement and counselling alongside it. Measure an untrained outcome (a speech-in-noise test not used in training, and a questionnaire) before and after, so that each client's benefit is documented.
    

  
    5. Tele-audiology platforms and remote programming
    
  
    Tele-audiology applies the programs above across a network. Unit 2 covers tele-diagnosis (2.5) and tele-rehabilitation (2.6) in detail; here the focus is on the software components.
    
  
      
    
    • Synchronous (real-time) remote testing. The audiologist controls a computer-based audiometer, OAE or ABR system at a remote site through remote desktop or vendor-provided remote-control software, with a video link to the patient and a facilitator. Requirements: calibrated equipment at the remote site, adequate network latency and stability, and a facilitator trained in transducer placement.
      • 
    
    • Asynchronous (store-and-forward). Tests are run locally by a facilitator or automatically (tablet audiometry, DIN, video otoscopy images), results are uploaded, and the audiologist reviews later. Suits screening programmes in villages and schools.
      • 
    
    • Remote hearing-aid programming. Manufacturers offer remote fine-tuning in which the audiologist's fitting software connects through a cloud service to the patient's phone app, which relays settings to the hearing aids; some also allow the patient to send a request describing a problem and receive new settings later. Remote cochlear implant programming has also been used, with the programming interface at the remote site.
      • 
    
    • Remote rehabilitation. Video sessions for counselling and auditory-verbal therapy, plus home training programs whose logs are visible to the clinician.
      • 
  
    
  
    Software design issues specific to tele-audiology: identity verification of patients and clinicians; logging of who changed what setting and when; fallback if the connection drops during programming (the hearing aid must keep a safe setting); bandwidth-adaptive video; data residency and consent; and documentation that the test conditions (facilitator, room noise, equipment) were adequate. Practical P13 asks you to design such a service.
    

  
    6. Accessibility and assistive software
    
  
    Beyond assessment and training, everyday software provides access to sound for people with hearing loss. Audiologists should know these tools and include them in rehabilitation plans.
    
  
    
    
    FunctionExamplesHow it worksClinical considerations
    
    
      
    Live transcription of conversationGoogle Live Transcribe (Android, released 2019)Automatic speech recognition (ASR) converts speech from the phone microphone to on-screen text in real timeAccuracy falls with noise, distance, accents and overlapping talkers; check which Indian languages are supported; privacy of recorded speech.
    
      
    Captions for media and calls on the deviceLive Caption (Android), Live Captions (iOS and macOS)On-device ASR captions any audio playing on the device, including videos and callsLanguage support varies by platform and region; useful for video calls with family and online teaching.
    
      
    Sound recognition alertsSound Notifications (in Google's Live Transcribe and Notification app, added 2020); Sound Recognition (iOS, from iOS 14)A machine-learning classifier on the device listens for sounds such as alarms, doorbells, a baby crying or water running, and shows a notification or vibrationA supplement, never a replacement, for certified alerting devices such as visual smoke alarms; false negatives are possible.
    
      
    Relay and captioned telephonyText relay, captioned telephone and video relay services where availableA human operator or ASR converts between speech and text or sign language during callsAvailability differs by country; check local services and emergency-call options.
    
      
    Hearing-aid streaming and settingsMade for iPhone hearing aids, Android ASHA (Audio Streaming for Hearing Aids), Bluetooth LE Audio and Auracast broadcast audioPhone audio streamed directly to hearing aids or implant processors; phone accessibility menus control streaming, audio balance and hearing-device settingsCompatibility depends on hearing aid, phone model and OS version; streaming uses battery; check with the manufacturer's compatibility list.
