    Examples only; availability and features change; check current information.
    
  
    ASR-based captions are now good enough in quiet one-to-one settings to change daily life for many deaf and hard-of-hearing people, and in classrooms and meetings they can be combined with a remote microphone placed near the talker to improve both hearing-aid input and captioning. Their errors are not random: names, technical terms, code-switched speech (mixing, for example, Malayalam and English in one sentence) and noisy rooms cause most failures. Teach clients to use them as one tool among several.
    

  
    7. Designing and building a computer-based test: the engineering view
    
  
    This section describes how a hearing test program is put together, so that audiology students can specify one, work with developers, build a research prototype (Practical P14) and recognise design faults in commercial products. The example throughout is a pure-tone or speech-in-noise test on a PC or tablet.
    

  
    7.1 Software architecture
    
  
    

  
    
  
    Figure 5.8d. Software architecture of a computer-based hearing test. The adaptive logic chooses what to play; the stimulus engine creates it; the calibration module converts the requested dB HL or SNR into a digital level; the audio output delivers it; responses (with timing and noise-monitor flags) return to the adaptive logic and are stored; reporting and export produce the clinical record.
    
  
      
    
    1. User interface (UI). Instructions (text, images, spoken and signed video where appropriate), practice trials, response buttons, progress display. Designed for the target users (section 8) and the tester.
      2. 
    
    2. Adaptive logic. The procedure: staircase, Hughson-Westlake, Bayesian. Holds the state (current level, reversals, posterior), applies stopping rules and computes the result. Should be a separate, testable module, verified by simulation with synthetic listeners (as in the demos above) before any human testing.
      3. 
    
    3. Stimulus engine. Generates tones with rise and fall ramps (to avoid spectral splatter), selects recorded speech items, mixes speech and noise at the requested SNR, applies randomisation, and ensures the output buffer never clips.
      4. 
    
    4. Calibration module. Stores the calibration table (the SPL produced at a reference digital level per frequency, per ear, per transducer model and device), the RETSPLs, and the maximum and minimum permitted levels; converts every request into a digital amplitude; refuses to play levels outside the verified range.
      5. 
    
    5. Audio output. The operating system audio API, sound card or external audio interface, headphone amplifier and transducer. The program must fix the sample rate and volume, disable system effects where possible and detect changes of output device (for example a Bluetooth headset connecting mid-test).
      6. 
    
    6. Response capture. Records which response, when (timestamp), and in which listening window; detects responses outside the window as possible false responses.
      7. 
    
    7. Noise monitor. If present, measures ambient noise through a microphone (ideally calibrated) in the bands relevant to the current test frequency, and pauses or flags trials when noise exceeds the permitted level.
      8. 
    
    8. Data storage. Stores results together with conditions (device, transducer, calibration date, software version, noise flags, tester), encrypted, with access control and an audit log.
      9. 
    
    9. Reporting and export. Audiogram or SRT with reliability indicators and plain-language interpretation appropriate to the purpose (screening or diagnosis); export to clinic records in agreed formats.
      10. 
  
    

  
    7.2 The audio output chain and calibration procedure
    
  
    Every sample the program writes is a number between −1 and +1 (full scale). Levels in the digital domain are expressed in dBFS (decibels relative to full scale). A sine wave that just reaches full scale has an RMS level of −3.01 dBFS (RMS equals peak divided by √2), which is the maximum RMS level a tone can have without clipping. Calibration links dBFS to dB SPL in a coupler, and RETSPLs link dB SPL to dB HL.
    
  
    

  
    
  
    Figure 5.8e. The calibration chain. The acoustic path (top) is measured once per device and transducer at each frequency; the three calculation steps (bottom) are then applied by the calibration module to every presentation.
    
  
    S(f)       = L_SPL,meas(f) − L_dBFS,ref          (dB SPL at 0 dBFS)
L_SPL,target = L_HL + RETSPL(f)
L_dBFS       = L_SPL,target − S(f)
where L_dBFS,ref is the RMS digital level of the calibration tone, L_SPL,meas is the level read on the sound level meter with the transducer on the coupler, and RETSPL(f) is the reference equivalent threshold SPL for that transducer and coupler at frequency f.
    
  
    RETSPL concept. 0 dB HL is defined as the median threshold of young otologically normal listeners. For a given transducer on a given coupler, the SPL in the coupler that corresponds to 0 dB HL is the RETSPL. It differs by frequency (it is much larger at 250 Hz than at 1 kHz because the ear is less sensitive at low frequencies) and by transducer and coupler type (supra-aural, circumaural and insert earphones have different tables). Standardised tables exist in the ISO 389 series for common audiometric transducers; for consumer headphones not in the standards, developers derive equivalent values by measuring thresholds of a group of normal-hearing young adults, which is a research study in itself.
    
  
    Worked example (illustrative numbers): a 1 kHz calibration tone at −12 dBFS RMS produces 95.0 dB SPL in the coupler, so S = 95 − (−12) = 107 dB SPL. With an illustrative RETSPL of 7 dB at 1 kHz, 20 dB HL requires 27 dB SPL, so the digital level is 27 − 107 = −80 dBFS RMS. The peak is 3.01 dB higher, −77.0 dBFS, an amplitude of 0.000141, which is about 4.6 quantisation steps at 16 bits (poor) but about 1186 steps at 24 bits (adequate). The maximum RMS level of a sine is −3.01 dBFS, so the maximum is 107 − 3.01 = 104 dB SPL, or 97 dB HL at 1 kHz for this chain.
    
  
    Calibration procedure (for a developer or a clinic verifying an app):
    
  
      
    
    1. Fix the chain: device model, operating system version, audio output route (wired), system volume position recorded by the app, audio enhancements off, sample rate fixed.
      2. 
    
    2. Mount the transducer on the correct coupler or ear simulator (IEC 60318 series) with the specified static force for supra-aural or circumaural headphones or the correct adaptor for inserts; connect a Class 1 sound level meter.
      3. 
    
    3. Play a pure tone at a known digital level (for example −20 dBFS RMS) at each test frequency, with the meter on the appropriate frequency weighting (flat or Z) and averaging; record the SPL. Repeat for each ear's transducer. Check the background noise reading is at least 10 dB below the measured level.
      4. 
    
    4. Compute S(f) for each frequency and ear, store it in the calibration table with date, equipment serial numbers and operator.
      5. 
    
    5. Verify: request several dB HL values, measure, and confirm the error is within tolerance (audiometer standards allow a few dB); check linearity by stepping down in 10 dB steps and confirming 10 dB changes until the meter's noise floor is reached; check for distortion at the highest level (total harmonic distortion within the audiometer standard's limit) and for crosstalk between ears.
      6. 
    
    6. Repeat the verification periodically, after any hardware change or operating-system update, and keep records. Between formal calibrations, perform daily listening checks.
      7. 
  
    

  
    
    
    Try it: calibration chain calculator
    
    
    Enter the calibration measurement (the SPL measured at a reference digital level) and the target hearing level. The calculator gives the digital level needed and warns if it would clip or approach the noise floor. RETSPL values are illustrative, similar in size to published supra-aural headphone values; always use the tabulated values for your transducer and coupler.
    
    
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
      
    
    
    
    
    
    
      
    S (SPL at 0 dBFS)
    
      
    Target SPL
    
      
    Required level (RMS)
    
      
    Peak amplitude in steps
    
      
    Usable range at this frequency
    
      
    Status
    
    
    
    
    required dBFS vs dB HLclipping limit (−3.01 dBFS RMS)noise floor + 10 dB margintarget
    
  
    

  
    7.3 Timing accuracy and latency
    
  
    Several tests depend on timing: gap detection needs sample-accurate gaps; dichotic tests need the two channels aligned; response-time windows need to know when the sound actually reached the ear; auditory training with audiovisual material needs lip-sync. Sources of timing error:
    
  
      
    
    • Output latency. The delay between the program's play command and sound at the transducer, set by audio buffer sizes and drivers; typically a few to tens of milliseconds on wired outputs, and often a hundred milliseconds or more over Bluetooth, varying between devices and connections.
      • 
    
    • Jitter. Variation in latency from trial to trial. Scheduling sounds on the audio clock (for example Web Audio's scheduled start times) instead of timers reduces it.
      • 
    
    • Input latency. Touch screens and keyboards add their own delays to response timestamps.
      • 
    
    • Resampling and processing. If the program's sample rate differs from the device rate, the operating system resamples, which can smear short gaps and alter levels slightly.
      • 
  
    
  
    Measure latency by recording the transducer output and a response trigger together (for example a microphone at the earphone and a loopback channel into an audio interface), and report it with the test. Build response windows with a margin for measured latency.
    

  
    7.4 Randomisation
    
  
    Randomise the order of items, the timing between presentations (to stop listeners responding to a rhythm), the position of catch trials, and in forced-choice tasks the interval containing the target. Use a pseudo-random generator seeded per session and store the seed, so that any session can be reproduced exactly for audit and debugging. For research, counterbalance list order and ear order across participants. For speech lists, confirm that lists are equivalent in difficulty before randomising which list each person receives.
    

  
    7.5 Data security and privacy
    
  
    Hearing results, voice recordings and questionnaire answers are personal data about health. In India, the Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023 (with the DPDP Rules notified in 2025) governs digital personal data. In outline, a program that collects data about Indian users should obtain free, specific, informed consent through a clear notice; use data only for the stated purpose; allow users to access, correct and erase their data and withdraw consent; protect data with reasonable security safeguards; notify breaches as required; and, for children's data, obtain verifiable parental consent. Clinical institutions also have their own policies and ethics committee requirements for research. Engineering measures include: encryption in transit (TLS) and at rest, minimal data collection (no location or contacts unless needed), pseudonymous identifiers in analytics, role-based access and audit logs, data retention limits, and a documented list of third-party services (cloud hosting, crash reporting, analytics) and where they store data.
    

  
    7.6 Usability testing
    
  
    Usability engineering for medical device software (IEC 62366-1) asks developers to identify use errors and test the design with representative users. For a hearing test app this means: formative testing with small groups during development (observe older adults, children and first-time smartphone users attempting the test without help, and note errors such as headphones reversed, volume changed, test started in a noisy room, result misunderstood); then summative testing of the final design on the critical tasks. Record completion rate, time, errors and user satisfaction. Test instructions in each language version, and with users who have the degree of hearing loss the app is meant to detect (who may not hear spoken instructions).
    

  
    7.7 Validation study design
    
  
    

  
    
  
    Figure 5.8f. Design of a validation study for a computer-based hearing test. The index test (the new program) and the reference test are given to the same people in randomised order, each scored without knowledge of the other; a retest of the index test gives reliability; the paired results give agreement and diagnostic accuracy.
    
  
      
    
    • Population and setting. Recruit people representative of the intended use: the same ages, degrees of hearing loss, languages and settings (for example a school, a primary health centre). A study in university students in a sound booth does not validate village screening by health workers.
      • 
    
    • Reference standard. Usually diagnostic audiometry by an audiologist with calibrated equipment in a suitable room. Define "hearing loss" before starting (for example a four-frequency average above a stated level in either ear).
      • 
    
    • Blinding and order. Testers and scorers of each test should not know the other result; randomise which test is done first to balance learning and fatigue.
      • 
    
    • Accuracy. For screening, report sensitivity and specificity with 95% confidence intervals, and a receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve if the cut-off is to be chosen from the data (then confirm the cut-off in a separate sample). Predictive values depend on prevalence and should be reported for the target setting.
      • 
    
    • Agreement. For threshold or SRT measures, correlation is not agreement. Use Bland-Altman analysis: plot the difference (index minus reference) against the mean of the two; report the bias (mean difference) and the 95% limits of agreement (bias ± 1.96 × SD of differences). Also report the percentage of thresholds within 5 and within 10 dB.
      • 
    
    • Reliability. Repeat the index test after a set interval; report intraclass correlation (ICC), the SD of test-retest differences, and the smallest change that exceeds measurement error.
      • 
    
    • Sample size. Plan it so that confidence intervals are narrow enough to be useful; for sensitivity this depends on the number of people with hearing loss, not the total sample.
      • 
    
    • Reporting. Follow a reporting guideline for diagnostic accuracy studies (STARD) and state the software version, device, transducer, calibration and environment.
      • 
  
    
  
    

    import numpy as np
ref = np.array([10, 25, 40, 15, 55, 30, 20, 45, 35, 60])   # clinical audiometer, dB HL
app = np.array([15, 25, 45, 10, 55, 35, 25, 45, 30, 70])   # tablet app, same ears, dB HL
d = app - ref
bias = d.mean()
sd = d.std(ddof=1)
print("differences:", d)
print("bias (mean difference): %.1f dB" % bias)
print("SD of differences: %.2f dB" % sd)
print("95%% limits of agreement: %.1f to %.1f dB" % (bias - 1.96 * sd, bias + 1.96 * sd))
print("within 10 dB: %d of %d" % (np.sum(np.abs(d) <= 10), len(d)))
    

    differences: [ 5  0  5 -5  0  5  5  0 -5 10]
bias (mean difference): 2.0 dB
SD of differences: 4.83 dB
95% limits of agreement: -7.5 to 11.5 dB
within 10 dB: 10 of 10
    
  
    
  
    Interpretation: the app reads on average 2 dB higher (poorer) than the audiometer, and 95% of differences are expected between −7.5 and +11.5 dB. All ten differences are within 10 dB, but the limits of agreement show that differences of more than 10 dB are expected occasionally. Whether this is acceptable depends on purpose: probably acceptable for screening, borderline for diagnostic use where manual test-retest differences are usually within about 5 to 10 dB. Real studies need far more than ten pairs.
    

  
    7.8 Regulatory pathway concept
    
  
    If the program is intended to screen for, diagnose, monitor or treat hearing loss or tinnitus, it is likely to be regulated as a medical device software (SaMD, or software in a medical device when it runs inside one). Regulators classify it by risk and require a quality management system (ISO 13485), a software life-cycle process (IEC 62304), risk management (ISO 14971), usability engineering (IEC 62366-1), cybersecurity and clinical evidence proportionate to the risk. In India, medical devices are regulated by the Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO) under the Medical Devices Rules, 2017, which use risk classes A to D, and CDSCO has issued guidance specifically on medical device software. In the USA the FDA uses 510(k), De Novo and premarket approval pathways (the AirPods Pro hearing aid software above used De Novo); in the European Union the Medical Device Regulation (EU MDR) applies, with a conformity assessment by a notified body for most classes. Research prototypes used under ethics approval in a study are handled under clinical investigation rules, not as marketed devices. The practical lesson for students: decide the intended use first, because it drives the evidence, documentation and approvals needed.
    

  
    8. Evaluation framework
    
  
    The checklist in 5.3 gives domains and red flags. The expanded version below is designed for a written review. Score each item 0 (not met or no information), 1 (partly met) or 2 (met with evidence); record the evidence and its source (manufacturer document, peer-reviewed paper, your own test); and treat a 0 in any item marked "critical" as a barrier to clinical use for that purpose.
    
  
    
    
    DomainQuestionsEvidence to seekCritical?
    
    
      
    Purpose and intended useAssessment, screening, intervention, monitoring or information? Stated claims?Instructions for use, app store description, regulatory labellingYes
    
      
    Target populationAges, degree and type of hearing loss, cognitive and literacy requirements, exclusionsStated population; validation sample characteristicsYes
    
      
    Languages, including Indian languagesWhich languages for instructions and for test or training material? Local accents and vocabulary?Language list; language-specific norms and validationYes for speech-based tests
    
      
    HardwareSupported devices, OS versions, transducers; wired or wireless; minimum specificationCompatibility list; behaviour when an unsupported device is connectedYes for assessment
    
      
    CalibrationHow is output level set and verified? Range? Recalibration interval? Volume locking?Calibration method, RETSPL basis, maximum output, verification you perform with a couplerYes for threshold tests
    
      
    Test environmentNoise monitoring? Required room conditions?Maximum permissible ambient noise; noise flags in reportsYes for threshold tests
    
      
    Procedure and algorithmWhich method (Hughson-Westlake, staircase, Bayesian)? Catch trials? Stopping rules? Target point?Technical description, publicationsNo, but required for interpretation
    
      
    Evidence of validityAccuracy against a reference in a comparable population; agreement statisticsPeer-reviewed studies with sensitivity, specificity, Bland-Altman results; independence of authorsYes
    
      
    ReliabilityTest-retest reliability? Quality indicators?ICC, retest differences, reliability flagsYes for monitoring
    
      
    UsabilityCan intended users complete it unaided? Practice items?Usability study; your own observation of two or three representative usersNo
    
      
    AccessibilityText and visual instructions, font scaling, screen reader support, colour contrast, motor demandsAccessibility statement, WCAG conformance, your own checkNo
    
      
    Data privacy and securityWhat is collected, where stored, who has access, third parties, deletion, consent, compliance with DPDP ActPrivacy policy, consent screens, data flow descriptionYes
    
      
    Outputs and integrationClear report with conditions? Referral advice? Export to clinic records?Sample report, export formatsNo
    
      
    CostPurchase, subscription, per-test fees, hardware, calibration and training costsPrice list, total cost over expected lifeNo
    
      
    Support and maintenanceUpdates, bug fixes, local support, training material, longevity of the developerUpdate history, support contacts, service agreementsNo
    
      
    Regulatory statusAuthorised as a medical device for this use in India or elsewhere?Registration or clearance details matching the claimed useYes for clinical claims
    
        
  
    

  
    Worked evaluation: hearWHO as a screening and awareness app
    
  
    The worked example uses only information published by WHO on its hearWHO question-and-answer page at the time of writing; students must check current information before relying on any item.
    
  
    
    
    DomainFindingsScore
    
    
      
    PurposeA hearing check for the public that encourages people with likely hearing loss to seek professional testing; not a diagnostic test.2
    
      
    Target populationIntended for people aged 12 years and above.2
    
      
    LanguagesWHO lists Chinese, English, Spanish, Russian and Dutch; no Indian language listed there. Digits are familiar to most Indian English speakers, but users more comfortable in other languages may be disadvantaged.1
    
      
    HardwareSmartphone with earphones; digits-in-noise design reduces dependence on absolute level.1
    
      
    CalibrationRelative-level (SNR) test; absolute calibration not required by design, but the user must set a comfortable audible level.1
    
      
    ProcedureDigits-in-noise: 23 sets of three digits over background noise; score 0 to 100 with advice bands (below 50: likely hearing loss; 50 to 75: annual checks; above 75: good hearing).2
    
      
    EvidenceWHO states that digits-in-noise testing has sensitivity and specificity above 85%; students should locate peer-reviewed validation of the app version and language they would use, since performance depends on version and population.1
    
      
    PrivacyTo be checked by the student from the app's current privacy policy (what is stored on the phone, what is shared).not scored
    
      
    Cost and supportFree public-health app from WHO.2
    
      
    Overall judgementSuitable for awareness and self-screening, with a clear message that a low score needs full assessment and a good score does not exclude hearing loss (for example high-frequency or unilateral loss). Not a substitute for audiometry, and language coverage should be checked for each client.
    
        
  
    

  
    9. Clinical applications, workflow, limitations and current developments
    
  
    9.1 Clinical applications and workflow integration
    
  
      
    
    • Community screening camps. A typical workflow: registration on a tablet (consent, demographic data, pseudonymous ID); otoscopy (with video otoscope images stored for remote review); screening by tablet audiometry at fixed screening levels or by DIN; automatic referral list with contact numbers; results synced to a central server when connectivity allows; audiologist reviews refer cases and ambiguous results; follow-up tracked.
      • 
    
    • Schools. Pure-tone screening with noise-monitored boothless systems in the quietest available room; game-based tests for younger children; referral letters generated automatically in the parents' language; results linked to the school health programme with parental consent.
      • 
    
    • Industry. Occupational hearing conservation programmes need baseline and annual audiograms with consistent procedures and reliable records. Automated and boothless audiometers support testing on site; software flags significant threshold shifts according to the programme's criteria for audiologist review.
      • 
    
    • Clinic efficiency. Automated air-conduction audiometry run by an assistant while the audiologist sees another patient; the audiologist then reviews, completes bone conduction and masking and handles difficult cases.
      • 
    
    • Tele-rehabilitation. Home auditory training with logs visible to the clinician, remote fine-tuning, video counselling; see 2.6.
      • 
    
    • Research. Custom programs in Python or MATLAB for speech-in-noise studies, perceptual learning and psychometric function estimation, using the calibration and validation methods of section 7.
      • 
  
    

  
    9.2 Limitations and troubleshooting
    
  
    
    
    ProblemLikely causeWhat to check or do
    
    
      
    Staircase estimate drifts to the bottom of the range1-up 1-down rule used with a forced-choice task (50% is chance)Use 2-down 1-up or 3-down 1-up for forced choice; check the task's guess rate.
    
      
    Very different "thresholds" from two programs for the same listenerDifferent target points, step sizes, stimuli or transducersCompare procedures and targets before comparing numbers; test with a simulated listener.
    
      
    Bayesian test ends quickly with an implausible resultEarly lapse (missed response) combined with a model that assumes no lapses; strong priorInclude a lapse parameter; use a broad prior; inspect the trial log; retest.
    
      
    Gap-detection thresholds poorer than expectedAudio resampling or processing smearing gaps; Bluetooth codecUse wired output at the file's native sample rate; record the output and inspect the gap.
    
      
    Test fails after operating-system updateChanged audio routing, volume behaviour or permissionsRe-verify calibration after every update; delay updates on test devices until verified.
    
      
    DIN results poor in listeners with normal audiogramsLanguage unfamiliarity, attention, noisy environment, device processingCheck language suitability and instructions; repeat after practice; test in a quiet room.
    
      
    Training scores plateau earlyDifficulty adaptation too slow or too fast; task too easyCheck adaptive rule and step size; add variety; review goals.
    
      
    Low adherence to home trainingSessions too long, unclear goals, language mismatch, technical problemsShorter sessions, reminders, clinician check-ins, local-language material, simpler setup.
    
      
    Captions unreliable in a meetingDistance from talker, noise, overlapping talkers, code-switchingPlace the phone or a remote microphone near the talker; ask talkers to take turns.
    
      
    Remote programming session dropsNetwork instabilityConfirm the aid keeps a safe program; reconnect; schedule when the patient has stable connectivity.
    
        
  
    

  
    9.3 Current developments
    
  
      
    
    • Machine-learning and Bayesian audiometry that estimates a full audiogram from fewer presentations by treating it as a continuous function of frequency and level; this approach is now used in consumer earbud testing (see section 3.5 and 2.7).
      • 
    
    • Smartphone-based testing extending from screening towards diagnostic use with calibrated transducers and noise monitoring, and wider adoption of digits-in-noise variants (including antiphasic versions) in more languages.
      • 
    
    • Hearing functions built into wearables: earbuds with authorised hearing-aid and hearing-test features, and hearing protection functions, raising questions about how in-situ results relate to clinical audiometry.
      • 
    
    • Gamification of tests and training for children and adults, with attention to whether game elements affect measurement validity.
      • 
    
    • Integration of device data logs, questionnaires and remote tests into clinic records for continuous monitoring between appointments.
      • 
    
    • Regulatory frameworks specific to medical device software and AI-enabled software, including recent CDSCO guidance in India.
      • 
  
    

  
    Demonstration
    
  
    Equipment and software: a computer-based or tablet audiometer with its specified headphones; a sound level meter with an appropriate coupler; a smartphone with a DIN screening app; a laptop with a web browser for the demos on this page; Python or MATLAB; a hearing-aid fitting software demonstration mode with NOAH (or a screenshot walk-through); a phone with live captions and sound recognition enabled; access to one auditory training program.
    
  
      
    
    1. Open the landscape figure and ask students to place five programs they know (from their phones or the clinic) on the three axes. Students should observe that purpose, platform and regulatory status are independent.
      2. 
    
    2. Run the psychometric function explorer. Show the 50%, 70.7% and 79.4% points for yes/no and 2AFC tasks. Students should see that the target point matters more for shallow slopes.
      3. 
    
    3. Run the staircase simulator with the same listener under all three rules and several seeds. Record the estimates on the board. Students should see that the rules converge on different levels and that step size and number of reversals change the spread.
      4. 
    
    4. Switch the simulator to 2AFC with 1-up 1-down and show the track sinking. Ask the class to explain why.
      5. 
    
    5. Run the DIN simulation with a true SRT near the illustrative cut-off and several seeds. Students should observe borderline cases flipping between pass and refer.
      6. 
    
    6. Calibration: play a reference tone from the test software into the coupler, read the SPL, enter the values into the calibration calculator and compute the digital level for 20 dB HL at 1 kHz and at 250 Hz. Show the effect of 16-bit versus 24-bit output.
      7. 
    
    7. Run a DIN app on a volunteer with the phone volume at a comfortable level, then repeat with the volume slightly lower. Students should see that the SRT changes little while the speech remains audible, illustrating the relative-level design.
      8. 
    
    8. Show the fitting software workflow: audiogram from NOAH, prescription targets, data logging screen, and (if available) a real-ear measurement against target.
      9. 
    
    9. Demonstrate live captions in a quiet room and then with background babble, and sound recognition with a recorded doorbell or alarm. Students should note the fall in caption accuracy with noise.
      10. 
    
    10. With the class, score one app against the evaluation framework in section 8.
      11. 
  
    

  
    Practical activity and assessment
    
  
    Linked practicals: P13 (tele-audiology design) and P14 (AI/ML audiology concept).
    
  
      
    
    1. Choose one computer-based assessment or intervention program available to you (from the catalogue tables or elsewhere).
      2. 
    
    2. Classify it on the three axes of section 1.
      3. 
    
    3. Find its documentation, privacy policy, regulatory status and at least one peer-reviewed study, if any exist.
      4. 
    
    4. If it is an assessment program, identify its procedure and target point; if it is a training program, identify its adaptive rule, feedback and dose.
      5. 
    
    5. If safe and appropriate, try it yourself and observe two classmates using it without help, noting any use errors.
      6. 
    
    6. Score it with the section 8 framework, with evidence for each item.
      7. 
    
    7. For P13: describe how the program would fit into a tele-audiology workflow for a primary health centre, including data flow and consent. For P14: describe how an adaptive or machine-learning method could make it more efficient and how you would validate that.
      8. 
  
    
  
    Deliverable: a four to six page review with the completed scoring table, a data-flow diagram, a one-paragraph recommendation (use, use with conditions, or do not use, for a stated purpose and population) and references.
    

  
    Exercises
    
  
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Classify (a) a PC-based automated audiometer in a clinic, (b) the hearWHO app, (c) a tinnitus sound therapy app from a hearing-aid manufacturer, by purpose, platform and likely regulatory category.
    Model answer: (a) assessment (diagnostic), clinic-based software with dedicated hardware, regulated medical device. (b) screening and awareness, mobile app, presented as a public-health screening tool; check its status in each country. (c) intervention (sound therapy), mobile app, often presented as a support or wellness tool; its status depends on its claims and jurisdiction.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Show why a 3-down 1-up rule converges on 79.4% correct. Which rule would you use for a 2AFC gap-detection task, and why not 1-up 1-down?
    Model answer: at equilibrium the probability of three correct in a row equals 0.5, so P^3 = 0.5 and P = 0.5^(1/3) = 0.794. For 2AFC, use 2-down 1-up or 3-down 1-up; 1-up 1-down targets 50%, which is chance in 2AFC, so the track would drift below the listener's real threshold.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    A yes/no listener has a logistic function with T = 40 dB and β = 0.25 per dB (no guessing or lapses). At what levels are 70.7% and 79.4% correct reached? What is the slope at the midpoint in % per dB?
    Model answer: 70.7%: 40 + 0.881/0.25 = 43.5 dB. 79.4%: 40 + 1.347/0.25 = 45.4 dB. Midpoint slope = β/4 = 0.0625, that is 6.25% per dB.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    A patient scores 17 key words correct on one QuickSIN list. Compute SNR-50 with the Spearman-Kärber formula and the SNR loss.
    Model answer: SNR-50 = 27.5 − 17 = 10.5 dB. SNR loss = 25.5 − 17 = 8.5 dB, meaning the patient needs about 8.5 dB better SNR than normal-hearing listeners for 50% key-word recognition on this material. A second list should be scored and averaged.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    At 4 kHz a tone at −20 dBFS RMS measures 88 dB SPL in the coupler. With an illustrative RETSPL of 9.5 dB, what digital level is needed for 30 dB HL, and what is the maximum dB HL available without clipping?
    Model answer: S = 88 − (−20) = 108 dB SPL. Target SPL = 30 + 9.5 = 39.5 dB SPL, so the level is 39.5 − 108 = −68.5 dBFS RMS. Maximum RMS for a sine is −3.01 dBFS, giving 104.99 dB SPL, that is about 95.5 dB HL at 4 kHz.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    A company reports a correlation of r = 0.95 between its app and clinical audiometry and says the app "agrees with audiometry". What further analysis would you ask for?
    Model answer: correlation shows association, not agreement: the app could read 10 dB higher for everyone and still have r = 0.95. Ask for Bland-Altman bias and 95% limits of agreement, the percentage of thresholds within 5 and 10 dB, results per frequency, test-retest reliability, and details of the population, device, transducer and environment.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Outline the design of a Malayalam sentence-in-noise training program for adult hearing-aid users, including adaptive rule, feedback, dose and outcome measures.
    Model answer: record equivalent sentence lists from several talkers; mix with babble; use a 2-down 1-up SNR rule (targets about 71%) with 2 dB steps; give feedback with replay and the correct text; sessions of about 20 minutes, five days a week for a few weeks, with logs visible to the clinician; outcomes: an untrained Malayalam speech-in-noise test and a validated questionnaire before and after, with a follow-up measurement; ensure consent and data protection under the DPDP Act; pilot usability with older adults.
    
    
    
    
    
      
    
      
    Plan a study to decide whether a boothless tablet audiometer can be used for screening children aged 6 to 10 years in government schools in Kerala.
    Model answer: target population: children in those schools, tested in the rooms actually available, by the health workers who would do screening. Index test: the tablet at the screening levels and frequencies; reference: diagnostic audiometry by an audiologist in a sound-treated room (plus otoscopy and tympanometry) within a short interval, with blinding. Randomise order where feasible. Define hearing loss in advance. Report sensitivity and specificity with 95% CIs, the number of children needed to obtain enough positives, ambient noise measurements and noise flags, test time, retest reliability on a subsample, usability observations and failures to complete. Obtain ethics approval and parental consent, and plan referral for all children found to have hearing loss.
